PREFACE 

Since the last publication of this book in 1926, many statutes have been enacted and numerous judicial decisions have been rendered which affect in no small degree the operation of contract law. This is illustrated by the enactment in New York in 1933 of statutes which materially amended in several respects the old English Statute of Frauds requiring certain classes of contracts to be evidenced by a written memorandum. So, too, statutes affecting the form of certain agreements which were never controlled by the Statute of Frauds became operative in May, 1934. Thus, in 

New York agreements made without consideration to postpone the time of payment of a debt are by legislative enactment made valid provided the agreement is in writing and signed by the parties thereto. 

The recent statutes and decisions are fully treated and cited in the text. As in the earlier editions, important articles in Law Reviews and Editorials in the New York Law Journal are noted for study. The Restatement of the Law of Contracts by the American Law Institute has not been without thoughtful attention. Although not frequently cited, we commend its study. Many of the restatements, however, are not in accord with New York law. 

This work is prepared and published primarily for use by law students. However, since lawyers have found that previous editions of this book have aided them in their search for authorities, they, too, may find this book of value. 

Although it is not designed as a treatise or exposition of 

New York law only, yet the New York law as applied to contracts is accentuated. The leading authorities in jurisdictions other than New York are liberally cited, especially where there is a conflict in the application of principles governing contractual obligations.

One cannot acquire either a practicable or a theoretical working knowledge of contracts from the text alone. The

iv 

PREFACE 

intellectual development, process of reasoning, and background must come from a thorough reading and study of cases in conjunction with the text. The reading of a case, however, means but little unless it is tied up to a principle which the case illustrates and supports. We are confident that a study of the text together with a careful reading of assigned cases and the authorities cited will give the student a broad and creditable knowledge of contract law. 

WILLIAM PAYSON RICHARDSON 

September, 1934

CONTRACTS CHAPTER I DEFINITION OF TERMS 

§ 1. 

Contract Defined. 

A contract is an agreement whereby one party acquires a legal right to an act or a forbearance on the part of another. Unless there is a promise to do, or to refrain from doing, something which gives the promisee a right in the future conduct of the promisor, there can be no contract. A contract must originate in a promise, a breach of which by the promisor will give to the other party (promisee) some legal remedy. If there is not a legal remedy, there is no contract. A written instrument acknowledging the receipt of a sum of money or goods is not a contract, for it is a mere record of a past transaction, containing no promise to do, or to refrain from doing, anything. Williston on Contracts, Vol. I, secs. 1 and 2. 

§ 2. Agreement Defined. 

An agreement is the expression of an intention by two or more persons to affect their legal relationship. If in the expression of the mutual intention a legal obligation is not imposed, there is only an agreement. If, however, a legal obligation is imposed, the agreement becomes a contract. Therefore, while mutual promises to perform illegal acts constitute agreements, they are not contracts, for they create neither legal rights nor obligations. Canute v. Minor, 232 App. Div. 325, 249 N. Y. S. 680, aff'd 

258 N. Y. 558, 180 N. E. 331. So, too, social engagements are not contracts, for the reason that the parties do not contemplate the creation of legal obligations.

2 

§ 3. Contract Obligations Distinguished from Obligations 

Imposed by Law.

An obligation imposes rights and duties recognized and enforced by law. Obligations are of two kinds: those that result from agreement; and those that result from operation of law. The former are contractual obligations and require the consent of the parties for their existence. Obligations arising by operation of law may be imposed irrespective of consent. The following will serve to illustrate obligations of the second type: 

1. Obligations arising from tort. The right of a person to be free from illegal interference with his person or with his property is conferred by law, independent of any contract. A violation of such right constitutes a tort, and binds the wrongdoer to compensate the injured person. 

Obligations ex contractu (arising out of contract) and obligations ex delicto (arising out of tort) are carefully distinguished in Rich v. New York Central & Hudson River 

R. R. Co., 87 N. Y. 382. See, also, Busch v. Interborough 

Rapid Transit Co., 187 N. Y. 388, 391, 80 N. E. 197.

This distinction is often very important for the reason, among others, that the time provided by the statute of limitations within which an action may be brought for the recovery of damages for an injury is generally longer in actions for breach of contract than in tort actions. Considerable difficulty has arisen in this connection in cases wherein the plaintiff has an election to sue either for breach of a contract or for a tort. For example, "The relation between a carrier and its passenger is more than a mere contract relation, as it may exist in the absence of any contract whatsoever. Any person rightfully on the cars of a railroad company is entitled to protection by the carrier, and any breach of its duty in that respect is in the nature of a tort and recovery may be had in an action of tort as well as for a breach of the contract." Gillespie v. Brooklyn 

Heights R. R. Co., 178 N. Y. 347, 70 N. E. 857, 66 L. R. A. 618.

DEFINITION OF TERMS 

3 2. Obligations arising out of the marriage relation. 

Marriage effects a change of status, and, by operation of law, many new incidental obligations are created, as, for instance, the duty of the husband to support his wife and children. 

These will be dis- 

3. Quasi-contractual obligations. cussed in the following section. 

§ 4. Quasi-Contractual Obligations Defined. 

Quasi-contractual obligations are obligations created by law. They are based on the theory that where a person is in possession of money or its equivalent, belonging to another, the law will create a fictitious contract to restore the same to its rightful owner although no such contract was, in fact, made or intended by the parties concerned. Quasi or constructive contracts rest solely on a legal fiction and are treated as contracts solely for the purpose of remedy, for they lack the element of agreement or consent which is essential to every true contract. Miller v. Schloss, 218 

N. Y. 400, 113 N. E. 337; Board of Highway Commissioners v. City of Bloomington, 253 Ill. 164, 97 N. E. 280. See, also, People ex rel. Dusenbury v. Speir, 77 N. Y. 144, 150, 57 How. Pr. 274.

A good illustration of a quasi-contractual obligation based on the theory of unjust enrichment is found in the case where money is paid under a mistake of fact. A recovery will be allowed, however negligent the party paying may have been in making the mistake, unless by that payment the situation of the defendant has been altered to his detriment. Hathaway v. County of Delaware, 185 N. Y. 368, 78 N. E. 153; Betz v. City of New York, 119 App. Div. 91, 103 N. Y. S. 886, aff'd 193 N. Y. 625, 86 N. E. 1122. Thus, in a case where plaintiff, under the erroneous impression that he owned a certain parcel of land, paid taxes to the city, it was held that he could recover the amount paid. 

Lesster v. Mayor, etc., City of New York, 33 App. Div. 350, 53 N. Y. S. 934, aff'd 161 N. Y. 628, 55 N. E. 1097.

The theory of a quasi-contractual obligation is further illustrated where a physician renders services to one who is injured and rendered unconscious by an accident. The right of the physician to recover for his services is well established. This right and liability are founded on necessity and humanity. There is no contract in fact, either express or implied, for an unconscious man could not give his assent; yet the law imposes an obligation in the same manner as though his assent were given. The liability to pay for the services rests upon a legal fiction invented in the interests of justice. Cotnam v. Wisdom, 83 Ark. 601, 104 

S. W. 164, 12 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1090; Edson v. Hammond, 142 App. Div. 693, 127 N. Y. S. 359.

Other illustrations are found in obligations to repay money received from another through fraud, duress, or undue influence, and in an infant's or an insane person's liability for necessaries. Sceva v. True, 53 N. H. 627, cited with approval in Edson v. Hammond, supra; Kilgore v. 

Rich, 83 Me. 305, 22 Atl. 176, 12 L. R. A. 859. On the same principle, sureties and guarantors may recover from their principals the money they have paid on account of their indebtedness to third persons. And if one surety pays more than his proportion, he may enforce contribution from his co-surety. Keener, Quasi-Contracts, pp. 19-25; Morgan v. Smith, 70 N. Y. 537. § 5.

Executory and Executed Contracts Defined. 

An executory contract is one, the conditions of which have not been fully performed; i.e., something remains to be done in the future on the part of one or both of the parties. Leadbetter v. Hawley, 59 Ore. 422, 117 Pac. 289.

An executed contract is one, the conditions of which have been fully performed; i.e., nothing remains to be done on the part of either party. Fletcher v. Peck, 6 Cranch (U.S.) 87, 136, 3 Law. Ed. 162.

A contract may be executed as to one party and execu-

DEFINITION OF TERMS 

5 tory as to the other, or it may be wholly executed or wholly executory as to both parties. Until fully executed by both parties, however, the contract is referred to as executory. 

§ 6. Bilateral and Unilateral Contracts Defined. 

Where one promise is given in return for another promise, the contract is bilateral. An offer that calls for a reciprocal promise can be accepted only by a promise of the offeree. 

But, if the offer of a promise is for an act, the performance of the act is the acceptance, and the contract is said to be unilateral. Such promise becomes binding only when the act is performed. This principle is frequently applied in the case of an offer of a reward, but it is not confined to this type of case. Assume that A promises B fifty dollars if he will move a certain safe from one office building to another. There is no obligation on B's part to move the safe because he has made no promise to do so. There is the offer of a promise for an act, and it requires the completion of the act to constitute an acceptance. If B promised to move the safe, the contract would be bilateral and B would be liable to A in case of his default. A unilateral contract does not properly imply any lack of mutuality of agreement, although it is sometimes considered in this sense. The real distinction between bilateral and unilateral contracts lies in the kind of acceptance called for. In bilateral contracts, the acceptance is by a promise; in unilateral contracts, the acceptance is by an act without a prior promise to perform the act. L'Amoreux v. Gould, 7 N. Y. 349, 57 Am. Dec. 524; Miller v. McKenzie, 95 N. Y. 

575, 47 Am. Rep. 85; Petterson v. Pattberg, 248 N. Y. 86, 161 N. E. 428.

The term "unilateral contract" is occasionally used in a loose sense by the courts to designate a mere offer by one party which has never been accepted by the other and which gives rise to no obligation because no contract has, in fact, been made. Such a transaction, though unilateral, is not a contract and should not be so termed. High Wheel

Auto Parts Co. v. Journal Co., 50 Ind. App. 396, 98 N. E. 

442, 443. For an illustration of such use of the term “unilateral contract," see Cohn v. Levine, 185 App. Div. 529, 173 N. Y. S. 289. 

§ 7. Express and Implied Contracts Defined. 

An express contract is one expressed in words, spoken or written. An implied contract is one that is to be inferred from the conduct of the parties. Gillan v. O'Leary, 124 App. Div. 498, 108 N. Y. S. 1024. The only difference, then, between an express and an implied contract is that in the former the parties arrive at an agreement by words, either verbal or written, while in the latter the agreement is arrived at by a consideration of their acts and conduct. 

People ex rel. Dusenbury v. Speir, 77 N. Y. 144, 150, 57 

How. Pr. 274.

Implied contracts must be distinguished from those obligations which are created by law without regard to the assent of the party upon whom the obligation is imposed. 

The latter obligations are founded on a legal fiction and are not true contracts. The former are contracts implied in fact; the latter are contracts implied in law. Miller v. 

Schloss, 218 N. Y. 400, 113 N. E. 337.

It is often difficult to determine from a given state of facts whether a contract may properly be implied from the conduct of the parties. Robinson v. Munn, 238 N. Y. 40, 143 N. E. 784. For a further discussion of contracts implied in fact and of the presumptions applicable thereto, see section 14, infra. 

§ 8. Void Contract Defined. 

A void contract is one which can have no legal effect. 

The parties thereto are, or should be placed, in the same position as they would have been had no agreement been made. The term is self-contradictory, since an agreement which produces no legal obligation cannot properly be called

DEFINITION OF TERMS 

7 a contract. It is convenient, however, to apply the term "void contract" to an agreement which purports to be a contract but which fails in its purpose. In this class are agreements which are contrary to law or against public policy. Many contracts are expressly declared by statute to be void; e.g., a wagering contract. Penal Law, sec. 992.

But the word "void" as used in the Statute of Frauds means merely unenforceable at the election of the party sued. See section 283, infra. 

§ 9. 

Voidable Contract Defined. 

A voidable contract is one that may be disaffirmed at the option of one of the parties. Such a contract is binding, however, until avoided by the party possessing the option. 

A good illustration is found in the contract of an infant with an adult. Although the infant may treat the contract as a binding one, should he so desire, and hold the adult to his promise, the adult, nevertheless, cannot hold the infant, because the latter has the right to disaffirm the contract on the ground of his infancy. Beardsley v. Hotchkiss, 96 N. Y. 201. Other illustrations are contracts to which the consent of one of the parties was obtained by fraud or duress. Conrow v. Little, 115 N. Y. 387, 393, 22 

N. E. 346, 5 L. R. A. 693; Oregon Pacific R. R. Co. v. Forrest, 128 N. Y. 83, 91, 28 N. E. 137. The right to avoid may be lost by waiver or by ratification. 

§ 10. Unenforceable Contract Defined. 

An unenforceable contract is one which, when proper objection is taken thereto, is incapable of such proof as is required by law. Such a contract may, nevertheless, be valid. 

In this class are oral contracts which are required by the Statute of Frauds to be in writing. Such oral contracts, being valid although not in the form prescribed by law, may be proved unless the defendant avails himself of the defense of the statute in his answer or, in proper cases, raises

the defense by motion. Moreover, an oral contract within the Statute of Frauds may, despite objection, become enforceable at a later time by reason of a written acknowledgment of its terms executed in compliance with the provisions of the statute. This subsequently executed written acknowledgment supplies the necessary legal proof of the existence of the contract. See Chapter XIII, infra. § 11.

Elements of Contract. 

In order that a contract may exist, the following four elements are requisite and will be discussed in subsequent chapters in the order named: 

1. Offer and acceptance; 

2. Competent parties; 

3. Legal subject matter; 

4. Sufficient consideration.

CHAPTER II  

$12.

How Offer and Acceptance May Originate. 

The agreement necessary to create a contract must originate in an offer or proposal on one side and its acceptance on the other. There are four ways in which the offer and acceptance may originate: 

1. Offer of a promise under seal; 

2. Offer of a promise for an act; 

3. Offer of an act for a promise; 

4. Offer of a promise for a promise. 

1. Offer of a Promise under Seal. Under the common law, if one party makes a gratuitous promise in writing and under seal to pay money to another, and the promisee assents to the proposal, both are bound. The assent is necessary because one cannot be forced to accept a gratuity. 

The common law rule which makes the seal conclusive evidence of a consideration is in New York, however, modified by statute. The Civil Practice Act, section 342, provides that a seal upon executory contracts raises a mere presumption of consideration which may be rebutted. The present 

New York rule is, therefore, that where a written promise under seal to pay money or to transfer property is wholly gratuitous without consideration-the promisor is not liable, for no promise is binding unless the promisor obtains something from the promisee. This something, which may be an act, a forbearance, or a promise, is called a consideration. In the case of executed instruments, such as releases and receipts under seal, the common law rule as to a conclusive presumption of consideration still prevails because they are not contracts or executory instruments. 

Stiebel v. Grosberg, 202 N. Y. 266, 95 N. E. 692, 36 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1147.

2. Offer of a Promise for an Act. This form of offer and acceptance is illustrated by an offer of reward. Where a reward is offered for the return of lost property, the return of the property is the act that constitutes the acceptance. The act creates a unilateral contract. Pierson v. 

Morch, 82 N. Y. 503. So, also, where a person sends an advertisement to a newspaper, its publication is an acceptance of the offer to pay for it. Ahern v. Standard Life Insurance 

Co., 32 N. Y. Super. (2 Sweeny) 441. 

3. Offer of an Act for a Promise. The offer of an act for a promise arises where one offers services or goods to those who promise to pay for them. For instance, if B steps into a public conveyance, he impliedly promises to pay for his transportation. This is the offer of an act for a promise. The one who accepts the offered services impliedly promises by his acceptance to pay for his transportation. The proposition is well illustrated in Day v. 

Caton, 119 Mass. 513, 20 Am. Rep. 347. 

4. Offer of a Promise for a Promise. The offer of a promise for a promise is the common type of contract resulting from mutual promises. Where A promises to pay

B a certain sum if B will promise to render certain services for him, and B promises, the contractual obligation is complete. The promises are mutual and each is the consideration for the other. White v. Corlies, 46 N. Y. 467. Without mutuality there can be no contractual obligation. Thus, a contract by which defendant agreed to furnish plaintiff with such coal as should be ordered by him up to 25,000 tons per year but which did not obligate plaintiff to purchase any quantity whatsoever was held unenforceable for lack of mutuality. Leach v. Kentucky Block Cannel Coal Co., 256 Fed. 686. See, also, Chicago & Great Eastern Ry. Co. v. 

Dane, 43 N. Y. 240.

There is no contract, therefore, until there is a definite meeting of the minds of the contracting parties upon all the essential terms to be agreed upon. This occurs only when a definite and unconditional offer has been made by one

party and unqualifiedly accepted by the other. Watts v. 

Thomas Carter & Son, Inc., 207 App. Div. 656, 659, 202 

N. Y. S. 852. 

§ 13. 

Offer and Expression of Intention Distinguished. 

A mere expression of an intention or willingness to do some act in the future is not an offer. An offer differs from an intention in that it expresses or imports a willingness on the part of the offerer to incur a legal obligation to the one to whom it is made. If A says to B, "I will sell you my horse for $400," it is an offer. If he says, "I intend to sell my horse for $400," it is merely a declaration of intention. The acceptance of the former would turn the offer into a promise, but the latter is incapable of acceptance for there is no offer. If A says to B, "Will you give me $500 for this horse?" and B says, "I will," or if B says to A, "Will you take $500 for this horse?" and A says, "I will," there is no agreement. In both illustrations the alleged offer is no more than an interrogation. While an offer need not be in any particular form of words, yet it must clearly import a willingness on the part of the offerer to be bound to the one to whom it is made. Homan v. Earle, 53 N. Y. 267; Knudsten v. Remmel, 141 App. Div. 

445, 126 N. Y. S. 249. It is not always an easy matter to determine whether a given conversation is to be construed as an offer or as a mere expression of intention. It is often a close question of fact which the court may submit to the jury. Thruston v. Thornton, 1 Cush. (Mass.) 89. 

§ 14. Offer and Acceptance by Words or by Conduct. 

An offer or its acceptance, or both, may be expressed in words, spoken or written. So, too, a contractual obligation may be created without a written or spoken word on the part of either party, as in the case where A renders services for B with the latter's knowledge, but without his express request, and under such circumstances that no reasonable person would suppose that A intended his services to

be gratuitous. The offer is the doing of the work; the acquiescence in its being done is the acceptance. Had there been an express contract for doing the work, the rights of the parties would be controlled by the terms of the contract; but in the absence of an express agreement, B will be held liable on his implied promise to pay a fair and reasonable compensation for the services. There is a true agreement, although unexpressed in words. This inference is one of fact which is usually to be drawn by the jury in the light of all of the circumstances. Lombard v. Rahilly, 127 Minn. 449, 149 N. W. 950. To illustrate: If a man leaves his automobile in a garage for repairs, the only reasonable inference to be drawn, in the absence of any special circumstances indicating a contrary intention, is that both parties intended compensation for the work done. 

This presumption of fact, however, may be entirely overcome by evidence of other facts, such as the sale of the car to its present owner by the garage owner with the understanding that it would run well or that he would make the necessary adjustments. Helber v. Schaible, 183 Mich. 379, 150 N. W. 145.

There is no presumption of an implied contract to pay for services rendered where, because of the relationship of the parties, it is natural that such services should be performed without expectation of pay. Fox v. Arctic Placer 

Mining & Milling Co., 229 N. Y. 124, 128 N. E. 154. For example, where services are rendered by a near relative, especially where such relative is a member of the recipient's household, no presumption of an implied contract to pay for such services will arise from the mere fact that the services were rendered and accepted. Hodge v. Hodge, 47 

Wash. 196, 91 Pac. 764, 11 L.R.A. (N.S.) 873. The same rule has been applied where there was no blood relationship between the parties, but they maintained with each other the ordinary relation of members of the same family, as in the case of stepfather and stepchild. William v. Hutchinson, 3 N. Y. 312, 53 Am. Dec. 301.

Similarly, where a salaried employee renders services outside the scope of his duties, no presumption of an implied agreement to pay an additional sum for the reasonable value of such services will arise, unless the services are so distinct from the ordinary duties of his employment and of such a nature that it would be unreasonable for the employer to assume that they were rendered without expectation of additional pay. Robinson v. Munn, 238 N. Y. 40, 143 N. E. 784.

The same principles apply to the case of services rendered by a physician. The general rule is that a physician who treats a patient with the latter's consent is entitled to recover from the patient the reasonable value of his services. But, where it appeared that the patient was under restraint and observation, due to a deranged mental condition, and was forced to submit to the visits of numerous doctors, including the plaintiff, his own physician having been excluded, it was held that the patient could not be deemed to have acquiesced in the employment and, therefore, could not be held liable to pay for the services, no necessity for their rendition having been shown. Edson v. 

Hammond, 142 App. Div. 693, 127 N. Y. S. 359. It is well settled that where a physician has been called in by a third person who is under no obligation to provide medical aid for the patient, there is no implied promise on the part of the one who summoned the physician to pay for his services. 

McGuire v. Hughes, 207 N. Y. 516, 101 N. E. 460, 46 L. R. 

A. (N.S.) 577.

Voluntary services, no matter how meritorious or beneficial they may be, as the saving of property from destruction by fire or water, afford no ground for liability. Bartholomew v. Jackson, 20 Johns. (N.Y.) 28, 11 Am. Dec. 237.

Likewise, a farmer cannot recover for the use of his barn or team of horses where he did not intend to make any charge at the time the services were rendered because of similar courtesies extended to him without charge by the defendant. 

Potter v. Carpenter, 76 N. Y. 157. The same is true in

the oft-repeated case where one nurses an invalid without the intention of charging for such services but merely in the hope of being remembered in a very substantial manner in the will of the invalid. When, after the death of the invalid, it is discovered that little or no money is left to the nurse, the latter cannot recover for the services from the estate of the deceased on any theory of contract, quasi-contract, or otherwise. Martin v. Wright's Administrators, 13 Wend. (N.Y.) 460, 28 Am. Dec. 468. § 15. 

Communication of Offer.

An offer must be communicated to the offeree before it can be accepted. If the offer is a promise for an act, the act must be performed with knowledge of the offer; otherwise there can be no acceptance, since one cannot assent to something of which he has no knowledge. 

An offer of a reward is an offer which requires the performance of an act as an acceptance. Since every contract, unilateral as well as bilateral, requires mutual assent, the act must be performed with an accepting mind. The first requisite of this accepting mind is the knowledge of the offer. Therefore, it is held in New York that one cannot recover a reward if he did not know of the offer when he performed the act for which the reward was offered. Fitch v. Snedaker, 38 N. Y. 248, 97 Am. Dec. 791; Howland v. 

Lounds, 51 N. Y. 604, 10 Am. Rep. 654. The weight of authority in other jurisdictions supports the New York rule. Broadnax v. Ledbetter, 100 Tex. 375, 99 S. W. 1111, 9 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1057. It has been held, also, that the act must be performed voluntarily, with a view to obtaining the reward, and not under compulsion or in the discharge of an official duty. Vitty v. Eley, 51 App. 

Div. 44, 64 N. Y. S. 397; Atwood v. Armstrong, 102 App. 

Div. 601, 92 N. Y. S. 596. For a discussion of the circumstances under which constables, peace officers, or other public officers may be entitled to claim rewards offered for the arrest of criminals, see 34 L. R. A. (N.S.) 924, note.

A few courts have permitted the plaintiff to recover a reward although he had performed part of the work called for prior to his learning of the reward. Coffey v. Commonwealth, 18 Ky. 646, 37 S. W. 575; Hoggard v. Dickerson, 180 Mo. App. 70, 165 S. W. 1135. The New York rule and the Restatement of the Law of Contracts are to the contrary. Restatement, Contracts, sec. 53; Fitch v. Snedaker, supra.

In some cases there is difficulty in determining when an offer may be said to have been communicated. The rule is thoroughly sound, however, that where an offer is brought to the attention of the offeree in such a manner that by the use of ordinary intelligence he should be aware of it, he is, by his acceptance, conclusively held to have assented to its terms. For example, where one accepts a written offer which he has had a fair opportunity to read, he is bound by its terms, in the absence of convincing proof of fraud or misrepresentation, even though he has not read the terms and hence does not know them. Farrell v. Chandler, 252 

Mass. 341, 147 N. E. 822. On this principle, express company receipts, bills of lading, steamship tickets, insurance policies, etc. contain many stipulations which are seldom read and yet, by the great weight of authority, are held to be binding terms of the contract. Steers v. Liverpool, New York & Philadelphia S. S. Co., 57 N. Y. 1, 15 Am. Rep. 

453; Metzger v. Aetna Insurance Co., 227 N. Y. 411, 125 

N. E. 814; Murray v. Cunard S. S. Co., 235 N. Y. 162, 139 

N. E. 226, 26 A. L. R. 1371.

The courts recognize a distinction, however, between the above-mentioned documents and railroad tickets, baggage transfer receipts, and parcel room checks, in that the former are understood by the parties receiving them to contain the terms of a contract of transportation or insurance, whereas the latter are generally accepted as mere tokens or vouchers to be used as evidence of the right to certain transportation or for identification of baggage. Mills v. 

Weir, 82 App. Div. 396, 81 N. Y. S. 801; Lichterman v.

Barrett, 95 Misc. 594, 159 N. Y. S. 929. In New York, conditions printed on railroad tickets or baggage checks are not held, as a matter of law, to constitute a part of the contract between the parties. Whether they constitute a part of such contract is held to be a question of fact depending on whether the attention of the holder is shown to have been directed to them. Grossman v. Dodd, 63 Hun 

324, 17 N. Y. S. 855, aff'd 137 N. Y. 599, 33 N. E. 642; 

Hutchins v. Pennsylvania R. R. Co., 181 N. Y. 186, 73 N. E. 

972, 106 Am. St. Rep. 537. The New York rule with respect to conditions printed on baggage checks has been followed in several other jurisdictions. Dodge v. Nashville, Chattanooga & St. Louis R. R. Co., 142 Tenn. 20, 215 

S. W. 274, 7 A. L. R. 1229; authorities reviewed in 27

A. L. R. 157, note. The reason for the distinction between the cases where the law imputes knowledge of the stipulations and those where it does not lies in the importance, character, and purpose of the instruments. 

Matter printed in small type at the top or bottom of he stationery on which an offer is written and not referred to in the body of the writing is not necessarily to be considered a part of such offer. Sturtevant Co. v. Fireproof 

Film Co., 216 N. Y. 199, 110 N. E. 440, L. R. A. 1916D 

1069. But it was held, in Poel v. Brunswick-Balke-Collender Co., 216 N. Y. 310, 323, 110 N. E. 619, that printed clauses which are as plain and as prominently displayed on the face of the instrument as the written terms of the offer and are not in conflict therewith are deemed a part of such offer, as a matter of law. § 16.

Intention To Effect Legal Relation. 

The parties to the agreement must intend to create legal obligations; otherwise there can be no contract. Therefore, an offer made in jest and known to be thus made by the party accepting it is without legal effect, as in the case of a marriage ceremony intended by both parties as a jest, or the giving of a check for $300 for a watch worth $15, "by

way of mere frolic and banter." McClurg v. Terry, 21 N. 

J. Eq. 225; Keller v. Holderman, 11 Mich. 248, 83 Am. 

Dec. 737; Crouch v. Wartenberg, 86 W. Va. 664, 104 S. E. 

117, 11 A. L. R. 212.

In Dorgeloh v. Murtha, 92 Misc. 279, 156 N. Y. S. 181, the plaintiff, a young girl, was persuaded to go through an actual marriage ceremony with the defendant in order that she might obtain a marriage certificate which would assist her to secure a theatrical engagement. Neither party intended a valid marriage, and the parties thereafter always continued to regard the ceremony as having no binding effect. An annulment was granted.

However, a person will not be heard to allege that he was jesting where his conduct and words would warrant a reasonable person to believe that he intended to be bound. Keller v. Holderman, supra; Crouch v. Wartenberg, supra. $ 17. 

Offer Distinguished from Preliminary Negotiations.

It is often important to distinguish between an offer intended to give rise to a contract when accepted by the offeree and mere preliminary negotiations intended to procure information or to induce the other party to make an offer. In other words, there is an obvious distinction between an offer to make a contract and an offer to negotiate in reference to the possibility of making a contract. "If a proposal is nothing more than an invitation to the person to whom it is made to make an offer to the proposer, it is not such an offer as can be turned into an agreement by acceptance. Proposals of this kind, although made to definite persons and not to the public generally, are merely invitations to trade. They go no further than what occurs when one asks another what he will give or take for certain goods. Such inquiries may lead to bargains, but do not make them. They ask for offers which the proposer has a right to accept or reject as he pleases." Pomeroy v. 

Newell, 117 App. Div. 800, 102 N. Y. S. 1098; Topliff v.

Schimpff, 196 App. Div. 198, 205, 187 N. Y. S. 617, 622, aff'd 233 N. Y. 584, 135 N. E. 927.

Common examples of proposals which our courts regard as preliminary negotiations or invitations to deal are: merchants' and brokers' circulars, catalogues, letters, and newspaper advertisements setting forth the terms upon which they stand ready to sell goods; advertisements for bids for municipal work, and this, too, even where a statute provides that the contract shall be awarded to the lowest bidder; advertisements of auction sales; and railroad time tables scheduling the departure and arrival of trains. Moulton v. Kershaw, 59 Wis. 316, 18 N. W. 172; Molloy v. New 

Rochelle, 198 N. Y. 402, 92 N. E. 94, 30 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

126; Gordon v. Manchester & Lawrence R. R. Co., 52 N. H. 

596, 13 Am. Rep. 97. In Moulton v. Kershaw, supra, the defendants wrote to the plaintiff as follows: "In consequence of a rupture in the salt trade, we are authorized to offer Michigan fine salt in full car-load lots of eighty to ninety-five bbls. delivered in your city at 85 cents per bbl. 

At this price it is a bargain, as the price in general remains unchanged. Shall be pleased to receive your order." 

On the following day the plaintiff telegraphed: "Your letter of yesterday received and noted. You may ship me 

2,000 bbls. Michigan fine salt, as offered in your letter. 

Answer." It was held that the letter and telegram did not together make a contract; the letter was construed as an advertisement to the effect that the writers were in a position to supply salt at the price named and requesting the person to whom it was addressed to deal with them, but not as an offer by which, if accepted, defendants were to be bound. Another good illustration is found in Lovett v. Loeser & Co., 124 Misc. 81, 207 N. Y. S. 753. In that case, a party sued a leading Brooklyn department store for an alleged breach of contract in refusing to deliver two radio sets ordered by the plaintiff in response to the defendant's advertisement in a New York newspaper. The 

Court held that the defendant's advertisement was "nothing

but an invitation to enter into negotiations" and "not an offer which may be turned into a contract by a person who signifies his intention to purchase some of the articles mentioned in the advertisement."

It has been held, however, that a newspaper advertisement may constitute a sufficiently definite offer and when accepted create a contract. The defendant inserted in several newspapers the following advertisement: "One hundred pounds reward will be paid by the Carbolic Smoke Ball Co. to any person who contracts the increasing epidemic of influenza colds, or any diseases caused by taking colds, after having used the ball three times daily for two weeks, according to the printed directions supplied with each ball." The plaintiff, relying upon the advertisement, used a smoke ball in accordance with the directions, but nevertheless contracted the influenza. It was held that a contract was established, and that the plaintiff was entitled to recover the promised sum. The Court said: "If the vendor of an article whether it be medicine smoke or anything else, with a view to increase its sale or use, thinks fit, publicly to promise to all who buy or use it that to those who shall not find it as surely efficacious as it is represented by him to be, he will pay a substantial sum of money, he must not be surprised if occasionally he is held to his promise." Carlill v. Carbolic Smoke Ball Co., 2 Q. B. Div. (Eng.) 484 (1892).

Quotation of price, even in response to a request, has been held not to constitute an offer. In Beaupre v. Pacific & Atlantic Telegraph Co., 21 Minn. 155, the plaintiff wrote: "Have you any more northwestern mess pork? Also extra mess? Telegraph price on receipt of this." 

The reply was telegraphed: "Letter received. No light mess here. 

Extra mess $28.75." The plaintiffs replied by telegraph: "Despatch received. Will take two hundred extra mess, price named." The Court held that there was no contract. 

Similarly, a railroad company is not liable for a failure to transport a passenger in accordance with its published

time table if it "had done all that due care and skill could do" to transport him punctually. Gordon v. Manchester & Lawrence R. R. Co., supra. "The publication of a time table, in common form, imposes upon a railroad company the obligation to use due care and skill to have the trains arrive and depart at the precise moments indicated in the time table; but it does not import an absolute and unconditional engagement for such arrival and departure, and does not make the company liable for want of punctuality which is not attributable to their negligence." Sears v. Eastern 

R. R. Co., 14 Allen (Mass.) 433, 92 Am. Dec. 780.

Invitations to negotiate, and not final offers, are made by such propositions as the following: A wrote a letter to B saying: "Advise us by wire Monday, if you can use about 

1500 creosote barrels, between now and January 1st, at 

95c. each, delivered in carload lots." B answered: "We accept your offer of fifteen hundred barrels, as per yours of the 7th inst." It was held that no contract was made, since A's letter was an invitation to enter into negotiations and was not intended to create a legal relation. Cherokee 

Tanning Extract Co. v. Western Union Telegraph Co., 143 

N. C. 376, 55 S. E. 777. In Baston v. Toronto Vinegar

Co., 4 Ontario Law. Rep. 20, the plaintiff wrote to the defendant on May 5th, saying: "Are you going to buy cucumbers this year, and what are you going to pay for them? As you have always dealt fair with me, I would like to sell you some more this year." Defendant replied: "We will be pleased to take all you grow at same price as last year. We will see you later and make final arrangements." No further communication took place between the parties until August of the same year, when the plaintiff sent several loads of cucumbers to defendants, who accepted and paid for them, nothing being said as to a contract. When defendant refused to take the balance of the crop, an action was brought for breach of contract. 

It was held that the defendant's letter was not such an offer as could be turned into a contract, either by accept-

ance or by delivery of the cucumbers, but was the statement of their readiness to make a contract upon terms to be arranged.

No precise rule can be deduced from the authorities and in close cases the courts frequently have difficulty in arriving at the intention of the parties, but where the terms of the proposal are definite and specific and the circumstances surrounding the transaction indicate that the parties intended to enter into a binding agreement, the proposal will be construed as an offer which ripens into a contract upon acceptance. For a review of early and leading cases in various jurisdictions illustrating the distinction between a definite offer and a mere preliminary step in the negotiation of a contract, see note to Cedar Rapids 

Lumber Co. v. Fisher, 129 Iowa 332, 105 N. W. 595, in 4 

L. R. A. (N.S.) 177. 

§ 18. Definiteness of Offer. 

The terms of the offer must be definite, for if an indefinite offer is accepted, it cannot be said that the minds of the parties have fully met upon all of the terms of the contract. 

The court cannot and will not make a contract for the parties, and it is powerless to enforce an agreement if unable to determine its exact character. The court is "not at liberty to revise while professing to construe." Sun Printing & 

Publishing Assn. v. Remington Paper & Power Co., 235 

N. Y. 338, 346, 139 N. E. 470; Varney v. Ditmars, 217 N. Y. 

223, 111 N. E. 822; Von Reitzenstein v. Tomlinson, 249 

N. Y. 60, 162 N. E. 584.

Thus, a promise to pay "a fair share" of the profits as additional compensation is unenforceable for indefiniteness. 

Varney v. Ditmars, supra. Similarly, an agreement to furnish news at a price "not exceeding three hundred dollars during each and every week that said news report is received" is too indefinite to allow a recovery of damages for a refusal to receive the service. United Press v. New York 

Press Co., 164 N. Y. 406, 58 N. E. 527, 53 L. R. A. 288.

However, where goods have been furnished or services rendered in reliance upon an agreement which is void for indefiniteness, a recovery based upon reasonable value will be allowed. Von Reitzenstein v. Tomlinson, supra. Moreover, a promise which is too indefinite to create a contract may by performance become definite and enforceable. Williston on Contracts, Vol. I, sec. 49; Grundt v. Shenk, 222 

App. Div. 82, 225 N. Y. S. 217, aff'd 248 N. Y. 602, 162 

N. E. 541; compare, Restatement, Contracts, sec. 33. 

§ 19. Definiteness of Offer-Terms Capable of Being 

Ascertained.

A contract containing one or more indefinite terms is not too indefinite to be enforced, provided the contract itself contains some definite means of ascertaining the obligations of the parties. For example, a promise to furnish enough money to operate certain mines until the said mines shall “yield sufficient returns to pay for working and operating the same" is enforceable, for "the test of the amount to be furnished or advanced is perfectly definite." San Remo 

Copper Mining Co. v. Moneuse, 149 App. Div. 26, 133 

N. Y. S. 509. Stern v. Premier Shirt Corp., 260 N. Y. 201, 183 N. E. 363.

Similarly, an offer to furnish three named steamships with coal for one year at a certain sum per ton, when accepted by the owner of the vessels, was held to create a valid contract for the sale of the amount of coal that the three steamers required for fuel in their ordinary work for the period of one year. Wells v. Alexandre, 130 N. Y. 642, 29 N. E. 142, 15 L. R. A. 218. The Court said, at p. 644: "It is very clear that the language employed by the plaintiff in the light of surrounding circumstances was intended to make as definite as possible, the quantity of coal which the defendants would be required to take. The quantity to be measured by the requirements of the three steamers for the year ensuing in an employment about which they had long been engaged. So, while at the date of the agreement the

1 quantity was indefinite, it was, nevertheless, determinable by its terms and, therefore, certain, within the maxim, certum est quod certum reddi potest."

So, also, an agreement is not rendered unenforceable because of indefiniteness where some of its terms are not expressed, but are, nevertheless, implied by law. For example, a contract calling for the delivery of merchandise is not rendered void for uncertainty because the time for delivery is not specified. The law will imply an agreement to deliver within a reasonable time after demand. Spiritusfabriek Astra v. Sugar Products Co., 176 App. Div. 829, 163 N. Y. S. 516, aff'd 221 N. Y. 581, 116 N. E. 1077. 

§ 20. Definiteness of Offer-Offer To Deliver What May 

Be Required.

An offer to deliver all that may be needed or required of a certain commodity in an established business during a specified period is not too indefinite to ripen into a contract upon acceptance. Phillips-Jones Co. v. Reiling & Schoen, 193 

App. Div. 716, 726, 184 N. Y. S. 387. But, where the promise to sell and deliver is supported by no consideration other than the promise of the buyer to purchase, the acceptance must bind the offeree to take all that is required in the business, for otherwise the agreement would be void for want of mutuality of obligation. See Wickham & Burton Coal 

Co. v. Farmers' Lumber Co., 189 Iowa 1183, 179 N. W. 417, 14 A. L. R. 1293. Such a promise may be implied, however, from the language used and the circumstances surrounding the contract. Edison Electric Illuminating Co. v. Thacher, 229 N. Y. 172, 128 N. E. 124; Ehrenworth v. Stuhmer & 

Co., 229 N. Y. 210, 128 N. E. 108. For example, in the case last cited, it appeared that the defendants, bakers, agreed to sell to the plaintiff, a dealer in bread with an established trade route, all the black bread which he would require for his customers as long as both parties remained in business, and the plaintiff agreed to sell no other black bread on his route. The Court, in upholding the contract, held

that the plaintiff thereby impliedly promised to buy all the black bread which he could use on his route. Such an agreement will be enforced, however, only where the offeree is engaged in an established business of such a nature that the quantity which is likely to be required can be estimated with a reasonable degree of accuracy. Schlegel Manufacturing Co. v. Cooper's Glue Factory, 231 N. Y. 459, 132 

N. E. 148, 24 A. L. R. 1348.

Where the goods are for the buyer's own use or for consumption in a factory or plant, it is comparatively easy to estimate the quantity likely to be required during a given period; e. g., the coal required for fuel on three steamships operating over a regular route, the manhole frames and cover castings required by an electric illuminating company, or all the steel castings required in an established manufacturing business. Wells v. Alexandre, 130 N. Y. 642, 29 N. E. 142, 15 L. R. A. 218; Edison Electric Illuminating 

Co. v. Thacher, supra; Lima Locomotive & Machine Co. v. National Steel Castings Co., 155 Fed. 77, 11 L. R. A. (N.S.) 713. Where the buyer's business is merely the resale of the commodity which is the subject of the contract, it has been contended that the agreement should be held void, on the ground that it leaves the buyer free to order an unlimited quantity in the event of a rising market or to place no order in the event of a decline in the market, his requirements depending entirely on the profit that can be realized on the contract. However, the courts have taken the view that there is no substantial basis for a distinction between the requirements of a wholesale or retail merchant with an established trade and those of a manufacturer who uses the commodity as raw material. Jenkins & Co. v. 

Anaheim Sugar Co., 247 Fed. 958, L. R. A. 1918E 293. 

See, also, Ehrenworth v. Stuhmer & Co., supra. In the language of the Court, in Jenkins & Co. v. Anaheim Sugar

Co., supra, at p. 961: "The temptation to speculate may be greater on the part of the dealer than of the manufacturer, but if the contract is honestly entered into, the law

ought not to refuse to enforce its terms for any such reason as possible misuse of its purposes."

Where the buyer is not engaged in an established business with a definite market for the goods, the agreement will be held void for want of mutuality of obligation in cases where the seller's promise to deliver is supported by no consideration other than the vendee's promise to buy. 

For example, in Schlegel Manufacturing Co. v. Cooper's

Glue Factory, supra, an agreement for the sale of the plaintiff's requirements of glue for one year was held invalid.

The Court, at p. 461, said: "The plaintiff, at the time, was engaged in no manufacturing business in which glue was used or required, nor was it then under contract to deliver glue to any third parties at a fixed price or otherwise. 

It was simply a jobber, selling, among other things, glue to such customers as might be obtained by sending out salesmen to solicit orders therefor. The contract was invalid since a consideration was lacking. Mutual promises or obligations of parties to a contract, either express or necessarily implied, may furnish the requisite consideration. The defect in the alleged contract here under consideration is that it contains no express consideration, nor are there any mutual promises of the parties to it from which such consideration can be fairly inferred. The plaintiff, it will be observed, did not agree to do or to refrain from doing anything. It was not obligated to sell a pound of defendant's glue or to make any effort in that direction. It did not agree not to sell other glue in competition with defendant's. 

The only obligation assumed by it was to pay nine cents a pound for such glue as it might order. Whether it should order any at all rested entirely with it." An interesting application of the principle is found in Nassau Supply Co., Inc. v. Ice Service Co., Inc., 252 N. Y. 277, 169 N. E. 383.

Where the parties have entered into a valid contract for the sale of a merchant's requirements of a given commodity, it is the duty of the seller to furnish the entire requirements of the business, provided the buyer acts in good faith, and

the fact that conditions are such that a much larger quantity is required than in previous years has been held to be no ground for refusal to deliver. New York Central Iron 

Works Co. v. United States Radiator Co., 174 N. Y. 331, 66 N. E. 967. But the buyer has no right to use the contract for speculative, as distinguished from regular and ordinary, business purposes, and the courts will not enforce his demand for the delivery of goods in excess of a reasonable supply for his legitimate trade. Wheeler Co. v. 

Mendleson, 180 App. Div. 9, 167 N. Y. S. 435. Authorities are reviewed in 7 A. L. R. 498, note.

A merchant who contracts to purchase his requirements of a commodity for a stipulated period of time cannot, unless authorized by the contract, avoid his obligation by terminating his business within such time. Wells v. Alexandre, supra; Central Power & Light Corp. v. United 

States Zinc Co., 60 Fed. (2d) 832, discussed in 18 Cornell 

Law Quarterly 269. § 21. Terms To Be Agreed upon Later.

Since no contract is created until the minds of the contracting parties have met upon all of the essential terms, it follows that no essential term can be left to be agreed upon in the future. It is well settled that where parties agree upon most of the terms of a proposed contract but leave at least one essential term open for future negotiations, their entire agreement is held to be unenforceable, on the ground that it is nothing more than an "agreement to agree." 

Mayer v. McCreery, 119 N. Y. 434, 23 N. E. 1045; Sun

Printing & Publishing Assn. v. Remington Paper & Power 

Co., 235 N. Y. 338, 139 N. E. 470; Ansorge v. Kane, 244 

N. Y. 395, 155 N. E. 683. To illustrate: In Brown v. New 

York Central R. R. Co., 44 N. Y. 79, it appeared that the parties agreed in writing to enter into a lease of a certain railroad at an annual fixed rental with the right to extinguish the rental "at a time to be agreed upon," by the payment of a stipulated sum of money. It was held that no

contract had been created, on the ground that "the time when the defendant should be at liberty to change its status from lessee to owner was purposely left open for future negotiations; as were also the form and covenants of the lease, the rental certificates and other details." 

§ 22. Option as to Quantity. 

An offerer may submit a limited choice of terms to the offeree. Such an offer may not be accepted by simple assent, however, for the reason that there can be no “meeting of the minds" until the offeree has made the terms of the contract definite by specifying his selection. To illustrate: A railroad company offered to transport from New York to Chicago upon stated terms "not exceeding six thousand tons" of iron during a specified period. The offeree replied: "I assent to your agreement, and will be bound by its terms." 

Holding that there was no contract binding on either party, the Court of Appeals said: "The plaintiff was at liberty to accept this proposition for any specified quantity not beyond that limited; and had it done so, a contract mutually obligatory would have resulted therefrom 

But the plaintiff did not so accept." Chicago & Great Eastern Ry. 

Co. v. Dane, 43 N. Y. 240. * * * Contracts for the sale of goods from a stated minimum to a stated maximum quantity are, however, created by a simple acceptance. They are construed to bind the seller to deliver, and the buyer to receive, the minimum amount and to vest an option in one party to increase the quantity up to, but not beyond, the stated maximum. Farquhar Co. v. 

New River Mineral Co., 87 App. Div. 329, 84 N. Y. S. 802; 

Editorial, New York Law Journal, October 31, 1932. For example, an offer to purchase "200 to 300 tons" of iron will ripen into a contract by a simple acceptance, with an option in the purchaser to increase the quantity not beyond 300 tons. Farquhar Co. v. New River Mineral Co., supra. It is frequently difficult to determine which party possesses the option. Editorial, New York Law Journal, supra.

§ 23. 

General Offer Defined.

A general offer is an offer made either to the public at large, in which case it may be accepted by any one, or to a designated class of persons, in which case it may be accepted by any one in that class. 13 Corpus Juris 273; Schorestene v. Iselin, 69 Hun 250, 23 N. Y. S. 557. A common illustration of a general offer is an offer of a reward for the return of lost property or for information leading to the arrest and conviction of a criminal. Pierson v. Morch, 82 

N. Y. 503; Fitch v. Snedaker, 38 N. Y. 248, 97 Am. Dec. 791. 

§ 24. Unequivocal Acceptance Essential. 

In order to create a binding contract, there must be not only a plain and unequivocal offer but also an unambiguous acceptance, showing a clear intention to accept the offer in every particular precisely as made. Barrow S. S. Co. v. 

Mexican Central Ry. Co., 134 N. Y. 15, 31 N. E. 261, 17 

L. R. A. 359; Barber-Greene Co., Inc. v. M. F. Dollard, Jr., Inc., 239 App. Div. 655, 269 N. Y. S. 211. Thus, a mere acknowledgment of an order, with the bare statement that it will receive attention, is not an acceptance. Krohn- 

Fechheimer Co. v. Palmer, 282 Mo. 82, 221 S. W. 353, 10

A. L. R. 673. And an attempted acceptance which varies the terms of the offer is inoperative to create a contract, but amounts, instead, to a new offer by the original offeree. 

See section 25, infra. 

§ 25. Effect of Qualified Acceptance. 

A proposal to accept an offer if modified, or an acceptance subject to other terms and conditions than those stated in the offer, is held to be equivalent to an absolute rejection of the offer. Poel v. Brunswick-Balke-Collender Co., 216 N. Y. 

310, 319, 110 N. E. 619. Such a proposal is, in effect, a counter offer, which, in order to ripen into a contract, must be accepted by the party making the original offer. Wittwer v. Hurwitz, 216 N. Y. 259, 110 N. E. 433; Seventeen 

West Fifth Street Corp. v. Tolerton, 107 Misc. 609, 177

N. Y. S. 897. In Wittwer v. Hurwitz, supra, for example, the defendants offered the plaintiff a commission of $2,500, provided he should effect a sale of certain property within a specified time for the sum of $25,000, $10,000 of which was to be paid in cash and the balance by assuming a certain mortgage. The plaintiff notified the defendants that he had a purchaser ready to take the property for $25,000, "subject to the mortgage thereon." The defendants replied that they would close, provided the purchaser would pay $10,000 before midnight of that day. The plaintiff failed to accede to this demand. The Court pointed out that the plaintiff had not complied with the conditions of the defendant's original offer by procuring a purchaser who would assume the mortgage, but had submitted a new offer to produce a purchaser who would take subject to the mortgage, and that the defendants, in turn, had made a qualified acceptance, conditioned on immediate payment of $10,000. Since this conditional acceptance was, in effect, a counter offer, which was not accepted by the plaintiff, it was held that no contract had been made. 

An offer which has been rejected by a counter proposition loses its vitality and cannot be revived by a subsequent acceptance. Minneapolis & St. Louis Ry. Co. v. Columbus 

Rolling Mill Co., 119 U. S. 149, 30 Law. Ed. 376, 7 Sup. Ct. 

Rep. 168; Shaw v. Ingram-Day Lumber Co., 152 Ky. 329, 153 S. W. 431, L. R. A. 1915D 145. For example, in Minneapolis & St. Louis Ry. Co. v. Columbus Rolling Mill Co., supra, the defendant offered the plaintiff 2000 to 5000 tons of iron rails on stated terms. The plaintiff, in reply, ordered 1200 tons, which the defendant refused to deliver. 

The plaintiff then ordered 2000 tons in compliance with defendant's original offer, but the Court held that the plaintiff had, in effect, rejected defendant's offer and could not thereafter accept.

It is important to distinguish between a qualified acceptance and a positive acceptance coupled with a request for a modification or explanation of the terms of an offer.

In the latter instance, whether or not the request is granted, a contract is created. American Woolen Co. v. Moskowitz, 159 App. Div. 382, 144 N. Y. S. 532; authorities cited in 

Williston on Contracts, Vol. I, sec. 79. 

§ 26. 

Communication of Acceptance.

Whether an acceptance shall consist of a promise or an act depends upon the nature of the offer. An offer which calls for a promise when duly accepted results in a bilateral contract; whereas an offer which requires the performance of an act results in a unilateral contract. In either case, there must be more than a mere mental determination to accept. In the case of a bilateral contract, the acceptance must be communicated to the officer. White v. Corlies, 46 

N. Y. 467; Fowler-Curtis Co. v. Dean, 203 App. Div. 317, 196 N. Y. S. 750; Mactier's Administrators v. Frith, 6 

Wend. (N.Y.) 103, 21 Am. Dec. 262. In Howard v. Daly, 61 

N. Y. 362, 19 Am. Rep. 285, a written offer of employment made to an actress was held to be accepted by her when it was signed, taken to the theatre, and there deposited in a letter box which was sometimes used as a place of deposit for contracts made between the actors and the producer. 

In the case of a unilateral contract, the performance of the required act is, ordinarily, a sufficient acceptance, and notice to the offerer of its performance is not necessary unless such notice has been made a condition of the offer. 

Leesley Bros. v. A. Rebori Fruit Co., 162 Mo. App. 195, 144 

S. W. 138. For example, a letter guaranteeing the account of a certain individual for a fixed sum was held to be accepted by a sale and delivery of goods in reliance thereon, so that it became a binding obligation without notice to the guarantor. American Woolen Co. v. Moskowitz, 159 

App. Div. 382, 144 N. Y. S. 532. In some jurisdictions, it is held that where the act is of such a nature that knowledge of its performance will not readily come to the promisor, the promisee is bound to give him notice within a reasonable time after performance of the act constituting

the acceptance, but this requirement is a condition subsequent which does not affect the formation of the contract. § 

27. Contracts by Mail.

It is well settled that an offer transmitted by mail is deemed accepted as soon as a letter of acceptance, duly addressed and stamped, is deposited in a post office or street letter box. The contract is deemed complete at that moment and cannot thereafter be repudiated by either party, even though the letter of acceptance never reaches the offerer. 

Adams v. Lindsell, 1 Barn. & Ald. 681, 106 Eng. Rep. 250; 

Mactier's Administrators v. Frith, 6 Wend. (N.Y.) 103, 21 Am. Dec. 262; Vassar v. Camp, 11 N. Y. 441. The theory advanced in modern decisions is that the offerer, by using the mail as his agent to transmit the offer, has given the offeree implied authority to use the same agency to receive the reply. Wester v. Casein Co. of America, 206 N. Y. 506, 513, 100 N. E. 488, 490.

It has been held, accordingly, that an acceptance which is transmitted by a different agency from that employed or requested by the offerer is not effective until delivery to the offerer. Scottish American Mortgage Co. v. Davis, 96 

Tex. 504, 74 S. W. 17, 97 Am. St. Rep. 932; Lucas v. Western Union Telegraph Co., 131 Iowa 669, 109 N. W. 191, 6 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1016. For example, in the case last cited, where an offer which did not specify any mode of acceptance was sent by mail and accepted by telegraph, it was held that no contract was made until the telegram was received by the offerer. 

§ 28. Offer Requiring Receipt of Acceptance. 

The offerer may make it a condition of his offer that no contract shall be made until an acceptance has been received by him, and where the language of the offer clearly indicates an intention to impose this condition, the contract will be held to be incomplete until the acceptance has actually been received. Postal Telegraph Cable Co. v. Louisville

Cotton Seed Oil Co., 140 Ky. 506, 131 S. W. 277; Lewis v. 

Browning, 130 Mass. 173. However, such intention must be evidenced by the language used. Vassar v. Camp, 11 

N. Y. 441. § 29. 

Contracts by Telegraph.

An acceptance of an offer by telegraph is governed by the same principles which apply to an acceptance of an offer by mail. Thus, the acceptance of an offer made by telegraph is complete when the telegram of acceptance is left with the telegraph operator. Trevor v. Wood, 36 N. Y. 

307, 93 Am. Dec. 511; Chesebrough v. Western Union Telegraph Co., 76 Misc. 516, 135 N. Y. S. 583, aff'd 157 App. 

Div. 914, 142 N. Y. S. 1112. 

§ 30. Mistake in Transmission of Offer by Telegraph. 

When a telegram making an offer is altered in the course of its transmission and is accepted in good faith by the offeree, the question arises as to whether the offerer is bound. The loss caused by the alteration must fall in the first instance upon either the sender or the receiver, leaving to the party upon which it falls such redress as he may have against the telegraph company. The better authorities hold that it is more equitable, as between the sender and the receiver, that the party who selects the telegraph as the means of communication shall bear the loss caused by the error of the telegraph company. Ayer v. Western Union 

Telegraph Co., 79 Me. 493, 10 Atl. 495, 1 Am. St. Rep. 353; 

Des Arc Oil Mill v. Western Union Telegraph Co., 132 Ark. 

335, 20 S. W. 273, 6 A. L. R. 1081, and authorities there cited. For an able opinion in support of the contrary view, see Pepper v. Western Union Telegraph Co., 87 Tenn. 554, 11 S. W. 783, 4 L. R. A. 660. The Court, in that case, after a careful review of the authorities, based its decision on the ground that a telegraph company should be regarded not as an agent of the sender but as an independent contractor and that, upon principles of contract law, the

offerer should not be bound by the terms of an offer which he did not make. $ 31.

Contracts by Telephone. 

There is little authority, and none in New York, on the question as to when a contract made over the telephone is complete. Presumably the rule in some jurisdictions is that such a contract is consummated when the offeree speaks his acceptance. Tyng & Co. v. Converse, 180 Mich. 

195, 146 N. W. 629; authorities cited in Williston on Contracts, Vol. I, sec. 82. If the telephone is comparable to the mail or telegraph, this rule is undoubtedly sound. However, on the theory that the telephone is simply another means of oral communication, it has been urged that the rules of acceptance governing oral conversations where the parties are in the presence of each other should properly be applied to acceptances by telephone. Restatement, Contracts, sec. 65; article by M. O. Tobriner on "Mistake in an Acceptance by Telephone," 98 Central Law Journal 63. § 32.

Mode of Acceptance Prescribed by Offerer. 

The offerer may prescribe not only the time within which his offer must be accepted but also the place where the acceptance is to be sent and the manner of indicating acceptance. Any such specifications in the offer must be strictly complied with by the offeree in order to create a contract. Thus, in Eliason v. Henshaw, 4 Wheat. (U.S.) 

225, 4 Law. Ed. 556, a letter containing an offer was sent from Harper's Ferry by a wagoner, with a request for an answer "by return of wagon." The wagoner, however, informed the offeree that he probably should not return to Harper's Ferry, whereupon the latter sent his acceptance by mail, addressed to the offerer at Georgetown, and it was there received by him. The Court held that no contract was made, on the ground that "an acceptance communicated at a place different from that pointed out by the plaintiffs, and forming a part of their proposal, imposed no

obligation binding upon them, unless they had acquiesced in it, which they declined doing." This is an extreme case, and it is doubtful whether many courts would go so far. 

A requirement that an offer be accepted at a specified time or place or in a prescribed manner must be distinguished from an offer which merely suggests a particular place, time, or manner of acceptance. In the latter instance, another method of acceptance, so long as the acceptance is received, may be employed; whereas in the former instance, only strict compliance with the terms of the offer will create a contract. Williston on Contracts, Vol. I, sec. 76; Restatement, Contracts, sec. 61. 

§ 33. Silence of Offeree as Acceptance. 

The only limitation on the power of an offerer is that he cannot, under ordinary circumstances, so phrase his proposition as to turn the silence of the offeree into an accept- He cannot, for example, by writing a letter stating that unless notified to the contrary he will consider his offer accepted, compel the offeree to reply to the letter if the offeree does not wish to be bound. Prescott v. Jones, 69 N. H. 305, 41 Atl. 352. In the language of the Court in More v. New York Bowery Fire Insurance Co., 130 N. Y. 

537, 547, 29 N. E. 757: "A person is under no obligation to do or say anything concerning a proposition which he does not choose to accept." He has the right to remain silent, as silence and inaction under ordinary circumstances cannot be construed as an acceptance. 

In the absence of special circumstances, where goods are shipped with an accompanying letter to the effect that if the goods are not returned by or before a certain date they will be considered sold, the party to whom the goods are sent is under no obligation to return the goods or to notify the sender of his intention not to buy. Where, however, there has been a prior course of dealing between the parties of such a nature that the offeree is under a duty to speak, for the reason that his silence is inconsistent with honest

dealings and calculated to mislead, silence may be interpreted as an acceptance. Hobbs v. Massasoit Whip Co., 158 Mass. 194, 33 N. E. 495; Wheeler v. Klaholt, 178 Mass. 

141, 59 N. E. 756; Realty Records Co. v. Pierson, 116 N. Y.

S. 547. In Hobbs v. Massasoit Whip Co., supra, for example, the plaintiff, on four or five prior occasions, had shipped eel skins to the defendant, which the latter had invariably accepted and paid for. The Court held that this prior course of dealing imposed upon the buyer a duty to act in regard to the shipment of eel skins in controversy, and that his silence coupled with the retention of the goods for an unreasonable length of time might be found by the jury to justify the seller in assuming that the goods had been accepted.

An interesting question arises where magazines or newspapers are mailed regularly to one who has not subscribed for them or whose subscription has expired. In Fogg v. 

Portsmouth Atheneum, 44 N. H. 115, 82 Am. Dec. 191, the successors to the former publishers of a paper found on the mailing list the name of a corporation which controlled a library and reading room and continued to send it the paper, in ignorance of the fact that the former publishers had been instructed to discontinue. The paper was taken from the mail regularly and placed in the reading room. 

The Court held that the corporation was liable on the theory of implied assent. Similarly, in Austin v. Burge, 156 Mo. 

App. 286, 137 S. W. 618, a former subscriber was held liable simply by reason of having taken a paper regularly from the post office, though he had twice notified the publisher to discontinue sending it.

Where goods are sent, without request, to a person who has no reasonable grounds for believing that they were intended as gifts, and who uses them, or in any way deals with them as his own, an acceptance is implied from such use. See authorities cited in 13 Corpus Juris 274. An interesting application of this principle is found in the case of 

Bowley v. Fuller, 121 Me. 22, 115 Atl. 466. Here the de-

fendant left hay stored in the barn on premises of which plaintiff became lessee without any agreement as to the price of storage. Plaintiff subsequently wrote defendant that, unless the hay was removed, a certain specified price would be charged for storage. Defendant failed to reply to the letter. The Court held that defendant's failure to reply was not an acceptance of such price, as a matter of law, so as to create a contract, declaring that a mere failure to object cannot be converted into an acceptance. § 34.

Intention To Embody Contract in Formal Writing. 

Where parties enter into an oral or informal written agreement with the intention of later reducing the agreement to a formal writing to be executed by them, the validity and effect of the informal agreement without the execution of the formal writing depends upon the intention of the parties. Mississippi & Dominion S. S. Co., Ltd. v. 

Swift, 86 Me. 248, 29 Atl. 1063; Editorial, New York Law

Journal, February 4, 1929. The question in each case is whether the parties intended the formal writing to be a mere record of a prior, although informal, contract, or whether the execution of the formal writing was intended to be a condition precedent to the creation of the contract. 

To ascertain the intention of the parties, the courts will consider whether the parties have agreed upon all the essential terms of the contract. If the informal agreement is silent as to any details which would ordinarily be included in such a contract, the informal agreement is merely a preliminary step in the negotiations and not a contract. 

Arnold v. R. Rothschild's Sons Co., 37 App. Div. 564, 56 

N. Y. S. 161, aff'd 164 N. Y. 562, 58 N. E. 1085; Sherry v. 

Proal, 131 App. Div. 774, 116 N. Y. S. 234; Spielvogel v. 

Veit, 197 App. Div. 804, 189 N. Y. S. 899. In numerous cases, on the other hand, it has been held that where the minds of the parties have fully met upon all of the essential terms of the contract, the informal agreement constitutes a contract. Sanders v. Pottlitzer Bros. Fruit Co., 144 N. Y.

209, 39 N. E. 75, 29 L. R. A. 431; Disken v. Herter, 73 

App. Div. 453, 77 N. Y. S. 300, aff'd 175 N. Y. 480, 67 

N. E. 1081.

Thus, in Sanders v. Pottlitzer Bros. Fruit Co., supra, the parties, by an exchange of letters and telegrams, had reached a point where a definite offer for the sale of ten carloads of apples had been unequivocally accepted, and the parties had agreed to draw a formal contract embodying the terms of their agreement. The plaintiff then prepared a contract containing precisely the terms agreed upon, but the defendant refused to sign it unless it was modified in several essential particulars. It was held that the contract was complete without the execution of a formal instrument and that both parties were bound by the terms of the informal agreement. As stated by the Court, at p. 

214: "Any other rule would always permit a party who has entered into a contract like this through letters and telegraphic messages to violate it whenever the understanding was that it should be reduced to another written form, by simply suggesting other and additional terms and conditions. If this were the rule the contract would never be completed in cases when by changes in the market or other events occurring subsequent to the written negotiations it became the interest of either party to adopt that course in order to escape or evade obligations incurred in the ordinary course of commercial business." 

In one case where the parties had agreed upon all of the substantial terms of the contract, the Court went so far as to hold that "the understanding that the contract should be formally drawn up and put in writing did not leave the transaction incomplete and without binding forces, in the absence of a positive agreement that it should not be binding until so reduced to writing and formally executed." 

Disken v. Herter, supra. The more general statement of the rule, however, is that, if it appears that the parties did not intend to be bound until the execution of a formal con-

tract, neither party is bound until that is done. Donnelly v. 

Currie Hardware Co., 66 N. J. L. 388, 49 Atl. 428.

It is frequently said that the fact that the parties intended to execute a formal instrument, although not conclusive, is strong evidence that they did not intend to be bound until that was done. Sullivan v. Happy Hour 

Amusement Co., 177 App. Div. 232, 236, 163 N. Y. S. 715; 

Potomac Insurance Co. v. Kelly, 173 App. Div. 791, 160 

N. Y. S. 161. § 35.

Duration of Offer-Reasonable Time. 

An offer, silent as to the time within which it may be accepted, is presumed to continue for a reasonable time. 

Chicago & Great Eastern Ry. Co. v. Dane, 43 N. Y. 240, 243. What constitutes a reasonable time depends upon the circumstances of each particular case. The nature of the contract is an important factor in reaching a decision. 

For example, the acceptance of proposals relating to commodities such as stocks, bonds, and grain that are momentarily changing in their market value should immediately follow the offer, whereas, in the case of an offer to sell real property, five days have been held not unreasonable. Minneapolis Linseed Oil Co. v. Collier Lead Co., 4 Dillon (U. S.) 

431, Fed. Cas. No. 9635; Kempner v. Cohn, 47 Ark. 519, 1 S. W. 869, 58 Am. Rep. 775. An offer of a reward for the arrest and conviction of a criminal has been held to remain open for acceptance over a period of years. See authorities cited in Williston on Contracts, Vol. I, sec. 54. 

The mode of communicating the offer is, also, of weight in determining the time within which an acceptance may reasonably be expected. An offer made at a personal interview between the parties ordinarily requires an acceptance at that time, unless it is expressly held open for the further consideration of the offeree. If the party addressed leaves and returns the next day, or the next hour-or it may be, if he leaves at all, and afterwards returns-and attempts to accept the offer, it will be too late unless the

offerer assents thereto. Averill v. Hedge, 12 Conn. 424.

A telegraphed offer generally calls for an acceptance within at least a few hours. See Minneapolis Linseed Oil Co. v. Collier Lead Co., supra. An offer sent by mail has been held to continue until the offeree has received the letter and has had a fair opportunity to answer it. Mactier's Administrators v. Frith, 6 Wend. (N. Y.) 103, 21 Am. Dec. 262. A period of forty-eight hours after receipt of the letter has been held to constitute an unreasonable delay as a matter of law. Averill v. Hedge, supra. 36. 

Revocation of Offer.

An unaccepted offer creates no right and, therefore, may be withdrawn at any time before acceptance. For example, an order for goods which has not yet been accepted by the firm to which it was sent may be canceled by the proposed buyer, even though he has expressly stipulated that the order could not be countermanded. Cary v. Appo, 84 

N. Y. S. 569; Howe Scale Co. v. Wolfshant, 170 N. Y. S. 943. 

Bids at auction sales are mere offers which may be withdrawn at any time before the fall of the hammer; and, except where the sale has been announced to be without reserve, the auctioneer may, before the contract is complete, withdraw the goods from sale. Uniform Sales Act, sec. 21, subd. 2; Personal Property Law, sec. 102, subd. 2.

Where an offer has been modified by the offerer, in the course of negotiations between the parties, the original offer has thereby been revoked and is no longer open for acceptance. Schenectady Stove Co. v. Holbrook, 101 N. Y. 

45, 4 N. E. 4. 

§ 37. Agreement To Hold Offer Open. 

Even though the party making the offer agrees to hold it open for acceptance for a definite time, in the absence of a consideration for his promise, he may, upon giving notice to the offeree, withdraw his offer before the time has expired. Dickinson v. Dodds, 2 Ch. Div. (Eng.) 463; Cole-

man v. Applegarth, 68 Md. 21, 11 Atl. 284, 6 Am. St. Rep. 

417. But unless revoked, such a proposal remains in force as a continuing offer until the expiration of the specified period, capable of ripening into a contract upon acceptance at any time during that interval. Boston & Maine R. R. 

Co. v. Bartlett, 3 Cush. (Mass.) 224.

Where a valid consideration has been given by the offeree in exchange for the offerer's promise to hold the offer open for a stated length of time, the offerer is not at liberty to withdraw his offer before the expiration of that period. 

This constitutes an independent contract between the parties, known as an option. Coleman v. Applegarth, supra. § 38. 

Revocation of Offer for Unilateral Contract.

Since no unilateral contract is created until the act for which the offer was made has been fully performed, the offerer may withdraw his offer at any time before the completion of the act, even though the offeree has begun performance, thereby expending time or money, or both, relying upon the continuance of the offer. Wherever possible in such cases, courts construe the offer as contemplating a bilateral contract and spell out an acceptance from the part performance.
