"The manufacturer of a land roller who uses in its construction a tongue made of wood unfit for the purpose in that it was cross-grained, had a knot in it and also a knothole, and who plugs up the hole, and then by means of putty and paint conceals the defects so that they cannot be seen by inspection, is liable in an action for damages for fraud and deceit for injuries sustained in consequence of such defects by a person who purchased the machine from a dealer who had purchased it from another dealer to whom the manufacturer had sold it." Kuelling v. Roderick Lean Manufacturing Co., supra.

However, it is an essential element of a cause of action for fraud and deceit that the alleged representations should be calculated to influence the plaintiff, or that he be one of the class to whom such representations were addressed and that such representations should have been calculated to influence members of such class. Hence, a representation made to the public to induce the purchase of certain stock is not sufficient to sustain an action for deceit by a broker who was merely induced to expend time and labor in acting as a broker in the sale of the stock. Pollak v. Dodge 

Manufacturing Co., 78 Misc. 350, 138 N. Y. S. 429. 

§ 73. That Other Party Acted upon Representation to 

His Injury.

In order to establish fraud, it is necessary to show that the complaining party believed the false representation to be true and acted upon the faith of it to his damage. 

Hotchkin v. Third National Bank, 127 N. Y. 329, 27 N. E. 

1050; Ochs v. Wood, 221 N. Y. 335, 117 N. E. 305. For example, where a purchaser obtained credit for a lot of top buggies bought in April by falsely representing himself to be worth $15,000, and in October of the same year purchased more buggies on credit, it was held that the

seller was not entitled to rescind the October sale for fraud because there was nothing to show that the October sale was induced in any way by the representation made in April. Hotchkin v. Third National Bank, supra. In every case of fraud there must be both deception and injury. One cannot be deceived if he does not know of the representation, or if he knows that it is false. Brackett v. Griswold, 112 N. Y. 454, 20 N. E. 376. And even if the representation is made directly to the complaining party, who is ignorant of its falsity, if he does not rely on such representation but makes an investigation for himself and acts on his own erroneous judgment, no relief will be granted. Where a prospective purchaser, for example, undertakes to make an investigation of his own and the vendor does nothing to prevent his investigation from being as full as he chooses to make it, the purchaser cannot afterwards claim that he relied on false representations made by the vendor. Southern Development Co. v. Silva, 125 U. S. 247, 31 Law. Ed. 

678, 8 Sup. Ct. Rep. 881.

In an action for damages for fraud, the plaintiff must prove that he has suffered pecuniary loss by the fraud of which he complains; otherwise, he cannot recover. Urtz v. 

New York Central & Hudson River R. R. Co., 202 N. Y. 

170, 95 N. E. 711; Krohnthal v. Ranger, 188 App. Div. 594, 177 N. Y. S. 280. For example, in Urtz v. New York Central & Hudson River R. R. Co., supra, in which the plaintiff sued for damages for fraudulently inducing her to release a claim, the Court held that she had no cause of action unless her original right of action had a value greater than the consideration paid for the release. 

However, the court of equity may decree the rescission of a contract, on the ground of fraudulent representations, prior to the time when pecuniary injury results. Harlow v. La Brum, 151 N. Y. 278, 45 N. E. 859. So, also, a party has been allowed to rescind a contract to purchase an orchard represented to be planted with a certain variety of trees, even though the variety actually planted was of

equal value. McGowan v. Willamette Valley Irrigated Land 

Co., 79 Ore. 454, 155 Pac. 705. $ 74. Remedies for Fraud.

Fraud permits the injured party either to rescind the contract or to affirm the contract and sue for damages for the tort. Even though the contract recites that it is made under the representations therein expressed and no other and should be incontestable for fraud, yet the defrauded party is not estopped from his remedies. One cannot grant immunity to himself from the consequences of his own fraud. Bridger v. Goldsmith, 143 N. Y. 424, 38

N. E. 458. However, in the field of life insurance, it is recognized that the insurer may make a binding agreement that the policy shall be incontestable even for fraud. Some courts have approved such clauses making the policy incontestable from the time of its issuance. See Union Central 

Life Insurance Co. v. Fox, 106 Tenn. 347, 61 S. W. 62, 82

Am. St. Rep. 885. Contra, Reagan v. Union Mutual Life Insurance Co., 189 Mass. 555, 76 N. E. 217, 2 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

821. By the weight of authority, however, such a clause is valid only if the policy is made incontestable after a stated period not unreasonably short. Murray v. State Mutual 

Life Insurance Co., 22 R. I. 524, 48 Atl. 800, 53 L. R. A. 

742. By statute in New York, no life insurance policy may be issued unless it contains "a provision that the policy shall be incontestable after it has been in force during the lifetime of the insured for a period of two years from its date of issue except for non-payment of premiums and except for violation of the conditions of the policy relating to military or naval service in time of war and at the option of the company provisions relative to benefits in the event of total and permanent disability and provisions which grant additional insurance specifically against death by accident may also be excepted." Insurance Law, sec. 101, subd. 2; Metropolitan Life Insurance Co. v. Conway, 252 

N. Y. 449, 169 N. E. 642.

The defrauded party is not restricted to a single remedy. 

He may affirm the contract and sue for damages or he may sue for a rescission. If he elects to rescind and recover the consideration paid, he may do so by restoring or offering to restore the thing received, or he may sue for a rescission of the contract. It should be observed that an important distinction lies between an action for a rescission and an action based on a prior rescission. In the latter instance the defrauded party, without the aid of a court of equity, of his own volition, rescinds; while in the former he asks. the court to make the rescission for him. Heckscher v. 

Edenborn, 203 N. Y. 210, 96 N. E. 441; Hedges v. Pioneer

Iron Works, 166 App. Div. 208, 151 N. Y. S. 495. In Wood v. Dudley, 188 App. Div. 136, 176 N. Y. S. 494, the remedies of a defrauded party are stated as follows: "(1) He may rescind the contract by promptly tendering back all that he has received under it. He may then bring an action at law upon the rescission to recover back what he has paid, or (2) defend an action brought against him on the contract, setting forth the fraud and rescission as a defense. (3) He may bring an action in equity for rescission, the tender may be made in the complaint and must be kept good at the trial, and the court will adjust the relief as equity requires upon the facts established. (4) 

He may affirm the contract and sue for his damages. (5) If sued upon the contract, he may counterclaim his damages.'

The limitations on the right to rescind a fraudulent contract are as follows: 99 

1. If, with knowledge of the fraud while the contract remains executory, a defrauded party does any act in affirmance of the contract, he thereby condones the fraud and can thereafter neither rescind nor maintain an action for deceit. People v. Stephens, 71 N. Y. 527. Thus, where a contract of sale has been induced by the fraud of the vendor, if the purchaser consummates the purchase after discovery of the fraud, he cannot thereafter rescind or sue for damages for deceit. Vernol v. Vernol, 63 N. Y. 45;

Pryor v. Foster, 130 N. Y. 171, 29 N. E. 123. And in 

Schagun v. Scott Manufacturing Co., 162 Fed. 209, it was held that the renewal of notes given in payment of a brick machine after the discovery that it was not as warranted precluded recovery. 

1a. If knowledge of the fraud is first obtained after the contract has been executed, and, with that knowledge, the defrauded party does any act in affirmance of the contract, he thereby loses the right to rescind, although he may still maintain a suit for damages. In Brennan v. National 

Equitable Investment Co., 247 N. Y. 486, 160 N. E. 924, for example, plaintiff commenced an action at law based upon a prior rescission to recover money paid for stock under a contract of sale alleged to have been fraudulently induced. While the action was pending, the plaintiff accepted and knowingly retained a dividend declared upon the said stock. It was held that the acceptance and retention of the dividend constituted an affirmance of the contract and a waiver of the rescission. 

2. Delay in rescinding after a discovery of the fraudlaches-may deprive the defrauded party of the right to rescind. Strong v. Strong, 102 N. Y. 69, 5 N. E. 799. 

3. Consideration must be returned as a condition precedent to the right to rescind. Gould v. Cayuga County 

National Bank, 86 N. Y. 75. However, the consideration is not required to be returned as a condition precedent to the right to rescind in the following cases: a. Where the consideration is destroyed or taken from the control of the defrauded party without his fault. Hammond v. Pennock, 61 N. Y. 145. b. Where the consideration is of no value. Babcock v. Case, 61 Pa. St. 427, 100 Am. Dec. 654. c. If, by natural causes or reasonable use, the value of the consideration is diminished, it may be returned in its depreciated condition. Baker v. Lever, 67 N. Y. 304, 23 Am. Rep. 117.

When he acts promptly, a party may repudiate the contract, even though the other party cannot be placed in status quo. The law will not undertake to indemnify the guilty party for expenditures made in the prosecution of his fraudulent purpose. Guckenheimer v. Angevine, 81 N. Y. 

394; Hammond v. Pennock, supra.

The right to rescind may be lost by third persons acquiring rights under the contract. If a fraudulent vendee sells the goods to a bona fide purchaser, the vendor's only remedy is an action for damages. An assignment by a fraudulent vendee to a bona fide creditor in payment of a preexisting debt would not pass title, because the creditor is not a bona fide purchaser. Uniform Sales Act, sec. 24; Personal 

Property Law, sec. 105; Parker v. Connor, 93 N. Y. 118, 45 Am. Rep. 178; Hotchkin v. Third National Bank, 127 

N. Y. 329, 27 N. E. 1050.

If possession of the property alone and not the title is acquired, the sale may be rescinded, even as against an innocent purchaser, because the person in possession has no title and, therefore, can give none. Kinsey v. Leggett, 71 N. Y. 387.

If the fraud goes to the identity of the person, and not to the quality of the goods or circumstances of the sale, the contract may be voidable for fraud or void for mistake. 

Phelps v. McQuade, 220 N. Y. 232, 115 N. E. 441, L. R. A. 

1918B 973; Mercantile National Bank v. Silverman, 148 

App. Div. 1, 132 N. Y. S. 1017, aff'd 210 N. Y. 567, 104

N. E. 1134. The question depends upon how the mistake as to identity was brought about; i. e., whether by correspondence or by sight and hearing-face to face.

CHAPTER VI  § 75. Duress Defined. 

To constitute duress sufficient to render a payment or consent to a contract involuntary, there must be an actual or threatened exercise of power by one person over the person or property of another, from which the latter has no means of immediate relief other than by making the payment or consenting to the contract. Radich v. Hutchins, 95 U. S. 210, 24 Law. Ed. 409; Hackley v. Headley, 45 

Mich. 569, 8 N. W. 511; Adrico Realty Corp. v. City of 

New York, 250 N. Y. 29, 164 N. E. 732, 64 A. L. R. 1.

The person exerting, or threatening to exert, such power must, however, have the means at hand, or the injured party must believe that such power is in fact possessed. 

Hence, a threat to kill a person in a distant state does not amount to duress. Barrett v. Mahnken, 6 Wyo. 541, 48

Pac. 202. The question in each case is whether, taking into consideration the age, sex, and condition of the parties, the will of the person who alleges duress was in fact overcome, so that his consent to the contract or payment, although actual, did not represent a true expression of his will. Galusha v. Sherman, 105 Wis. 263, 81 N. W. 495, 47 

L. R. A. 417.

The present doctrine of duress, which is stated above, is considerably broader than the early common law rule which limited the defense of duress to cases where the will of the party was overcome by fear of illegal imprisonment or great bodily harm. No threatened loss of land or property was then sufficient to constitute duress in a court of law. Galusha v. Sherman, supra. $ 76. Effect of Duress. 

Duress renders a contract voidable, not void, at the option 88

of the injured party. Since the contract is valid until avoided, and since it may be ratified after the duress has ceased to operate, one entitled to avoid a contract on the ground of duress must do so promptly. Oregon Pacific 

R. R. Co. v. Forrest, 128 N. Y. 83, 28 N. E. 137. § 77. Duress, Fraud, and Undue Influence Distinguished.

It is often difficult to draw a sharp line between duress, fraud, and undue influence. This is true for the reason that two or all three elements often occur in the same transaction. Between fraud and duress, however, there is a clear and essential difference: fraud always involves deception; duress always involves coercion or compulsion. 

It is not so easy to draw a line between duress and undue influence, because of the fact that the terms are often used interchangeably and that which is called duress in some jurisdictions is called undue influence in others. See Haydock's Executors v. Haydock, 34 N. J. Eq. 570, 38 Am. Rep. 

385; Adams v. Irving National Bank, 116 N. Y. 606, 23 

N. E. 7, 6 L. R. A. 491. Where the coercion is not sufficient to amount to duress, but a social, moral, or domestic force is exerted on a party, which controls the free action of his will and prevents any true consent in the making of a contract, it is regarded as undue influence. Munson v. 

Carter, 19 Neb. 293, 27 N. W. 208. Duress may be said to involve a "physical" force; whereas undue influence may be said to involve a "psychological" force. § 78. Fear of Bodily Injury.

Under the early common law rule, fear of bodily injury was not sufficient to constitute duress unless the party entered into a contract: (1) for fear of loss of life; (2) for fear of loss of limb; (3) for fear of mayhem; or (4) for fear of imprisonment. Many early decisions in this country accepted this rule. Hence, under the early rule, fear of assault and battery was not sufficient to constitute duress, and this view was taken in this country, particularly in the

earlier decisions. See Brown v. Pierce, 7 Wall. (U.S.) 205, 215, 19 Law. Ed. 134; Secor v. Clark, 54 N. Y. Super. Ct. (22 J. & S.) 494. It is not, however, the present rule in this country. Fear induced by a threat of assault and battery may be sufficient to constitute duress. Foshay v. Ferguson, 5 Hill (N.Y.) 154. The ultimate fact in each case is whether or not the will of the party was free. Joannin v. 

Ogilvie, 49 Minn. 564, 52 N. W. 217, 16 L. R. A. 376. Unlike the common law rule, the question today is not whether there were acts or threats sufficient to overcome the mind of a courageous, firm, prudent, or constant man, but rather taking into consideration all relevant circumstances, whether the will of the person who alleges duress was in fact overcome. A person is entitled to protection even though he possesses less courage than that of the average man. Galusha v. Sherman, 105 Wis. 263, 81 N. W. 495, 47 

L. R. A. 417; Eadie v. Slimmon, 26 N. Y. 9, 82 Am. Dec. 

395, 63 L. R. A. 861. 

§ 79. Fear of Imprisonment or Criminal Prosecution. 

Much confusion exists among the authorities on the question of imprisonment as duress. The following elements must be taken into consideration: (1) the imprisonment may be wholly without authority of law or under legal process; (2) there may be an actual imprisonment or merely a threat of imprisonment, immediate or remote; (3) there may be sufficient or insufficient grounds for arrest and imprisonment; (4) the party procuring the imprisonment may be acting in good faith or in bad faith and solely for the purpose of coercing the other party; (5) arrest and imprisonment may occur as incident to a criminal or civil action.

Clearly, imprisonment by virtue of an arrest wholly without legal authority constitutes duress. Baker v. Morton, 12 Wall. (U.S.) 150, 20 Law. Ed. 262; Guilleaume v. Rowe, 94 N. Y. 268, 46 Am. Rep. 141.

It is not necessary, however, that imprisonment actually

occur in order to establish duress. By the weight of authority, a threat of arrest and criminal prosecution is sufficient to constitute duress. Williamson-Halsell, Frazier Co. v. Ackerman, 77 Kan. 502, 94 Pac. 807, 20 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

484; Adams v. Irving National Bank, 116 N. Y. 606, 23 

N. E. 7, 6 L. R. A. 491. Similarly, if a prior threat of imprisonment continues to influence the mind of the party at the time the contract is signed, it constitutes duress. 

Eadie v. Slimmon, 26 N. Y. 9, 82 Am. Dec. 395, 63 L. R. A. 

861. However, some courts require that imprisonment be imminent. Beath v. Chapoton, 115 Mich. 506, 73 N. W. 

806. Contra, Bertschinger v. Campbell, 99 Wash. 142, 168 

Pac. 977, L. R. A. 1918C 65. See, also, Thompson v. Niggley, 53 Kan. 664, 35 Pac. 290, 26 L. R. A. 803.

A threat to bring about the lawful imprisonment of a person upon a well-grounded charge has been held not to constitute duress, especially when there is a just cause and no improper motive. Knapp v. Hyde, 60 Barb. 80; Bolyard v. Bolyard, 79 W. Va. 554, 91 S. E. 529, L. R. A. 1917D 440.

However, by the weight of authority, the use of criminal proceedings to coerce another into the making of a contract is in itself unlawful. See Burton v. McMillan, 52 

Fla. 469, 42 So. 849, 8 L. R. A. (N.S.) 991. Hence, when threats of lawful prosecution are resorted to for the purpose of overcoming the will of the party threatened, by intimidating or terrifying him, they amount to duress. 

Adams v. Irving National Bank, supra; Pendleton v. 

Greever, 80 Okla. 35, 193 Pac. 885, 17 A. L. R. 317; Barrett v. Weber, 125 N. Y. 18, 25 N. E. 1068. $ 80. 

Fear of Civil Proceedings.

The rule is generally stated to be that a threat to institute a civil action does not constitute duress. McGuire & Co. v. H. G. Vogel Co., 164 App. Div. 173, 149 N. Y. S. 756; 

Zebold v. Hurst, 65 Okla. 248, 166 Pac. 99, L. R. A. 1917F 

579 and authorities there cited. However, in a number of instances in recent years, where a threat to institute a civil

proceeding was accompanied by other circumstances tending to coerce the will of the person executing a contract, duress has been held to exist. For example, the surrender of a life insurance policy, procured during the illness of the insured under a threat to "throw the case into court," has been held to be voidable on the ground of duress. Heinlein v. Imperial Life Insurance Co., 101 Mich. 250, 59 N. W. 

615, 25 L. R. A. 627. This subject is also discussed in section 81, infra. 

§ 81. Duress of Property. 

In no subdivision of the law in respect to duress is the important distinction between the older rule of the court of law on the one hand and the equitable and modern view of the subject on the other brought out more clearly than in the field of what is known as "Duress of Property," or, more commonly, "Duress of Goods." The latter title is misleading, however, as the same general principles have been applied to real estate as well as to personal property. 

Joannin v. Ogilvie, 49 Minn. 564, 52 N. W. 217, 16 L. R. A. 

376. Thus, where money was wrongfully exacted as a condition precedent to the cancelation of a mortgage on real estate, the payment was held to be involuntary and the payor allowed to recover it. Kilpatrick v. Germania Life 

Insurance Co., 183 N. Y. 163, 75 N. E. 1124, 2 L. R. A. (N.S.) 574.

Under the early common law rule, fear of loss of, or injury to, property could not amount to duress. The rule in equity, which has tended to become the modern rule, is broader and permits relief in cases where the duress is directed towards the complainant's property. The inconsistency between the two rules led, and in jurisdictions where the distinction is still maintained may still lead, to absurd results. Thus, if one pays money to obtain possession of goods unlawfully detained, he may generally sue in quasi-contract to recover the money so paid. If, however, he had given his promissory note for the same purpose,

he could not, on the ground of duress, defeat an action at law to recover on the note, since such an action is strictly one at law. See discussion in Oliphant v. Markham, 79 

Tex. 543, 15 S. W. 569. However, there is no valid distinction between executory and executed transactions so far as duress is concerned, and the present weight of authority in this country is that the wrongful detention of goods may constitute duress which may be interposed as a defense in an action at law on an executory contract. Oliphant v. 

Markham, supra; Harris v. Cary, 112 Va. 362, 71 S. E. 

551; Foshay v. Ferguson, 5 Hill (N.Y.) 154. In this connection, it is well to note that in New York equitable defenses may be pleaded in an action at law. Civil Practice 

Act, sec. 262. There is clearly no question as to the right to recover money paid under such circumstances. Scholey v. Mumford, 60 N. Y. 498. The detention must be wrongful; if rightful, such payment is not regarded as involuntary. Buck v. Houghtaling, 110 App. Div. 52, 96 N. Y. S. 

1034; York v. Hinkle, 80 Wis. 624, 50 N. W. 895.

A few illustrations of the application of the modern doctrine may be given.

Where the plaintiff's raft was forced by wind on to the defendant's wharf and the latter wrongfully exacted money before he would release the raft, the plaintiff was allowed to recover the money so exacted. Chase v. Windal, 7 Greenl. (Me.) 134. A similar case is Clinton v. Strong, 9 Johns. (N.Y.) 370. A city official threatened to turn the water off unless a foundry paid an illegal license fee. The latter paid to keep from having the supply of running water stopped. Recovery was allowed. Westlake v. St. Louis, 77

Mo. 47. In order to remain in law school, a student paid an excessive tuition charge. Recovery was allowed. Niedermeyer v. Curators of University of Missouri, 61 Mo. App. 

654. Excess freight charges paid to procure possession of goods are clearly recoverable. Harmony v. Bingham, 12 

N. Y. 99, 62 Am. Dec. 142; Heiserman v. Burlington, Cedar 

Rapids & Northern Ry. Co., 63 Iowa 732, 18 N. W. 903.

However, no recovery of money obtained by duress is allowed if the same was legally due. The sole remedy in such cases is an action in quasi-contract which can be maintained only when the retention of the money by the defendant is inequitable and against good conscience. Scholey v. Mumford, supra; City of Chicago v. Malkan, 119 Ill. App. 

542, aff'd 217 Ill. 471, 75 N. E. 548. § 82. 

Person by Whom Duress Must Be Committed.

As a rule, to constitute a defense to an action on a contract, the duress must have been exercised by the other party to the contract, or by some one acting either on his behalf or in collusion with him. If exercised by an entirely independent party, it is not duress. Sherman v. Sherman, 47 N. Y. St. Rep. 404, 20 N. Y. S. 414; Fairbanks v. Snow, 145 Mass. 153, 13 N. E. 596.

In a number of instances, however, on the ground of duress, the courts have refused to enforce a contract where one of the contracting parties was coerced into giving his consent by a member of his family, even though the other party to the contract had no knowledge of the coercion. 

See National Bank of the Republic v. Cox, 47 App. Div. 

53, 62 N. Y. S. 314; Barry v. Equitable Life Assurance 

Society, 59 N. Y. 587; Central Bank v. Copeland, 18 Md. 

305, 81 Am. Dec. 597. Thus, in National Bank of the

Republic v. Cox, supra, a mother executed a mortgage to a bank to protect her son from prosecution for forgery. It was held that the mortgage was executed under duress, though the mortgagee and its agents knew nothing of the coercion brought to bear upon the mother by her son and his friend. § 83. 

Person against Whom Duress Must Be Directed.

At common law as a rule, in order to affect the validity of a contract, the duress must have been directed against the party to the contract, or the husband, wife, parent, or child of such party. See Adams v. Irving National Bank,

116 N. Y. 606, 23 N. E. 7; Williamson-Halsell, Frazier Co. v. Ackerman, 77 Kan. 502, 94 Pac. 807, 20 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

484. In New York, the rule has not been extended to include a brother-in-law of the party to the contract. Solinger v. Earle, 82 N. Y. 393, 60 How. Pr. 116. However, in other jurisdictions the doctrine has been widely extended to include such relationships as grandmother and grandson, aunt and nephew, brother and brother or sister, mother-in-law and son-in-law, and brothers-in-law. The cases are collected in Kronmeyer v. Buck, 258 Ill. 586, 101 N. E. 935, 45 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1182; Fountain v. Bigham, 235 Pa. 

35, 84 Atl. 131. In such cases there are usually facts other than the mere relationship uniting the parties. 

A charge of duress cannot be sustained on the ground that the consent of one of the contracting parties was induced by a threat of the other party to commit suicide, because the maker and the object of the threats are the same. 

Wright v. Remington, 41 N. J. L. 48, 32 Am. Rep. 180, aff'd 43 N. J. L. 451; Metropolitan Life Insurance Co. v. 

Meeker, 85 N. Y. 614; Gerty v. Standard Oil Co., 1 App. 

Div. 224, 37 N. Y. S. 369.

CHAPTER VII REALITY OF CONSENT UNDUE INFLUENCE § 84. Undue Influence Defined. 

Undue influence is a term used to denote the power which a stronger or dominant party wrongfully exercises over a weaker party to induce the latter to consent to a contract which speaks the will of the dominant party, and not his own. Stout v. Smith, 98 N. Y. 25, 50 Am. Rep. 632. Thus, undue influence may be produced by working upon the fears and hopes of weak-minded persons, by artful and cunning contrivances, by constant pressure, persuasion, and effort, so that the person is not left to act intelligently and understandingly. Marx v. McGlynn, 88 N. Y. 358; Rider v. 

Miller, 86 N. Y. 507. But honest argument, modest persuasion, or mere appeals to the affections, made to induce consent to a contract, do not constitute undue influence. Marx v. McGlynn, supra; Latham v. Udell, 38 Mich. 238; Lamport v. Smedley, 212 N. Y. 82, 106 N. E. 922. $ 85. Effect of Undue Influence.

Undue influence operates to render the contract voidable, and not void, at the option of the oppressed party. Since the contract is binding until avoided and since it may be affirmed at any time after the undue influence has ceased to operate, one entitled to avoid a contract on the ground of undue influence must do so promptly. White v. Ross, 160 Ill. 56, 43 N. E. 336. 

§ 86. Presumption and Burden of Proof. 

The general rule is that undue influence must be proved and cannot be presumed. Doheny v. Lacy, 168 N. Y. 213, 61 N. E. 255. But the relation in which the parties to a transaction stand to each other is often a material circumstance and may of itself be sufficient, in some cases, to 96

REALITY OF CONSENT-UNDUE INFLUENCE 

97 raise a presumption of its existence. Transactions between persons in a fiduciary relationship with each other naturally excite suspicion. In such cases, the person claiming the benefit or advantage has the burden of relieving himself from the suspicion thus engendered, and of showing that the transaction was free from undue influence. In re Smith, 95 N. Y. 516; Barnard v. Gantz, 140 N. Y. 249, 35 N. E. 430. 

§ 87. Relations Giving Rise to Presumption of Undue 

Influence. 

A presumption of undue influence arises when the relationship between the parties is such that they do not deal upon terms of equality. Godley, Outline of Equity, 2d Ed., sec. 81. This inequality is frequently found in the parental relationship, which includes not only the actual relationship between parent and child, and of one standing in loco parentis to the child, but extends to husband and wife, brother and sister, and to any member of a family who, from age, character, or circumstances exercises superior authority in the family. Green v. Roworth, 113 N. Y. 462, 21 N. E. 165; Cowee v. Cornell, 75 

N. Y. 91, 31 Am. Rep. 428; Hall v. Perkins, 3 Wend. (N.Y.) 

626; Allen v. La Vaud, 213 N. Y. 322, 107 N. E. 570. But the mere fact that one of the parties is the aged grandfather and employer of the other is not sufficient to create a presumption of undue influence. Cowee v. Cornell, supra. 

Antenuptial agreements because of the confidential relations between the parties, while valid when fairly made, are guarded with the utmost care. The law presumes against the validity of such contracts. Pierce v. Pierce, 71 

N. Y. 154, 27 Am. Rep. 22. § 88. Gift from Parent to Child.

In Sawyer v. White, 122 Fed. 223, a father, eighty-eight years of age, who had been a paralytic for fifteen years and so feeble that he had required constant attendance and as-

sistance, had intrusted his business to his son, who had managed it for him and had supplied his wants with rare assiduity and kindness. The father had perfect confidence in this son and was grateful to him for his kindness. For many years he had intended, at his death, to leave his property to his son. His mind was impaired, his powers of speech and locomotion were very limited, but he knew what he was doing, and he intentionally conveyed land worth $20,000 to this son without any actual consideration; so that the son of a deceased daughter received no part of it. The Court held that the deed was not void on account of the lack of mental capacity, the fiduciary relation, or undue influence. In determining the competency of a grantor to execute a deed, said the Court, the question is not whether or not his mental powers were impaired, or whether or not he had ordinary capacity to do business when he executed it, but whether or not he had any-the smallest capacity to understand what he was doing, and to decide intelligently whether or not he desired to do it. 

The existence of a fiduciary relation between a parent and his child is not necessarily fatal to a deed or gift from the former to the latter, because such a deed or gift is natural and reasonable and is sustained by the presumption that it was inspired by parental affection and devotion. The affection, confidence, and gratitude of a parent to a child which inspire a deed or gift are natural and lawful influences and will not render the deed or gift voidable, unless they have been so used as to confuse the judgment and to control the will of the donor. 

§ 89. Other Confidential Relations. 

Other confidential relations, not arising out of family ties, may raise a presumption of undue influence. The most frequent of these relations are: 

1. Attorney and client; 

2. Trustee and beneficiary; 

3. Guardian and ward;

REALITY OF CONSENT UNDUE INFLUENCE 

99 

4. Physician and patient; 

5. Principal and agent. 

McParland v. Larkin, 155 Ill. 84, 39 N. E. 609; Marx v. 

McGlynn, 88 N. Y. 358; Rodkinson v. Haecker, 248 N. Y. 

480, 162 N. E. 493. In the last named case, it was held that the mere existence of the relation of attorney and client is not fatal to an account stated between the parties. § 90. 

Necessities and Distress.

A court of equity will not interfere merely because a bargain is hard or unreasonable. Whether a man's bargains are wise or unwise is not, ordinarily, a subject for legal or equitable relief, and this is true even though one of the parties is a "reckless, dissipated, improvident, and weak-minded young man." Parmelee v. Cameron, 41 N. Y. 

392. However, a court of equity may, in the exercise of its discretion, refuse to decree specific performance of a hard bargain. Scheinberg v. Scheinberg, 249 N. Y. 277, 164 N. E. 98. In some cases where a person has acted under great hardship or oppression, equity will give affirmative relief. This principle is usually applied to expectant heirs, reversioners, and holders of other expectant interests where the amount or value of the interest sold and the time of enjoyment are uncertain and were disposed of at an inadequate price. The reason for the rule is to prevent advantage to be taken of a man's present necessities which will deprive him of a future estate without reasonable compensation. But where the expectant interest has become vested, the rule granting relief does not apply because the interest is no longer "expectant." Parmelee v. 

Cameron, supra.

CHAPTER VIII  § 91. In General. 

Capacity to contract is one of the elements in testing whether there is a contractual obligation. No one can be bound on his contract who is without capacity to contract. 

But there are many instances where, upon the ground of public policy, one will be legally obligated to compensate another who has supplied him with the necessaries of life. 

Thus, an infant, an insane or an unconscious person would be liable for food, clothing, and shelter. The law, independent of contract, imposes upon such persons a liability to pay for necessaries of life, and therefore the obligation is said to be quasi-contractual.

The development of capacity of parties to contract will be from the negative side; i. e., to that particular class of persons, corporations, or governments that may lack contractual capacity. It must necessarily follow then that those who are not incapacitated have contractual capacity. 

The question of incapacity will be considered in the following classes of cases: Infants; 1. 2. 

Insane persons; 

3. Drunken persons; 4. Spendthrifts; 5. 

Married women; 

6. Aliens; 

7. Convicts; 

8. Corporations; 

9. Federal and state governments; 

10. Foreign states and governments. 100

§ 92. 

Infants.

In common speech the word "infant" ordinarily signifies a child of tender and helpless age. But, in the law, it has a technical meaning and is applied to any person who has not reached the age of twenty-one. In many states, including New York, a person under the age of twenty-one is designated by statute a "minor." Domestic Relations Law, sec. 2. By a somewhat fanciful application of the ancient theory that, since the law cannot compute fractional parts of a day, the first and the last day are each to be considered a full day (a theory now obsolete in other branches of the law), it has become firmly established that a youth becomes of age on the day before the twenty-first anniversary of his birth. Thus, a person born any hour on the first day of January, 1900, even a few minutes before twelve o'clock of the night of that day, would be of full age at the first instant of the thirty-first day of December, 1920. United 

States v. Wright, 197 Fed. 297; Banco De Sonora v. Bankers' Mutual Casualty Co., 124 Iowa 576, 100 N. W. 532.

The common law fixed the age of majority for both male and female at the completion of the twenty-first year, and this rule is in force not only in New York but throughout the United States generally. In some states, however, by statute, females become of age at the end of the eighteenth year. Domestic Relations Law, sec. 2; Banco De Sonora v. Bankers' Mutual Casualty Co., supra.

By statute in New York the age of legal consent for marriage is eighteen years. Domestic Relations Law, sec. 7. 

All persons eighteen years of age or upwards may bequeath personal property, but not real property, by will. 

Decedent Estate Law, secs. 10 and 15. 

§ 93. Effect of Infant's Emancipation. 

While the emancipation of an infant from parental control gives him a right to his own earnings, it does not in any way enlarge the contractual capacity of the infant. Cohen v. Delaware, Lackawanna & Western R. R. Co., 150 Misc.

450, 269 N. Y. S. 667; Commonwealth v. Graham, 157 Mass. 

73, 76, 31 N. E. 706, 16 L. R. A. 578. § 94.

Void Contracts. 

In the absence of a statute declaring certain contracts of infants to be void, the contracts of an infant are never deemed to be void, but merely voidable. For many years, however, it was held that the appointment of an agent or the execution of a power of attorney by an infant was the exercise of a contractual power and, in New York and many other jurisdictions, it was declared to be "absolutely void." The infant had no capacity to delegate his authority to another. Fonda & Hoag v. Van Horne, 15 Wend. (N.Y.) 

631, 30 Am. Dec. 77; Robbins v. Mount, 33 How. Pr. 24, 4 

Rob. (N. Y. Super.) 553; Trueblood v. Trueblood, 8 Ind. 

195, 65 Am. Dec. 756. But the modern trend of authority and the New York rule now are that such appointments are voidable and not void. Casey v. Kastel, 237 N. Y. 305, 142 N. E. 671, 31 A. L. R. 995; Benson v. Tucker, 212 Mass. 

60, 98 N. E. 589, 41 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1219; Patterson v. 

Lippincott, 47 N. J. L. 457, 1 Atl. 506. 

§ 95. Voidable Contracts Ratification and Disaffirm ance. 

As a general rule, the contracts of infants are voidable. 

This means that they may be either ratified or disaffirmed at the will of the infant. When ratified, the infant assumes all rights and liabilities arising from the contract; when disaffirmed, he avoids all liability arising under it. Beardsley v. Hotchkiss, 96 N. Y. 201; Stromberg v. Rubenstein, 19 Misc. 647, 44 N. Y. S. 405. § 96.

When Infant Can Ratify. 

An infant cannot effectively ratify his contract before reaching his majority, as this could have no greater effect than his original contract. The rule which precludes him from making a binding contract prior to his majority pre-

cludes him from so ratifying it. Sanger. v. Hibbard, 104 

Fed. 455. 

§ 97. When Infant May Disaffirm. 

At any time during his minority or within a reasonable time after he reaches his majority, an infant may disaffirm any voidable contract, except executed contracts for the sale of real estate. The repudiation puts an end to the existence of the contract, both as to the infant and as to the adult with whom he contracted. Rice v. Boyer, 108 Ind. 472, 9 N. E. 420; Aborn v. Janis, 62 Misc. 95, 113 N. Y. S. 309, aff'd 121 App. Div. 923, 106 N. Y. S. 1115.

Due to the fixed character of real property as distinguished from personal property, a postponement of the right to disaffirm a deed of land until the infant reaches his majority does not subject him to loss, but where personal property is involved, delay in avoidance might result in a total loss of the property or the value thereof. Such would be the case where there is a change of possession and the property becomes damaged, is destroyed, or its identity is lost. Therefore, disaffirmance of a conveyance of land by an infant may be made at any time within the period prescribed by the statute of limitations, which is ten years after he has reached his majority, such period being regarded as reasonable for this purpose. O'Donohue v. 

Smith, 130 App. Div. 217, 114 N. Y. S. 536; Eagan v. 

Scully, 29 App. Div. 617, 51 N. Y. S. 680, aff'd 173 N. Y. 

581, 65 N. E. 1116. 

§ 98. When Disaffirmance Is Necessary. 

Executed contracts of infants will generally be deemed ratified until they are disaffirmed. So, too, where under a contract an infant acquires rights in permanent property or property which involved continuous rights and liabilities, or where he has been the recipient of benefits, the contract is binding until rescinded. Beardsley v. Hotchkiss, 96 N. Y. 

201; Youmans v. Forsythe, 86 Hun 372, 33 N. Y. S. 474.

§ 99. When Ratification Is Necessary. 

If the contract of an infant remains wholly executory at his majority or was entered into merely for a temporary purpose, an affirmative act of ratification at such time becomes necessary; otherwise the contract will be regarded as disaffirmed. Whitney v. Dutch, 14 Mass. 457, 7 Am. Dec. 229. § 100.

What Constitutes Disaffirmance. 

A disaffirmance may be expressed by a definite notice of disaffirmance, or it may be implied, as where the infant's conduct is clearly inconsistent with the existence of the contract. Pyne v. Wood, 145 Mass. 558, 14 N. E. 775.

The usual mode of disaffirming a deed made in infancy is by an action of ejectment or a suit in equity to set aside the deed. But to maintain the action, there must be a disaffirmance otherwise than by bringing an action. Tomezek v. Wieser, 58 Misc. 46, 108 N. Y. S. 784; O'Donohue v. 

Smith, 130 App. Div. 214, 114 N. Y. S. 536. The disaffirmance of a chattel mortgage executed by the infant may be evidenced by a sale and delivery of the property by the infant to a third person. Chapin v. Shafer, 49 N. Y. 407. 

See, also, Walsh v. Powers, 43 N. Y. 23, 3 Am. Rep. 654. 

§ 101. Ratification-Effect. 

Ratification puts an end to the right of disaffirmance, but to constitute a ratification there must be some affirmative action on the part of the infant evidencing an intention to affirm the contract. It is not always an easy question to determine whether certain acts constitute a ratification. 

For instance, the mere making of a part payment after majority on property purchased during infancy, although evidence of affirmance, does not necessarily constitute ratification. International Text Book Co. v. Connelly, 206 N. Y. 

188, 99 N. E. 722, 42 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1115; Pedro v. Pedro, 71 Misc. 296, 127 N. Y. S. 997. Retaining and using the property or accepting its benefits is evidence of ratification

but is not conclusive of the question. Thus, where an infant buys books on the instalment plan, a repudiation of his contract with an offer to return the books is a disaffirmance, even though he retains possession of the books by reason of the vendor's refusal to receive them. Grolier Society of 

London v. Forshay, 157 N. Y. S. 776. It must be observed that the ratification or disaffirmance must be as to the whole of the contract. An infant cannot ratify such part of a contract as he deems beneficial to him and repudiate the part that is detrimental. § 102.

Disaffirmance-Effect. 

A disaffirmance renders a contract void ab initio, and the contract cannot thereafter be ratified. The rights of the parties are as though the contract had never existed. Mac- 

Greal v. Taylor, 167 U. S. 688, 42 Law. Ed. 326, 17 Sup. 

Ct. Rep. 961.

As a rule, the disaffirmance avoids the contract from its inception, and third parties can, therefore, acquire no rights under a disaffirmed contract. Thus, at common law, an infant may repudiate a contract of sale and recover the article sold, even from one who purchased it in good faith from the one to whom the infant sold it. However, it is now provided by statute in New York, and in certain states where the Uniform Sales Act has been adopted, that "when the seller of goods has a voidable title thereto, but his title has not been avoided at the time of the sale, the buyer acquires a good title to the goods, provided he buys them in good faith, for value, and without notice of the seller's defect of title." This section changes the common law rule only in respect to contracts of sale of personal property. 

Uniform Sales Act, sec. 24; Personal Property Law, sec. 

105; Miles v. Lingerman, 24 Ind. 385; Millard v. Hewlett, 19 Wend. (N.Y.) 301; Stafford v. Roof, 9 Cowen (N.Y.) 626.

§ 103. Knowledge of Rights. 

To make the ratification by the infant binding, it is not requisite that he have actual knowledge of his legal right to avoid a contract made during infancy. As a matter of law, he is presumed to know that he has a right to disaffirm such a contract. Taft v. Sergeant, 18 Barb. 320; Bestor v. Hickey, 71 Conn. 181, 41 Atl. 555. Many jurisdictions, however, hold to the contrary. Hatch v. Hatch, 60 Vt. 160, 13 Atl. 791. 

§ 104. Estoppel To Assert Infancy. 

At common law and by a preponderance of American authority, an infant is not estopped from setting up his infancy as a defense to an action on a contract, even though he induced the other party to enter into the contract by falsely representing himself to be of age. He cannot remove his disability by his own declarations that he is not an infant. To hold otherwise would be to deprive an infant of the protection which the law extends to him. Sternlieb v. 

Normandie National Securities Corp., 263 N. Y. 245, 188 

N. E. 726, 90 A. L. R. 1437; International Text Book Co. v. 

Connelly, 206 N. Y. 188, 99 N. E. 722, 42 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

1115. Contra, County Board of Education v. Hensley, 147 

Ky. 441, 144 S. W. 63, 42 L. R. A. (N.S.) 643. § 105. 

How Question of Disaffirmance May Arise.

The question of an infant's right to avoid or disaffirm his contract presents no difficulty so long as the contract remains wholly executory or where it is fully executed on his part and remains completely executory on the part of the adult. But where the contract has been partially or fully executed on the part of the adult, or where it has been partially or fully executed by both the infant and the adult, the right of the infant, upon disaffirmance, to retain what he has received, or to recover what he has given, presents many complex legal questions. The perplexing problem in each of these two instances is whether the infant, upon dis-

affirmance, is required to restore the property he has received or, where it has been dissipated, to return its equivalent. The federal and state decisions on this latter point are in hopeless discord. The contract of an infant may be in any one of four stages at the time the infant elects to disaffirm. It may be: a. Entirely executory; b. Executed by the infant only; C.

Executed by the adult only; d. Partially or fully executed by both the infant and the adult.

The question of an infant's rights upon disaffirmance will be considered in the order noted. a.

Where the contract is entirely executory; that is, it has not been performed by either the adult or the infant. 

In this case, it may be disaffirmed by the infant without any liability on his part. Beardsley v. Hotchkiss, 96 N. Y. 201. b. Where the contract has been executed on the part of the infant only. In this case, the infant may disaffirm it and recover from the adult the consideration given. 

Kamil v. New York College of Dentistry, 168 N. Y. S. 527; 

Danziger v. Iron Clad Realty & Trading Co., 80 Misc. 510, 141 N. Y. S. 593. 

The recent decision in Joseph v. Schatzkin, 259 N. Y. 241, 181 N. E. 464, 83 A. L. R. 910, has given rise to much controversy over the right of an infant to have a recovery from an adult upon disaffirmance of the contract. This case is generally construed or contrasted with Green v. Green, 69 

N. Y. 553, 25 Am. Rep. 233, and Rice v. Butler, 160 N. Y. 

578, 55 N. E. 275, 47 L. R. A. 303, which involved fully or partially executed contracts in which the infant had received in whole or in part the benefits of the contract. In the instant case, however, the infant had received no benefits whatsoever. He had transferred to the defendant, a stockbroker, a marginal trading account, consisting of four

hundred shares, which he had previously opened with another stockbroker. Subsequently, because of a declining market, the infant's equity in the four hundred shares decreased considerably, whereupon the infant rescinded the contract with the defendant and commenced an action to recover the value of his equity in the said shares of stock as of the time of the transfer to the defendant. The Court of Appeals permitted the infant to recover only an amount equal to the value of his equity in the stock as of the time of disaffirmance. The decision is in accord, we submit, with the earlier authorities on the subject. Since the infant had received no benefits from the contract and hence had nothing to restore to the adult, there was no necessity to invoke the doctrine either of Rice v. Butler, supra, or Green v. 

Green, supra.

To further illustrate, if an infant opens a speculative account with a stockbroker and deposits jewels or a definite sum of money as security, the infant may, following a declining market, recover the jewels or the full amount of cash deposited, irrespective of the worthlessness of the stocks purchased for his account. This is upon the theory that since the contract is executed on the part of the infant only, he may upon disaffirmance recover what he parted with. Mordecai v. Pearl, 63 Hun 553, aff'd 136 N. Y. 625, 32 N. E. 1014.

C. Where the contract has been executed on the part of the adult only. In this case, the infant may disaffirm or refuse to perform his promise and no liability will attach, except that, if he has in his possession any article or consideration received under the contract, he must restore it to the adult; but where the infant has disposed of or squandered the consideration which he received, the adult is without any remedy whatsoever. Williston on Contracts, Vol. I, sec. 238; McCarthy v. Bowling Green Storage & 

Van Co., 182 App. Div. 18, 169 N. Y. S. 463; Shutter v. 

Fudge, 108 Conn. 528, 143 Atl. 896.. d. Where the contract has been partially or fully ex-

ecuted by both the adult and the infant. In this instance, if the infant still has in his possession that which he received from the adult, he must return the same upon disaffirmance. Moreover, by the weight of authority in this country, even though the infant has dissipated that which he received, he may nevertheless disaffirm the contract and demand the return of that which he delivered to the other party. Millsaps v. Estes, 137 N. C. 535, 50 S. E. 227, 70 

L. R. A. 170.

In New York, the prevailing view that an infant, although he has dissipated the consideration or for other reasons cannot restore to the adult that which he had received, might nevertheless disaffirm the contract has been unsettled by recent decisions. Thus, in Casey v. Kastel, 237 N. Y. 

305, 314, 142 N. E. 671, 31 A. L. R. 995, the Court of

Appeals declared that the right of an infant to avoid or rescind contracts made during his minority does not depend on his ability to restore the consideration or otherwise make restitution to the other party, citing Green v. Green, supra. 

This is but dictum in Casey v. Kastel, supra, though it was the basis of the decision in Green v. Green, supra, wherein real property was involved. In Rice v. Butler, supra, wherein personal property was involved, the Court of Appeals held that an infant cannot disaffirm his contract if he has had the benefit of it, unless he accounts for such benefit or returns its equivalent. Rice v. Butler, supra, was followed by the United States Supreme Court in Myers v. Hurley Motor Co., 273 U. S. 18, 71 Law. Ed. 515, 47 

Sup. Ct. Rep. 277. 

§ 106. Infant's Contracts Permitted by Statute. 

Where a statute expressly empowers an infant to enter into a certain kind of contract, contracts so made are valid. 

In such a case, the right of avoidance does not exist. In 

New York, the Banking Law, section 383, makes provision for juvenile savings shares in savings and loan associations, in which cases the common law disability of infants is

modified. So, also, under the Insurance Law, section 55, an infant fifteen years of age or upwards may make a valid contract of insurance upon his own life. Too, marriage contracts, not including, however, the contract for a future marriage, cannot be avoided. Domestic Relations Law, sec. 

7; Cunningham v. Cunningham, 206 N. Y. 341, 99 N. E. 

845, 43 L. R. A. (N.S.) 355. In 1916, Congress impliedly authorized persons above the age of eighteen years to enlist in the military service of the United States, without consent of their parents. 9 Federal Statutes Annotated (2d 

Ed.), sec. 27. Although an infant employer may disaffirm his contract as employer, he cannot, under the Workmen's Compensation Law, section 26, avoid his statutory liability as to compensation where the accident happened prior to a disaffirmance of the employment contract. Matter of Rahman v. Bethel, 236 App. Div. 182, 258 N. Y. S. 286. § 107.

Infant's Liability for Necessaries. 

An infant is liable for the reasonable value of necessary articles furnished to him. The Personal Property Law, section 83, is merely declaratory of the common law. This statute provides: "Capacity to buy and sell is regulated by the general law concerning capacity to contract, and to transfer and acquire property. Where necessaries are sold and delivered to an infant, or to a person who by reason of mental incapacity or drunkenness is incompetent to contract, he must pay a reasonable price therefor. Necessaries in this section mean goods suitable to the condition in life of such infant or other person, and to his actual requirements at the time of delivery." It will be observed that the obligation is imposed by law rather than by contract. 

Strictly speaking, it is inaccurate to say that the infant's contracts, if for necessaries, are binding upon him. The liability is not contractual but quasi-contractual. For instance, if an infant gives his promissory note for necessaries, he will not be liable on his note either to the payee

or to an indorsee who is a holder in due course. His liability is limited to the fair and reasonable value of the articles supplied to him. The plaintiff seeking to recover from an infant must aver and prove that the things furnished were strictly necessaries. Morgan v. Smith, 130 Misc. 412, 224 

N. Y. S. 400; Mauldin v. Southern Shorthand & Business 

Univ., 3 Ga. App. 800, 60 S. E. 358; Kilgore v. Rich, 83 

Me. 305, 22 Atl. 176, 12 L. R. A. 859. § 108.

What Are Infant's Necessaries. 

No positive rule can be formulated for determining what are necessaries. Each case must be determined by the particular circumstances surrounding it. Necessaries may be said to include: 

1. Food and clothing; 

2. Lodging; 

3. Physician's services; 4. Education; 

5. Also whatever is necessary to maintain the particular person in the state, station, and degree in life in which he is. Things necessary for one may not be necessary for another. International Text Book Co. v. Connelly, 206 

N. Y. 188, 99 N. E. 722, 42 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1115 and note; 

Allen v. Lardner, 78 Hun 603, 29 N. Y. S. 213.

Other matters which have been held to be necessaries are: a. Horse back riding for health, but not for pleasure. b. A common school, but not a classical or professional, education. International Text Book Co. v. Connelly, supra. c. Legal services pertaining to the personal relief, protection, or liberty of an infant, or to the prosecution of actions for personal injuries, but not for promotion or protection of property interests. Petrie v. Williams, 68 Hun 

589, 23 N. Y. S. 237; Grissom v. Beidleman, 35 Okla. 343, 129 Pac. 853, 44 L. R. A. (N.S.) 411 and note. d. Room rent and board while attending college.

Gregory v. Lee, 64 Conn. 407, 30 Atl. 53, 25 L. R. A. 618; 

Kilgore v. Rich, 83 Me. 305, 22 Atl. 176, 12 L. R. A. 859. 

§ 109. What Are Not Infant's Necessaries. 

In order to constitute necessaries, the articles furnished must relate to the infant's person and not to his estate. 

The following are not necessaries: a.

Horses. In a case involving an infant's liability for the purchase price of a horse, the Court said: "But it has been pithily and happily said that necessaries do not include horses, saddles, liquors, bridles, pistols, powder, whips, and fiddles." Price v. Sanders, 60 Ind. 310; House v. 

Alexander, 105 Ind. 109, 4 N. E. 891. b. Articles purchased for business, agricultural, or commercial purposes. House v. Alexander, supra. c. Dwelling house or repairs on a dwelling house. Allen v. Lardner, 78 Hun 603, 29 N. Y. S. 213. d. Bicycles. Rice v. Butler, 160 N. Y. 578, 55 N. E. 

275, 47 L. R. A. 303; Pyne v. Wood, 145 Mass. 558, 14 

N. E. 775. e. Money is not deemed a necessary, even though expended for necessaries, since it may be used for improper, as well as proper, purposes. Price v. Sanders, supra; Bent v. Manning, 10 Vt. 225. But in equity, the party lending the money is subrogated, to the extent that such money is spent for necessaries, to the rights of the party furnishing such necessaries. Watson v. Cross, 2 Duv. (Ky.) 147; 

Marlow v. Pitfield, 1 P. Wms. 558, 24 Eng. Rep. 516. See, also, MacGreal v. Taylor, 167 U. S. 688, 42 Law. Ed. 326, 17 Sup. Ct. Rep. 961. Upon the same principle, if the lender, at the request of the infant, pays money to a third person for necessaries supplied the infant, the infant is liable to the lender. Randall v. Sweet, 1 Denio (N.Y.) 460; 

Miller v. Smith, 26 Minn. 248, 2 N. W. 942. f. When an infant lives at home with his parents and is properly maintained by them, he is not liable for the

purchase of even the common necessaries of life. Goodman v. Alexander, 165 N., Y. 289, 59 N. E. 145, 55 L. R. A. 781; 

International Text Book Co. v. Connelly, 206 N. Y. 188, 99 

N. E. 722, 42 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1115. Nothing can be a necessary if the infant has already been supplied. Kline v. 

L'Amoureux, 2 Paige Ch. (N.Y.) 419, 22 Am. Dec. 652. g. Theatre tickets are not necessaries for a college student. Gray v. Sands, 66 App. Div. 572, 73 N. Y. S. 322. § 110.

Liability of Infant Husband. 

In view of the fact that the law permits an infant to make a valid contract of marriage, all necessaries furnished to his wife are in point of law necessaries furnished to the infant himself. So, too, he is liable for the decent burial of his wife and of his lawful children. Chapple v. Cooper, 13 Mees. & Wels. 253, 153 Eng. Rep. 105. The liability in such cases is quasi-contractual. Cantine v. Phillips' Administrator, 5 Harr. (Del.) 428. 

§ 111. Contracts in Fulfilment of Legal Duty. 

If an infant is under legal obligation to do an act, he may bind himself by contract for the purpose of discharging the obligation. Typical instances of the application of this principle arise where an infant executes a release of a mortgage or of other obligations which have been legally satisfied, or where an infant acknowledges his paternity of an illegitimate child and voluntarily executes a bond for its support which he could have been compelled by bastardy proceedings to give. People v. Moores, 4 Denio (N.Y.) 

518, 47 Am. Dec. 272. 

§ 112. Liability for Tort Arising out of Contract. 

As a general rule, an infant is liable for his torts, but the courts are solicitous in protecting the immunity given him against liability on his contracts, and hold that the breach of an infant's contract cannot be treated as a tort.

To hold an infant liable for a tort, it must be a distinct, wilful, and positive wrong of itself. It has been said that the only satisfactory test in determining whether an infant is liable for a tort connected with a breach of contract is- Can the infant be held liable without directly or indirectly enforcing his promise? If so, he may be held liable, though the tort may have arisen out of a contract. Collins v. 

Gifford, 203 N. Y. 465, 96 N. E. 721, 38 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

202. An infant, for example, is not liable on a warranty of property which he sells, although he knows his warranty is false and although he makes it with the intention to defraud the buyer. So, also, infancy is a defense to an action of conversion against an infant, based on his failure to return an article entrusted to him for sale and to be returned if not sold, where no actual tortious appropriation is shown. 

Stone v. Rabinowitz, 45 Misc. 405, 90 N. Y. S. 301.

The rule under discussion finds frequent application in bailment cases where an infant hires the use of property. 

Injury to property arising out of unskillfulness, lack of discretion, and want of proper care on the part of the infant is not actionable, because the act resulting in loss is regarded as essentially a breach of the implied contract of due care; but if the infant wilfully and intentionally misuses the property, or uses the property for an entirely different purpose, it is a positive wrong or tort for which he will be liable. Accordingly, an infant who hires a horse to drive to a certain town and deliberately drives to a different place or in a different direction takes upon himself all the consequences following therefrom, because the wilful act amounts to a disaffirmance of the contract. Young v. Muhling, 48 App. Div. 617, 63 N. Y. S. 181. The leading American case is Campbell v. Stakes, 2 Wend. (N.Y.) 

137, 19 Am. Dec. 561. See, also, Lowery v. Cate, 108 Tenn. 

54, 64 S. W. 1068, 57 L. R. A. 673, 680; Patterson v. 

Kasper, 182 Mich. 281, 148 N. W. 690, L. R. A. 1915A 1221.

§ 113. Liability of Infant in Tort for Misrepresentation of 

Age.

An adult who has been induced to enter into a contract with an infant by a false representation by the infant as to his age cannot hold the infant liable in tort. "To hold otherwise would in many cases deprive infants of the protection extended to them at an age when the mind and judgment are conclusively presumed to be immature and they need to be shielded from their own imprudence and folly." International Text Book Co. v. Connelly, 206 N. Y. 

188, 197, 99 N. E. 722, 42 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1115.

Although the non-liability of an infant in tort connected with a contract for falsely representing himself to be of full age seems to be well settled in New York, the courts in other jurisdictions are in hopeless confusion. For a discussion and collation of authorities, see Editorial, New 

York Law Journal, March 30, 1934.

INSANE PERSONS 

§ 114. Insanity Defined.

Insanity is a disease or disordered condition of the physical organs through which the mind receives impressions or manifests its operations, and by which the will and judgment are impaired and the conduct rendered irrational. In order to affect one's contractual capacity, the mental impairment must be more than mere weakness of mind, but it need not be such as to completely dethrone all reason. 

The tests are: Does the person whose sanity is in question understand the nature and effect of a particular transaction? Is he capable of transacting business? Is he capable of seeing things in their true relation? If so, he is not insane, for this purpose. Martin v. Harsh, 231 Ill. 384, 83 N. E. 164, 13 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1000. 

§ 115. Partial Derangement. 

A person's insanity may affect his mental capacity only

on certain subjects. In such cases, the insanity affects only such contracts as relate to the subjects on which his delusion exists. Riggs v. American Tract Society, 95 N. Y. 503. 

§ 116. Lucid Intervals. 

A person may be insane and yet have lucid intervals.

If he has not been adjudged insane by competent authority, all of his contracts made during a sane interval are valid. 

Curtis v. Brownell, 42 Mich. 165, 3 N. W. 936; Smith v. 

Smith, 108 N. C. 365, 12 S. E. 1045. 

§ 117. Effect of Insanity on Contracts. 

Contracts made by a person after he has been adjudged insane are void, the inquisition of insanity being regarded as notice, actual or constructive, to all the world of the fact of insanity; but contracts made by an insane or incompetent person before such adjudication are voidable only, the ground of avoidance being his incapability of giving assent, due to his infirmity of intellect. Blinn v. 

Schwartz, 177 N. Y. 252, 69 N. E. 542; Smith v. Ryan, 191 N. Y. 452, 84 N. E. 402, 19 L. R. A. (N.S.) 461. The adjudication of insanity can have no retroactive effect. The question of incompetency is limited and confined to the time of the inquiry, so that even if testimony given at the incompetency proceedings indicates insanity at an earlier date, it will not render void contracts made prior to the adjudication. Goldberg v. McCord, 251 N. Y. 28, 166 N. E. 793. 

§ 118. Avoidance of Contract. 

Insanity is a complete defense to an action on an executory contract of an insane person, although the other party entered into the contract without knowledge of the insanity, and before any adjudication of insanity. Where the insane person, however, has had the benefits of the contract, as where he borrows money on his note, and the other party acted in good faith, insanity is no defense. In this re-

spect his avoidance differs from that of an infant. Hosler v. 

Beard, 54 Ohio St. 398, 43 N. E. 1040, 35 L. R. A. 161.

So, too, where the contract is fully executed by both parties, the contract can be avoided by the incompetent only upon a restoration of the consideration to the other party. Haines v. Scott, 35 App. Div. 515, 54 N. Y. S. 844.

The liability of the lunatic in such cases is not on the ground of contract but on the fact that he has received and enjoyed a benefit for which, in equity and justice, he ought to pay. Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Hunt, 79 N. Y. 541; 

Riggs v. American Tract Society, 95 N. Y. 503. For a full discussion and collation of cases on the effect of insanity and right of avoidance, see McCarthy v. Bowling Green 

Storage & Van Co., 182 App. Div. 18, 169 N. Y. S. 463. 

The right of an insane vendor to avoid a contract of sale is lost when the property is sold by the vendee to a bona fide purchaser. Goldberg v. McCord, 251 N. Y. 28, 166 

N. E. 793; Uniform Sales Act, sec. 24; Personal Property 

Law, sec. 105. 

§ 119. Ratification of Contract. 

An insane person when restored to his proper faculties may ratify his contract. Any evidence of a clear and unequivocal character showing an intention to ratify or affirm will bind him, and it is not necessary that the ratification be as solemn as the original act itself. Acquiescence, with other circumstances, may establish ratification. Clearly, he will be deemed to have ratified the contract by knowingly taking the benefits of it. Blinn v. Schwartz, 177 

N. Y. 252, 69 N. E. 542; Gibson v. Western New York & 

Pennsylvania R. R. Co., 164 Pa. St. 142, 30 Atl. 308. 

§ 120. Liability for Necessaries. 

The liability of an insane person for necessaries stands on the same footing as that of an infant. In other words, his liability for their reasonable value is quasi-contractual. 

The rules for determining what are necessaries are substan-

tially the same as in infancy. Gilgallon v. Bishop, 46 App. 

Div. 350, 61 N. Y. S. 467. 

§ 121. Torts of Insane Persons. 

An insane person is liable for his torts the same as a sane person, except those where malice is a necessary ingredient. An insane person is often incapable of conceiving a malicious intent. The rule as to liability is based on the principle that, where a loss must be borne by one of two innocent persons, it shall be borne by him who occasioned it. It also precludes tort-feasors from pretending to be insane in order to escape liability for damages for injuries caused by them. McIntyre v. Sholty, 121 Ill. 660, 13 N. E. 239, aff'd 241 Ill. App. 605; Williams v. Hays, 143 N. Y. 442, 38 N. E. 449, 26 L. R. A. 153 and note. § 122. 

Burden of Proof as to Good Faith.

Where the question of good faith is involved, after the defense has offered evidence of insanity, the burden of proof is upon the plaintiff to show that he acted in good faith and without knowledge of the defendant's insanity. Hosler v. Beard, 54 Ohio St. 398, 43 N. E. 1040, 35 L. R. A. 161.

DRUNKEN PERSONS 

§ 123.

Contracts with Drunken Persons. 

A contract made by a person who, at the time, was in such a state of intoxication as not to know what he was doing or the consequences of his act is voidable. Slight intoxication does not affect the validity of a contract. The intoxication must be so excessive as to destroy one's understanding. Each case must be decided on its own merits, without regard to previous decisions in cases differing in their facts. Conley v. Nailor, 118 U. S. 127, 30 Law. Ed. 

112, 6 Sup. Ct. Rep. 1001; McKeon v. Van Slyck, 22 N. 

Y. 392, 119 N. E. 851; Wright v. Waller, 127 Ala. 557, 29 

So. 57, 54 L. R. A. 440 and note.

An habitual drunkard is not incompetent to make a deed or will unless, at the time of its execution, his reason was so dethroned that he did not know the nature of his act. 

Van Wyck v. Brasher, 81 N. Y. 260.

A marriage solemnized when one of the parties was intoxicated to such an extent as not to know what was taking place may be annulled. Sloan v. Kane, 10 How. Pr. (N.Y.) 

66; Anderson v. Hicks, 150 App. Div. 289, 134 N. Y. S. 1018. 

§ 124. Ratification. 

The general rule is that a contract, voidable by reason of the intoxication of one of the parties, may be ratified by him when he becomes sober. Johnson v. Harmon, 94 

U. S. 371. He is allowed a reasonable time after he understands the nature and effect of the transaction in which to disaffirm it; but, if he takes no steps to disaffirm it within a reasonable time after he has such knowledge, he is deemed to have ratified it. Carpenter v. Rodgers, 61 Mich. 384, 28 

N. W. 156; Matz v. Martinson, 127 Minn. 262, 149 N. W. 

370, L. R. A. 1915B 1121. § 125.

Validity of Contracts for Necessaries. 

A completely intoxicated person is generally placed on the same footing as an insane person in reference to liability for necessaries furnished him, and he is therefore liable on an implied contract in law for such necessaries. The implied contract arises by operation of law and is in no sense a true contract, but is quasi-contractual in nature. The obligation to pay for necessaries is based on humanity and necessity. McCrillis v. Bartlett, 8 N. H. 569. 

§ 126. Adjudication of Incompetency. 

By statute in New York, the Supreme Court is given jurisdiction of the custody of the person and the care of the property of a person incompetent to manage himself or his affairs in consequence of habitual drunkenness. Provision is made for the appointment of a committee for such a per-

son. Civil Practice Act, sec. 1356, et seq. Similar statutory provisions exist in other states. During the existence of such commission, contracts entered into by the person so adjudged incompetent are void. Carter v. Beckwith, 128 

N. Y. 312, 28 N. E. 582; McCarthy v. Bowling Green Storage & Van Co., 182 App. Div. 18, 169 N. Y. S. 463.

SPENDTHRIFTS § 127. In General. 

The common law laid no restraint on the power of a spendthrift to contract. The same is true of the law of New 

York and of most states to-day. In several states, however, such persons may be placed under guardianship, in which event, they lose capacity to make contracts on their own behalf. See Sullivan v. Lloyd, 221 Mass. 108, 108 N. E. 

923; Sturgis v. Sturgis, 51 Ore. 10, 93 Pac. 696, 15 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1034.

MARRIED WOMEN § 128. In General. 

The common law disabilities of coverture, as to the capacity of a married woman to contract, have been almost entirely removed by statute. A married woman now has the same rights in this respect as a feme sole. See Domestic 

Relations Law, sec. 51.

ALIENS 

§ 129. 

Rights of Aliens.

An alien is one who is the subject of, and owes allegiance to, a foreign government. McGregor v. McGregor, 33 How.

Pr. (N.Y.) 456. He has the same power to contract that a citizen has, and he may sue and be sued. Bradstreet v. 

Oneida County Supervisors, 13 Wend. (N.Y.) 546. The former rule that an alien woman who marries a citizen becomes a citizen, and that an American woman who marries an alien is expatriated is no longer the law since Septem-

ber 22, 1922, the change having been made by the "Cable" 

Act, 42 United States Statutes 1021. Under the statute, an alien woman does not become a citizen by marriage to an American citizen nor does an American woman lose her American citizenship by marriage to an alien. Another provision of the Act is to the effect that naturalization of a husband no longer naturalizes his wife, but merely his minor children resident in the United States at the time of his naturalization or coming to the United States before reaching their majority with the intention of making the United States their permanent residence. 

§ 130. 

Rights of Alien Enemies. 

An alien enemy is one who is the subject of a country at war with our own country. The effect of war is to suspend all commercial intercourse between citizens of hostile powers. Hence, contracts made between subjects of belligerent nations after the outbreak of war are usually declared void. See Heidner v. St. Paul & Tacoma Lumber Co., 124 Wash. 652, 215 Pac. 1. Commercial intercourse between citizens of the belligerents is incompatible with a state of war. The tendency of the courts, however, is to preserve and not to destroy contracts made before war. Therefore, contracts made before war will be merely suspended during the war, unless from their nature they are incapable of suspension, in which case, they will not revive at the close of the war. Watts, Watts & Co. v. Unione Austriaca Di 

Navigazione, 248 U. S. 9, 63 Law. Ed. 100, 39 Sup. Ct. Rep. 

1. However, in a decision growing out of the World War, it was held that a will by a citizen in favor of an alien enemy is not rendered invalid by the prohibition against trading with the enemy. In re Keilsmark's Will, 188 Iowa 

1378, 177 N. W. 690, 11 A. L. R. 156. 

§ 131. 

Alien Enemy as Plaintiff.

An alien enemy who resides in the enemy's country cannot, during a war, prosecute an action in the courts of the

opposing belligerent. Otherwise, he might "strengthen the hands of the enemy." Hungarian General Credit Bank v. 

Titus, 182 App. Div. 826, 169 N. Y. S. 926. But the trend of recent decisions has been to recognize and enforce the civil rights of alien enemies, domiciled in this country, provided they respect and obey the laws of this country and observe the rules of conduct laid down for their guidance by competent authority. Arndt-Ober v. Metropolitan Opera 

Co., 182 App. Div. 513, 169 N. Y. S. 944; Krachanake v. 

Acme Manufacturing Co., 175 N. C. 435, 95 S. E. 851, L. R. A. 1918E 801; Techt v. Hughes, 229 N. Y. 222, 128 

N. E. 125, 11 A. L. R. 166. § 132. 

Alien Enemy as Defendant.

An alien enemy may be sued on a contract entered into before the beginning of the war. The reason which denies him the right to sue can have no application where he is sued. As plaintiff, the courts are called upon to render him assistance which might aid his country; as defendant, the action is to his disadvantage. Compagnie Universelle de 

Telegraphie et de Telephonie sans Fil v. United States Service Corp., 84 N. J. Eq. 604, 95 Atl. 187; Watts, Watts & Co. v. Unione Austriaca Di Navigazione, 248 U. S. 9, 63 Law. 

Ed. 100, 39 Sup. Ct. Rep. 1. CONVICTS 

§ 133. Capacity To Contract. 

A convict undergoing a sentence of imprisonment, even for life, or awaiting execution is not thereby rendered incapable of making valid contracts. In New York, it is provided by statute that a person sentenced to imprisonment for life shall be deemed thereafter civilly dead. Penal Law, sec. 511. Similar provisions exist in other states. Such a person may dispose of his property by will or by deed and may make contracts, for the breach of which his estate would be liable, although, on his part, the contract would be

unenforceable. This is because the prisoner's civil rights are suspended; while the rights of creditors are not suspended. Stephani v. Lent, 30 Misc. 346, 63 N. Y. S. 471; 

Avery v. Everett, 110 N. Y. 317, 18 N. E. 148, 1 L. R. A. 

264; Byers v. Sun Savings Bank, 41 Okla. 428, 139 Pac. 

948, 52 L. R. A. (N.S.) 320.

As to the care of the estate of a person sentenced to life imprisonment, provision is made by statute for the appointment of a committee for such period of imprisonment. See 

Prison Law, sec. 370, et seq.

CORPORATIONS AND SOVEREIGNS 

§ 134. Corporations.

A corporation has contractual capacity within its chartered powers. Contracts not within the corporation's chartered powers are said to be ultra vires. However, in New 

York, when the contract has been fully performed on the part of either party, the plea of ultra vires cannot be sustained. Whitney Arms Co. v. Barlow, 63 N. Y. 62, 20 Am. 

Rep. 504; Holmes & Griggs Manufacturing Co. v. Holmes & 

Wessell Metal Co., 127 N. Y. 252, 27 N. E. 831. No attempt is here made to treat fully of the contractual capacity of corporations, because the question properly belongs to corporation law. § 135. 

Federal and State Governments.

The capacity of the government of the United States and of each of the states to contract is co-extensive with the duties and powers of government. Therefore, for the purpose of government, they have full contractual capacity. 

This is incidental to the general rights of sovereignty. 

Wilson v. Shaw, 204 U. S. 24, 51 Law. Ed. 351; Danolds v. 

New York, 89 N. Y. 36, 42 Am. Rep. 277. However, an action cannot be brought against the federal government, or against any other sovereign government without its consent. See Chisholm v. Georgia, 2 Dall. (U. S.) 419.

Furthermore, by virtue of the eleventh amendment to the Constitution of the United States, no action can be brought by a citizen of another state, or by a citizen or subject of any foreign state, against any state of the United States. 

Gables v. Girard, 46 Fed. 500; Hans v. Louisiana, 134

U. S. 1. The United States Supreme Court, however, has jurisdiction over actions between states of the United 

States. Federal Constitution, Art. IV. The federal govern ment has provided the Court of Claims for the adjustment of claims against the United States. The jurisdiction and procedure of the Federal Court of Claims are regulated by 

Acts of Congress. See 5 Federal Statutes Annotated (2d 

Ed.), secs. 136-187. The states, by statutory provision, in many instances provide courts for the hearing and determination of claims against themselves. The organization and jurisdiction of the Court of Claims of New York are regulated by statute. See Court of Claims Act, sec. 15. 

§ 136. Foreign States and Sovereigns. 

Foreign states and sovereigns may make contracts and sue thereon, but can be sued only when they voluntarily submit to the jurisdiction of the court. See Honduras v. 

Soto, 112 N. Y. 310, 19 N. E. 845; French Republic v. 

Inland Navigation Co., 263 Fed. 410. Foreign ambassadors, ministers, and other diplomatic agents, as well as their officials and servants, are immune from criminal and civil prosecutions in our courts. See In re Baiz, 135 U. S. 403, 34 Law. Ed. 222, 10 Sup. Ct. Rep. 854. Severe penalties are imposed upon those who sue out any writ or process against such persons. 3 Federal Statutes Annotated (2d 

Ed.), sec. 4064. This does not mean, however, that a state would be obliged to tolerate the continued presence of a diplomatic representative who flouted its laws; the representative is supposed to respect them, and if a situation were to arise where the representative should show disregard for them, the nation could ask that he be recalled.

The civil and criminal exemption, however, does not extend to consuls who are commercial, rather than diplomatic, agents. Bors v. Preston, 111 U. S. 252, 28 Law. Ed. 419, 4 Sup. Ct. Rep. 407.

CHAPTER IX  

§ 137.

Necessity of Legal Subject Matter.
