An agreement, to result in a contract, must create an obligation; if, therefore, the purpose or object of the contract is the performance or the doing of an illegal act, there can be no contract, because there is no obligation which the courts will enforce. A contract, though based on consent and contractual capacity, derives its obligatory force from the sanction of the law. It is therefore a fundamental principle of the law of contracts that a contract must have a lawful purpose. Buckley v. Humason, 50 Minn. 195, 52 

N. W. 385, 16 L. R. A. 423 and note. 

§ 138. Classification of Illegal Agreements. 

For convenience, illegal agreements may be classified as follows: 

1. Agreements in violation of a prohibitive statute; 

2. Agreements in violation of express rules of common law; 

3. Agreements in violation of public policy. 

Agreements in violation of common law and public policy are so closely related as to be indistinguishable in many instances. Agreements against public policy are illegal because they are in violation of common law. The term "public policy," as applied to this subject, is comprehensive and covers a wide range. It is evidenced by the trend of legislation, judicial decisions, and the principles of the common law. It embraces all acts or contracts which "tend clearly to injure the public health, the public morals, the public confidence in the purity of the administration of the law or which tend to undermine that sense of security for individual rights, whether of personal liberty or of private 126

property, which any citizen ought to feel." Holland v. 

Sheehan, 108 Minn. 362, 122 N. W. 1, 23 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

510; Johnston v. Fargo, 184 N. Y. 379, 77 N. E. 388, 7

L. R. A. (N.S.) 537. § 139. 

Agreements in Violation of Statutes.

A contract is illegal if it violates a constitutional statute or if it cannot be performed without a violation of such a statute. Thus, where a valid city ordinance, duly authorized and enacted, makes a particular act or business unlawful, or makes it unlawful for an unlicensed person to engage in a designated business, any contract made in violation thereof is void. Johnston v. Dahlgren, 166 N. Y. 354, 59 

N. E. 987; Sirkin v. Fourteenth Street Store, 124 App. 

Div. 384, 108 N. Y. S. 830; Fifth Avenue Coach Co. v. New 

York, 194 N. Y. 19, 86 N. E. 824, 21 L. R. A. (N.S.) 744.

It is, however, only a violation of a constitutional statute which will render a contract illegal; and the mere fact that an act of the legislature is on the statute books is not conclusive of its constitutionality. The statute may be in contravention of the provisions of the federal or state constitution, or it may constitute an unlawful exercise of the police power of the state. The police power is not unlimited in scope; it may be exercised only for the protection of the public welfare, health, safety, and morality. To be valid the enactment must tend in some appreciable way to accomplish the purposes for which the power may be exercised. Under the guise of protecting public interests, the legislature cannot arbitrarily infringe upon the property rights or liberties of individuals, or interfere with private business, or impose unnecessary restrictions upon lawful occupations. Therefore, if the legislature has exceeded its police powers in the enactment of a statute, a contract in violation thereof would not be illegal. People ex rel. Wineburgh Advertising Co. v. Murphy, 195 N. Y. 126, 88 N. E. 

17, 21 L. R. A. (N.S.) 735; Lochner v. New York, 198 U. 

S. 45, 49 Law. Ed. 937, 25 Sup. Ct. Rep. 539. In deter-

mining whether a particular statute is a proper exercise of the police power, the court will consider the social and economic circumstances which prompted its enactment. "Although an emergency may not call into life a power which has never lived, nevertheless emergency may afford a reason for the execution of a living power already enjoyed." Blaisdell v. Home Building & Loan Assn., 290 

U. S. 398, 78 Law. Ed. 255, 54 Sup. Ct. Rep. 231. See, also, Nebbia v. New York, 291 U. S. 502, 78 Law. Ed. 563, 54 Sup. Ct. Rep. 505. 

§ 140. Status of Contract Violative of Constitutional 

Statute.

While a contract violative of a constitutional statute is illegal, it is not necessarily void. Contracts may be illegal for certain purposes, and yet enforceable in other respects. 

They are void only: 1. 

Where the statute expressly declares them to be void; 

2. Where the purpose of the statute would be frustrated by the enforcement of the contract in question. 

§ 141. Contract Expressly Prohibited by Statute. 

When contracts are prohibited, the prohibition may be brought about by express words or by implication; and whether they are impliedly prohibited is a question of construction. Where a statute expressly prohibits an act, or expressly declares that certain kinds of contracts shall be void, there is no room for construction, because there is no doubt of the legislature's intention. Thus, in New York usurious and gambling contracts are void because they are expressly declared to be so by statute. General Business 

Law, sec. 373; Penal Law, secs. 991 and 992; Oppikofer v. Murphy, 146 App. Div. 581, 131 N. Y. S. 168. So, also, where a statute declares void all contracts whereby an employee agrees to accept in advance of his employment something other than money, a contract in violation thereof is

invalid. Hancock v. Yaden, 121 Ind. 366, 23 N. E. 253, 6 

L. R. A. 576; Levison v. Boas, 150 Cal. 185, 88 Pac. 825, 12 

L. R. A. (N.S.) 575, 583 and note. Similarly, a contract to procure rebates from railroad companies for a shipper was held void where the statute "prohibited and declared to be unlawful" such contracts. It is not, however, necessary that a statute, in express words, declare an act void in order to render a contract violating it a nullity. Parks v. Jacob 

Dold Packing Co., 6 Misc. 570, 27 N. Y. S. 289. § 142.

Contract Prohibited by Implication. 

Frequently a statute imposes a penalty for the doing of an act, without either expressly prohibiting it or expressly declaring it illegal or void. Some courts hold that a penalty implies a prohibition; others hold that it is thus implied only when the statute is for the protection of the public welfare or for the prevention of fraud. The latter view is adopted in New York and in most jurisdictions. The question is largely one of legislative intention and interpretation. The courts will always consider the: 

1. Language of the statute; 

2. Subject matter; 

3. Wrong or evil sought to be remedied; 

4. Purpose to be accomplished.

If from all these it appears that it was the intention of the legislature to render the prohibited act void, the courts will so hold. Pangborn v. Westlake, 36 Iowa 

546; Burck v. Taylor, 152 U. S. 634, 38 Law. Ed. 578, 14

Sup. Ct. Rep. 696. Thus, a contract for the payment of wages of employees otherwise than semi-monthly and in cash is void where the statute makes such companies liable to criminal prosecution for failure to pay at such time and in such manner. New York Central & Hudson River R. R. 

Co. v. Williams, 199 N. Y. 108, 92 N. E. 404, 35 L. R. A. (N.S.) 549. So, also, the imposition, by statute, of a penalty for a violation of an excise law which is enacted for

public protection, renders a contract in violation thereof void by implication. Griffith v. Wells, 3 Denio (N. Y.) 

226. Likewise, where the charter of a bank forbids the taking of more than six per centum as interest, but does not expressly declare a contract for more to be void, such a contract may nevertheless be declared void as contravening the statute. United States Bank v. Owens, 2 Pet. (U.S.) 527, 7 Law. Ed. 508.

Where, however, a statute imposes a penalty merely for the purpose of revenue and not for public protection, as in the case where a tax or license fee is required to be paid before engaging in business or where a revenue stamp is required to be affixed to an instrument, a contract, though in violation of the statute, is valid. The reason advanced is that the doing of the act penalized by statute is permitted upon payment of the penalty; that the penalty is a species of license money exacted for the privilege of doing the act; and that the act is not otherwise unlawful unless expressly so declared. Hughes v. Snell, 28 Okla. 828, 115 

Pac. 1105, 34 L. R. A. (N. S.) 1133. But where the statute expressly declares the act unlawful, there can be no recovery whatsoever for the illegal services performed. Plumber's case, Johnston v. Dahlgren, 166 N. Y. 354, 59 N. E. 987. 

In Uhlmann v. Kin Daw, 97 Ore. 881, 193 Pac. 435, the 

Court said: "Of course, if a statute expressly declares that an agreement made in contravention of it is void, then the inquiry is at an end; but, in the absence of such a declaration, the court may take the statute by its four corners and carefully consider the terms of the statute, its object, the evil it was enacted to remedy, and the effect of holding agreements in violation of it void, for the purpose of ascertaining whether it was the legislative intent to make such agreements void; and if from all these considerations it is manifest that the lawmakers had no such intention, the agreements should be held to be legal contracts and enforceable as such."

§ 143. Statutes Requiring License To Engage in Profes sion, Trade, or Business.

Where a statute or an ordinance makes the practice of a particular profession or carrying on of a particular business unlawful for unlicensed persons, any contract made in such profession or business by one not licensed is void. 

The license is required for public protection and not for revenue purposes only. It protects in that it prevents improper and unqualified persons from acting in a particular capacity. Thus, a person acting without license as an attorney-at-law cannot recover for his services. Penal 

Law, secs. 271 and 271-a; Kaplan v. Berman, 37 Misc. 502, 75 N. Y. S. 1002; Buxton v. Lietz, 139 N. Y. S. 46; Matter of Co-operative Law Co., 198 N. Y. 479, 92 N. E. 15, 32

L. R. A. (N.S.) 55. The same doctrine applies to unlicensed physicians, dentists, and school teachers. Accetta v. Zupa, 54 App. Div. 33, 66 N. Y. S. 303; O'Beirne v. 

Carey, 150 N. Y. S. 666; O'Connor v. Francis, 42 App. 

Div. 375, 59 N. Y. S. 28.

The same principle has been applied where, for the protection of the public, a license is required in order to carry on a particular trade or business, such as that of pawnbroker, plumber, undertaker, or the like. Thus, the contracts of a pawnbroker who fails to take out a license or to comply with the requirements of the statutes and ordinances as to recording the contracts which he makes with his customers are void, and he can claim no lien upon the pledges which he has received. Levison v. Boas, 150 Cal. 

185, 88 Pac. 825, 12 L. R. A. (N.S.) 575. In Johnston v. 

Dahlgren, 166 N. Y. 354, 59 N. E. 987, the Court held that a failure to comply with the statute providing for the registration of plumbers precluded a recovery for such work performed by a plumber. Undertaker's case, People v. 

Ringe, 197 N. Y. 143, 90 N. E. 451, 27 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

528. The provisions of the Real Property Law requiring real estate brokers to obtain licenses are constitutional, and an unlicensed broker cannot recover for services rendered.

Real Property Law, sec. 440-a. See, also, Editorial, New 

York Law Journal, October 15, 1930.

The legislature, however, has not the power to impose a license or other restriction on a person as a prerequisite to engaging in a certain business where the occupation to which it relates is of such a character that the public interest and welfare do not require its regulation. If the legislature has thus attempted to exercise its power by statutory regulation, the statute is unconstitutional. Fisher Co. v. 

Woods, 187 N. Y. 90, 79 N. E. 836, 12 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

707; Lincoln Trust Co. v. Williams Building Corp., 229 N. 

Y. 313, 128 N. E. 209.

Statutes Regulating Dealings in Articles of Com- 

Statutes regulating dealings in articles of commerce have been held to render void sales which contravene their provisions; e.g., statutes requiring weights and measures to be approved and sealed by the proper officer, or requiring goods to be inspected, branded, labeled, tagged, and weighed. These statutes are for the protection of the public against fraud and imposition from sales of spurious articles. No recovery can be had for the purchase of articles sold in violation of the statute. Gaulding Fertilizer Co. v. Driver, 99 Ga. 623, 25 S. E. 922; Bisbee v. 

McAllen, 39 Minn. 143, 39 N. W. 299. For a list of properties, the use of which has been controlled by statute, see 

Lincoln Trust Co. v. Williams Building Corp., 229 N. Y. 

313, 317, 128 N. E. 209. § 144. merce. $ 145. 

Contracts in Violation of Sunday Laws.

The common law does not prohibit the making of contracts on Sunday. Therefore, in the absence of a statute, a contract made on Sunday is as valid as if made on any other day. The statute is the only guide for determining the validity of Sunday contracts. Due to the want of uniformity in the phraseology of the statutes, there is much

conflict of authority in the decisions in different states as to the validity of a contract which is in violation of Sunday laws. The main provisions of the New York law are found in the following sections of the Penal Law:

Sunday labor. Section 2143. All labor on Sunday is prohibited, except the works of necessity or charity. In works of necessity or charity is included whatever is needful during the day for the good order, health, or comfort of the community.

The Penal Law, section 2146, further provides that all trades, manufactures, agricultural or mechanical employment upon the first day of the week are prohibited, except when they constitute works of necessity, in which case they may be performed on that day in their usual and orderly manner, so as not to interfere with the repose and religious liberty of the community. 

Other provisions to be noted in the Penal Law are as follows: Section 2144 protects one who labors on Sunday in a quiet manner, if he observes another day of the week as holy time. Section 2145 prohibits public sports on Sunday, but permits baseball games after two o'clock in cities which permit the same. Section 2147 prohibits, with limitations, the sale of food and refreshments on Sunday. Sections 2152 and 2154 prohibit theatrical and motion picture exhibitions and the like on Sunday, except that cities may permit motion picture exhibitions after two o'clock. Section 2152-a authorizes, within certain limitations, the performance of concerts and recital dances after two o'clock on Sunday, provided the legislative body of the city, town, or village permits such concerts and dances. Section 2153 prohibits barbering throughout the state on Sunday. 

These sections do not condemn or render void a contract made on Sunday. A deed delivered on Sunday is valid. 

McCormick v. Hazard, 77 Misc. 190, 136 N. Y. S. 91.

Private sales, as distinguished from public sales, are legal, for a private sale, where the property is not "exposed to sale," is not within the provisions of the statute. Penal

Law, sec. 2147; Eberle v. Mehrbach, 55 N. Y. 682; Batsford v. Every, 44 Barb. 618. Selling or publicly offering real property for sale on Sunday is not prohibited, because such acts were lawful at common law and are not condemned by statute. The rule is that anything not prohibited by statute may lawfully be done on Sunday. People v. Dunford, 207 N. Y. 17, 100 N. E. 433.

Since all labor on Sunday, except works of necessity and charity, is prohibited, a contract which requires prohibited services to be performed on Sunday is void. Thus, one who teaches music or instructs in the art of photography on Sunday cannot recover for services rendered. Bilordeaux v. Bencke Lithographing Co., 30 N. Y. St. Rep. 656, 9 N. Y.

S. 507. So, also, a contract by which an opera singer agrees to perform on a Sunday is void because the service is labor within the meaning of the statute. Hallen v. Thompson, 48 Misc. 642, 96 N. Y. S. 142. Paying a debt, making a will, and settling litigation or disputes are not condemned because the acts are not regarded as labor within the meaning of the Sunday laws. Rapp v. Reehling, 124 Ind. 36, 23 N. E. 777, 7 L. R. A. 498; Shank v. Shoemaker, 18 N. 

Y. 489.

As a rule, whatever must be done in order to preserve life, health, or property, and which must be done on Sunday if done at all, is a work of necessity or charity as thus defined. But if the work is only to prevent a loss on a secular day, as where a mill or wheel is cleaned on Sunday because to do so on another day will close the mill, it is not a work of necessity. McGrath v. Merwin, 112 Mass. 467, 17 Am. 

Rep. 119.

There is no liability to pay for services rendered on Sunday during prohibited hours. The Sunday act has been held constitutional in New York and in most jurdisdictions, but unconstitutional in Illinois and certain other states. People v. Havnor, 149 N. Y. 195, 43 N. E. 541; Eden v. People, 161 Ill. 296, 43 N. E. 1108, 32 L. R. A. 659; State v. Murray, 104 Neb. 51, 175 N. W. 666, 8 A. L. R. 563.

§ 146. Sunday Newspapers. 

Contracts for advertising in Sunday newspapers are expressly declared to be valid by statute. General Business 

Law, sec. 333. Aside from such statutory provision, it has been held that publishing a newspaper on Sunday is a work of necessity. The opinion is based on the great service the press renders to humanity on Sunday as well as on every other day of the week, and the view that its beneficence is more potent on Sunday than on any other day because of the leisure afforded for reading. Pulitzer Publishing Co. v. McNichols, 170 Mo. App. 709, 181 S. W. 1, L. R. A. 1916C 1148. § 147. Holidays Other Than Sunday.

All contracts and business transactions made and performed on a legal holiday other than Sunday are valid. Negotiable instruments maturing on a legal holiday become payable on the next succeeding business day. However, public offices of the state and of the counties must be closed on legal holidays. Page v. Shainwald, 169 N. Y. 246, 62 

N. E. 356, 57 L. R. A. 173.

The following days are declared holidays by the General 

Construction Law, section 24: New Year's Day; Lincoln's and Washington's birthdays; Memorial Day; the Fourth of July; Labor Day; Columbus Day; Armistice Day; 

Christmas Day; and if any of such days falls on Sunday, the next day thereafter is deemed a holiday. General election day and Thanksgiving Day are also holidays under the statute. The term half-holiday is applied to every Saturday from noon until midnight if not otherwise a holiday. 

Half-holidays present a comparatively new problem in the law. The provision in the General Construction Law, section 25, which allows "the payment of money or the performance of a condition" on a holiday to be performed on the next succeeding business day does not apply to a Saturday half-holiday, which for this purpose is not regarded as

a holiday. Andes Co-operative Dairy Co. v. Commercial 

Casualty Insurance Co., 207 App. Div. 102, 201 N. Y. S. 

664, aff'd 237 N. Y. 622, 143 N. E. 767.

GAMBLING OR WAGERING CONTRACTS § 148. General Nature of Gambling and Wagering Con tracts.

A gambling contract arises between parties who agree to play by cards, dice, or other contrivance whereby one shall lose and the other gain. "Illegal gambling implies gain and loss between the parties by betting, such as would excite a spirit of cupidity." Harris v. White, 81 N. Y. 

532, 539.

A wager is a promise to give something upon the determination or ascertainment of an uncertain event in which the parties have no interest except that which they create by the wager. Jordan v. Kent, 44 How. Pr. (N.Y.) 206. 

See, also, Cooney v. Hauck, 112 Kan. 562, 211 Pac. 617. $149. Legal at Common Law.

Wagering and gambling contracts were valid at common law. Da Costa v. Jones, 2 Cowper (K. B. Div.) 729, 98 

Eng. Rep. 1331; Thompson v. Hayes, 59 Misc. 425, 111 

N. Y. S. 495; Campbell v. Richardson, 10 Johns. (N.Y.) 

406. But public gaming houses were held to be nuisances because they bred idleness and brought disorderly persons together in great numbers. Ordinary betting is not a crime, but keeping a gambling house is punishable as such. 

Penal Law, sec. 973; People v. Langan, 196 N. Y. 260, 89 

N. E. 921; Thompson v. Hayes, supra. 

§ 150. Illegal by Statute. 

Statutes in New York and in most states now expressly declare all gaming and wagering contracts to be void. The 

State Constitution in New York, Article 1, section 9, expressly provides, inter alia: "Nor shall any lottery or the

sale of lottery tickets, pool-selling, book-making, or any other kind of gambling hereafter be authorized or allowed within this State." The Penal Law, section 992, provides that all contracts for, or on account of, any money or property wagered, bet, or staked shall be void. And the Penal

Law, section 993, provides that securities of whatsoever nature given for money lost at gaming, "shall be utterly void." Therefore, notes, checks, mortgages, conveyances, etc. given in payment of gambling losses are void. Hollingsworth v. Moulton, 53 Hun 91, 6 N. Y. S. 362, aff'd 119 

N. Y. 612, 23 N. E. 1143; Larschen v. Lantzes, 115 Misc. 

616, 189 N. Y. S. 137.

The gambling statute also imposes a penalty for exacting payment of money or property won at cards, faro, etc. 

Penal Law, sec. 989.

Property staked under a bet or wager may be recovered of the winner or stakeholder. Penal Law, sec. 994; Kohler v. Rosenthal, 135 App. Div. 438, 120 N. Y. S. 325. But where under this section, plaintiff, a casual better, sued defendant, a professional gambler, to recover money paid for bets, the defendant was not permitted to set off his losses against the plaintiff's claim, on the ground that the legislature intended to discourage gambling and, hence, could not have intended to confer the benefits of the section upon a professional gambler. Watts v. Malatesta, 262 

N. Y. 80, 186 N. E. 210, 88 A. L. R. 1072.

Losers of certain sums at a gaming table may recover their losses. Penal Law, sec. 995.

For the statutory provisions in reference to lotteries, see the Penal Law, sections 1370 to 1386.

As to prizes, purses, and betting at horse races, see the 

Laws of 1934, Chapters 233 and 310. 

§ 151. Wager Contracts Disguised as Future Sales. 

A form of wager often difficult to detect exists where an apparent contract of sale is made for future delivery, in

which the parties do not intend delivery of the thing contracted for, but expect to settle the contract by paying and receiving the difference between the market price and the price agreed upon. Such a contract is in reality a bet or a wager on the market price at the time fixed. But if the parties intend, in good faith, a performance of the contract by actual delivery of the property and payment therefor according to the terms of the contract, it will be valid. The actual intention of the parties may be difficult to prove or disprove; but when once the intention is established, there is no difficulty in applying the law. West v. Wright, 86 Hun 

436, 33 N. Y. S. 898; Weld v. Postal Telegraph Cable Co., 199 N. Y. 88, 92 N. E. 415; Mohr v. Miesen, 47 Minn. 228, 49 N. W. 862. 

§ 152. Construction of Option Contracts and Stock Trans actions.

Option contracts which can be interpreted as legal in their intent will not be readily condemned. The courts will not presume an illegal intent on the part of the parties. In 

Story v. Salomon, 71 N. Y. 420, 422, the Court said: "We may guess that the parties were speculating upon the fluctuations in the price of the stock, and that the defendant was not to be required to take or deliver any stock in any case, but simply to pay the difference. But a contract which can have a legal interpretation and effect should not be condemned, without any proof, in that way." In re Taylor & 

Co., 192 Pa. St. 304, 43 Atl. 973; Hurd v. Taylor, 181 N. Y. 

231, 73 N. E. 977. To make a future sale a wager, the intention to settle the differences must be entertained by both parties to the transaction; such an intention by one party alone will not invalidate the contract. Springs v. James, 137 App. Div. 110, 121 N. Y. S. 1054, aff'd 202 N. Y. 603, 96 N. E. 1131. § 153. Lotteries. "A lottery' is a scheme for the distribution of property

by chance, among persons who have paid or agreed to pay a valuable consideration for the chance, whether called a lottery, raffle, or gift enterprise or by some other name." 

Penal Law, sec. 1370. “A lottery is unlawful and a public nuisance." Penal Law, sec. 1371. A scheme providing for the distribution of money and cigars among purchasers of certain brands of cigars, such articles to be awarded to those who should most closely estimate the number of cigars of all brands upon which the government would collect taxes during a month named, has been held to be embraced within the statutory prohibition against a lottery. 

People ex rel. Ellison v. Lavin, 179 N. Y. 164, 71 N. E. 

753, 66 L. R. A. 601.

USURIOUS CONTRACTS 

§ 154. 

At Common Law.

At common law, any rate of interest agreed upon was enforceable, but to protect necessitous borrowers against unconscionable exactions, usury laws, prescribing a legal rate of interest, have been enacted in most states. General Business Law, sec. 370. To constitute usury, there must be an agreement to pay more than the legal rate of interest. Bill v. Fish, 40 Hun 639, 1 N. Y. St. Rep. 473. In the great majority of usurious transactions a subterfuge of one kind or another is resorted to for the purpose of giving the transaction a legal appearance. Such appearance, however, never deters the court from pronouncing the transaction usurious, as soon as that fact appears. Schanz v. 

Solscheck, 167 App. Div. 202, 152 N. Y. S. 851; Diehl v. 

Becker, 227 N. Y. 318, 125 N. E. 533. But clear and cogent proof is necessary to establish the defense of usury. 

Coast Finance Corp. v. Ira F. Powers Furniture Co., 105 

Ore. 339, 209 Pac. 614, 24 A. L. R. 855. 

§ 155. By Statute. 

The New York statute provides that usury renders void all bonds, bills, notes, and other contracts or securities of

whatsoever nature. There is no right to recover for money loaned where the contract is tainted with usury. General 

Business Law, sec. 373; Oppikofer v. Murphy, 146 App. 

Div. 581, 131 N. Y. S. 168. See Editorial, New York Law

Journal, August 10, 1926. But the usury laws are not restricted to cases of actual loans of money; they apply to agreements for the "forbearance of any money" as well. 

Hence, a usurious guarantee of a mortgage and an extension agreement, both executed as a part of one transaction, are void, even though preceded by no loan of money. Lonlon v. Toney, 263 N. Y. 439, 189 N. E. 485. 

If a promissory note be void in its inception for usury, it remains void even though it subsequently comes into the hands of a bona fide purchaser for value. Sabine v. Paine, 223 N. Y. 401, 119 N. E. 849, 5 A. L. R. 1444. By statute in New York, however, an exception is made in a case where a bank has in good faith discounted negotiable paper for value before maturity and without notice that it is tainted with usury. The defense of usury is, under these circumstances, not available against the bank. Banking 

Law, sec. 114; Richardson on Bills and Notes, 4th Ed., sec. 152. 

As to the rate of interest which may be charged by pawnbrokers, see the General Business Law, section 46.

As to the rate of interest which may be charged by state banks, see the Banking Law, section 114.

As to the rate of interest which may be charged by licensed lenders, see the Banking Law, sec. 352.

Demand loans of five thousand dollars or upward may bear any rate of interest agreed upon by the parties. General Business Law, sec. 379.

Corporations and joint stock companies are prohibited from interposing usury as a defense. General Business 

Law, sec. 374; Hubbard v. Todd, 171 U. S. 474, 4 Law. Ed. 

246, 19 Sup. Ct. Rep. 14. So, also, indorsers and guarantors or sureties of corporate obligations cannot interpose

usury as a defense. Salvin v. Myles Realty Co., 227 N. Y. 

51, 124 N. E. 94, 6 A. L. R. 581. A usurious loan to a corporation is not invalid, even though the corporation was itself formed as part of a plan whereby the borrower would be unable to interpose the defense of usury. Such procedure is not a device for avoiding the usury law, but is simply a method of complying with its terms. Jenkins v. Moyse, 254 N. Y. 319, 172 N. E. 521, 74 A. L. R. 205.

A lender, by agreement with the borrower, has a right to deduct from the amount to be paid by him on a loan, the actual expenses incurred in making the loan and such sums as will meet future expenses in connection therewith, reasonably and honestly to be anticipated. Brown v. Robinson, 224 N. Y. 301, 120 N. E. 694, 21 A. L. R. 777. Moreover, an agreement to pay an amount which may be more or less than the legal rate of interest, depending upon a reasonable contingency, is not ipso facto usurious. The question in each case is whether there has been good faith or an intent to evade the usury laws. Hartley v. Eagle Insurance Co., 222 N. Y. 178, 118 N. E. 622. 

§ 156. Compound Interest. 

Agreements from time to time to pay interest on interest which has already become due is not usurious. However, an agreement in advance for the payment of interest upon interest, as the same shall accrue, is invalid, not because it is usurious but because it is against public policy, as it might be made oppressive to the debtor. Stewart v. Petree, 55 

N. Y. 621, 14 Am. Rep. 352; Reusens v. Argenburgh, 135 

App. Div. 75, 119 N. Y. S. 821. 

157. Usury as a Crime.

The offense of taking certain property as security for usurious loans is a misdemeanor. Penal Law, sec. 2400.

§ 158. 

Agreement To Commit Crime.

An agreement to commit a crime or a promise to give a reward for the commission of a crime is clearly void. But in addition to agreements to commit acts expressly made criminal or illegal by statute, there is a large class of contracts said to be void as against public policy. § 159. 

Public Policy Defined.

There is no precise definition of public policy, and consequently no absolute rule by which a contract can be measured or tested to determine whether or not it is contrary to public policy. Each case, as it arises, must be judged and determined according to its own circumstances. Veazey v. 

Allen, 173 N. Y. 359, 368, 66 N. E. 103, 62 L. R. A. 362.

The public policy of the state or of the nation is to be found in its constitution, statutes, and judicial decisions. United 

States v. Trans-Missouri Freight Assn., 166 U. S. 290, 41 Law. Ed. 1007, 17 Sup. Ct. Rep. 540. Under the common law, public policy will not permit individuals to obligate themselves by a contract either to do or not to do anything, when the thing to be done or omitted is, in any degree, clearly injurious to the public. The test is the evil tendency of the contract and not its actual injury to the public in a particular instance. In Messersmith v. American Fidelity Co., 232 N. Y. 161, 133 N. E. 432, 19 A. L. R. 

876, the Court of Appeals, Cardozo, J., declared: "The public policy of this state when the legislature acts is what the legislature says it shall be. 

Courts are slow to substitute their own varying views of policy for those which have found embodiment in settled institutions, in every day beliefs and practices, which have taken root and flourished. * * * The field of discretion is still narrower when there has been statutory sanction, tacit, if not express, of callings and forms of conduct which it would have been easy to condemn." Steen v. Modern Woodmen, 296 Ill. 104, 129 N. E. 546, 17 A. L. R. 406. * ** *

The following are instances of agreements against public policy: An agreement to influence legislation is void, although the legislation sought may be clearly beneficial. An agreement to influence an appointment to office is void, although the intent may be to secure the best qualified person. So, also, agreements whose tendency is to establish a monopoly are void, although, in the particular case, they may not destroy competition or enhance prices. The law looks to the general tendency of such agreements, and it closes the door to temptation by refusing them recognition in any of its courts. Providence Tool Co. v. Norris, 2 Wall. (U.S.) 45, 17 Law. Ed. 868. An agreement made at the inception of a liability to the effect that the statute of limitations shall not be pleaded as a defense thereto is void in New York. There is authority to the contrary in other jurisdictions. Shapley v. Abbott, 42 N. Y. 443; Mutual

Life Insurance Co. v. United States Hotel Co., 82 Misc. 

632, 144 N. Y. S. 476. § 160. 

Agreements To Commit a Tort.

An agreement to commit a tort is illegal and void as against public policy. "A court will not lend aid to a party who has committed a tort to enable him to recover from another the price agreed to be paid for his wrongful act. 

The defense is allowed not as a protection to a defendant, but as a disability to the plaintiff." Reiner v. North American Newspaper Alliance, 259 N. Y. 250, 256, 181 N. E. 561, 83 A. L. R. 23. An agreement to publish a libelous book or article is void. 

§ 161. Agreements To Deceive the Public or Third 

Persons.

Agreements made with the intent that they shall operate as a fraud upon the private rights of third persons are void as against public policy. Thus, in Church v. Proctor, 66 

Fed. 240, it was held a good defense to an action for the breach of a contract for the sale of menhadden that the

buyer intended to pack and sell them as mackerel. In this case, however, there was participation in the illegal act by the seller. An agreement for the use of the name of a music director by a band with which he was not connected was held to be void, as its purpose was to deceive the public. Blakely v. Sousa, 197 Pa. 305, 47 Atl. 286. Likewise, a contract for the sale of the editorial influence of a newspaper is illegal and void, such editorials being understood by the public to express the true and unbought views of the publishers. King v. Raleigh & Pamlico Sound 

R. R. Co., 147 N. C. 263, 60 S. E. 1133.

Agreements in fraud of creditors are void. Thus, a composition agreement between a debtor and his creditors is void where, in order to procure the consent of some particular creditor, the debtor secretly promises to pay him an additional sum over the other creditors. But in New York it is held that only the secret agreement with the one creditor is void and that the composition is otherwise valid. Hanover National Bank v. Blake, 142 N. Y. 404, 37 N. E. 519, 27 L. R. A. 33. So, too, agreements which have for their object the fraudulent raising or lowering of prices at auction sales by the employment of "puffers" to make fictitious bids or by attempts to refrain from bidding and thus stifle competition, are void. Hence, a "puffer" is without remedy to recover his compensation because his contract is illegal, and the purchaser may avoid the contract of purchase on the ground of fraud. Bowman v. McClenahan, 20 App. Div. 346, 46 

N. Y. S. 945. However, not every agreement which may, to some extent, lessen competition among bidders is illegal. 

For example, an agreement of several persons to unite in making a joint bid at an auction sale may be actuated by a proper motive, in which event it is valid. Stack v. Roth 

Bros. Co., 162 Wis. 281, 156 N. W. 148. A and B enter into a contract. Thereafter, A and C, who knows of the existence of the contract between A and B, enter into a new contract, the performance of which

will, to the knowledge of both parties, necessarily involve the breach of the earlier contract. If A subsequently sues 

C to recover damages for the breach of the second contract, can C successfully plead the defense of illegality? 

Budd v. Morning Telegraph, Inc., 241 App. Div. 142, 271 

N. Y. S. 538. 

§ 162. 

Agreements Relieving Tort-Feasors from Liability. 

The rule, not without qualification, has often been asserted that a person cannot by contract obtain relief from liability for a tort which he may commit in the future. 

Appleton & Co. v. Warbasse, 92 Misc. 42, 155 N. Y. S. 987; 

Shohoney v. Quincy, Omaha & Kansas City R. R. Co., 231 

Mo. 131, 132 S. W. 1059. At all events, however, contracts exempting a tort-feasor from liability will be strictly construed against such tort-feasor. The weight of authority sustains the view that an employer cannot release himself by contract in advance from liability for injury to his employee caused by the employer's negligence. The reason for this holding is the interest the state has "in the conservation of the lives and of the healthful vigor of its citizens, and if employers could contract away their responsibility at common law, it would tend to encourage on their part laxity of conduct in, if not an indifference to, the maintenance of proper and reasonable safeguards to human life and limb." 

The highest incentive to the exercise of due care rests in a consciousness that a failure in this respect will fix liability to make full compensation for the injury. Johnston v. 

Fargo, 184 N. Y. 379, 77 N. E. 388, 7 L. R. A. (N.S.) 537.

But a person may elect to accept certain compensation in lieu of his common law remedies and thus waive his common law rights. Barnhart v. American Concrete Steel Co., 227 N. Y. 531, 125 N. E. 675. § 163.

Contracts Waiving Statutory Rights. 

Statutes are often passed to protect persons against certain types of contracts. The very purpose of such statutes

would be defeated if their provisions could be waived by contract. For example, an instalment sale contract which gave to the vendor the right to retake possession upon default of payments by the vendee and to sell the property either at public or private sale and which further provided that the vendee waived all his statutory rights relating thereto was held void in so far as such waiver was concerned, since the purpose of the statute is the protection of the poor or necessitous, and this is accomplished by preserving some rights to the vendee by statute which the courts will not allow him to waive by contract. Crowe v. 

Liquid Carbonic Co., 208 N. Y. 396, 102 N. E. 573. The

Uniform Conditional Sales Act to-day expressly forbids a waiver by the conditional sales vendee of the statutory protection afforded him. Uniform Conditional Sales Act, sec. 26; Personal Property Law, sec. 80-f. But a new agreement made upon a new consideration after the vendee has become in default is valid. Adler v. Weis & 

Fisher Co., 218 N. Y. 295, 112 N. E. 1049. In accordance with the same general principle, under statutes providing that wages must be paid in lawful money, a clause in a contract waiving such right is invalid. Hancock v. 

Yaden, 121 Ind. 366, 23 N. E. 253, 6 L. R. A. 576. So, too, that portion of a contract whereby the promisor waives in advance his statutory rights of exemption in his property is void in most jurisdictions, on the theory that the statute is enacted for the protection of necessitous debtors, and to allow them to contract away their rights in advance would be to defeat the purpose of the statute. Kneettle v. 

Newcomb, 22 N. Y. 249, 78 Am. Dec. 186. § 164.

Agreements Contrary to Good Morals. 

The most important aspect of immorality with which courts of law have dealt on the ground of public policy is sexual immorality. But in rare instances, other forms of immorality have been held to render a contract void. Thus, where two children sought to divide up the family inheri-

tance by a secret agreement between them irrespective of the will of their parent, the agreement was declared illegal because it encouraged disrespect towards parents. Mercier v. Mercier, 50 Ga. 546, 15 Am. Rep. 694. A contract may be valid though moral turpitude may be involved in its performance. While it is true that a contract may be void when it involves immoral conduct, it is equally true that it is not void simply because there is something immoral in its surroundings or connections. To have this effect, the direct object of the contract must be the promotion of immorality. 

Moore v. Remington, 34 Barb. 427; Baumeister v. Markham, 101 Ky. 122, 41 S. W. 816; Anheuser-Busch Brewing 

Assn. v. Mason, 44 Minn. 318, 46 N. W. 558, 9 L. R. A. 506.

But contracts may be illegal because of immorality even though they do not involve sexual intercourse between the parties to the contract. Thus, a contract for the sale of indecent publications may be void. See St. Hubert Guild v. Quinn, 64 Misc. 336, 118 N. Y. S. 582. So, also, where the lessor knows and intends that premises shall be leased for immoral purposes, the lease is void, and the obligation of the lessee to pay rent will not be enforced. 

Ernst v. 

Crosby, 140 N. Y. 364, 35 N. E. 603.

An agreement in consideration of future illicit cohabitation is void. Steinfeld v. Levy, 16 Abb. Pr. (N. S.) 26. In 

Trovinger v. McBurney, 5 Cowen (N. Y.) 253, an agreement to board an illegitimate child and its mother, the father to be allowed to continue the illicit relations, was held void. See, also, Tyrell v. York, 57 Hun 292, 10 N. Y. 

S. 611. A promise made in consideration of past illicit cohabitation is not illegal; it is regarded as a promise in reparation of wrong-doing. Bum v. Winthrop, 1 Johns. 

Ch. (N.Y.) 329; Todd v. Weber, 95 N. Y. 181, 47 Am.

Rep. 20. So, also, a note given in consideration of past cohabitation has been held not to be illegal. People v. 

Hayes, 140 N. Y. 484, 35 N. E. 951, 23 L. R. A. 830.

§ 165. Agreements Tending To Injure Public Service or 

Good Government.

The efficiency of public service is a matter of vital concern to the public, and, therefore, all agreements tending to injure public service or good government are void as being contrary to public policy. Thus, agreements to control the business operations of the government, or the appointment to public office, or the ordinary course of legislation are invalid, and this, too, without reference to the parties' motives, whether corrupt or otherwise, or whether improper means are contemplated or used in their execution. Trist v. Child, 21 Wall. (U.S.) 441, 22 Law. Ed. 623.

A combination to obtain contracts from the government is not in itself illegal, and contracts to combine for this purpose will be enforced by the courts. Hegness v. Chilberg, 224 Fed. 28. Thus, an agreement between A and B, whereby B agrees to give A a certain percentage of the profits of a contract that A may obtain from the government for B, is valid. Beck v. Bauman, 187 App. Div. 774, 175 N. Y. S. 

881; Dunham v. Hastings Pavement Co., 118 App. Div. 127, 103 N. Y. S. 480, aff'd 189 N. Y. 500, 81 N. E. 1163. But it is otherwise when the contract provides for the use of improper means and influence which tend to affect the public injuriously. For example, an agreement for services in securing contracts by favoritism from state officials, by reason of social and political relations with such officials, is void. Drake v. Lauer, 93 App. Div. 86, 86 N. Y. S. 

986, aff'd 182 N. Y. 533, 75 N. E. 1129.

The federal decisions, holding that contracts for compensation in consideration of procuring contracts with the government to furnish it supplies are void, are directly contrary to the New York decisions. Hazelton v. Sheckells, 202 U. S. 71, 50 Law. Ed. 939, 26 Sup. Ct. Rep. 567; Southard v. Boyd, 51 N. Y. 177; Beck v. Bauman, supra. In order to invalidate such a contract in New York, it must appear that the agreement itself contemplated the illegal act, and it is not rendered illegal merely because condem-

nable acts were done thereunder if they were not contemplated by the agreement. Drake v. Lauer, supra. 

Agreements to secure legislative action are valid when they contemplate the use of lawful means. For instance, an agreement for professional services in drafting petitions to the legislative body, in collecting evidence, preparing arguments and submitting them, either orally or in writing, to legislative committees, and the like, is as free from the taint of illegality as a contract for the retaining of professional services in a court of law. Cheseborough v. Conover, 140 

N. Y. 382, 35 N. E. 633. But if an agreement contemplates the use of unlawful means in procuring favorable legislation, or if legislation is sought to further an unlawful purpose, it is void. Veazey v. Allen, 173 N. Y. 359, 66 N. E. 

103, 62 L. R. A. 362.

The constitutional right to petition those invested with the powers of government cannot be surrendered by a citizen. Hence, the by-law of a mutual benefit society providing for the expulsion of members who petition the legislature to repeal a statute which has the sanction of the governing body of the society is void, as an infringement of the constitutional right of petition. Spayd v. Ringing Rock 

Lodge No. 665, 270 Pa. 67, 113 Atl. 70, 14 A. L. R. 1443. 

§ 166. Agreements Tending To Prevent the Course of 

Justice.

Agreements which tend to obstruct or interfere with the administration of justice are void. They may be classified as follows: 

1. Agreements compounding crimes; 

2. Agreements ousting courts of jurisdiction; 

3. Agreements tending to encourage litigation-champerty and maintenance. 

§ 167. Agreements Compounding Crimes. 

Criminal prosecutions should be instituted or dismissed solely for the good of the state and not for private gain,

Therefore, agreements to stifle criminal prosecutions are void because they tend to obstruct public justice. Maxfield v. Hoecker, 49 Hun 605, 2 N. Y. S. 77. But a contract to make reparation is valid, provided it does not stipulate that the prosecution for the offense shall be suppressed or stayed. Barrett v. Weber, 125 N. Y. 18, 25 N. E. 1068; 

Board of Education v. Angel, 75 W. Va. 747, 84 S. E. 747,

L. R. A. 1915E 139. An agreement to suppress the evidence of a crime alleged to have been committed is illegal regardless of the fact that the person charged is innocent. 

Union Exchange National Bank v. Joseph, 231 N. Y. 250, 131 N. E. 905, 17 A. L. R. 323. A promise not to prosecute is not illegal, where it is made not for the sake of gain but from motives of kindness or on account of relationship. 

Hatch v. Collins, 34 Hun 314; Dodson v. Swan, 2 W. Va. 

511, 98 Am. Dec. 787.

The act of one who takes money under an agreement to compound or conceal a crime is itself made criminal in New 

York. Penal Law, sec. 570. § 168.

Offenses Which May Be Compromised. 

The New York Code of Criminal Procedure provides for the compromise of misdemeanors where the wrongdoer is both civilly and criminally liable, except in certain enumerated cases. Code of Criminal Procedure, secs. 663 and 

664; Cleaveland v. Cromwell, 110 App. Div. 82, 96 N. Y. S. 

475. In the absence of such a statute, the compounding of a misdemeanor is illegal. Aycock v. Gill, 183 N. C. 271, 111 

S. E. 342, 24 A. L. R. 1449. 

§ 169. Agreements Ousting Courts of Jurisdiction at Com mon Law.

Under the common law rule, the parties cannot by contract oust the courts of their jurisdiction. Therefore, an agreement requiring all differences and disputes arising under such agreement to be settled by arbitration to the exclusion of the courts is void. Although it is permissible

to submit to arbitration pending lawsuits or existing differences, it is not permissible for persons to stipulate in advance that in the event of differences arising in the future they will deny themselves the right to resort to the courts. 

Blodgett Co. v. Bebe Co., 190 Cal. 665, 214 Pac. 38, 26 A.

L. R. 1070. The reason for the common law rule is that, as the state has an interest in all its inhabitants, and it is to the state's interest that the rights of all shall be protected and enforced and that its courts shall be always open for the redress of wrongs, no person should be permitted by contract to deprive himself in advance of the right to appeal to them. But even under the common law rule the parties may agree in advance to leave to arbitration the determination of questions of fact, such as the amount of loss or damage or any other matters that do not leave to the arbitrators the sole question of liability for a breach of the contract. Hamilton v. Liverpool, London & Globe 

Insurance Co., 136 U. S. 242, 34 Law. Ed. 417, 10 Sup. Ct. 

Rep. 945; National. Contracting Co. v. Hudson River Co., 170 N. Y. 439, 63 N. E. 450, same case, 192 N. Y. 209, 84 

N. E. 965.

Agreements determining the jurisdiction in which actions shall be brought are void. An important decision in 

New York on this point is Meacham v. Jamestown, Franklin & Clearfield R. R. Co., 211 N. Y. 346, 105 N. E. 653; 

Nashua River Paper Co. v. Hammermill Paper Co., 223 

Mass. 8, 111 N. E. 678, L. R. A. 1916D 691. § 170. 

Arbitration Law.

It is now provided by statute in New York that a written contract to settle by arbitration a controversy arising between parties to a contract shall be valid, enforceable, and irrevocable, save upon such grounds as exist for the revocation of any contract. Arbitration Law, sec. 2. Similar statutes have been enacted in other states. The New York statute has been held to be constitutional. Berkovitz v.

Arbib & Houlberg, 230 N. Y. 261, 130 N. E. 288; Webster v. Van Allen, 217 App. Div. 219, 216 N. Y. S. 552. 

§ 171. Agreement Tending To Encourage Litigation 

Champerty and Maintenance. "Champerty" is a bargain between a person and the plaintiff to divide the matter sued for. "Maintenance" is the officious interference in a lawsuit by one who has no interest therein. For a history of the law in relation to champerty and maintenance, see Thallhimer v. Brinckerhoff, 3 Cowen (N.Y.) 623, 15 Am. Dec. 308. At common law, champertous agreements were unlawful because they encouraged litigation. Sedgwick v. Stanton, 14 N. Y. 289.

However, the common law relating to champerty and maintenance no longer obtains in New York except as preserved by statute. The only statutes relating thereto are section 274 of the Penal Law, and section 474 of the Judiciary Law. The former section prohibits an attorney from buying a claim for the purpose of bringing an action thereon. Bundy v. Newton, 65 Hun 619, 19 N. Y. S. 734, aff'd 

138 N. Y. 643, 34 N. E. 513; Browning v. Marvin, 100 N. 

Y. 144, 2 N. E. 635. It also prohibits an attorney from promising or giving to any person a valuable consideration as an inducement to place in his hands a demand for the purpose of having an action brought thereon. But this provision does not expressly prohibit an attorney from taking a case on a contingent fee, nor does it expressly deny him the right to take a case under an agreement with his client whereby he agrees to pay all necessary disbursements. Ransom v. Cutting, 188 N. Y. 447, 81 N. E. 324; 

McCoy v. Gas Engine & Power Co., 152 App. Div. 642, 137 N. Y. S. 591, aff'd without opinion, 208 N. Y. 631, 102

N. E. 1106. By the weight of authority in this country, a contract for a contingent fee by which an attorney agrees to save his client harmless from the costs and expenses of the proposed litigation is void. Stark County v. Mischel, 42 N. D. 332, 173 N. W. 817, 6 A. L. R. 174 and note. A 

---

contingent fee of fifty per centum is not necessarily unconscionable. In re Fitzsimmons, 174 N. Y. 15, 66 N. E. 

554; In re Meng, 227 N. Y. 264, 125 N. E. 508.

Agreements between an attorney and a layman or a corporation for "splitting fees" as an inducement or reward for the procuring of business are void. An attorney making such an agreement is guilty of a misdemeanor. Penal Law, sec. 274; In re Newman, 172 App. Div. 173, 158 N. Y. S. 

375; In re Clark, 184 N. Y. 222, 77 N. E. 1. 

§ 172. Lien of Attorney Secret Settlement between 

Parties. 

Quite commonly in this country, the attorney for the plaintiff is given by statute a lien for his services on the judgment or reward recovered. See Nichols v. Orr, 63 

Colo. 333, 166 Pac. 561, 2 A. L. R. 449. The statutory lien of an attorney for his services is created in New York by the Judiciary Law, section 475. Such lien cannot be defeated by a secret settlement between the parties. Peri v. New York Central & Hudson River R. R. Co., 152 N. Y. 

521, 46 N. E. 849. However, a stipulation in the contract between the attorney and client prohibiting the client from settling the litigation without the consent of the attorney is void as against public policy, though it does not prevent the lien of the attorney from attaching to the judgment. 

In re Snyder, 190 N. Y. 66, 82 N. E. 742, 14 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

1101; Beagles v. Robertson, 135 Mo. App. 306, 115 S. W. 

1042; Martin v. Camp, 219 N. Y. 170, 114 N. E. 46. 

§ 173. Agreements in Restraint of Marriage. 

It is the policy of the law to encourage marriage and to discourage any interference therewith. Therefore, agreements creating general restrictions on marriage are contrary to public policy and are void. Robinson v. Martin, 200 N. Y. 159, 93 N. E. 488; In re Seaman, 218 N. Y. 77, 112 N. E. 576. But a reasonable restraint, designed to prevent hasty or imprudent marriages, is permissible.

Sterling v. Sinnickson, 5 N. J. L. 756; Lewis v. Johnson, 212 Mo. App. 19, 251 S. W. 136; Barnes v. Hobson, 250

S. W. (Tex.) 238. Thus, an agreement among four sisters to bequeath their property to each other, to the exclusion as far as legally possible of any future husband, has been upheld. Kloberg v. Teller, 103 Misc. 641, 171 N. Y. S. 947.

But a promise by a man to an unmarried woman that, if she continued in his employ and cared for his wants until his death and did not marry until after his death, his executor would then pay her a specified sum has been held void as an agreement in general restraint of marriage. 

Lowe v. Doremus, 84 N. J. L. 658, 87 Atl. 459, 49 L. R. A. (N.S.) 632. Contra, King v. King, 63 Ohio St. 363, 59 

N. E. 111, 52 L. R. A. 157. So, too, an agreement to settle an action for breach of promise of marriage and to pay the plaintiff a specified amount three years after date if she is then alive and unmarried is void. McCoy v. Flynn, 169 Iowa 622, 151 N. W. 465, L. R. A. 1915D 1064.

It is not contrary to public policy, however, for a person having a parental interest in another to restrict marriage along certain lines, such as infancy and relationship. So also, agreements not to marry a person under a certain age, or in a certain degree of relationship, or into a certain family, or not to marry a second time have been held valid on the ground that the restraint is reasonable. Hogan v. Curtin, 88 N. Y. 162, 42 Am. Rep. 244.

The rule that renders void contracts which are in general restraint of marriage has no force when applied to second marriages. Contracts in restraint of a second marriage are uniformly affirmed, for the reason that: "Neither the conservation of morals nor public policy furnishes a basis for the rule as applied to the right of a husband or wife to withhold his or her estate from passing to the support of a second husband or wife, as the case might be."

For a general discussion of this question and the authorities, see Appleby v. Appleby, 100 Minn. 408, 111 N. W. 

305, 10 L. R. A. (N.S.) 590.

§ 174. Separation Agreements. 

On the ground of public policy, contracts between husband and wife which provide for their future separation are void, as in derogation of the marriage relation, the living apart of husband and wife being considered violative of public policy. Winter v. Winter, 191 N. Y. 462, 84 N. E. 382, 16 L. R. A. (N.S.) 710; Moore v. Moore, 255

Fed. 497. But where the spouses have separated, or where an immediate separation is the object of the contract whereby provision is made for the separate support of the wife, the contract is valid. Winter v. Winter, supra; Tirrell v. Tirrell, 232 N. Y. 224, 133 N. E. 569; La Montagne v. 

La Montagne, 239 App. Div. 352, 267 N. Y. S. 148. A subsequent reconciliation terminates the separation agreement. Zimmer v. Settle, 124 N. Y. 37, 26 N. E. 341.

Agreements to restore marital relationship upon a money consideration are not invalid as opposed to public policy in New York. Rodgers v. Rodgers, 229 N. Y. 255, 128 N. 

E. 117, 11 A. L. R. 274. A contrary rule is applied in some jurisdictions. Thus, in Merrill v. Peaslee, 146 Mass. 460, 16 N. E. 271, the Court said: "It is as much against public policy to restore interrupted conjugal relations for money, as it is to continue them without interruption for the same consideration." In New York, the contract is upheld on the ground that it is in the interest of public policy to restore peace and harmony between husband and wife and renew their conjugal relations. The New York view is sustained by a decided weight of authority. Darcey v. 

Darcey, 29 R. I. 384, 71 Atl. 595, 23 L. R. A. (N.S.) 886. 

§ 175. Marriage Brokerage Contracts. 

Agreements to pay for bringing about a marriage or to pay to assist a person in getting married by means of introductions to persons of the opposite sex are void, both at law and in equity, as being opposed to morality and public policy. The courts stress the fact that the broker's self interest leads to the temptation to exercise undue and

pernicious influence by holding out false and seductive hopes to the parties and thus tends to bring about a marriage without regard to their welfare or happiness. Duval v. Wellman, 124 N. Y. 156, 26 N. E. 343; Antcliff v. June, 81 Mich. 477, 45 N. W. 1019, 10 L. R. A. 621; Editorial, New York Law Journal, March 19, 1934. 

§ 176. Agreements in Restraint of Trade. 

Agreements in general and unlimited restraint of trade, when unreasonable, are void, but contracts in reasonable restraint of trade are valid. In all cases the test question is: Is the restraint reasonable? Standard Oil Co. v. United 

States, 221 U. S. 1, 55 Law. Ed. 619, 31 Sup. Ct. Rep. 502; 

Quincy Oil Co. v. Sylvester, 238 Mass. 95, 130 N. E. 217, 14 A. L. R. 111. If the protection to a successor in a business or profession is greater than is necessary to protect his rights, the restraint is unreasonable. What would be reasonable in one case, however, might be most unreasonable in another. Thus, restrictions as to space which might be reasonable when applied to a wholesale dealer, such as denying him the right to again engage in the same business in certain states, would be wholly unreasonable when applied to a local retail dealer. Whatever restraint is greater than is necessary for the protection of the party can be of no benefit to either. It can be only oppressive, and if oppressive, in legal contemplation, it is unreasonable. Samuel Stores v. Abrams, 94 Conn. 248, 108 Atl. 

541, 9 A. L. R. 1450; Gibbs v. Baltimore Consolidated Gas 

Co., 130 U. S. 396, 32 Law. Ed. 979, 31 Sup. Ct. Rep. 502.

Under modern decisions, the restraint may in some cases be practically unlimited as to both time and space. Diamond Match Co. v. Roeber, 106 N. Y. 473, 13 N. E. 419; 

Camors-McConnell Co. v. McConnell, 140 Fed. 412, aff'd p. 987; Central New York Telephone & Telegraph Co. v. 

Averill, 199 N. Y. 128, 92 N. E. 206, 32 L. R. A. (N.S.) 494.

A valid restrictive covenant by a vendor is deemed an incident of the business to which it relates, and it will pass

to, and may be enforced by, a subsequent purchaser of the business. Diamond Match Co. v. Roeber, supra; American 

Ice Co. v. Meckel, 109 App. Div. 93, 95 N. Y. S. 1060. § 177.

Restriction in Contract of Employment. 

Agreements by employees, ancillary to contracts of employment, restricting their right to labor along the same lines, either for themselves or others, upon the termination of their present employment have given rise to much controversy. In some jurisdictions, it is held that such agreements violate public policy, because the covenantors' means of procuring a livelihood for themselves and their families are thereby diminished, and where general and unlimited as to scope of territory are void. Taylor Iron & Steel Co. v. Nichols, 73 N. J. Eq. 684, 69 Atl. 186, 24 

L. R. A. (N.S.) 933; Kinney v. Scarborough Co., 138 Ga. 

77, 74 S. E. 772, 40 L. R. A. (N.S.) 473 and note. In other jurisdictions, the test of the validity of such contracts is based not so much on the question whether they are prejudicial to public interest, but whether they are reasonably necessary for the fair protection of the employer's business rights. Eureka Laundry Co. v. Long, 146 Wis. 205, 131 N. W. 412, 35 L. R. A. (N. S.) 119. The latter view is adopted in New York. McCall Co. v. Wright, 198 N. Y. 

143, 91 N. E. 516, 31 L. R. A. (N.S.) 249. See, also, Kaumagraph Co. v. Stampagraph Co., 235 N. Y. 1, 138 N. E. 

485. An agreement by an ordinary workman not to enter the employment of a rival for eight years has been held unreasonable and void. Clark Paper & Manufacturing Co. v. Stenacher, 236 N. Y. 312, 140 N. E. 708, 29 A. L. R. 1325.

The general principles applicable in determining the validity of contracts in restraint of trade are also applicable in determining the validity of agreements which place restrictions on an employee as a condition of his employment. 

McCall v. Wright, supra. In 9 A. L. R. 1450-1496, the authorities are collected and reviewed.

§ 178. Agreements Not To Disclose Trade Secrets. 

Agreements not to disclose secrets communicated in the course of confidential employment, whether they be of trade or of title, or other secrets of the party important to his business, will be enforced. The most frequent application of this rule is found in contracts involving some secret process or formula of manufacture. Tode v. Gross, 127 

N. Y. 480, 28 N. E. 469, 13 L. R. A. 652; Kaumagraph Co. v. Stampagraph Co., 235 N. Y. 1, 138 N. E. 485. But the rule of law sustaining agreements to keep inviolate trade secrets is not limited to manufacturing processes. 

It includes such matters as lists of customers and information of any kind pertaining to the employer's business. Witkop & 

Holmes Co. v. Boyce, 61 Misc. 126, 112 N. Y. S. 874, aff'd 

131 App. Div. 922, 115 N. Y. S. 1150. See, also, Editorial, New York Law Journal, September 14, 1927. 

§ 179. Monopoly Contracts. 

Monopolies have always been held to be in violation of common law, whether granted by the sovereign power or created by contract between private citizens. The word "monopoly" embraces any combination, the tendency of which is to prevent competition in its broad sense or to control prices to the detriment of the public. Love v. Kozy 

Theatre Co., 193 Ky. 336, 236 S. W. 243, 26 A. L. R. 364.

Therefore, many agreements or combinations in restraint of trade, commerce, or competition are void, and courts will not aid parties seeking to enforce such agreements. Arnot v. Pittston & Elmira Coal Co., 68 N. Y. 558, 23 Am. Rep. 

190; Judd v. Harrington, 139 N. Y. 105, 34 N. E. 790; 

Strauss v. American Publishers' Assn., 231 U. S. 222, 58 

Law. Ed. 192, 34 Sup. Ct. Rep. 84. See the Penal Law, section 580, subdivision 6, wherein it is made a crime for two or more persons to conspire to do any act injurious to trade or commerce. However, contracts giving a person the exclusive right to handle goods in a certain locality are not condemned. Walter A. Wood Mowing & Reaping

Machine Co. v. Greenwood Hardware Co., 75 S. C. 378, 55 S. E. 973, 9 L. R. A. (N.S.) 501. As between members of an unlawful combination or association, there is no remedy for the enforcement of their agreements; but contracts between members of the illegal combination, or the illegal combination itself, and persons who are in no way connected with the combination, are enforceable, provided the contracts in such instances are wholly collateral to the unlawful purpose of the combination. Connolly v. 

Union Sewer Pipe Co., 184 U. S. 540, 46 Law. Ed. 679, 22 Sup. Ct. Rep. 431. See, also, Small Co. v. Lamborn & 

Co., 267 U. S. 248, 69 Law. Ed. 597, 45 Sup. Ct. Rep. 300.

The leading case denying the right to enforce contracts between members of an unlawful monopoly is Continental 

Wall Paper Co. v. Louis Voight & Sons Co., 212 U. S. 227, 53 Law. Ed. 486, 29 Sup. Ct. Rep. 280. See, also, International Harvester Co. v. Judge Smith, 163 Mich. 55, 127 

N. W. 695, 30 L. R. A. (N.S.) 580 and note.

The success of business methods alone does not show illegality, although such success may render insuperable the difficulties which rivals must face. Federal Trade Commission v. Curtis Publishing Co., 260 U. S. 568, 67 Law. 

Ed. 408, 43 Sup. Ct. Rep. 210. 

§ 180. Restraint of Trade by System of Contracts. 

Repeated attempts have been made within recent years to control prices of articles of commerce by a system of contracts fixing the prices between manufacturers and wholesale and retail merchants, thus eliminating all competition and fixing the amount which the consumer shall pay. These contracts are uniformly condemned and held invalid as in restraint of trade both at common law and under the anti-trust laws. Dr. Miles Medical Co. v. John 

D. Park & Sons Co., 220 U. S. 373, 55 Law. Ed. 502, 31

Sup. Ct. Rep. 376. But in England at the present day, such agreements are valid and enforceable. See Elliman 

Sons & Co. v. Carrington & Son, 2 Ch. (Eng.) 275 (1901);

Dunlop Pneumatic Tyre Co., Ltd. v. Selfridge & Co., Ltd.,

App. Cas. (Eng.) 847 (1915). 

The cases on this subject fall into four main classes: 

1. Cases wherein the original vendor of an article attempts to control the price at which the article shall be sold by a third person who later purchases the same from the vendee of the original vendor. For example, the manufacturer of a motion picture machine attempts to restrict the use of the same by all subsequent purchasers from the original vendee or other purchasers in succession. The contract whereby the original vendee agrees to such stipu lations is void. Motion Picture Patents Co. v. Universal 

Film Manufacturing Co., 243 U. S. 502, 61 Law. Ed. 871, 37 Sup. Ct. Rep. 416. 

2. Cases wherein the vendee makes a contract with the vendor whereby the vendee agrees that he, the vendee, will resell the article in question only at a fixed price. Such a contract is void, even though the article is produced under a patent held exclusively by the vendor, from whom alone the vendee could have purchased the article. Boston Store v. American Graphophone Co., 246 U. S. 8, 62 Law. Ed. 

551, 38 Sup. Ct. Rep. 257. 

3. Cases wherein the vendee, who has violated the void contract to maintain a fixed price, seeks relief by reason of the fact that the vendor has refused to make any further sales to such vendee merely because the vendee has violated such prior agreement. In such cases, the vendee is without relief. The vendor has the legal right to refuse to make any additional sales, and his motive is immaterial. 

United States v. Colgate & Co., 250 U. S. 300, 63 Law. Ed. 

992, 39 Sup. Ct. Rep. 465. The Court in Federal Trade 

Commission v. Raymond Co., 263 U. S. 565, 572, 68 Law. 

Ed. 448, 44 Sup. Ct. Rep. 162, said: "If real competition is to continue, the right of the individual to exercise reasonable discretion in respect of his own business methods, must be preserved."

161.

However, notwithstanding the right of the manufacturer to refuse to sell to one who lowers prices, the manufacturer acts unlawfully if, by means of a scheme of espionage and methods of intimidation, he unduly hinders and obstructs the business of the dealer who lowers prices. Federal 

Trade Commission v. Beech-Nut Co., 257 U. S. 441, 66 Law. 

Ed. 307, 42 Sup. Ct. Rep. 150.

Not only may the vendor refuse to sell goods in the future to one who lowers prices but he may also give a rebate to a dealer who maintains a certain price. Walsh v. Dwight, 40 App. Div. 513, 58 N. Y. S. 91. 

4. Cases wherein the vendor does not sell the goods to the dealer to whom he transfers possession but merely makes such dealer the vendor's agent to sell and requires such agent to agree not to sell such goods for less than a fixed price. Such contracts are valid. Whitney v. Biggs, 92 Misc. 424, 156 N. Y. S. 1107. Such agent, like other agents, must obey orders given to him by his principal. 

For example, a manufacturer of automobiles may refuse to permit his agent to attach to his cars a device made by a third person. McMaster v. Ford Motor Co., 122 S. C. 

244, 115 S. E. 244, 29 A. L. R. 230.

For a consideration of the relation of the National Recovery Act to state and federal anti-trust laws, see Mayers, A Handbook of NRA, 2d Ed., sections 12, 13, and 133.

CHAPTER X  § 181. In General. 

Illegality may affect only a part or the whole of a contract. The illegal parts may or may not be capable of separation. If a contract is bad only in part, and that which is bad can be separated from that which is good, the courts will enforce the part which is good. It should be observed that in the following discussion of the rights of the parties to an illegal contract, the plaintiff is the promisee and furnishes the consideration; the defendant, the promisor. 

Every illegal contract will assume one of four forms: 

1. One promise for one consideration; 

2. One promise for several considerations; 3. 

Several promises for one consideration; 

4. Several promises for several considerations. § 182. One Promise for One Consideration.

Where one promise is given for one consideration, the illegality of the consideration vitiates the promise, and the promisee cannot maintain an action based on the contract. 

Arnot v. Pittston & Elmira Coal Co., 68 N. Y. 558, 23 Am. 

Rep. 190; Brent v. Gay, 149 Ky. 615, 149 S. W. 915, 41 L. 

R. A. (N.S.) 1034.

The same is equally true where the promise is illegal. 

In such cases, the agreement is void, even though the con sideration is legal. Sayres v. Decker Automobile Co., 239 

N. Y. 73, 145 N. E. 744. $183. 

One Promise for Several Considerations.

If there are several considerations for one legal promise, some of which considerations are legal and others illegal, the promise is wholly void, as it is impossible to say 162

whether it was the legal or the illegal consideration that induced the promise. Thus, if the consideration for a promissory note is the sale of goods, some of which it is legal and some of which it is illegal to sell, the note cannot be enforced. Sirkin v. Fourteenth Street Store, 124 

App. Div. 384, 108 N. Y. S. 830; In re Snyder, 190 N. Y. 

66, 82 N. E. 742; Santa Clara Valley Mill & Lumber Co. v. 

Hayes, 76 Cal. 387, 18 Pac. 391. § 184. 

Several Promises for One Consideration.

Where an agreement consists of several promises based upon one consideration, the illegality of the consideration vitiates all the promises. But if the consideration is legal and the promises are severable, the illegality of one of them does not invalidate the rest. Such contracts are said to be divisible. Erie R. R. Co. v. Union Locomotive & 

Express Co., 35 N. J. L. 240; Central New York Telephone & Telegraph Co. v. Averill, 199 N. Y. 128, 92 N. E. 206, 32 L. R. A. (N.S.) 494.

The result of the rules that partial illegality of consideration vitiates a promise founded thereon and that the illegality of one of several separable promises based upon the same consideration does not affect the validity of the others is to make the validity of an agreement consisting of mutual promises depend upon the party who seeks to enforce it. Thus, if A promises B $100 in consideration of B's promise to do two acts, one of which is lawful and the other unlawful, A may enforce B's lawful promise. B, however, cannot enforce A's promise, because it is founded on a consideration which is in part illegal. Bishop v. Palmer, 146 Mass. 469, 16 N. E. 299. $ 185. 

Several Promises for Several Considerations.

Where an agreement consists of several promises based upon several considerations, the illegality of one or more of the considerations will not avoid all the promises if those which were made upon lawful considerations are sev-

erable from the others. Leavitt v. Palmer, 3 N. Y. 19, 51 Am. Dec. 333; Osgood v. Bauder, 75 Iowa 550, 39 N. W. 

887, 1 L. R. A. 655. Thus, a contract whereby A agrees, for compensation, to make an illegal bid on certain realty for B and to manage such realty at a certain rate of wages per month is enforceable as to the latter but not as to the former promise. McVicker v. McKenzie, 136 Cal. 656, 69 

Pac. 495. § 186.

Object Unlawful but Intention Innocent. 

If the thing to be done under the agreement is unlawful, even though the intention is innocent, the agreement is void. Want of guilty knowledge will not save the contract. Ignorance or mistake of law is no excuse. Saratoga County Bank v. King, 44 N. Y. 87.

An interesting case in this connection is Medoff v. Fisher, 257 Pa. 126, 101 Atl. 471, wherein an architect sued upon a contract with the defendants for services in the preparation of plans and specifications for a building. The building was to contain a motion picture theatre and also dwellings, bathhouse in the cellar, and stores. A statute provided that no portion of any building thereafter erected, and used for the exhibition of motion pictures "shall be occupied or used as a dwelling or tenement house, apartment house, hotel or department store." It was held that the architect was a party to an agreement to do an unlawful act and hence could not recover.

The Court said: "The principle that, since one may change his mind before the actual perpetration of a forbidden act, the mere intention to commit a wrong is no offense has no proper application under the circumstances at bar; for even though, after an erection of this building, the defendants might not have put it to any forbidden use, yet that fact does not change the status of the case so far as the plaintiff is concerned. The latter's position, therefore, is simply this: All men are supposed to know the law, and, further, one holding himself out as an architect

is particularly charged with knowledge of the statutory regulations and restrictions governing the erection and use of buildings; therefore, we must assume both the plaintiff and defendants knew that the uses to which the latter contemplated putting the proposed structure were forbidden under a criminal penalty by the statutes of Pennsylvania.' 

If the drawing of the plans is to be regarded as a participation in the intent or as aiding or abetting the ultimate illegal act, the decision is sound; otherwise it is not. 

§ 187.

Object Innocent but Intention Unlawful. 

In many jurisdictions it is held that where the object of the agreement is innocent in itself, but the intentions of the parties are unlawful, the agreement is void. But, by the weight of authority, knowledge by one of the parties of the unlawful intentions of the other does not affect the contract, unless the party does some act to help carry out the unlawful intention and act of the other. Thus, it has been held that, if liquor is sold for the express purpose of enabling the buyer to retail it in violation of law, the sale is void. Kohn v. Milcher, 43 Fed. 641, 10 L. R. A. 439.

And it would seem that where goods are sold for the purpose of enabling the buyer to accomplish an unlawful act, the sale should be held illegal on the same theory. In such a case, the illegal intent is not only known to the seller but is encouraged by the sale. Graves v. Johnson, 156 Mass. 

211, 30 N. E. 818, 15 L. R. A. 834. These cases come very close to the English rule, which is that, if the direct object of the contract is innocent in itself but one of the parties has an unlawful intention or purpose which is known to the other, the agreement is void. Thus, in Pearce v. Brooks, 1 Exch. (Eng.) 213, a coach-builder sued a prostitute for money due for the hire of a brougham, let to her with the knowledge that it was to be used by her in the furtherance of her immoral trade. It was held the coach-builder could not recover.

In New York, however, and in the United States gen-

erally, mere knowledge of the unlawful intention and purpose is held insufficient to taint the contract with illegality; there must be an intent to aid, or an act in aid of the accomplishment of the illegal purpose. Anheuser-Busch 

Brewing Assn. v. Mason, 44 Minn. 318, 46 N. W. 558, 9 L. 

R. A. 506; Fineman v. Faulkner, 174 N. C. 13, 93 S. E. 

384, L. R. A. 1918A 337.

For example, in furtherance of an attempt to form a monopoly, B leased a distillery from A. A knew B's motive and intention but did not become a party to the conspiracy. The Court held that A could recover his rent. 

Brooklyn Distilling Co. v. Standard Distilling Co., 193 N. 

Y. 551, 86 N. E. 564; Tracy v. Talmage, 14 N. Y. 162, 67 

Am. Dec. 132. In Hull v. Ruggles, 56 N. Y. 424, the action was for goods sold and delivered. The defense was that the goods, which consisted of what is known as prize candy packages with some articles of silverware, were intended to be used as a lottery, of which plaintiff had notice, and that he prepared the goods for that purpose. The Court said: "The plaintiff cites, as an authority in his favor, Tracy v. Talmage, 14 N. Y. 162. That case does hold, that mere knowledge by the vendor, that the purchaser intends to make an unlawful use of the property is not a defense to an action for its price. That is perhaps all that was necessary to decide in that case, for the determination of the questions there involved. But it is also said there, that if the vendor, with knowledge of the intent of the purchaser, do anything beyond making the sale, to aid or further the unlawful design, he cannot recover for the property." The Court held that the plaintiff could not recover because he aided in carrying out the unlawful design. 

Clearly, if a party aids in the accomplishment of the illegal purpose, the contract is illegal. Materne v. Horwitz, 101 

N. Y. 469, 5 N. E. 331.

If, however, the seller knows or has reason to believe that the goods are to be used for a felonious or treasonable purpose, he cannot recover, the English rule, stated above,

being applied in such grave cases. Hanauer v. Doane, 12 

Wall. (U.S.) 342, 20 Law. Ed. 439; Fuller v. Hunt, 182 

Mass. 299, 65 N. E. 390.

Merely lending money with the knowledge that it will be used for gambling purposes does not make the contract void. But the rule is otherwise if there was an express agreement that it should be used for such purposes. Tyler v. Carlisle, 79 Me. 210, 9 Atl. 356. Thus, in Pennsylvania 

R. R. Co. v. Rosenfeld, 249 Fed. 964, it was held that one who lends money to the loser in an illegal transaction, such as gaming or betting, can recover the loan, notwithstanding he knows the loser is going to pay his indebtedness with it. It is obvious that the loan is disconnected from the illegal act and that the cause of action is founded on a distinct and collateral consideration. § 188.

Relief of Party in Pari Delicto. 

As between parties in pari delicto, the courts as a rule will not enforce an illegal contract or any supposed rights growing out of it. The rule is often expressed that in such cases the law will leave the parties where it finds them. Haynes v. Rudd, 102 N. Y. 372, 7 N. E. 287; Hunter v. Wheate, 289 Fed. 604, 31 A. L. R. 980.
