Neither will the courts give relief to the plaintiff where his action arises out of his own moral turpitude or out of his violation of the general law enacted to effectuate the public policy of a state or a nation. Levy v. Kansas City, 168 Fed. 524, 22 L. R. A. (N.S.) 862; Platt v. Elias, 186 N. Y. 374, 79 

N. E. 1, 11 L. R. A. (N.S.) 554. § 189.

Relief of Party Not in Pari Delicto. 

Where the parties are not in pari delicto, the courts will afford relief to the innocent party, notwithstanding the illegality of the contract and the fact that it is executed. The rule governing cases in which the parties are not in pari delicto is most frequently applied where the transaction is in violation of a law made for the protection of one party

against the acts of the other. As they are not both at fault, the party protected may recover. Duval v. Wellman, 124 

N. Y. 156, 26 N. E. 343; Mason v. McLeod, 57 Kan. 105, 45 

Pac. 76, 41 L. R. A. 548. For example, a statute may declare a contract to be void, and yet only one of the parties may be guilty of its violation. Enactments of this character are often for the purpose of protecting one class of persons from the oppression and imposition of another class. Such is the case with all laws which declare usurious contracts void. The lender is not allowed to take advantage of the statute, because he is the guilty party, but the borrower may do so, because he is not particeps criminis. 

He is regarded as the victim of the usurer. The principle applies to every contract declared to be void by statute, in the making of which one of the parties has acted illegally. Tracy v. Talmage, 14 N. Y. 162, 67 Am. Dec. 132.

For example, a statute forbidding an attorney-at-law to promise or give to any person a valuable consideration as an inducement to place in his hands a demand for the purpose of having an action brought thereon will not prevent the person who places the demand in his hands from recovering the agreed compensation, because the parties are not in pari delicto. Irwin v. Curie, 171 N. Y. 409, 64 N. E. 

161, 58 L. R. A. 830.

So, also, where a woman in a nervous and broken condition agreed to pay an attorney an exorbitant contingent fee for the procurement of a divorce, it was held that the parties were not in pari delicto so as to prevent a recovery by the woman from the attorney. In re Sylvester, 195 Iowa 

1329, 192 N. W. 442, 30 A. L. R. 180. 

§ 190. Locus Poenitentiae. 

The phrase locus poenitentiae (place for repentance) refers to the opportunity to repent and withdraw from the projected contract before it has become fully executed. 

Thus, if money is paid or goods are delivered for an illegal purpose, they may be recovered before the purpose is

carried out. Congress & Empire Spring Co. v. Knowlton, 103 U. S. 49, 26 Law. Ed. 347; Bernard v. Taylor, 23 Ore. 

416, 31 Pac. 968, 18 L. R. A. 859. In these cases, the recovery is based not on the illegal contract but on the repudiation thereof. A recovery is allowed because it has the effect of discouraging the making of illegal contracts. Falkenberg v. Allen, 18 Okla. 210, 90 Pac. 415, 10 L. R. A. (N.S.) 494. See, also, Greenberg v. Evening Post Assn., 91 Conn. 371, 99 Atl. 1037, as to the right of recovery of money paid on an illegal executory contract respecting a newspaper prize contest. 

§ 191. What Law Governs. 

If a contract is made in a foreign state and is there deemed illegal and therefore unenforceable, it cannot, as a rule, be enforced in any other state. Swing v. Dayton, 124 App. Div. 58, 108 N. Y. S. 155, aff'd 196 N. Y. 503, 89 N. E. 1113; Butterick Publishing Co. v. Mistrot-Munn 

Co., 167 App. Div. 632, 153 N. Y. S. 61, aff'd 217 N. Y. 

678, 112 N. E. 1055. It is equally true that, even though valid where made, the contract will not be enforced in another state if it is deemed contrary to the public policy of the state in which the action for its enforcement is brought. 

Meacham v. Jamestown, Franklin & Clearfield R. R. Co., 211 N. Y. 346, 105 N. E. 653; City Bank v. Perkins, 29 

N. Y. 554, 86 Am. Dec. 352. However, if a contract is made and is to be performed in a state wherein it is valid, an action for its breach may be brought in another state wherein the contract if made in such state would be illegal, provided, however, that the enforcement of the contract is not regarded as contrary to the public policy of the state wherein the action is instituted. Bank v. Doherty, 42 Wash. 317, 84 Pac. 872, 4 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1191; Manhattan Life Insurance Co. v. Johnson, 188 N. Y. 108, 80 

N. E. 658, 9 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1142.

CHAPTER XI CONSIDERATION 

§ 192. 

Consideration Defined.

Every contract, as a rule, must have a consideration. 

Without it there can be no enforceable promise. It lies at the base of all contractual obligations. The promisee-the plaintiff must furnish a consideration for the promise of the defendant, who, in an action based on the contract, is the promisor. A mere gratuitous promise is not binding. 

There must be a quid pro quo-something for something. 

Therefore, consideration is something done or something promised to be done by the promisee in return for a promise. In determining whether or not a consideration exists, the test is: Did the promisee (plaintiff) do something or promise to do something, concerning which he was not already obligated, in return for the promisor's (defendant's) promise? If so, there is a consideration; otherwise, there is not. Street v. Galt, 136 App. Div. 724, 121 N. Y. S. 514, aff'd 202 N. Y. 575, 96 N. E. 1133. Consideration has also been defined as some right, interest, profit, or benefit accruing to the promisor, or some forbearance, detriment, loss, responsibility, act, labor, or service given, suffered, or undertaken by the promisee. Ward v. Erie R. R. Co., 87 

Misc. 365, 149 N. Y. S. 717, aff'd 215 N. Y. 629, 103 N. E. 

1095. But this definition is too broad and does not conform to the test given above. Various definitions of consideration will be found in Hamer v. Sidway, 124 N. Y. 538, 27 N. E. 256, 12 L. R. A. 463. The act of the promisee may be one of forbearance, or it may consist of the surrender of some legal right, and even though this does not involve financial loss to the promisee or gain to the promisor, the forbearance to do something, or the doing of something he was not legally bound to do, is a sufficient consideration. 

Even if the forbearance of the right be beneficial to the 170

CONSIDERATION 

171 party forbearing, it is nevertheless a sufficient consideration. Thus, the giving up of the right to use tobacco, to swear, to play cards, or to abstain from the use of intoxicating liquor, is sufficient consideration, even though it is quite impossible to discover any trace of pecuniary loss to the promisee and despite the fact that the forbearance may have been highly beneficial to him. Hamer v. Sidway, supra. Also, where A gave his note to B for $10,000, in consideration that B name his child after A, the Court, holding the naming of the child to be a sufficient consideration, said: "Where a party contracts for the performance of an act which will afford him pleasure, gratify his ambition, please his fancy, or express his appreciation of a service another has done him, his estimate of value should be left undisturbed, unless, indeed, there is evidence of fraud." Wolford v. Powers, 85 Ind. 294, 44 Am. Rep. 16.

So, too, the privilege of naming a child was held to be a valid consideration for a promise to pay money. In Gardner v. Denison, 217 Mass. 492, 105 N. E. 359, 51 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1108, the Court said: "He [the child] loses the opportunity of receiving a more advantageous name, and is compelled to bear whatever detriment may flow from the name imposed upon him." Likewise, a contract to pay the plaintiff a certain amount each week as long as he abstained from the sale of a specified newspaper was held to be not without consideration. Rague v. New York Evening Journal Publishing Co., 164 App. Div. 126, 149 N. Y. S. 668.

In an English case, dated 1661, a woman, after the death of her husband, said to one of his creditors: "If you prove that he owed you 20 pounds, I will pay it." Proof of such claim was declared to be sufficient consideration to support an action against the woman on her promise. Traverse v. 

Meres, T. Raymond 32, 83 Eng. Rep. 18. § 193. 

Necessity of Consideration.

Aside from a few well-defined exceptions, consideration is necessary to the validity of every contract, and this is true

irrespective of its form. At common law, a sealed instrument was conclusively presumed to have a consideration, and it was upon this presumption that a gratuitous promise under the "hand and seal" of the promisor was enforceable. The common law rule has been changed in many states by the complete abolition of private seals. In other states, the statutes modify, but do not entirely abrogate, the common law rule. As to the peculiar rule by statute in New Jersey, see Aller v. Aller, 40 N. J. L. 446. In New 

York, the Civil Practice Act, section 342, modifies the common law presumption of consideration by providing that a seal upon an executory instrument is only presumptive evidence of consideration. This presumption, however, is rebuttable; while at common law it was irrebuttable. The common law rule still obtains as to executed instruments, such as receipts, releases, etc. The mere attachment of a seal after the maker's signature does not constitute a sealed instrument. To be such, there must be a recognition of the seal in the body of the instrument by some such phrase as "witness my hand and seal" or "signed and sealed." Matter of Pirie, 198 N. Y. 209, 91 N. E. 587; Stiebel v. Grosberg, 202 N. Y. 266, 95 N. E. 692, 36 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1147; 

Cook v. Bradley, 7 Conn. 57, 18 Am. Dec. 79; Orthey v. 

Bogan, 236 N. Y. 351, 140 N. E. 722. 

In O'Keefe v. French, 239 App. Div. 498, 268 N. Y. S. 

102 (1933), the Court held that notwithstanding no mention of a seal was made in the body of the note, the word "seal" after the signature was of itself sufficient to constitute the note a sealed instrument. The only distinction between Matter of Pirie, supra, and O'Keefe v. French, supra, in the matter of the seal is that in the former case the seal was annexed after the maker signed the note, while in the latter case the word "seal" was on the note before it was signed. The Court of Appeals denied a motion for leave to appeal solely on the ground that the order was not final. O'Keefe v. French, 264 N. Y. 65, memo. The

General Construction Law, section 44, was operative prior

CONSIDERATION 

173 to the decision in Matter of Pirie, supra. Is the reasoning for distinguishing the cases sound? See note, Brooklyn 

Law Review, April, 1934, p. 348.. § 194. 

Consideration and Motive.

While the consideration stated in a contract may be the legal motive for a promise, a motive for a promise may exist and yet may not amount to a consideration. If a motive alone were equivalent to a consideration, every promise made free from fraud and duress would be enforceable without a consideration. The promisor has a motive in keeping every promise which he makes; namely, the maintaining of his reputation for keeping his promises. A promise to distribute articles for advertising purposes may be impelled by purely selfish and mercenary motives, but such motives are not a substitute for consideration. Fink v. Cox, 18 Johns. (N.Y.) 145, 9 Am. Dec. 191. § 195. 

Exception to the Rule Requiring Consideration.

To the rule that a consideration is essential to the validity of every contract there are the following exceptions: 1. 

Holder in due course of negotiable instrument. Want of consideration is no defense in an action by a holder in due course of a negotiable instrument. As to such a holder there is a conclusive presumption of a consideration. Negotiable Instruments Law, secs. 54 and 96; Dougherty v. 

Salt, 227 N. Y. 200, 125 N. E. 94. 

2. Validity of certain agreements made without consideration. From time immemorial, an agreement between a debtor and a creditor whereby the creditor extends to a fixed future the time of payment of a matured debt has been held by the New York courts to be invalid because of want of consideration. But this rule, so long sustained by 

Kellogg v. Olmsted, 25 N. Y. 109, and Olmsted v. Latimer, 158 N. Y. 313, 53 N. E. 5, 43 L. R. A. 685, has been completely nullified by statute. In April, 1934, the New York 

Legislature, by statutory enactment, validated such an

agreement, although it is without consideration, provided the extension agreement is in writing and signed by both parties thereto. The Personal Property Law, section 33, subdivision 2, provides: "A mutual agreement between a creditor or obligee and a debtor, obligor, surety or guarantor to extend or postpone to a fixed or determinable future time the payment of any debt or other obligation shall be valid without consideration other than such mutual agreement, provided that a note or memorandum of such agreement be in writing and signed by the parties thereto." So, too, the time of payment of any debt or obligation secured in whole or in part by real property may be postponed to a fixed date by mutual agreement in writing signed by the parties thereto. Real Property Law, sec. 279. Prior to legislative enactment, all such agreements were, for lack of consideration, void. The statutory enactments are in conformity with the weight of authority in other jurisdictions. See The Justinian (Brooklyn Law School), June 

7, 1934, p. 2. 

3. Performance of gratuitous service. A promise to perform a gratuitous service, although not enforceable as a promise, imposes a duty to use due care and skill in its performance if one enters upon the undertaking. Thorne v. Deas, 4 Johns. (N.Y.) 84. Thus, if one gratuitously agrees to take charge of another's horse as a favor to the other, he is under an obligation to water and feed the horse, although there would be no liability for his failure to enter upon a performance of his gratuitous promise. 

Glanzer v. Shepard, 233 N. Y. 236, 135 N. E. 275, 23 A. 

L. R. 1425; Carr v. Maine Central R. R., 78 N. H. 502, 102 Atl. 532, L. R. A. 1918E 389. 

4. Obligations created by statute. A statutory obligation on a bond or other obligation made valid and authorized by positive law needs no consideration to support it. 

The legislature may dispense entirely with the necessity of consideration, or it may limit its operation. Mittnacht v.

CONSIDERATION 

175 

Kellermann, 105 N. Y. 461, 12 N. E. 28; Carpenter v. Taylor, 164 N. Y. 171, 58 N. E. 53. 

5. Promises enforceable by doctrine of estoppel. Promises may be enforced on the ground of estoppel rather than on the basis of a consideration where the promisee, relying on the promise, has assumed obligations or changed his position so that he must suffer loss if the promise is not fulfilled. Ricketts v. Scothorn, 57 Neb. 51, 77 N. W. 365, 42 L. R. A. 794. This doctrine is comparatively modern and it is interesting to note that the leading authority on the question, the Ricketts case, supra, has never been cited with approval. The doctrine finds some apparent support in De Cicco v. Schweizer, 221 N. Y. 431, 117 N. E. 807, L. R. A. 1918E 1004, and in Spitzli v. Guth, 112 Misc. 630, 183 N. Y. S. 743. However, the exact limits of the doctrine have never been clearly defined. 

§ 196. Unreal Consideration. 

Various types of unreal considerations may be classified as follows: 

1. Promise based upon past consideration or moral obligation; 

2. Promise based upon performance of legal obligation; 

3. Promise to do the impossible; 

4. Promise that is vague and uncertain; 

5. Promise based upon "good" as distinguished from "valuable" consideration. § 197. 

Past Consideration-Moral Obligation.

A consideration as to time may be executed or executory, but it cannot be past. A past consideration is an act or forbearance whereby a person has been benefited, but at the time he received such benefit was under no legal obligation to pay for it. If he subsequently promises to pay for the favor, we say the promise is based on a past consideration, as it relates to acts in the past; the promisee does nothing,

nor does he promise to do anything in return for the promise made to him. While either an executed or an executory consideration is sufficient to support a promise, a past consideration is not. Under past considerations may be classified many so-called moral obligations. For example, a person may make certain promises because he feels in honor bound to do so on account of past favors of which he has been the recipient; or he may be prompted by motives of conscience, honor, or friendship; but such promises are unenforceable because the consideration is past, even though there may be a moral obligation resting upon the promisor to confer a benefit on the promisee. When one promises to pay for past services or for past hospitality, he gains nothing by making the promise, nor does the promisee do anything because of the promise; therefore, there is no consideration to support the promise. Mills v. 

Wyman, 3 Pick. (Mass.) 207; Dearborn v. Bowman, 3 Met. (Mass.) 155; Myers v. Dean, 11 Misc. 368, 32 N. Y. S. 

237; Pershall v. Elliott, 249 N. Y. 183, 163 N. E. 554. See, also, notes 53 L. R. A. 353, 26 L. R. A. (N.S.) 520. 

§ 198. Past Consideration-Exceptions. 

To the rule that a past consideration is not a sufficient consideration to support a promise, there are certain exceptions. All, or nearly all, of such exceptions are cases wherein there was at one time a legal liability which may now be avoided by the promisor taking advantage of some defense given to him by law. The promisor may waive, or decline to take advantage of, the defense thus given to him by law. A renewal of the promise would constitute a waiver. The promisor then would be in the same position as he was in before his release by law. The new promise is binding even though it is without any new consideration. 

The well-recognized exceptions are: a. New promise after bar of the statute of limitations.

A debt barred by the statute of limitations is revived by a subsequent promise to pay it. In New York and many

CONSIDERATION 

177 other jurisdictions, the promise must be in writing, except where a new promise is implied from a part payment of interest or principal. Civil Practice Act, sec. 59; Harper v. 

Fairly, 53 N. Y. 442. b. New promise after discharge in bankruptcy. It is well settled that a new promise to pay a debt discharged in bankruptcy is enforceable without a new consideration to support it. Fonville v. Wichita State Bank & Trust Co., 161 Ark. 93, 255 S. W. 561, 33 A. L. R. 125. In many states, an oral promise is sufficient for this purpose. 

Vachon v. Ditz, 114 Wash. 11, 194 Pac. 545. However, in many states including New York, such promise is not enforceable unless reduced to writing. Personal Property 

Law, sec. 31. In New York the promise must be expressed, since no implied promise to pay the balance can be inferred from a part payment of a debt discharged in bankruptcy. Lawrence v. Harrington, 122 N. Y. 408, 25 N. E. 

406; Dusenbury v. Hoyt, 53 N. Y. 521, 13 Am. Rep. 543.

A conflict of opinion exists as to whether or not a new promise to pay a debt discharged by bankruptcy proceedings is enforceable without any new consideration to support it, when there has been a composition of the creditors assented to and accepted by the one seeking to enforce such new promise. Many courts have held that the new promise is unenforceable, on the theory that the discharge in this instance is by act of the parties and not by operation of law. Shepard v. Rhodes, 7 R. I. 470, 84 Am. Dec. 

573. But in New York such a promise is enforceable. 

Herrington v. Davitt, 220 N. Y. 162, 115 N. E. 476, 1 A. 

L. R. 1700 and note. c. New promise after majority. A promise by an adult to perform a contract made during his infancy needs no new consideration. An infant who has reached the age of twenty-one is supposed to have acquired the power of deciding for himself whether in equity and in conscience he ought to discharge the debt, even though the law would not compel him to do so. He may waive the privilege of infancy,

and if he chooses to do so, the new promise will operate upon the preceding consideration and will constitute a ratification of the original contract. Henry v. Root, 33 N. Y. 

526. An interesting case in this connection is Parsons v. 

Teller, 188 N. Y. 318, 80 N. E. 930, wherein an agreement made during infancy was without consideration, and therefore a new promise based thereon was held to be unenforceable, though made after the infant had attained majority. d. Waiver by indorser of right to notice of dishonor. An indorser of a note who has been discharged from liability for want of due notice of dishonor may waive his discharge. 

This well-settled rule is now embraced in the Negotiable Instruments Law, section 180, which provides, as follows: "Notice of dishonor may be waived, either before the time of giving notice has arrived, or after the omission to give due notice, and the waiver may be express or implied." No new consideration is necessary to make such waiver effective. Ross v. Hurd, 71 N. Y. 14, 27 Am. Rep. 1.

The principle on which the foregoing exceptions to the rule that a past consideration or moral obligation is no consideration rest, is "that, where the consideration was originally beneficial to the party promising, yet, if he be protected from liability by some provision of the common or statute law, meant for his advantage, he may renounce the benefit of that law, and if he promises to pay the debt, which is only what an honest man ought to do, he is then bound by the law to perform it." Shepard v. Rhodes, supra. e. Act performed at the request of the promisor. An exception to the rule that a past consideration is insufficient to support a promise is said to exist where the act was done at the request of the promisor. But this exception must be confined to cases where the request is made under such circumstances that the request implies a promise to pay. It does not include instances where an act has been done as a mere favor. In such cases, the subsequent promise is unenforceable for want of consideration. Hicks v. Burhans, 10 

Johns. (N.Y.) 253; Moore v. Elmer, 180 Mass. 15, 61 N. E.

CONSIDERATION 

179 

259; Blanshan v. Russell, 32 App. Div. 103, 52 N. Y. S. 

963, aff'd 161 N. Y. 629, 55 N. E. 1093; Edson v. Poppe, 24 S. D. 466, 124 N. W. 441, 26 L. R. A. (N.S.) 534. § 199. "Volunteer" Not Favored by Law.

It is no longer the rule that, where one person voluntarily performs the legal obligation of another person and the latter afterwards promises to pay him therefor, the promise is supported by a sufficient consideration. "Such an individual" (volunteer), said Surrogate Wingate in Matter of Welton, 141 Misc. 674, 253 N. Y. S. 128, "is permitted to suffer the usual legal fate of an intermeddler, and is denied any rights whatsoever against any one, as a result of the transaction. The usual characteristics of human nature naturally tend to make such a situation of rare occurrence.” McNamee v. Zimmett, 207 App. Div. 

60, 202 N. Y. S. 372, aff'd 239 N. Y. 602, 147 N. E. 213.

To the same effect is Massachusetts Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Green, 185 Mass. 306, 70 N. E. 202. See, also, Editorial, New York Law Journal, May 19, 1932. 

§ 200. Unreal Consideration-Promise Based on Performance of Prior Legal Obligation or Contract. 

As a rule, doing or promising to do that which one is already legally bound to do is an insufficient consideration to support a promise, for the promisee gets nothing more than he is already entitled to. The new promise creates no new legal obligation on the part of the promisor. Lingenfelder v. Wainwright Brewing Co., 103 Mo. 578, 15 S. W. 

844; Robinson v. Jewett, 116 N. Y. 40, 22 N. E. 224. The rule is well stated in McDevitt v. Stokes, 174 Ky. 515, 192 

S. W. 681, L. R. A. 1917D 1100, as follows: "A promise to do what the promisor is already bound to do cannot be a consideration, for if a person gets nothing in return for his promise but that to which he is already legally entitled, the consideration is unreal. This legal obligation may arise from (1) the law independent of contract, or it may

arise from (2) a subsisting contract. *** Where a party is under duty created or imposed by law to do what he does, or promises to do, his act or promise is clearly of no value and is not sufficient consideration for a promise given in return."

The preexisting legal obligation which the promisor promises to perform may have been created either (a) by contract, or (b) by law, independent of contract.

Suppose A, in the performance of an excavation contract with B, discovers that the earth which he had supposed to be sand is solid rock, in consequence of which he refuses performance of his contract unless B promises to pay him an additional $25,000, and B so promises. Is B liable? Weston v. State of New York, 262 N. Y. 46, 186 

N. E. 197.

There are, however, a few most exceptional cases holding that unforeseen difficulties and burdens may be of such a character as to support a promise for additional compensation. United Steel Co. v. Casey, 262 Fed. 889; Meech v. City of Buffalo, 29 N. Y. 198; Linz v. Schuck, 106 Md. 

220, 67 Atl. 286, 11 L. R. A. (N.S.) 789. Perhaps the better theory in this connection, and one which has been resorted to by some of the authorities in order to render the promise to pay the additional sum enforceable, is that the new agreement is a substitution for the old agreement, whether the latter is abandoned while wholly executory or after partial performance. Morecraft v. Allen, 78 N. J. L. 

729, 75 Atl. 920, L. R. A. 1915B 1 and note. 

§ 201. Promise Made by a Third Person To Induce Performance of Existing Contract. 

While the rule is quite generally recognized that a promise made to induce the other party to perform his contract with the promisor is without consideration, a serious conflict exists in the cases as to whether the performance of an existing contract between two parties, or a promise to perform it, is a sufficient consideration to support a prom-

CONSIDERATION 

181 ise made by a third person to induce said performance. 

Thus, if A is under contract with B, is a promise by Y to A to induce him to perform his contract with B enforceable? In England and Massachusetts this question is answered affirmatively. Shadwell v. Shadwell, 9 C. B. (N.S.) 159, 142 Eng. Rep. 62; Abbott v. Doane, 163 Mass. 

433, 40 N. E. 197, 34 L. R. A. 33 and note. In New York and other jurisdictions, the question is answered negatively. In De Cicco v. Schweizer, 221 N. Y. 431, 117 N. E. 

807, L. R. A. 1918E 1004, Judge Cardozo, in delivering the opinion of the Court, said: "The courts of this state are committed to the view that a promise by A to B to induce him not to break his contract with C is void." To the same effect are Arend v. Smith, 151 N. Y. 502, 45 N. E. 

872, and Vanderbilt v. Schreyer, 91 N. Y. 392, 12 Abb. N.C. 

390. However, in New York, prior to the decision in De

Cicco v. Schweizer, supra,. it had been held by the Appellate 

Division of the Supreme Court, Second Department, that, if the promisor has an interest in the performance of the contract which the promisee has made with a third party, such interest is sufficient consideration to make the promise enforceable. Manetti v. Doege, 48 App. Div. 567, 62

N. Y. S. 918. This position has been upheld in other jurisdictions. Abbott v. Doane, supra; Bradley v. Glenmary 

Co., 64 N. J. Eq. 77, 53 Atl. 49. 

So, too, in De Cicco v. Schweizer, supra, the fulfilment of a marriage contract was held to be a sufficient consideration for an agreement by the bride's father with the intended groom to pay an annuity to the bride. In the instant case, the daughter of the defendant was engaged to be married. The defendant thereafter promised the daughter's intended husband that, if the marriage took place, he would pay his daughter an annuity. They were married, and the assignee of the husband and wife sued on the promise. The Court held that he could recover. The basis of the holding is that, although each was bound to the other, together they were not bound to a third person;

182

they performed an act which no third person had a legal right to call upon them to do, and which, therefore, was a valid consideration. Although the case arrives at a conclusion contrary to the weight of authority, it cannot be regarded as in direct conflict therewith, because of the rather novel circumstances of the case. Judge Crane, in concurring with Judge Cardozo, held that the contract could be supported as a marriage settlement. § 202. 

Part Payment in Satisfaction of Debt.

The payment of a smaller sum in satisfaction of a larger one is not a sufficient consideration to support a promise to release the debtor from liability as to the entire debt. To have this effect, there must be something else given or some additional benefit or legal possibility of benefit to the creditor. For example, Foakes v. Beer, 9 App. Cas. (Eng.) 

605 (1884): A owes B $500, which is past due; the latter promises to discharge the debt if the former will pay $300. 

The payment of $300 by A does not discharge him. B may subsequently sue A for the balance, $200. But "the gift of a horse, a hawk or a robe, in satisfaction is good. For it shall be intended that a horse, a hawk or a robe might be more beneficial to the plaintiff than money, in respect of some circumstance, or otherwise the plaintiff would not have accepted it in satisfaction." Pinnel's Case, 5 Coke 

117a, 77 Eng. Rep. 237. "A negotiable security may operate, if so given and taken, in satisfaction of a debt of a greater amount; the circumstance of negotiability making it in fact a different thing and more advantageous than the original debt which was not negotiable." Jaffray v. Davis, 124 N. Y. 164, 26 N. E. 351, 11 L. R. A. 710.

In Jaffray v. Davis, supra, it was held, that where one, indebted on an open book account, gave to his creditor his promissory notes for one-half of the debt, secured by a chattel mortgage, under an agreement with the creditor that he would accept the same in full satisfaction and discharge of the debt, and the debtor paid the notes as they became due,

CONSIDERATION 

183 and the creditor satisfied the mortgage, the new agreement was valid and supported by a sufficient consideration. 

A case apparently in conflict with this rule is Shanley v. 

Koehler, 80 App. Div. 566, 80 N. Y. S. 679, aff'd 178 

N. Y. 556, 70 N. E. 1109. This was an action for the specific performance of a contract. The defendant, plaintiff's judgment creditor for $226.29, agreed with the plaintiff that, upon receiving $50 in cash and plaintiff's negotiable note for $50, if the note was paid at maturity, he would accept the same in full settlement of the judgment of record. The note was paid when due, but the defendant refused to keep his part of the agreement. The Court held that the promise was without consideration. The decision is based on the ground that the receipt of the note did not put the creditor in a better position than he was before such note was given.

To the rule that the payment of a smaller sum in satisfaction of a larger one is not a sufficient consideration for discharging the entire debt, certain exceptions are recognized, as follows: 

1. Where a receipt under seal acknowledges the receipt of an amount in excess of what was actually paid, the instrument is conclusive of the fact stated therein. A receipt under seal is, in effect, a release. While an ordinary receipt is prima facie evidence of the facts stated, yet evidence is admissible to show that the facts are other than as stated. But where the receipt is under seal there is an irrebuttable presumption of sufficiency of consideration. 

The common law rule that a seal raises a conclusive presumption of consideration still prevails in New York as to all instruments other than "executory instruments." A receipt or release is not an "executory instrument," as defined by the Civil Practice Act, section 342. Stiebel v. 

Grosberg, 202 N. Y. 266, 95 N. E. 692, 36 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

1147. But see, Orthey v. Bogan, 236 N. Y. 351, 140 N. E. 722. 

2. The creditor may make a gift of the debt to the debtor.

No formal words or expressions are required to constitute a gift. It is a question of intent, and the inquiry is as to what was intended by that which was said and done. The gift may be accomplished by a delivery of any evidence of indebtedness. The delivery of a note is evidence tending to show an intent to give. Furthermore, the intention to make a gift of it may be evidenced by a receipt in full. Gray v. 

Barton, 55 N. Y. 68, 14 Am. Rep. 181; McKenzie v. Harrison, 120 N. Y. 260, 24 N. E. 458, 8 L. R. A. 257. Thus, a father indorsed, on a mortgage given to him by his son, a receipt of a sum of money which he had not received, to be applied on the bond and mortgage, intending it to be a gift of the amount. It was held to be a valid gift and not merely a promise to give without consideration. Carpenter v. Soule, 88 N. Y. 251, 42 Am. Rep. 248. 

3. The rule that the payment of a lesser sum will not discharge a greater applies only when the amount is past due and is definite and certain. Where the amount due is in dispute or unliquidated, the payment of a sum less than is claimed is sufficient consideration to support a promise to release the debtor from all further liability. The doctrine of accord and satisfaction applies in such cases. This subject is treated in Chapter XXII, infra. Fuller v. Kemp, 138 N. Y. 231, 33 N. E. 1034, 20 L. R. A. 785 and note; 

Nassoiy v. Tomlinson, 148 N. Y. 326, 42 N. E. 715. 

4. Payment by a third person of a lesser sum than the amount due is sufficient consideration to support a discharge of the entire debt. This is sound in theory for the reason that the debtor alone is legally bound. Clearly, any payment by the third person is the doing of something which he is not legally bound to do. Sigler v. Sigler, 98 

Kan. 524, 158 Pac. 864, L. R. A. 1917A 725; Marshall v. 

Bullard, 114 Iowa 462, 87 N. W. 427, 54 L. R. A. 862. 

§ 203. Performance of Public Duty Imposed by Law. 

Public policy and sound morals, alike, forbid that a public officer should demand or receive, for services performed

CONSIDERATION 

185 by him in the discharge of official duty, any other or further remuneration or reward than that prescribed and allowed by law. Therefore, a promise to pay a peace officer, whose duty it is to make an arrest, or a promise to pay a witness, whose fees are fixed by law, extra compensation is without consideration. Duty imposed by law stands upon the same footing as duty imposed by contract. Smith v. Whildin, 10 

Pa. St. 39; Hatch v. Mann, 15 Wend. (N.Y.) 44. A public officer has no right to claim a reward for making an arrest which it was his duty to make. Atwood v. Armstrong, 102 

App. Div. 601, 92 N. Y. S. 596; Somerset Bank v. Edmund, 76 Ohio St. 396, 81 N. E. 641, 11 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1170.

However, if more is done than the legal duty requires, there is a sufficient consideration. Thus, in Reif v. Paige, 55 Wis. 496, 13 N. W. 473, 42 Am. Rep. 731, the defendant's wife was in a burning hotel, whereupon the defendant said: "I will give $5,000 to any person who will bring the body of my wife dead, or alive, out of that building.' 

The plaintiff was a paid officer of the fire department. 

With a view to claiming the reward he rescued the body. 

In an action for the reward, the Court held: "that inasmuch as the plaintiff could not rescue the body of Mrs. Paige from the burning building without imminent peril of losing his own life, and inasmuch as it was not his duty as a paid officer and member of the fire department to do so, he is in a position to claim the reward alleged to have been offered by the defendant for such rescue." To the same effect is Hartley v. Granville, 216 Mass. 38, 102 N. E. 942, 48 L. R. A. (N.S.) 392. 99 § 204.

Performance of a Private Duty Imposed by Law. 

A promise to perform a domestic or private duty is without consideration. A promise by a husband to pay his wife for her services as housekeeper is void. Coleman v. 

Burr, 93 N. Y. 17, 45 Am. Rep. 160. Likewise, the promise of a blind woman to bequeath property to the man to whom she had already become engaged to marry is not en-

forceable where the sole consideration received is the promise of the husband to nurse and support her after marriage, duties which would devolve upon him in any event. 

Ryan v. Dockery, 134 Wis. 431, 114 N. W. 820, 15 L. R. A. (N.S.) 491. So, too, a promise to surrender goods wrongfully withheld is not sufficient consideration to support a promise made by the owner of such goods to obtain their possession. Tolhurst v. Powers, 133 N. Y. 460, 31 N. E. 326. § 205. 

Vague and Uncertain Promise as Consideration.

A promise which is the consideration for another promise may be too vague and uncertain for enforcement. If so, the agreement is void. Where, for instance, in an action on a note given by a son to his father, the son pleaded a promise made by his father to discharge him from liability on the note, in consideration of his ceasing to make certain complaints to the effect that he had not enjoyed as many advantages as the other children, it was held that the son's promise was no more than a promise "not to bore his father," and was too vague to constitute a consideration for the father's promise. "A man," said the Court, "might complain that another person used the public highway more than he ought to do, and that other might say, 'do not complain and I will give you five pounds.' It is ridiculous to suppose that such promises could be binding." White v. Bluett, 2 C. L. R. 301; Hart v. Georgia R. R. Co., 101 

Ga. 188, 28 S. E. 637. In other words, the requirement that the consideration be certain is merely a part of the general requirement that all of the terms of an agreement must be definite in order to be enforceable. § 206. 

Good Consideration-Term Defined. 

The term "good consideration" as distinguished from "valuable consideration" has no place in the law of contracts, but belongs in the field of conveyances in connection with such subjects as fraudulent conveyances, resulting

CONSIDERATION 

187 trusts, and covenants to stand seised to uses. In some instances, the terms good consideration and valuable consideration are used interchangeably. Aden v. City of Vallejo, 139 Cal. 165, 72 Pac. 905. Good consideration has been defined as a consideration founded upon natural love and affection between near relatives. Groves v. Groves, 65 Ohio 

St. 442, 62 N. E. 1044. Such a consideration, standing alone, is insufficient to render enforceable a promise based thereon. For example, the general duty of a husband to support his wife is not a sufficient consideration to validate a promissory note given by a husband to his wife without further consideration. Whitaker v. Whitaker, 52 N.

Y. 368, 11 Am. Rep. 711. A promise induced by gratitude and affection, to make provision for a friend is unenforceable as a contract. Parsons v. Teller, 188 N. Y. 318, 80 

N. E. 930. Likewise, a promissory note given by the maker to the payee in consideration of love and affection is unenforceable as between the immediate parties thereto. Sullivan v. Sullivan, 122 Ky. 707, 92 S. W. 966, 7 L. R. A. (N.S.) 156 and note. A few decisions to the contrary must be regarded as against the great weight of authority. Gooch v. Gooch, 178 Iowa 902, 160 N. W. 333, L. R. A. 1917C 582.

A distinction should be noted between the gift of the donor's own note or check and the assignment by him, without consideration, of the note or check of another. The former, being executory in character is revocable because of the lack of consideration as to the donee, but in the case of the assignment of the note or check of a third party, there has been an executed gift which cannot be recalled by the donor. See Richardson v. Richardson, 148 Ill. 563, 36 N. E. 608, 26 L. R. A. 305 and note; Woods v. Sturges, 116 Miss. 412, 77 So. 186, L. R. A. 1918C 338 and note. § 207.

Gifts Engagement Ring-Engagement Presents. 

The right of a rejected suitor to recover an engagement ring which he has given to a woman has been the subject

of much litigation in the United States as well as in England. The controversy arises over the questions: What is the consideration for the gift? Did the suitor part with all "dominion over the property"? Is title to the ring vested in the woman? Would the absence of the gift of an engagement ring be a defense to an action for breach of a promise to marry? The weight of authority holds that, irrespective of the lapse of time between the mutual promises to marry and the gift of the ring, the gift is conditional and not absolute and title to the ring vests in the woman only upon a consummation of the marriage. However, if the man is in default without legal justification or is guilty of fraud, the gift becomes absolute. If the woman without legal justification breaks off the engagement, she must return the ring. Beck v. Cohen, 237 App. Div. 729, 262 N. Y. S. 716. Frequently, many articles are exchanged between affianced persons in a sentimental spirit of giving, without any thought of attaching conditions to the gifts. 

In such instances, the claim of the jilted one for a return of the gifts is sympathetically regarded by the courts. Antaramian v. Ourakian, 118 Misc. 558, 194 N. Y. S. 100.

These cases must be distinguished from gifts made to influence the friendship or the favor of another, or as tokens of appreciation of another's kindness and hospitality. Such gifts are unconditional-the title vests in the donee-hence, the donor cannot later, because of the ingratitude of the donee, recover the gifts. Editorial, New York Law Journal, July 21, 1932. 

§ 208. Forbearance To Exercise a Legal Right. 

A promise not to do what otherwise one has a legal right to do is a sufficient consideration. This may relate to a right of action or to personal conduct in general. Hamer v. 

Sidway, 124 N. Y. 538, 27 N. E. 256, 12 L. R. A. 463. Refraining from bidding at a sale is a sufficient consideration to support a promise. White v. McMath & Johnson, 127

CONSIDERATION 

189 

Tenn. 713, 156 S. W. 470, 44 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1115; Rauch v. Donovan, 126 App. Div. 52, 110 N. Y. S. 690. 

§ 209. Forbearance To Sue. 

A promise not to sue on a claim may constitute a sufficient consideration for the forbearance of such a right. The test in determining whether forbearance to sue on a claim is sufficient consideration is: Was the dispute honest or fraudulent? If honest, it affords the basis for an accord between the parties, which the law favors. Post v. Thomas, 212 N. Y. 264, 106 N. E. 69; Blount v. Dillaway, 199 Mass. 

330, 85 N. E. 477, 17 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1036. But where the claim is not even doubtful, colorable, or plausible, in that there is no reason for an honest belief that it has some foundation in law or equity, forbearance to sue is without consideration. Di Iorio v. Di Biazio, 21 R. I. 208, 42 Atl. 

1114; Springstead v. Nees, 125 App. Div. 230, 109 N. Y. S. 

148. Forbearance to sue on an illegal claim, for example, a note given for a gambling debt, is an insufficient consideration to support a promise. This is true for the reason that such new contract might be an indirect affirmance of the prior illegal one. Union Collection Co. v. Buckman, 150 Cal. 159, 88 Pac. 708, 9 L. R. A. (N.S.) 568. It is well settled that an agreement to forbear to sue, in consideration of a promise by a third person to pay the debt of another, constitutes a valid contract. Traders' National 

Bank v. Parker, 130 N. Y. 415, 29 N. E. 1094. 

§ 210. Agreement To Forbear. 

The agreement to forbear may be either expressed or implied. The time need not be fixed and definite. A reasonable time, which is a mixed question of law and fact, is presumed, in the absence of an express agreement as to length of time of forbearance. Strong v. Sheffield, 144 N. Y. 392, 39 N. E. 330; Traders' National Bank v. Parker, 130 N. Y. 

415, 29 N. E. 1094; Scherer v. East Side National Bank of 

Buffalo, 263 N. Y. 190, 188 N. E. 645. It has been held

that mere forbearance or indulgence at the request of another is insufficient and that there must be an agreement to forbear and actual forbearance under such agreement. 

Queal & Co. v. Peterson, 138 Iowa 514, 116 N. W. 593, 19 L. R. A. (N.S.) 842. But such decisions are sound only when the offer called for a promise to forbear rather than a mere act of forbearance. Strong v. Sheffield, supra. 

§ 211. Compromise of Claim. 

Analogous to the relinquishment of a right to sue is the compromise of a disputed claim whereby the claim is extinguished. Whenever there is a dispute as to a doubtful or unliquidated claim, the parties may adjust their differences by a compromise, the mutual release of their respective rights being a sufficient consideration for the compromise. 

After a compromise has been agreed upon in good faith, the merits of the original controversy cannot be called into question; and this is true, though it should afterwards appear that one has thereby received property to which he was not legally entitled. But if the claimant knows his demand to be wholly groundless and forces the other party to a settlement by threats of suit, the compromise will be void. "It is not necessary to uphold a promise, based upon the surrender or composition of a claim, that it was a valid claim—one that could be enforced by law. A promise made upon the settlement of a dispute and to prevent litigation is made upon a good consideration. The settlement of a doubtful claim will uphold a promise to pay a stipulated sum, or do any other lawful act." White v. Hoyt, 73 N. Y. 

505, 514; Sears v. Grand Lodge, Ancient Order of United 

Woodmen, 163 N. Y. 374, 57 N. E. 618, 50 L. R. A. 204; 

Post v. Thomas, 212 N. Y. 264, 106 N. E. 69; Schnell v. 

Perlmon, 238 N. Y. 362, 144 N. E. 641, 34 A. L. R. 1023.

In Post v. Thomas, supra, the Court declared: "It is the settled law of this State that if a debt or claim be disputed or contingent at the time of payment the payment when accepted of a part of the whole debt is a good satisfaction,

CONSIDERATION 

191 and it matters not that there was no solid foundation for the dispute. The test in such cases is: Was the dispute honest or fraudulent? If honest it affords the basis for an accord between the parties, which the law favors, the execution of which is the satisfaction." But if the claim is not doubtful or uncertain, the new promise is without consideration.

The settlement of claims, illegal or void by statute or public policy, affords no consideration to support a promise. 

For an exhaustive note on whether void, invalid, or unfounded claims may be the subject of a valid compromise, see Armijo v. Henry, 14 N. M. 181, 89 Pac. 305, 25 L. R. A. (N.S.) 275.

The compromise of a doubtful claim; i.e., doubtful as to whether there is any claim or as to the amount of the claim, is to be distinguished from a forbearance to sue upon a claim of a definite amount. In forbearance, the one forbearing simply postpones his suit; he does not agree to accept a smaller sum in full payment. 

§ 212. Composition with Creditors. 

The agreement to pay a lesser sum in satisfaction of a greater, in the case of a composition deed with creditors, may appear, at first sight, to be an infraction of the rule that the payment of a part of a debt is an insufficient consideration to support a promise to discharge the whole of the debt, but it is not an exception to the rule. The consideration, in a composition with creditors, is the substitution of a new agreement, with new parties, and a new consideration. The consideration is the mutual forbearance of all the creditors to insist upon their claims. The rights and interests of others become involved in the new arrangement, and, hence, there is no violation of the rule that the payment of a lesser sum will not discharge a greater. A composition deed is an agreement between the creditors themselves as well as between them and their debtor. White v.

Kuntz, 107 N. Y. 518, 14 N. E. 423; Perkins v. Lockwood, 100 Mass. 249, 1 Am. Rep. 103. 

§ 213. 

Mutual Promises.

When a promise is given for a promise, a bilateral contract is formed, each promise being the consideration for the other, and therefore a sufficient consideration to support the contract resulting from the mutual promises, at least where there are promises to do what the parties are not otherwise legally bound to do. Thus, in the exchange of promissory notes, the note of each finds consideration in the note of the other. Each is bound to do some act in the future. Tradesmen's National Bank v. Curtis, 167 N. 

Y. 194, 60 N. E. 429, 52 L. R. A. 430. Mutual promises of merchants to refrain from engaging in business after a certain hour of the evening each day give rise to a sufficient loss or detriment in the way of financial transactions, or to a sufficient gain and advantage from a social or healthful standpoint to support a contract to close their places of business at that hour. Stovall v. McCutchen & Co., 107 

Ky. 577, 54 S. W. 969, 47 L. R. A. 287. 

The promises need not necessarily be expressed. One may be expressed and the other implied from the particular circumstances of the case. Wood v. Lucy, Lady Duff Gordon, 222 N. Y. 88, 118 N. E. 214; Grossman v. Schenker, 206 

N. Y. 466, 100 N. E. 52.

But in Sorrentino v. Bouchet, 161 N. Y. S. 262, where a contract of employment failed to specify that the employee would work for the time mentioned, it was held to be merely unilateral, the Court refusing to imply a promise to work for the time specified.

A close case illustrating a unilateral contract in form, and therefore not binding on the promisor, is Levin v. 

Dietz, 194 N. Y. 376, 87 N. E. 454, 20 L. R. A. (N.S.) 251.

In this case, the defendant, the owner of land, wrote a letter to the plaintiff saying that, if the latter would appear

CONSIDERATION 

193 at a certain time and place with the price in cash, he would receive a deed to the property. The plaintiff appeared at the time and place with the money, prepared to demand the deed, but the defendant was not present. The Court held that since the plaintiff was not bound to buy, the contract was lacking in mutuality. See, also, Cohen & Sons v. 

Lurie Woolen Co., 232 N. Y. 112, 133 N. E. 370; Sun Printing & Publishing Assn. v. Remington Paper & Power Co., 235 N. Y. 338, 139 N. E. 470. § 214. 

Contingent Promises.

When the consideration for a promise is a promise, the whole contract may be contingent and may never come into effect, save at the will of one of the parties. A says to B, "If you will furnish goods to C, I will pay for them." Bis not bound to furnish the goods, but if he does, he may recover on A's promise to pay for them. Willetts v. Sun 

Mutual Insurance Co., 45 N. Y. 45, 6 Am. Rep. 31.

Again, if A promises to supply, at a certain price, all the goods of a certain kind that B may need in his business, and B promises to buy such goods, the promises are mutual and binding. Should B need no goods, he is not bound to pay anything, but in the event of the contingency of his needing them, he is bound to buy them of A. Wells v. Alexandre, 130 N. Y. 642, 29 N. E. 142, 15 L. R. A. 218.

Other instances of a similar character are agreements to buy all, or a certain part of all, the goods of a certain kind that one may produce in a given time. McCall v. Icks, 107 

Wis. 232, 83 N. W. 300; Burgess Sulphite Fibre Co. v. 

Broomfield, 180 Mass. 283, 62 N. E. 367. In Jenkins & Co. v. Anaheim Sugar Co., 247 Fed. 958, L. R. A. 1918E 293, the mutual promises of the parties, one to supply and the other to buy exclusively from the other all sugar required in the purchaser's business during a specified period, were held to create an enforceable agreement.

§ 215. Charitable Subscriptions-Doctrine of Promissory 

Estoppel.

Subscriptions for benevolent, educational, and religious purposes have furnished very fruitful sources of litigation. 

The question most frequently presented is as to the consideration therefor. As a general rule, a mere promise to donate money to a charitable purpose is gratuitous and, therefore unenforceable. Matter of Taylor, 251 N. Y. 257, 167 N. E. 434.

In Presbyterian Church of Albany v. Cooper, 112 N. Y. 

517, 20 N. E. 352, 3 L. R. A. 468, which is a leading case in this State, the deceased, among others, had promised to pay the sum of $5,500 in order to wipe out a church mortgage in consideration of $1 nominally in hand paid to him "and the agreement of each other in this contract contained." A condition was that the sum of $45,000 be subscribed. On compliance with the condition, the trustees of the church brought suit against the decedent's estate for the sum of $3,500, being the balance due on his $5,500 subscription. The Court of Appeals refused to allow a recovery for four reasons: (1) the dollar was not in hand paid; (2) the promises of the other subscribers did not constitute consideration so far as the church was concerned; (3) the efforts of the church trustees to raise money were not at the decedent's request, express or implied-at least the

Court so found; (4) the trustees in any case were under a duty to act on behalf of the church. For these reasons the 

Court held that decedent's promise was purely gratuitous and was unenforceable.

However, later cases hold that the subscriber is liable, upon the theory that the subscription carries with it an implied or express request to the promisee to render services or incur liabilities and that after such expenditures or obligations, the donor should be estopped to plead want of consideration. In Keuka College v. Ray, 167 N. Y. 96, 60 N. E. 325, the Court purported to find such an implied request on the part of the subscriber to the Trustees of

CONSIDERATION 

195 Keuka College to perform services in order to raise funds for the College and, therefore, held the subscriber liable. 

In the comparatively recent case of Allegheny College v. 

National Chautauqua County Bank, 246 N. Y. 369, 159 

N. E. 173, 57 A. L. R. 980, the subscription to a Memorial

Fund which was to be used to educate students for the ministry was held to be binding on the subscriber. The 

Court referred also to the doctrine of "promissory estoppel" without, however, passing upon this issue.

From a moral and equitable standpoint, promises of this nature should be held binding where the beneficiary has gone ahead relying upon the agreement. Though the promise is without consideration in its inception, it is submitted that a consideration was supplied from the moment there was affirmative action by the donee on the strength of the promise.

Mutual subscriptions for a business purpose may be distinguished from subscriptions for a charitable purpose, in that, in such cases, there is usually some valuable consideration moving to the subscriber; namely, a promise to deliver to him certain shares of stock. Martin v. Meles, 179 Mass. 

114, 60 N. E. 397. 

§ 216. Adequacy of Consideration-In Law. 

As a rule, the adequacy of the consideration is immaterial. Courts of law will not make bargains for the parties to a suit, and, if a man gets what he has contracted for, will not inquire whether it was equivalent to the promise which he gave in return. Hamer v. Sidway, 124 N. Y. 538, 27 N. E. 256, 12 L. R. A. 463. If courts were compelled to pass upon the adequacy of the consideration, their time would be consumed in making contracts for the parties. 

This would violate the fundamental principle that there should be the utmost liberty and freedom in the making of contracts, and in refraining from making them if the individual so desires. "The value of all things contracted for," said Hobbes, "is measured by the appetite of the con-

tractors, and therefore the just value is that which they be contented to give." Cited in Pollock on Contracts, sec. 172. "A valuable consideration," said the Court, in Lawrence v. McCalmont, 2 How. (U.S.) 426, 11 Law. Ed. 326, "however small or nominal, if given or stipulated for in good faith, is, in the absence of fraud, sufficient to support an action on any parol contract. *** A stipulation in consideration of one dollar is just as effectual and valuable a consideration as a larger sum stipulated for or paid." 

If an article be of the slightest value to either vendor or vendee, or of no value whatsoever, it will suffice, by way of consideration, for a promise to pay the agreed price, however disproportionate to the real value. Thus, where one purchased mulberry trees which turned out to be of no value by reason of being decayed and almost lifeless, it was held that, as there was neither fraud nor warranty, the inadequacy of the consideration was no defense to an action on the note given for the purchase price. Johnson v. Titus, 2 Hill (N.Y.) 606. So, also, the following have been held sufficient to constitute consideration: allowing the promisor to weigh certain boilers; surrendering a worthless document; and transferring worthless stock in a corporation. 

Bainbridge v. Firmstone, 8 Ad. & E. 743, 112 Eng. Rep. 

1019; Wilton v. Eaton, 127 Mass. 174; State Bank v. Gates, 114 Iowa 323, 86 N. W. 311.

For the same reason, where a person traveled for his own pleasure and benefit at the request of another, it was held that this was sufficient to support a promise by the latter to reimburse him for his expenses. Devecmon v. 

Shaw, 69 Md. 199, 14 Atl. 464. § 217. 

When Value Is Fixed.

The doctrine of inadequacy of consideration does not apply where the agreement is for the mere exchange of sums of money or coin whose value is fixed by law, and the element of time is of no importance. Thus, a promise to pay one dollar is no consideration for a promise to pay one

CONSIDERATION 

197 thousand dollars. Shepard v. Rhodes, 7 R. I. 470, 84 Am. 

Dec. 573. In Schnell v. Nell, 17 Ind. 29, 79 Am. Dec. 453, the Court said: "It is true that, as a general proposition, inadequacy of consideration will not vitiate an agreement. 

But this doctrine does not apply to a mere exchange of sums of money, of coin, whose value is exactly fixed, but to the exchange of something of, in itself, indeterminate value, for money or perhaps, for some other thing of indeterminate value. In this case, had the one cent mentioned been some particular one cent, a family piece, or ancient, remarkable coin, possessing an indeterminate value, extrinsic from its simple money value, a different view might be taken. As it is, the mere promise to pay $600 for one cent, even had the portion of that cent due from the plaintiff been tendered, is an unconscionable contract, void at first blush, upon its face, if it be regarded as an earnest "2 one.

The practice which has grown up, in certain sections of the country, of reciting "in consideration of one dollar each to the other paid" appears to involve a mere exchange of sums of money of equal value and has, therefore, been condemned as insufficient to constitute consideration. Hall v. Allfree, 51 Ind. App. 387, 99 N. E. 813; Velie Motor Car 

Co. v. Kopmeier Motor Car Co., 194 Fed. 324. 

§ 218. Adequacy of Consideration-In Equity. 

As a rule, inadequacy is not sufficient ground for relief in equity, the rule in this respect being the same in equity as at law. Thus, in Parmelee v. Cameron, 41 N. Y. 392, the Court said: "There is no general rule of equity which relieves a party from hard and unreasonable bargains after they become executed, merely because they are such. On the contrary, mere inadequacy is not a sufficient ground for avoiding a sale, unless the inadequacy is so gross as to afford presumptive evidence of actual fraud ** * or is attended with actual fraud, surprise, ignorance, mistake, delusion, or imbecility of mind."

It should be noted also that, in rare instances, a court of equity will enforce agreements where there is, seemingly, no consideration. Thus, in Freeman v. Freeman, 43 N. Y. 

34, 3 Am. Rep. 657, the court of equity decreed specific performance of a promise by a father to make a gift of a farm to his son, where the son, relying upon such promise, had made substantial improvements upon the premises. The 

Court said: "It is further insisted, that an executory promise, not founded upon any valuable consideration, is a mere nude pact furnishing no grounds for an action at law, and that performance of such a promise will not be enforced in equity. This is true so long as the promise has no consideration. *** Expenditures made upon permanent improvements upon land with the knowledge of the owner, induced by his promise, made to the party making the expenditure, to give the land to such party, constitute in equity a consideration for the promise." 

§ 219. Failure of Consideration. 

Strictly speaking, there is no such thing as a failure of consideration. The promisor either receives the consideration he has bargained for, or he does not. If he does not receive the consideration, there is no contract, for there is no consideration; and if there is consideration, there is no failure of it. If there be consideration at the outset, how can it afterwards fail? But it must be admitted that the terms "want" and "failure" of consideration are frequently used as synonymous terms. Hurlburt v. Kephart, 50 Colo. 

353, 115 Pac. 521; Ward v. Textile Commission Co., 139 

App. Div. 109, 123 N. Y. S. 918. § 220.

Nominal Consideration. 

The term nominal consideration is often applied to a cent, a dollar, a farthing, or other small sum recited as consideration in a contract. On principle and, by the weight of authority, such sum is sufficient consideration to sup-

CONSIDERATION 

199 port a contract. Thus, in Ross v. Parks, 93 Ala. 153, 8 So. 

368, 11 L. R. A. 148, fifty cents was held to be sufficient consideration to support a contract to grant an option for the purchase of a tract of land for $200. However, in certain jurisdictions, a nominal consideration has been held insufficient to support a contract. Thus, in Rude v. Levy, 43 Colo. 482, 96 Pac. 560, 24 L. R. A. (N.S.) 91, the payment of one dollar was held not to be a proper and fair consideration to support an option to purchase real estate worth nearly $1,000. 27 Michigan Law Review 314, note, reprinted, Editorial, New York Law Journal, February 9, 1929. § 221. 

Presumption of Consideration.

The rule is, clearly, that there is no presumption of consideration in an action on a simple or an informal contract. 

The promisee must both allege and prove consideration as an essential part of his case. Richardson on Evidence, 4th

Ed., sec. 186. The presumption of consideration is now controlled by statute in the following specific instances: 

1. A presumption of consideration arises in an executory contract under seal. But the presumption is rebuttable. Civil Practice Act, sec. 342. In an executed contract under seal, the presumption of consideration is conclusive. Section 193, supra. 

2. A negotiable instrument in the possession of the payee raises a presumption of consideration. The presumption, however, is not conclusive. But when such an instrument is in the possession of a third party who is a holder in due course, the presumption of consideration becomes conclusive or irrebuttable. Negotiable Instruments 

Law, secs. 50 and 54; Richardson on Bills and Notes, 4th 

Ed., sec. 10; Dougherty v. Salt, 227 N. Y. 200, 125 N. E. 94. 

3. Mutual written agreements signed by the parties thereto extending the time of payment of matured debts

raise conclusive presumptions of consideration. Otherwise expressed, such agreements are valid without consideration. 

Personal Property Law, sec. 33, subd. 2; Real Property 

Law, sec. 279 (effective since April 5, 1934).

CHAPTER XII FORM OF CONTRACT § 222. Classification of Contracts. 

Contracts are either (1) informal, or (2) formal. Informal contracts are those which require no particular form to make them effective. All other contracts are formal and are either: a. 

Contracts of record; b. Contracts under seal; or

C. Contracts required to be in writing. 

§ 223. Contracts of Record. 

Contracts of record are of two main classes: a. Judgments; b. Recognizances. § 224. Judgments.

A judgment is an obligation created by a judicial act. 

It is clearly not a contract because it lacks the element of agreement. But since a judgment was enforced in an action of assumpsit under the common law at a time when all civil actions were regarded either as actions ex contractu or actions ex delicto, it has, for certain purposes, been classified as a contract. O'Brien v. Young, 95 N. Y. 428, 47 Am. Rep. 64, aff'd 146 U. S. 162, 36 Law. Ed. 925, 13 

Sup. Ct. Rep. 54; Wyman v. Mitchell, 1 Cowen (N.Y.) 316.

It is well settled that a judgment is not a contract within the provision of the United States Constitution forbidding a state to pass any law impairing the obligation of contracts. Morley v. Lake Shore & Michigan Southern Ry. 

Co., 146 U. S. 162. However, the term "contract" is sometimes construed to include judgments. Thus, in Gutta 

Percha & Rubber Manufacturing Co. v. Houston, 108 N. Y. 201

276, 15 N. E. 402, a judgment was held to be within the provision of the Civil Practice Act, section 902, which permits a warrant of attachment to be obtained in an action for a "breach of contract, express or implied." 

§ 225. Recognizances. 

A recognizance is an acknowledgment of a debt of record. It has many of the attributes of a judgment. The term is commonly applied to the various forms of security for the appearance of the accused in criminal proceedings and is often used synonymously with the term "bond." 

Wright v. State, 22 Tex. App. 670, 3 S. W. 346; National 

Surety Co. v. Nazzaro, 233 Mass. 74, 123 N. E. 346. A recognizance is not a contract between individuals; hence, the word "contracts" as used in a statute has been held not to include recognizances. State v. Weatherwax, 12 

Kan. 463. The common law objection to treating a recognizance as a contract was that the recognizance had been made with the sovereign with whom a subject could not contract. § 226. 

Contracts under Seal.

A contract under seal is sometimes called a deed, a bond, or a specialty, and is the only formal contract known to the common law. A sealed instrument derives its validity neither from the fact of an agreement nor from the consideration, but from the form in which it is expressed. 

The act of sealing an instrument is deemed to be done with great deliberation and solemnity, implying great caution and the fullness of consent. It has been said that: "The solemnity which for centuries has been attached to instruments under seal still remains anchored as something tangible and of substance, although in this practical age we are apt to think it rests upon fiction." Williams v. 

Magee, 76 App. Div. 512, 78 N. Y. S. 550, aff'd 177 N. Y. 

534, 69 N. E. 1132. In Peterson v. City of New York, 194 

N. Y. 437, 87 N. E. 772, Judge Cullen remarked that:

FORM OF CONTRACT 

203 "Though in many respects the effect of a seal has by recent statutes and decisions been very much impaired, nevertheless a sealed instrument still retains in law certain characteristics peculiar to itself." In Crowley v. Lewis, 239 

N. Y. 264, 146 N. E. 374, the Court said: "The importance of the seal in this State has been much diminished. * * * 

We had no thought, however, that all distinctions between sealed and unsealed instruments were swept aside. Such an idea would have been impossible if for nothing else because of the rules contained in our statutes with regard to the limitations of actions.” § 227.

How Seal May Be Made. 

At common law, in order that a seal might exist in legal contemplation, there must have been an impression upon wax or other adhesive substance. A mere scrawl with a pen was insufficient to constitute a seal, though, in some states, it was established at an early date that a mere flourish of the pen was sufficient to constitute a seal. Warren v. Lynch, 5 Johns. (N.Y.) 239. The General Construction Law, section 44, now provides that: "The private seal of a person, other than a corporation, to any instrument or writing shall consist of a wafer, wax or other similar adhesive substances affixed thereto, or of paper or other similar substance affixed thereto, by mucilage or other adhesive substance, or of the word 'seal,' or the letters 'L.S.,' opposite the signature." A seal of a court, public officers, or corporation may be impressed directly upon the instrument or writing to be sealed, or upon wafer, wax, or other adhesive substance affixed thereto, or upon paper or other similar substance affixed thereto by mucilage or other adhesive substance. General Construction Law, sec. 43.

However, in New York and certain other states, the mere attaching of a seal after a signature, without any recognition of it in the body of the instrument by some such phrase as "witness my hand and seal" or "signed and sealed," is insufficient, however, to constitute the writing a sealed in-

strument. To hold otherwise "would open the door to frauds and forgeries and enable evil-disposed persons to prevent the running of the six years' Statute of Limitations, by merely attaching at the end of the instrument a seal." Matter of Pirie, 198 N. Y. 209, 91 N. E. 587. In 

O'Keefe v. French, 239 App. Div. 498, 268 N. Y. S. 102, the

Appellate Division held that a reference in the body of an instrument to the seal is no longer necessary to constitute the writing a sealed instrument. The decision is in distinct conflict with the earlier authorities in this State and is, we submit, unsound. Brooklyn Law Review, April, 1934, p. 348, note. In some states, however, the recital of the seal in the instrument is not necessary. Wing v. Chase, 35 Me. 260.

An expression of an intention in a document to make it a sealed instrument is of itself insufficient to make it one. 

Thus, the recital "signed, sealed, and delivered" or "whereunto we have affixed our seals" is not sufficient to constitute a sealed instrument if in fact nothing which the law recognizes as a seal is affixed to the signatures. Empire Trust 

Co. v. Heinze, 242 N. Y. 475, 152 N. E. 266. 

§ 228. Adopting Seal Previously Affixed. 

If the impression or mark upon the paper is of such a nature as to be recognized in law as a seal, as where the seal is printed on a blank form, the obligor may adopt it as his own seal. Furthermore, the same seal may be adopted by more than one party to the instrument when two or more sign. Rusling v. Union Pipe & Construction Co., 5 App. 

Div. 448, 39 N. Y. S. 216, aff'd 158 N. Y. 737, 53 N. E. 

1131; Ryan v. Cooke, 172 Ill. 302, 50 N. E. 213.

EFFECT OF SEAL § 229. Estoppel-Sealed Instruments. 

The general rule is that the truth of the statements in a sealed instrument cannot be disputed by the party who made them. He is estopped from denying them. Metro-

FORM OF CONTRACT 

205 politan Life Insurance Co. v. Bender, 124 N. Y. 47, 26 

N. E. 345, 11 L. R. A. 708; Watkins v. Robertson, 105 Va. 

269, 54 S. E. 33, 5 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1194. However, the modern tendency is to apply the same rule in this connection to both sealed instruments and simple contracts. Hastings v. Lovejoy, 140 Mass. 261, 2 N. E. 776. § 230. 

Consideration—Sealed Instruments. 

At common law, contracts under seal required no consideration. In many states, this rule has been modified or abrogated by the complete abolishment of private seals. 

In Stiebel v. Grosberg, 202 N. Y. 266, 95 N. E. 692, 36 

L. R. A. (N.S.) 1147, the Court said: "At common law the seal to a written instrument was conclusive evidence of a sufficient consideration, and its conclusive character could not be changed by parol testimony. This rule of the common law, however, was modified by the statute [Civil Practice Act, section 342], which provides that a seal upon an executory instrument, hereafter executed, is only presumptive evidence of a sufficient consideration, which may be rebutted, as if the instrument was not sealed. Neither a receipt nor a release is a contract or an executory instrument. They are merely declarations or admissions in writing, and consequently it was held that the modification of the statute with reference to seals upon executory instruments does not extend to releases, which, when under seal, continue to be conclusive evidence of a sufficient consideration." However, in Orthey v. Bogan, 236 N. Y. 351, 140 

N. E. 722, the Court said: "It is urged that the seal is 'conclusive evidence' of the payment of a sufficient consideration. The rule is much too broadly stated. * * * Discussion of the conclusive effect of the seal on an executed instrument which does not require the presence of a seal for its validity, as bearing on the question of consideration, may properly be reserved for a case where it is essential to the decision,"

§ 231. Merger-Sealed Instruments. 

A sealed instrument is of a higher nature than an informal written contract or one not under seal. Therefore, if, after making an informal contract, the parties enter into another contract under seal relating to the same subject matter, the former is merged into the latter. Clifton v. 

Jackson Iron Co., 74 Mich. 183, 41 N. W. 891; Butler & 

Vosburgh v. Miller, 1 Denio (N.Y.) 410; Schoonmaker v. 

Hoyt, 148 N. Y. 425, 42 N. E. 1059. 

§ 232. Statute of Limitations. 

Wherever the common law prevails and private seals have not been abolished, the period of the statute of limitations within which an action may be brought to enforce a contract under seal is generally much longer than in the case of contracts not under seal. Clarke v. Pierce, 215 

Mass. 552, 102 N. E. 1094. Thus, in New York, a right of action on informal contracts is barred if it is not brought within six years after such action accrues. A longer period, twenty years, prevails with sealed instruments. Civil Practice Act, secs. 47 and 48. 

§ 233. Delivery Sealed Instruments. 

To render a sealed instrument operative, it must be delivered and accepted. What becomes of it after delivery is immaterial; it may even be redelivered to the person who executed it. King v. Fragley, 19 Cal. App. 735, 127 Pac. 

813. However, possession by the obligee is prima facie evidence of delivery and acceptance. The mere formal execution and retention of a deed by the grantor is insufficient to pass title to the grantee, and no delivery thereof to the grantee could be presumed from these facts alone. But the recording of a deed is presumptive evidence of delivery. 

Sweetland v. Buell, 164 N. Y. 541, 58 N. E. 663. 

§ 234. Delivery in Escrow. 

A deed or other instrument may be delivered in escrow.

FORM OF CONTRACT 

207 This occurs when the obligor delivers the deed to a third party, to be held by him until some contingency occurs or some condition is complied with, upon the happening of which the instrument is delivered to the obligee and becomes of full force and effect. If the depositary delivers the deed to the grantee before the conditions are complied with, such delivery has no validity as between the grantor and grantee. Hogueland v. Arts, 113 Iowa 634, 85 N. W. 818. 

§ 235. Necessity of Seal. 

Although the seal is uniformly used on all important contracts, deeds, and the like, yet its absence does not invalidate the instrument. Contracts of corporations and deeds of land are valid even though not executed under seal. 

Leinkauf v. Calman, 110 N. Y. 50, 17 N. E. 389; Leask v. 

Horton, 39 Misc. 144, 79 N. Y. S. 148. In some jurisdictions, a seal is made necessary in the case of particular contracts. Paine v. Guardians, 8 Q. B. Div. 326, 115 Eng.

Rep. 899. But there is no statute in New York requiring contracts of any nature whatsoever to be under seal. In some states, the use of the private seal has been abolished by statute, the effect of which is to obliterate all distinctions between sealed and unsealed instruments. 24 Ruling Case 

Law 689; Pacific Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Webb, 157 

Fed. 155.

CHAPTER XIII  § 236. At Common Law. 

The common law makes no provision requiring that any contract, irrespective of its purpose or object, should be in any prescribed form. An oral or implied contract is as effective, under the common law, as a written contract, except where the written contract is under seal. The common law gives to written instruments under seal certain benefits not enjoyed by any other form of contract. 

§ 237. By Statute. 

The principal contracts which are required to be in writing are those which fall within the provisions of the old English Statute of Frauds. But there is constantly an increasing number of contracts which do not fall within the Statute of Frauds that are required by statute in New York to be in writing. These contracts are not fully developed but are only noted as preliminary to a consideration of those within the Statute of Frauds and are: Negotiable 2. A promise to pay a debt when its recovery is barred by the statute of limitations. Civil Practice Act, sec. 59. 

3. A promise to pay a debt discharged in bankruptcy. 

Personal Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 5. 

4. Mutual agreements between creditor and debtor for the extension of the time of payment of a debt which is without consideration other than the mutual agreement. 

So, too, such agreements must be in writing where the debt is secured by real property. Personal Property Law, sec. 

33, subd. 2; Real Property Law, sec. 279 (1934). 

1. The acceptance of a bill of exchange. 

Instruments Law, sec. 220. 

5. A promise to convey or assign a trust in personal property. Also a contract to bequeath property or make 208

a testamentary provision of any kind. Personal Property 

Law, sec. 31, subds. 4 and 7. 

6. Marriage contracts wherein the parties become husband and wife without having the marriage solemnized by a clergyman or a public officer. Domestic Relations Law, sec. 11, subd. 4. 

7. Special agreements of innkeepers as to liability for the loss of property of guests. General Business Law, sec. 200. 

8. An assignment or other transfer of an interest in the estate of a deceased person. Personal Property Law, sec. 32. 

9. The authorization of an agent to convey or create an interest in real property or to lease real property for a term of more than one year. Real Property Law, sec. 242. 

See section 253, infra. 

10. The authorization of an agent to execute a contract for the sale of real property, or an interest therein, or for the leasing of real property for a period of more than one year. Real Property Law, sec. 259 (1934). See section 

253, infra.

Similar provisions are common in other jurisdictions. 

Under federal statutes, the assignment of a copyright or patent right must be in writing. 2 Federal Statutes Annotated (2d Ed.), sec. 42.

STATUTE OF FRAUDS § 238. Origin and History. 

One of the most important statutes ever promulgated, either in England or in this country, is the celebrated Statute of Frauds. The statute was enacted in England in 1676 (29 Car. 11, c. 3).
