The act was entitled "An Act for Prevention of Frauds and Perjuries." The object of the statute is to prevent certain fraudulent practices which are commonly endeavored to be upheld by perjury. 

The fourth and seventeenth sections of the statute have

been reenacted by most of the states, though with slight deviations. In all the states, contracts for the sale of real property are required to be in writing, but this is not true in reference to contracts for the sale of personal property. 

The Statute of Frauds is in force in this country only where, and to the extent to which, it has been adopted by the legislature. It is not a part of the common law which was received and adopted by the states irrespective of legislation. McKennon v. Winn, 1 Okla. 327, 33 Pac. 582, 22

L. R. A. 501. In the state of Maryland, however, by virtue of its Declaration of Rights, Article V, which declares in force in Maryland all English statutes enacted prior to July 4, 1776, and applicable to local circumstances, the Statute of Frauds became a part of the law of Maryland. 

Lewis v. Tapman, 90 Md. 294, 45 Atl. 459, 47 L. R. A. 385.

A similar situation exists in other states. See note in 22 

L. R. A. 508. § 239.

Section Four-Statute of Frauds. 

The fourth section of the English Statute of Frauds provides that: "No action shall be brought whereby to charge any executor or administrator, upon any special promise, to answer damages out of his own estate; or whereby to charge the defendant upon any special promise to answer for the debt, default, or miscarriages of another person; or to charge any person upon any agreement made upon consideration of marriage; or upon any contract or sale of lands, tenements, or hereditaments, or any interest in or concerning them; or upon any agreement that is not to be performed within the space of one year from the making thereof; unless the agreement upon which such action shall be brought, or some memorandum or note thereof, shall be in writing, and signed by the party to be charged therewith, or some other person thereunto by him lawfully authorized."

§ 240. 

Section Seventeen- Section Seventeen 

Statute of Frauds.

The seventeenth section of the English Statute of Frauds 

"No contract for the sale of any goods, wares and merchandises, for the price of ten pounds sterling or upwards, shall be allowed to be good; except the buyer shall accept part of the goods so sold, and actually receive the same, or give something in earnest to bind the bargain, or in part of payment, or that some note or memorandum in writing of the said bargain be made, and signed by the parties to be charged by such contract or their agents thereunto lawfully authorized." 

§ 241. 

New York Statutes.

The following statutory provisions in New York correspond to the provisions contained in the fourth and seventeenth sections of the original Statute of Frauds. The fourth section of the original statute includes the first five of the following subdivisions; the seventeenth section includes the sixth subdivision. These are: 

1. A special promise by an executor or administrator to answer for the damages or to pay the debts of the testator or intestate out of his own estate. Decedent Estate 

Law, sec. 113. 

2. Any special promise to answer for the debt, default, or miscarriage of another person. Personal Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 2. 

3. Agreements made in consideration of marriage. 

Personal Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 3. 4.

Agreements not to be performed within one year from the making thereof, or the performance of which is not to be completed before the end of a lifetime. Personal 

Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 1. The latter part of this subdivision was not included within the provisions of the original Statute of Frauds. It was added to the New York statute by the legislature in 1933.

5. Contracts for the sale of any real property or any interest therein, or a lease for more than one year. Real 

Property Law, sec. 259. Also a contract to establish a trust or to make a devise of real property. Real Property 

Law, sec. 259-a (1933). 

6. Contracts for the sale of goods or choses in action of the value of fifty dollars or upward, unless a. The buyer accepts and receives a part of the goods; b. Pays part of the purchase price; c. The sale is by auction. randum is sufficient. secs. 85 and 31, subd. 6.

The auctioneer's memo- 

Personal Property Law, 

§ 242. Promise by Executor or Administrator. "No executor or administrator shall be chargeable upon any special promise to answer damages, or to pay the debts of the testator or intestate, out of his own estate, unless the agreement for that purpose, or some memorandum or note thereof, be in writing, and signed by such executor or administrator, or by some other person by him thereunto specially authorized." Decedent Estate Law, sec. 113.

The phrase "or pay debts of the testator or intestate" was inserted in the New York statute. This provision does not apply where the executor or administrator promises to pay a debt which is not a debt of the estate. Mackin v. Dwyer, 205 Mass. 472, 91 N. E. 893. Even though the claim against the estate did not arise until after the death of the deceased, the executor is not personally liable on his oral promise to pay the claim if it is a debt of the estate. Dillaby v. Wilcox, 60 Conn. 71, 22 Atl. 491, 13 L. R. A. 643. However, if the executor makes a personal oral promise to pay a debt which is not a binding obligation on him as executor and which arose after the death of the testator, such promise is not within the statute. Fehlinger v. Wood, 134 Pa. 

St. 517, 19 Atl. 746. Moreover, if the promise by the executor is made to subserve some purpose of his own, the

statute does not apply. It is then regarded as an original and independent promise. Bellows v. Sowles, 57 Vt. 164, 52 Am. Rep. 118; Wales v. Stout, 115 N. Y. 638, 21 N. E. 1027. 

§ 243. Special Promise To Answer for the Debt, Default, or Miscarriage of Another Person. 

"Every agreement, promise or undertaking is void, unless it or some note or memorandum thereof be in writing, and subscribed by the party to be charged therewith, or by his lawful agent, if such agreement, promise or undertaking is a special promise to answer for the debt, default or miscarriage of another person." Personal Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 2. Despite the use of the word "void" in the statute, it is well established that such agreement, promise, or undertaking is merely unenforceable. Crane v. 

Powell, 139 N. Y. 379, 34 N. E. 911.

The term "debt" means a definite amount; while "default" refers to an indefinite amount of loss or damage resulting from a breach of a contract. "Miscarriage" is applied to tort cases, as where one agrees to answer for the wrongful act of another. Richardson v. Crandall, 48 N. Y. 

348; Kirkham v. Marter, 2 Barn. & Ald. 613, 106 Eng.

Rep. 490. The phrase "of another person" refers to one other than the promisor.

The promise of a third person to answer for another's debt, where the promise is made contemporaneously with the creation of the debt or subsequent thereto, may be either original or collateral, depending entirely upon the intentions of the parties. If the promise is direct and unconditional, it is considered an original undertaking and, therefore, not within the statute. But where it appears that credit is not given in the first instance to the third person who promises to pay for the goods sold or the credit extended to another, the undertaking is collateral and must be in writing. To bring the promise within the statute there must be three parties; namely, the debtor, the creditor, and

the guarantor (third person). Thus, if property is delivered or services are rendered to one person, upon an oral promise of payment by another, the goods or services being charged only to the person to whom delivery was so made or for whom services were so rendered, the promise of such other person is collateral to that of the principal debtor and, if not in writing, void. Shay v. Cruxton, 116 N. Y. S. 

1123. In ascertaining to whom the credit was given, whether to the party sought to be charged or to the debtor, the intention of the parties, as gathered from all the circumstances of the transaction, will control and this, too, regardless of the language used in creating the obligation. 

Davis v. Patrick, 141 U. S. 479, 35 Law. Ed. 826, 12 Sup. 

Ct. Rep. 58; Richardson Press v. Albright, 224 N. Y. 497, 121 N. E. 362, 8 A. L. R. 1195. 

A promise "to see another paid" or "to pay if the debtor does not" is usually treated as collateral and within the 

Statute of Frauds. If A says to B: "Let X have what groceries he wants and I will pay for them," A's promise is original and not within the statute; but if A says to B, "If X does not pay I will," the undertaking is collateral. 

Mallory v. Gillett, 21 N. Y. 412; Brown v. Weber, 38 N. Y. 

187; Nugent v. Wolfe, 111 Pa. St. 471, 4 Atl. 15. $244. Promise To Answer for Debt or Default of Another Made for Benefit of Promisor. 

Where a promise is made for the benefit of a promisor and to subserve his own ends and purposes, the promise is original and not collateral and, therefore, not within the Statute of Frauds although it is in effect a promise to pay the antecedent debt of another. And this is true even though the nature of the promise is collateral in form and would be so construed but for the fact that it was made for the promisor's own benefit. This proposition of law finds frequent application in building contracts. Raabe v. 

Squier, 148 N. Y. 81, 42 N. E. 516. For example: Where, after the abandonment of a building contract by the prin-

cipal contractor, the owner of the building makes an oral agreement with a person to whom the principal contractor had sublet the iron work required by the contract, and who, prior to the abandonment, had furnished a portion of such iron work, that, if such sub-contractor would finish the said work, the owner would pay him for all the iron work, such promise is an original promise, founded upon a valid consideration moving to the owner, and entitles the sub-contractor to recover from the owner, not only for work done and materials furnished subsequent to the making of the new promise, but for that which had been done and furnished prior thereto. Reisler v. Silbermintz, 99 App. Div. 131, 90 N. Y. S. 967. 

In White v. Rintoul, 108 N. Y. 222, 227, 15 N. E. 318, the

Court said: "Where the primary debt subsists and was antecedently contracted, the promise to pay it is original when it is founded on a new consideration moving to the promisor and beneficial to him, and such that the promisor thereby comes under an independent duty of payment irrespective of the liability of the principal debtor." But the mere fact that the promisor may have an interest in the performance of a contract by another, or that he may derive some possible benefit thereby, cannot alone determine whether his promise is original or collateral. In Richardson Press v Albright, 224 N. Y. 497, 121 N. E. 362, 8

L. R. A. 1195, the Court held that the oral promise in question to pay the debt of another was within the Statute of Frauds, even though the new consideration for the promise was beneficial to the defendant. In addition to the beneficial nature of the consideration, the question still remains whether or not the promisor comes under an independent duty to make payment, irrespective of the liability of the principal debtor. Whether in any particular case there is an independent duty to make payment is a question concerning which there is an irreconcilable conflict in the authorities. This is especially noticeable in building contracts where the owner of a building in process of construction

promises to see that a materialman or sub-contractor is paid if the latter will complete his contract which he has refused to do unless the owner promises to pay him. Cases holding that the interest of the owner is sufficient to constitute an original promise are considered in Voska, Foelsch & Sidlo v. Ruland, 172 App. Div. 616, 158 N. Y. S. 780; 

Howell v. Harvey, 65 W. Va. 310, 64 S. E. 249, 22 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1077 and note; Windsor Construction Co. v. Ruland, 173 App. Div. 94, 159 N. Y. S. 446. Cases holding that the interest of the promisor is not sufficient to constitute an original undertaking and, therefore, that the promise is within the Statute of Frauds are: Snyder v. Monroe Eckstein Brewing Co., 107 App. Div. 328, 95 N. Y. S. 144, aff'd 

188 N. Y. 576, 80 N. E. 1120; Roscoe Lumber Co. v. Reynolds, 124 App. Div. 539, 108 N. Y. S. 1018. The interest of a stockholder of a corporation, even though the stockholder may be an officer of the corporation and receiving the direct and immediate benefit of the contract, is not of itself sufficient to constitute an original and independent promise. 

Thus, an oral promise of an officer and stockholder of a corporation to become individually responsible for a loan made or goods sold to the corporation is collateral and within the statute, the benefit accruing to the stockholder from such loan or sale being remote and indirect. Richardson Press v. 

Albright, supra.

The pivotal point, in all cases where it is a close question whether the promise is within or without the Statute of Frauds, rests on the intention of the parties, and evidence of the conditions and circumstances of the parties at the time the promise in question was made is admissible for the purpose of determining the intention of the promisor. His interest may be such as to clearly justify the conclusion that at the time he made the promise it was his intention to pay the debt. If so, his promise is original and not within the statute; otherwise it is collateral and, therefore, within the Statute of Frauds. The cases support the conclusion that it is the intention as evidenced by the interest

that controls the application of the statute. Witschard v. 

Brody & Sons, Inc., 257 N. Y. 97, 177 N. E. 385. For a collation of authorities and a thoroughly prepared study of when a promise to answer for the debt or default of another is within the Statute of Frauds, see Brooklyn Law Review, April, 1934, p. 270. 

§ 245. Indemnity Contract Not Promise To Answer for 

Debt or Default.

A promise by one person to indemnify another for becoming a surety for a third person is not within the Statute of Frauds and, therefore, need not be in writing. An oral promise by B to C that he will indemnify C for any loss he may sustain by becoming a surety for D is enforceable. A promise by the payee of a note that, if another will sign the same as surety for the maker, he, the payee, will indemnify and save the surety harmless is not within the statute. The theory is that the statute is applicable only to promises made to persons to whom another is answerable, that is, the creditor. Posten v. Clem, 201 Ala. 529, 78 

So. 883, 1 A. L. R. 381, 383 and note; Assets Realization 

Co. v. Roth, 226 N. Y. 370, 123 N. E. 743; Tighe v. Morrison, 116 N. Y. 263, 22 N. E. 164, 5 L. R. A. 617. 

§ 246. Agreement Made in Consideration of Marriage. 

"Every agreement, promise or undertaking is void, unless it or some note or memorandum thereof be in writing, and subscribed by the party to be charged therewith, or by his lawful agent, if such agreement, promise or undertaking is made in consideration of marriage, except mutual promises to marry." Personal Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 

3. Though a similar provision exists in nearly all states, such agreements, though oral, are enforceable in Pennsylvania. Flory v. Houck, 186 Pa. St. 263, 40 Atl. 482. This provision of the statute embraces all antenuptial agreements to pay money or to settle property upon one of the parties if the marriage is consummated. Hence, an oral

antenuptial agreement is void, and even after the marriage, the wife cannot enforce specific performance on the ground that she has performed her part of the agreement. Hunt v. Hunt, 171 N. Y. 396, 64 N. E. 159, 59 L. R. A. 306. But where the agreement has been fully carried out according to its terms, the Statute of Frauds no longer affects the transaction. Miller v. Sire, 224 Fed. 424, L. R. A. 1916D 

1211; Lyons v. Knights of the Maccabees, 192 App. Div. 

109, 182 N. Y. S. 212. Furthermore, when there is a consideration for the contract other than the marriage of the parties, the promise is not within the statute. Thus, where a father promised to convey land to the promisee if the latter would marry the son of the promisor in order to settle a prosecution for seduction, the promise was held not to be within the statute. Bader v. Hiscox, 188 Iowa 986, 174 

N. W. 565, 10 A. L. R. 316. 

§ 247. Agreement Not To Be Performed within One Year. 

"Every agreement, promise or undertaking is void, unless it or some note or memorandum thereof be in writing, and subscribed by the party to be charged therewith, or by his lawful agent, if such agreement, promise or undertaking by its terms is not to be performed within one year from the making thereof or the performance of which is not to be completed before the end of a lifetime." The latter part was added by the legislature in 1933. Personal 

Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 1. This provision requires contracts to be reduced to writing, if they cannot, by their terms, be performed within one year after they have been made.

In a recent case, the defendant claimed to be discharged as a surety because of a change in the time of payment on a bond and mortgage without his consent. It appears that there was an oral agreement between the holder of the mortgage and the purchaser of the mortgaged premises, providing for payments on the bond and mortgage quarterly instead of monthly. It was held that the oral agree-

ment was within the statute because it could not be performed within a year and was, therefore, unenforceable. · 

Hence, the original mortgagor was not discharged. Though clearly legally sound, this seems to be a harsh ruling. Hermann v. Gressel, 148 Misc. 775, 266 N. Y. S. 263; David 

Taylor Co. v. Fansteel Products Co., Inc., 234 App. Div. 

548, 255 N. Y. S. 270, aff'd 261 N. Y. 514, 185 N. E. 718.

Is a promise to pay commissions to a realty broker provided the tenant renews a five year lease within the statute? 

Allwin Realty Co. v. Barth, 161 App. Div. 568, 146 N. Y. S. 

960. Although the rule that agreements not to be performed within one year must be in writing is very definite and not difficult to understand when literally interpreted, yet by innumerable judicial decisions the construction placed upon it has become extremely artificial. In construing the rule, the courts have held the following oral contracts to be valid: 

1. If the contract be fully performed by one of the parties within the year, although not required by the terms thereof, it need not be in writing. This is the prevailing rule in the United States and England. Great Western 

Turnpike Co. v. Shafer, 57 App. Div. 331, 68 N. Y. S. 5, aff'd 172 N. Y. 662, 65 N. E. 1121; Donellan v. Read, 3 

Barn. & Ald. 899, 110 Eng. Rep. 330. An oral contract for the sale of wood was taken out of the statute by a delivery of all the wood, though a portion thereof was not to be delivered within one year. Barkley v. Rensselaer & Saratoga 

R. R. Co., 71 N. Y. 205; Rague v. New York Evening Journal Publishing Co., 164 App. Div. 126, 149 N. Y. S. 668. 

2. If, by any possibility, the contract may by its terms be performed within a year although the parties may have intended that its operation should extend through a longer period, the statute will not apply. A testator orally agreed to set aside $3,000 for Jones if Jones would give up his position and pass the entrance examination for Pratt Institute and enter the institute for a two-year course of study. Jones passed the examination and entered the In-

stitute. Jones fully performed his promise within a year. 

Since he did not undertake to complete the course in two years, the testator's oral promise was not within the statute. 

Robinson v. Hayes' Estate, 207 App. Div. 718, 202 N. Y. S. 

732, aff'd 239 N. Y. 512, 147 N. E. 175. A contract to build a house within fifteen months is not within the statute, since it may be performed within a year. So, too, a contract not to be performed within a year is taken out of the statute where either party may rightfully terminate it within a year. Thus, an oral contract for nine years which could be terminated by either party at the end of six months is not within the statute and is therefore valid. 

Standard Bitulithic Co. v. Curran, 256 Fed. 68; Gallagher v. Finch, Pruyn & Co., 211 App. Div. 635, 207 N. Y. S. 403. 

3. The interpretation of the old English Statute of 

Frauds that the statute did not apply if the death of either party within a year would leave the purpose for which the contract was made fully performed no longer applies, because the statute in 1933 was amended by adding "or the performance of which is not to be completed before the end of a lifetime." Personal Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 1.

Formerly, an agreement for a person's support during life was not within the statute, because death within a year would leave the purpose for which the contract was made fully performed. But since 1933, such an agreement is within the statute because performance of the contract is not to be completed before the end of a lifetime. The former rule is supported by McCabe v. Green, 18 App. Div. 

625, 45 N. Y. S. 723. No case involving the amendment has, up to this time, been reported.

As to whether agreements to refrain from engaging in business for a definite number of years are without the statute on the theory that the death of the promisor would leave the purpose of the contract fully performed, see Mc- 

Girr v. Campbell, 71 App. Div. 83, 75 N. Y. S. 571; Shapiro v. Balaban, 210 App. Div. 47, 205 N. Y. S. 208.

§ 248. 

Employment Contracts for One Year or Longer. 

An oral employment contract for one year and one day or longer is clearly within the statute and unenforceable unless in writing. If the contract is for one year, the employment to commence on the day of making the contract, it is not within the statute. Although the authorities uniformly support the principles of law as stated relative to employment contracts for longer than one year and those which are to begin on the date of making, yet they are in hopeless confusion as to whether a contract for one year to commence on a day following the date of its making is by its terms "not to be performed within one year from the making thereof." For instance, is an oral contract of employment made March 31st for one year to commence April 1st, which, by its terms "is not to be performed within one year from the making thereof" within the statute? If within the statute, it is unenforceable; otherwise it is valid. Cases holding that employment contracts for one year to begin on a day following the making thereof are within the statute are Fanger v. Caspary, 87 App. Div. 

418, 84 N. Y. S. 410; Billington v. Cahill, 51 Hun 132, 4 

N. Y. S. 660; Jonap v. Preger, 59 Misc. 187, 110 N. Y. S. 

483. Decisions to the contrary are Prokop v. Bedford 

Waist & Dress Co., 105 Misc. 573, 173 N. Y. S. 792, aff'd 

187 App. Div. 662, 176 N. Y. S. 376; Goldman v. Ackerman, 225 App. Div. 829, 232 N. Y. S. 564. 

§ 249. Effect of Part Performance of Employment Con tract.

While the decisions are in conflict, yet the weight of authority holds that part or even full performance on one side does not take an oral contract of employment for more than a year out of the statute.

The question becomes important whenever an employee sues for services already rendered under an oral contract that was not to be completed within a year. Although he cannot recover the contract price, he may recover on

quantum meruit for the reasonable value of his services, which may be considerably less than the agreed rate. For a collation of the authorities and discussion thereof, see 

Editorial, New York Law Journal, July 21, 1930. In Tyler v. Windels, 186 App. Div. 698, 174 N. Y. S. 762, aff'd 227 

N. Y. 589, 125 N. E. 926, the oral employment contract was for eighteen months. It provided for a stipulated compensation and an additional bonus. The plaintiff had fully performed his part of the contract and had been paid the agreed compensation for eighteen months and the bonus for the first twelve months. The Court held that the contract was not taken out of the statute and, therefore, that the plaintiff could not recover the unpaid promised bonus. 

To the same effect is Sophie v. Ford, 230 App. Div. 568, 245 N. Y. S. 470. 

§ 250. Agreement To Marry Not within Statute. 

The Statute of Frauds has no application to an agreement to marry. Therefore, an agreement to marry at a time not "within one year from the making thereof" is not within the operation of the Statute of Frauds. To require such a contract to be reduced to writing is said to be contrary to practice, usage, and human nature. Brick v. Gannar, 36 Hun 52. Contra, Ullman v. Meyer, 10 Fed. 241. 

The ruling in Brick v. Gannar, supra, has been approved in Nearing v. Van Fleet, 151 N. Y. 643, 45 N. E. 1133.

The authorities on this subject are collected in the note in 

L. R. A. 1915D 1190. 

§ 251. Lease of Real Property. 

In New York, it has been held that the statute relating to contracts not to be performed within one year has no application to contracts concerning or relating to land. Hence, a lease for one year to commence at some time in the future need not be in writing. Ward v. Hasbrouck, 169 N. Y. 

407, 62 N. E. 434.

The Real Property Law, section 259, was amended in

May, 1934, in relation to the written authority of agents to contract. See sections 252 and 253, infra. 

§ 252. Contract for Sale of Real Property. 

"A contract for the leasing for a longer period than one year, or for the sale, of any real property, or an interest therein, is void, unless the contract, or some note or memorandum thereof, expressing the consideration, is in writing, subscribed by the lessor or grantor, or by his lawful agent thereunto authorized by writing." Real Property Law, sec. 

259 (1934). This provision was derived from section 4 of the original Statute of Frauds and is similar to provisions contained in the statutes of every state. 

§ 253. Authority of Agent To Deal in Real Property. 

Prior to the 1934 amendment to section 259 of the Real Property Law, it was held that an agent could be orally authorized to execute a written contract for the sale of land or to make a written contract for a lease for more than one year. Worrall v. Munn, 5 N. Y. 229, 55 Am. Dec. 330; 

Brune v. Vom Lehn, 112 Misc. 342, 183 N. Y. S. 360, aff'd 

196 App. Div. 907, 187 N. Y. S. 928. By virtue of section 

242 of the Real Property Law, however, the authorization of an agent to execute a deed or a lease for more than one year was then, as now, required to be in writing. The 

1934 amendment to section 259 requires the agent's authority to execute a contract for the sale of an interest in real property, or to execute a contract for a lease for more than one year, to be in writing. This amendment changes the rule as laid down in Worrall v. Munn, supra, and in 

Brune v. Vom Lehn, supra.

The effect of section 242 and the present section 259 upon the methods of creating an agent's authority may be summarized as follows: a. The authority of an agent to convey or create an interest in real property or to lease real property for more

than one year must be in writing. Real Property Law, sec. 242. b. The authority of an agent to execute a contract for the sale of an interest in real property or to execute a contract for a lease for more than one year must be in writing. 

Real Property Law, sec. 259 (1934). § 254.

Contract for Sale of Interest in Real Property. 

An interest in real property means some substantial interest in the land, an estate which may be conveyed or assigned, and not a remote or inappreciable interest. A contract for services in investigating the title to land and to find a purchaser therefor, does not give rise to an "interest in real property." Heyn v. Phillips, 37 Cal. 529.

But a contract to give a mortgage is a contract for the sale of an "interest in real property" and is, therefore, within the statute. Sleeth v. Sampson, 237 N. Y. 69, 142 N. E. 

355. An agreement between a first and a second mortgagee of the same real property disposing of the proceeds of rents collected by a receiver in foreclosure does not constitute a sale of an interest in real property. Monica Realty Corp. v. 

122 Fifth Avenue Corp., 264 N. Y. 52, 189 N. E. 778. But an assignment of the purchaser's interest under a real property contract is within the statute. Flinner v. McVay, 37 

Mont. 306, 96 Pac. 340, 19 L. R. A. (N.S.) 879 and note. 

§ 255. Sale of Standing Timber. 

Contracts for the sale of standing timber and of bark on standing trees are for the sale of an interest in land. Hirth v. Graham, 50 Ohio St. 57, 33 N. E. 90, 19 L. R. A. 721; 

Green v. Armstrong, 1 Denio (N.Y.) 550; Thomson v. 

Poor, 57 Hun 285, 10 N. Y. S. 597. This has been held to be the rule even though the parties contemplate that the trees shall be immediately severed from the land by the purchaser. Hirth v. Graham, supra. However, in the latter case, there is authority to the effect that such a contract does not involve the sale of an interest in land and is,

therefore, not within the provision of the Statute of Frauds in question. Leonard v. Medford, 85 Md. 666, 37 Atl. 365, 37 L. R. A. 449. Trees, permanent shrubbery, and bushes are a part of the land. Allen v. Oscar G. Murray Railroad 

Employees' Fund, 112 Misc. 156, 182 N. Y. S. 369. 

Contract To Devise Real Property. 

An agreement to devise land involves the transfer of an interest in realty and is, therefore, within the statute. Pond v. Sheean, 132 Ill. 312, 23 N. E. 1018, 8 L. R. A. 414; Lasher v. McDermott, 173 App. Div. 79, 158 N. Y. S. 708, appeal dismissed 219 N. Y. 554, 114 N. E. 1070. For instance, an oral agreement to devise land to another in consideration of services to be rendered to the promisor is void. Ellis v. 

Cary, 74 Wis. 176, 42 N. W. 252, 4 L. R. A. 55. The Real

Property Law, section 259-a (1933), declares that a contract to establish a trust of real property or to make a devise is void unless the contract is in writing and subscribed by the party to be charged therewith. § 257. Licenses and Easements.

A license is merely permission or authority to enter upon or pass over the land of another and is not intended to convey an interest in the land. Therefore, an agreement to give a license is not within the statute. Wiseman v. 

Lucksinger, 84 N. Y. 31, 38 Am. Rep. 479. But an easement in land is an interest therein and, therefore, an agreement to create an easement is within the statute and must be proved by a written instrument. Nellis v. Munson, 108 

N. Y. 453, 15 N. E. 739.

In some states, however, the statute is confined to estates in land and is held not to include agreements to create easements. Warner v. Texas & Pacific Ry. Co., 164 U. S. 418, 41 Law. Ed. 495, 17 Sup. Ct. Rep. 147.

§ 258. 

Contract for Sale of Building To Be Removed. 

A contract for the sale of a building to be demolished and removed from the land of the vendor is not a sale of an interest in real property. The contract operates as a constructive severance of the building from the land, and the building is thereafter regarded as personal property. Melton v. Fullerton-Weaver Realty Co., 214 N. Y. 571, 108 

N. E. 849. In Long v. White, 42 Ohio St. 59, the Court said: "In applying the statute of frauds, buildings are not classed with forest trees, but with growing crops, nursery trees, and fixtures attached to realty." 

§ 259. 

Products of the Soil. 

It often becomes an important question whether the products of the soil are real property or personal property, since if they are treated as real property the contract is within the fourth section of the Statute of Frauds, while if considered as personal property the seventeenth section of the statute controls. All growing products of the soil are either: 

1. Fructus industriales, or 2. Fructus naturales. 

In determining whether growing crops are fructus industriales or fructus naturales, the intention of the parties may be taken into account as well as the nature of the transaction. Minneapolis Iron Stove Co. v. Branum, 36 

N. D. 355, 162 N. W. 543, L. R. A. 1917E 298. 

§ 260. Fructus Industriales. 

Annual crops, such as wheat, corn, oats, and potatoes raised by yearly labor and cultivation, are, although unsevered from the soil, to be regarded as personal property and this is true regardless of whether the crops are growing or have matured. Under no circumstances do they constitute an interest in land. For this reason, a sale of a corn crop, although only a few inches high at the time

of making the contract, was held in Northern v. State, 1 Ind. 113, to be a sale of personal property. Therefore, contracts for the sale of growing crops are controlled by the Statute of Frauds as embodied in the Personal Property 

Law, section 85. Green v. Armstrong, 1 Denio (N.Y.) 

550; Sexton v. Breese, 135 N. Y. 387, 32 N. E. 133; First 

National Bank v. Cazort & McGehee Co., 123 Ark. 605, 186 

S. W. 86, L. R. A. 1917C 7. 

§ 261. Fructus Naturales. 

Natural productions of the earth are a part of the land, and a contract for their sale constitutes a sale of an interest in land. Matter of Chamberlain, 140 N. Y. 390, 35 N. E. 

602. To this class belong growing trees, grass, and hops. 

Fruit trees, such as apple and peach trees, do not require annual planting and cultivation. The same is true of grapes and hops. Although they are cultivated, yet cultivation is not necessary to their perennial production. Ross v. Cook, 71 Kan. 117, 80 Pac. 38. Hops were considered as personal property in Frank v. Harrington, 36 Barb. 415, on the ground that they are produced by annual cultivation and by the industry of the owner. But since annual cultivation, though quite advisable to obtain a large yield, is unnecessary to produce the crop, the correctness of the decision seems questionable unless all distinctions between fructus industriales and fructus naturales are to be disregarded. The hop roots when rooted in the ground are a part of the real estate. Webster v. Zielly, 52 Barb. 482.

Blackberry bushes are perennial and from one planting yield successive crops. They grow wild, but like every other kind of fruit or berry are improved by cultivation. quantity and quality of the yield are largely dependent upon the amount of annual care expended upon them. Blackberries while growing on the bushes have been held not to be personal property. Sparrow v. Pond, 49 Minn. 412, 52 N. W. 36, 16 L. R. A. 103. Although the authorities are in conflict, the weight of authority is that fruit grow- The

ing on trees constitutes personal property and not real property. Simmons v. Williford, 60 Fla. 359, 53 So. 452, L. R. A. 1912C 736; Sparrow v. Pond, supra. By analogy it would seem that if a building which is a part of the realty becomes personalty when sold to be removed by the vendee, fruit on trees or bushes when sold to be picked by the purchaser should also be treated as personal property. Melton v. Fullerton-Weaver Realty Co., 214 N. Y. 571, 108 N. E. 849. 

§ 262. Parol Reservation of Crops. 

A reservation of growing crops in favor of a stranger to a conveyance of land may be established by parol although there is no reservation clause in the deed, and this, too, notwithstanding the rule of the common law that growing crops pass by a conveyance of the realty as appurtenant to the land, while the natural products of the soil must be reserved by a written agreement. In New York, however, a parol reservation of growing crops in favor of the grantor cannot be shown as against the grantee. Easterday, Law of Real Property I, sec. 27; Austin v. Sawyer, 9 Cowen (N.Y.) 39; Beck v. McLane, 129 App. Div. 745, 114 N. Y. S. 44, aff'd 198 N. Y. 567, 92 N. E. 1078. 

§ 263. English Statute of Frauds-Section Seventeen. 

The provisions of the English Statute of Frauds, section 

17 (1676), are substantially reenacted in the Uniform Sales 

Act, section 4, and in the Personal Property Law, Sales

Act, section 85. The New York statute provides: 

"1. A contract to sell or a sale of any goods or choses in action of the value of fifty dollars or upwards shall not be enforceable by action unless the buyer shall accept part of the goods or choses in action so contracted to be sold or sold, and actually receive the same, or give something in earnest to bind the contract, or in part payment, or unless some note or memorandum in writing of the contract or

sale be signed by the party to be charged or his agent in that behalf. "2. The provisions of this section apply to every such contract or sale, notwithstanding that the goods may be intended to be delivered at some future time or may not at the time of such contract or sale be actually made, procured, or provided, or fit or ready for delivery, or some act may be requisite for the making or completing thereof, or rendering the same fit for delivery; but if the goods are to be manufactured by the seller especially for the buyer and are not suitable for sale to others in the ordinary course of the seller's business, the provisions of this section shall not apply. "3. There is an acceptance of goods within the meaning of this section when the buyer, either before or after delivery of the goods, expresses by words or conduct his assent to becoming the owner of those specific goods." 

The Personal Property Law, section 31, subdivision 6, provides that: "If goods be sold at public auction, and the auctioneer at the time of the sale, enters in a sale book, a memorandum specifying the nature and price of the property sold, the terms of the sale, the name of the purchaser, and the name of the person on whose account the sale was made, such memorandum is equivalent in effect to a note of the contract or sale, subscribed by the party to be charged therewith."

Therefore, in New York a contract for the sale of goods or choses in action of the value of fifty dollars or more must be in writing, unless the buyer: a. Accepts and receives a part of the goods; b. Pays part of the purchase price; or

C. 

The sale is by auction. $ 264. 

Nature and Purpose of Statute of Frauds.

The purpose of the Statute of Frauds is to guard against not merely the dishonesty of the parties and the perjury of

witnesses but also the misunderstanding and mistakes of honest men. If the contract is reduced to writing, this object is effectually attained. Where this is omitted, the fact that the buyer has paid a part of the price, or the seller has delivered and the buyer has accepted a part or all of the property, furnishes unequivocal evidence of the existence of a contract of some kind between the buyer and the seller, although its terms and provisions must, after all, depend on the recollection of witnesses. Where the agreement is not in writing, the requirements of the statute can be satisfied only by something done, subsequent to the sale, indicating the agreement of the parties. Shindler v. Houston, 1 N. Y. 261, 49 Am. Dec. 316.

At common law, contracts for the sale of goods, regardless of their value, were not required to be in writing and this common law rule still prevails in many states. Odom v. Clark, 146 N. C. 544, 60 S. E. 513. § 265.

Goods Incapable of Present or Part Delivery. 

The statutory provision relating to a "contract to sell or a sale of goods" includes agreements to sell property to be acquired later as well as contracts to sell property which the seller has already acquired. The distinction between a contract to sell and a sale is that in the former no title passes to the buyer at the time of the making of the contract, while in the latter title does pass at such time. While a delivery of a part of the property sold is effective to relieve the contract from the operation of the statute, the courts have refused to hold that the statute applies only to goods of which a part may be delivered. For example, an animal is not susceptible of part delivery, yet the sale of a horse or a cow of the value of fifty dollars or upwards is within the statute. Tisdale v. Harris, 20 Pick. (Mass.) 

9. So, too, a contract for the sale of goods not in esse, or not owned by the seller, and, therefore, incapable of delivery at the time of sale, is within the operation of the statute. R. & L. Co. v. Metz, 175 App. Div. 276, 160

N. Y. S. 145, aff'd 219 N. Y. 556, 114 N. E. 1082; Franklin v. Matoa Gold Mining Co., 158 Fed. 941, 16 L. R. A. (N.S.) 381. § 266.

Subject Matter of Sale. 

The English statute uses the phrase "goods, wares and merchandise." The controversy as to whether a chose in action is within the Statute of Frauds was settled by the enactment of the Personal Property Law, Sales Act, sec tion 85, subdivision 1, the present law in New York, which employs the phrase "goods or choses in action." The term comprehends all kinds of corporeal and incorporeal movable property and, therefore, includes such property as bills, notes, checks, bonds, stocks, assignment of claims, etc. 

Tompkins v. Sheehan, 158 N. Y. 617, 53 N. E. 502. It includes also sales of foreign money when the same is treated as a commodity, as well as transfers of credit between different points by cable. Reisfeld v. Jacobs, 107 Misc. 1, 176 N. Y. S. 223. 

§ 267. 

Contract for Work and Labor. 

A contract for work and labor is not within the operation of the statute. But where the contract is for the sale of goods not in existence at the time the agreement was made but to be thereafter manufactured or produced, it was long a controverted question whether the contract is for work and labor or for "goods, wares and merchandise." 

The controversy resulted in adopting three distinct rules, known as the English, the Massachusetts, and the New York rules, which are fully discussed in Cooke v. Millard, 65 N. Y. 352, 22 Am. Rep. 619. 

The English rule is as follows: If the contract be such that it will result in the sale of a chattel, then it constitutes a sale; but if the work and labor be bestowed in such a manner that the result would not be anything which could properly be said to be the subject of sale, the action is for work and labor. Lee v. Griffin, 1 Best & Smith 272, 121

Eng. Rep. 716. Under the English rule, a contract for the manufacture and sale of a set of false teeth, though made especially for the buyer, is within the statute, but a contract to supply labor and materials in repairing a carriage is not within the statute.

The New York rule prior to the Sales Act of 1911 was that where the substance of the contract is work and labor to be done in converting raw materials into a new and totally different article, although the transaction is to result in a sale, the contract is not within the statute; but if at the time of the agreement the article sold substantially existed in its ultimate form, even though acts remained to be done to finish it, the agreement is a contract for a sale, and is within the statute. Under the former New York rule, the goods sold should exist in substantially their ultimate form at the time of making the contract; otherwise the contract is for work and labor. 

For example, a contract to manufacture paper or to make a tent was held to be one for work and labor. Parsons v. 

Loucks, 48 N. Y. 17, 8 Am. Rep. 517; Higgins v. Murray, 73 N. Y. 252. But a contract for the sale of specific lumber which is to be cut and dressed by the seller, or for the sale of furniture to be covered or finished in a particular way, was held to be within the statute because the articles are in existence as the subject matter of the sale at the time of the formation of the contract. Cooke v. Millard, supra; 

Flint v. Corbitt, 6 Daly (N.Y.) 429.

The Massachusetts rule is that a contract for the sale of articles then existing, or such as the vendor in the ordinary course of his business manufactures or procures for the general market, whether on hand at the time or not, is a contract for the sale of goods, to which the statute applies. 

Goddard v. Binney, 115 Mass. 450, 15 Am. Rep. 112. 

§ 268. Contract for Goods To Be Manufactured-New 

York Statute.

The Massachusetts rule as accepted in New York is em-

bodied in the Personal Property Law, Sales Act, section 85, subdivision 2. The statute provides: "The provisions of this section apply to every such contract or sale, notwithstanding that the goods may be intended to be delivered at some future time or may not at the time of such contract or sale be actually made, procured, or provided, or fit or ready for delivery, or some act may be requisite for the making or completing thereof, or rendering the same fit for delivery; but if the goods are to be manufactured by the seller especially for the buyer and are not suitable for sale to others in the ordinary course of the seller's business, the provisions of this section shall not apply." Under the present statute, a contract of sale is within the operation of the statute even though the articles sold are not in existence at the time of making the contract. If they are to be manufactured thereafter and are suitable for sale to others in the ordinary course of the seller's business, the contract is not for work and labor but is one of sale and, therefore, must be in writing. Goldowitz v. Henry 

Kupfer & Co., 80 Misc. 487, 141 N. Y. S. 531. Under the present statute, a contract to assemble and install a sodawater fountain, with counter, base, and superstructure of particular dimensions and special design is one for work and labor and not for the sale of goods. So, too, a contract for the sale of a suit of clothes to be made to measure out of cloth selected by the customer is not within the Statute of Frauds. The contract is not for the sale of cloth selected, but for a suit to be made especially for the customer according to his size, and which is to be suitable only for him. Davis v. Blanchard, 138 N. Y. S. 202; Schneider v. Lezinsky, 162 N. Y. S. 769. The distinction between the old and the new rule is discussed in Berman Stores Co., Inc. v. Hirsch, 240 N. Y. 209, 148 N. E. 212. A contract to sell goods to be manufactured especially by a third person is within the statute, and not within the exception contained at the end of the section, since the statutory exception is confined to cases where the goods are to be manufactured 1

"by the seller." Eagle Paper Box Co. v. Gatti-McQuade 

Co., 99 Misc. 508, 164 N. Y. S. 201. 

§ 269. Amount or Value. 

To bring the transaction within the provisions of the statute the value of the property must be "fifty dollars or upwards." It should be noted that while section 17 of the original statute used the word "price," the Sales Act uses the word "value." The exact significance of this change in the law is uncertain. Is the value to be determined by the amount which the buyer agrees to pay? If so, there is little or no distinction between the terms.

Where several articles are sold at the same time, though a separate price is placed on each article, the transaction is generally regarded as a single sale, and if the aggregate value is fifty dollars or more, the transaction is brought within the operation of the statute. Allard v. Greasert, 61 

N. Y. 1. But the circumstances may be such as to show an intent to make separate contracts. In Aldrich v. Pyatt, 64 Barb. 391, an agreement for the sale of apples and barley to be delivered at different times and at different prices was held to be composed of separate contracts. So, too, where separate shares of stock of several persons are sold to the same buyer, each sale is a separate contract. 

Tompkins v. Sheehan, 158 N. Y. 617, 53 N. E. 502. 

§ 270. Acceptance and Receipt. 

If the buyer accepts and receives a part of the goods, the contract is taken out of the statute and becomes enforceable even though it is not reduced to writing. 

Acceptance and receipt are distinct terms. There may be receipt without acceptance, or acceptance without receipt. In order to comply with the statute, the buyer must both accept and receive. But the acceptance may be expressed by words or by conduct and may either precede or follow delivery of the goods. Even if the goods tendered fully comply with the oral contract, the buyer is protected

in his refusal to accept them. Personal Property Law, sec. 85; Spirt & Co., Inc. v. Prior, 93 Conn. 639, 107 Atl. 

513; Cross v. O'Donnell, 44 N. Y. 661, 4 Am. Rep. 721; 

Tonkelson v. Malis, 119 Misc. 717, 197 N. Y. S. 309; Kieser & Son Co. v. Hallock, 201 App. Div. 186, 194 N. Y. S. 737.

Acceptance is defined as a manifestation by the purchaser to become the owner of the goods. This is in accord with the authorities both prior and subsequent to the Sales 

Act (1911). Flanigan v. Waterman, 117 Misc. 617, 191 

N. Y. S. 646.

It should be noted that where the goods are in the possession of a third party, there can be no delivery until the third party acknowledges that he holds the goods for the buyer. The Sales Act provides: "Where the goods at the time of sale are in the possession of a third person, the seller has not fulfilled his obligation to deliver to the buyer unless and until such third person acknowledges to the buyer that he holds the goods on the buyer's behalf." Personal Property Law, sec. 124, subd. 3. This principle is well illustrated in bailment cases. Thus, if the bailor, by oral contract, sells an article which is in the possession of a bailee, the purchaser is not protected until he receives notice from the bailee that he holds the article for him. Borenco 

Importing Corp. v. Sperber, 205 App. Div. 417, 199 N. Y. S. 

469; Flanigan v. Waterman, supra.

Delivery of goods subject to an examination is not sufficient to constitute an acceptance. The buyer must have an opportunity to make an examination and pronounce the goods satisfactory or to do some act showing his intention to accept them. Stone v. Browning, 68 N. Y. 598. The receipt by the buyer may be, and often is, for the express purpose of deciding whether he will accept or reject. 

Cooke v. Millard, 65 N. Y. 352, 368, 22 Am. Rep. 619; 

Cleveland Worsted Mills Co. v. J. C. Brownstone & Co., 190 N. Y. S. 601.

§ 271. Mutuality of Acceptance and Receipt. 

The design of the statute is that neither party can create the evidence which shall prove an oral contract as against the other. Both parties must participate in the acceptance and receipt which takes the contract out of the statute. Accordingly, where the property is already in the purchaser's possession, his acts indicative of the transfer of the title cannot alone constitute acceptance and receipt within the meaning of the statute; as where a person already in possession of law books, which he claims to have purchased, pastes his name labels thereon and assumes authority over them as his own. Young v. Ingalsbe, 208 N. Y. 503, 102 

N. E. 590; Broom v. Joselson, 211 App. Div. 157, 206

N. Y. S. 841. Clearly, the return of the goods after a proper acceptance and receipt in no way affects the enforceability of the oral contract. Koster v. Koedding, 34 

Misc. 765, 68 N. Y. S. 794. § 272. 

Time of Acceptance and Receipt.

A delivery and acceptance of any part of the goods which is the subject of an oral contract of sale, and within the Statute of Frauds, at any time subsequent to making the contract and prior to an action to enforce the contract takes it out of the statute and renders it enforceable to the same extent as if reduced to writing when made. McKnight v. 

Dunlop, 5 N. Y. 537, 55 Am. Dec. 370; Jackson v. Tupper, 101 N. Y. 515, 5 N. E. 65.

The rule remains unchanged under the Sales Act. Gorden v. Witty, 198 App. Div. 333, 190 N. Y. S. 381. But acts done after the commencement of an action to enforce the contract are inadmissible to prove an acceptance. 

Drake Hardware Co. v. Dewitt, 142 App. Div. 189, 126 

N. Y. S. 868. 

§ 273. Constructive or Symbolic Acceptance and Receipt. 

The receipt and acceptance may be evidenced by a symbolic act, such as delivery and acceptance of a key, a bill of

lading, or a warehouse receipt. Rodgers v. Phillips, 40 

N. Y. 519; Whitlock v. Hay, 58 N. Y. 484. So, too, with bulky and ponderous articles, it is not required that these should be manually delivered by the seller. Bowe v. Ellis, 3 Misc. 92, 22 N. Y. S. 369.

An acceptance of samples will satisfy the requirements of the statute where the samples are included in the bulk of sale. But if the samples are not treated by both parties as a part of the goods sold, the statute is not satisfied. 

Richardson v. Smith, 101 Md. 15, 60 Atl. 612, 70 L. R. A. 

321 and note; Duncan v. Wohl, South & Co., 201 App. Div. 

737, 195 N. Y. S. 381. 

§ 274. Delivery to Common Carrier. 

Delivery to a common carrier is not sufficient to take the contract out of the statute, even though the vendee designates the carrier, if the vendee gives to the carrier no other authority than to transport the goods. Rodgers v. Phillips, 40 N. Y. 519; Pierson v. Crooks, 115 N. Y. 539, 22 N. E. 

349; Dolan Mercantile Co. v. Marcus, 276 Pa. 404, 120

Atl. 396. But the vendee may give authority to the carrier to both "accept and receive," in which case acceptance and receipt by the carrier satisfy the statute. Cross v. O'Donnell, 44 N. Y. 661, 4 Am. Rep. 721; Wilcox Silver Plate Co. v. Green, 72 N. Y. 17. 

§ 275. When Part Payment May Be Made. 

Prior to the Sales Act, the statute required part payment to be made "at the time" of the making of the contract of sale. The Sales Act changed the law by omitting the words "at the time." Therefore, a part payment made at any time before an action is brought to enforce the contract is sufficient to take it out of the statute. Jackson v. 

Tupper, 101 N. Y. 515, 5 N. E. 65; Widmeyer v. Crane, 121 

Misc. 309, 200 N. Y. S. 875.

§ 276. Payment in Money, Property, or Commercial 

Paper.

It is not necessary that the part payment be made in money. Such part payment may be made in property other than money or in the mere use of property. So, too, the statute applies to barter and exchange as well as to contracts strictly for the sale of goods. Personal Property 

Law, sec. 90, subd. 2; Herrick v. Carter, 56 Barb. 41; W. R. 

Grace & Co. v. Nagle, 275 Fed. 343; Weir v. Hudnut, 115 

Ind. 525, 18 N. E. 24.

Considerable controversy has arisen over cases wherein part payment has been made by means of a negotiable instrument; for example, where the buyer has given to the seller, his, the buyer's, own promissory note in part payment. Such payment has been held insufficient to satisfy the statute for the following reason: "It is but an agreement that a promise of future part payment shall operate as part payment, while the statute requires something more than words; it requires part payment, and not a promise of part payment to be received as payment." 

Krohn v. Bantz, 68 Ind. 277. Accordingly, the fact that the seller has merely received the note of the buyer is not sufficient to constitute part payment. Combs v. Bateman, 10 

Barb. 573. The same is true of the receipt by the seller of the check of the buyer, especially where payment of the check was stopped. Hessberg v. Welsh, 147 N. Y. S. 44.

Even though payment is not stopped, if the payee subsequently returns the check, the statute is not satisfied. Bates v. Dwinnell, 101 Neb. 712, 164 N. W. 722, L. R. A. 1918B 

900. However, it is recognized that the acceptance of the note of a third party in absolute payment is sufficient to satisfy the statute. Combs v. Bateman, supra. So, also, authority is not wanting to the effect that the check of the buyer himself may be received in absolute payment if such is the agreement of the parties; in which case the statute is satisfied. Bates v. Dwinnell, supra; Groomer v. McMillan, 143 Mo. App. 612, 128 S. W. 285. But the burden of

proof rests upon the buyer to show clearly that the seller agreed to receive the check in absolute payment. Groomer v. McMillan, supra. 

§ 277. Giving Something in Earnest. 

The giving of something "in earnest" which is derived from the civil law means merely a part payment of the purchase price. De Waal v. Jamison, 176 App. Div. 756, 163 

N. Y. S. 1045, aff'd 226 N. Y. 644, 123 N. E. 862; Wenger v. Grummel, 136 Mo. 80, 110 Atl. 206. § 278. 

Auction Sales.

The usual memorandum made in a sales book by the auctioneer, or under his direction, of goods sold at public auction is equivalent to a memorandum of a contract or sale subscribed by the party to be charged therewith. The sales book entry must disclose: (a) the nature of the property, (b) the price, (c) the terms of sale, (d) the name of the purchaser, and (e) the name of the seller. Personal Property Law, sec. 31, subd. 6; Meyer v. Redmond, 205 N. Y. 

478, 98 N. E. 906, 41 L. R. A. (N.S.) 675.

NOTE OR MEMORANDUM § 279. Form Required. 

No particular form of note or memorandum is required to satisfy the Statute of Frauds. Moreover, the memorandum may consist of several writings. Telegrams, or letters and telegrams, are sufficient if, when taken together, they disclose all the necessary elements of the contract. 

Trevor v. Wood, 36 N. Y. 307, 93 Am. Dec. 511; Peck v. 

Vandermark, 99 N. Y. 30, 1 N. E. 41; Brewer v. Horst- 

Lachmund Co., 127 Cal. 643, 60 Pac. 418, 50 L. R. A. 240 and note. A case illustrating the insufficiency of letters to constitute a contract within the statute is Poel v. Brunswick-Balke-Collender Co., 216 N. Y. 310, 110 N. E. 619.

Where the contract consists of several writings, telegrams,

letters, etc., they must not only contain the substance of a complete agreement but must also refer to each other. 

Parol evidence is not admissible to connect the various writings or to supply any of the material terms of the contract. 

Thus, the contents of an unsigned paper must, in order that it may become a part of the memorandum, be referred to by a paper which is signed by the party sought to be charged therewith. Furthermore, the writings must show on their face the nature of the entire agreement. Editorial, New York Law Journal, August 9, 1927.

If a material term of the agreement is omitted or left for future determination as, for instance, the amount to be paid, the memorandum is insufficient. Ansorge v. Kane, 244 N. Y. 395, 155 N. E. 683; Brauer v. Oceanic Steam 

Navigation Co., 178 N. Y. 339, 70 N. E. 863; Wilson v. 

Lewiston Mill Co., 150 N. Y. 314, 44 N. E. 959; Marks v. 

Cowdin, 226 N. Y. 138, 123 N. E. 139; Editorial, New 

York Law Journal, June 7, 1928. 

Sufficiency of Memorandum. § 280.

The memorandum must contain: a. A description of the subject matter of the contract so that, if not specifically named, it may be identified. Tallman v. Franklin, 14 N. Y. 584; Evans v. Pelta, 146 App. 

Div. 749, 131 N. Y. S. 411. A memorandum for the sale of "all the paper in rolls now stored at the warehouse at 405 East Tenth Street, New York City" is sufficient description of the property sold. Northeastern Paper Co. v. Concord 

Paper Co., 214 App. Div. 537, 212 N. Y. S. 318, aff'd 242 

N. Y. 562, 152 N. E. 428. So, too, evidence of a trade name or usage is admissible to identify the subject matter. 

Franklin Sugar Refining Co. v. Lipowicz, 247 N. Y. 465, 160 N. E. 916; Brewer v. Horst-Lachmund Co., 127 Cal. 

643, 60 Pac. 418, 50 L. R. A. 240. b. The names of the parties or a description of them. 

Mentz v. Newwitter, 122 N. Y. 491, 25 N. E. 1044, 11 L. R.

A. 97; Irvmor Corp. v. Rodewald, 253 N. Y. 472, 171 N. E. 747.

C. A statement of the consideration. The note or memorandum of the agreement must expressly, or by implication, state the consideration. It should be observed that it is the plaintiff who furnishes the consideration, and, therefore, what the plaintiff did, or what he promised to do, in return for the defendant's promise must appear in the memorandum. Seymour v. Warren, 179 N. Y. 1, 71 N. E. 

260; Standard Oil Co. of New York v. Koch, 260 N. Y. 150, 183 N. E. 278. Under the Real Property Law, sections 258 and 259, the consideration must be expressed. d. The signature required by statute. In contracts involving the sale of personal property of the value of fifty dollars or more, the statute requires the memorandum to be signed by the party to be charged. It follows, therefore, that when the memorandum is signed by only one of the parties it is not enforceable against the one who did not sign. Thus, an action cannot be maintained on a contract by the seller against the purchaser of goods where the seller signed the memorandum but the purchaser did not. Levitt v. Sliman, 137 N. Y. S. 904; Houser v. Hobart, 22 Idaho 

735, 127 Pac. 997, 43 L. R. A. (N.S.) 410. Since the memorandum is not the contract but only evidence of it, the signing of the memorandum by only one of the parties is not evidence of lack of mutuality. There may be mutuality of agreement without mutuality as to the remedy or evidence of the agreement. The statute presupposes a contract by parol.

In contracts involving the leasing of real property for more than one year, or the sale of real property, the New York statute expressly requires merely a subscribing by the grantor or lessor, or by his agent. There is no requirement that the buyer or lessee must sign. Real Property Law, sec. 259; Brune v. Vom Lehn, 112 Misc. 342, 183 N. Y. S. 

360, aff'd 196 App. Div. 907, 187 N. Y. S. 928. Although

the statute requires the note or memorandum to be subscribed only by the grantor or lessor, or by his agent, the defense of non-compliance with the Statute of Frauds is available to both parties. And, although the note or memorandum is subscribed by the grantor or lessor, or by his agent, it becomes enforceable only when the grantee or lessee has in some manner accepted it as evidence of a binding agreement. 300 West End Avenue Corp. v. 

Warner, 250 N. Y. 221, 165 N. E. 271. So, also, in Kittel v. Stueve, 10 Misc. 696, 31 N. Y. S. 821, aff'd without opinion 146 N. Y. 380, 41 N. E. 89, it is held to be unnecessary to the validity of a contract for the sale of real estate that the buyer sign.

Contracts within the fourth section of the English Statute of Frauds, now embodied in the Personal Property Law, section 31, and Real Property Law, section 259, must be "subscribed." Contracts within the Decedent Estate Law, section 113, and the seventeenth section of the English Statute of Frauds, now contained in the Personal Property 

Law, Sales Act, section 85, may be "signed" instead of "subscribed." To subscribe a writing, the signature must be written at the physical end thereof. James v. Patten, 6 N. Y. 9, 55 Am. Dec. 376; Coe v. Tough, 116 N. Y. 273, 22 N. E. 550. In "signing" an instrument the signature may be placed "either at the top, in the middle, or at the bottom," provided it is placed thereon by the party himself or by his agent for the purpose of authenticating the instrument. Clason v. Bailey, 14 Johns. (N.Y.) 484; 

Doughty v. Manhattan Brass Co., 101 N. Y. 644, 4 N. E. 

747. The "signing" may be done by the adoption of a printed signature. Goldowitz v. Henry Kupfer & Co., 80 

Misc. 487, 141 N. Y. S. 531. But a printed signature is insufficient to constitute a subscription. Subscribing means a manual writing of one's name. Vielie v Osgood, 8 Barb. 130.

Whether a signature on any part of a memorandum other than when the signature is subscribed thereto is sufficient

to constitute a signing has been a controversial question with the courts from early times. The quite recent case of 

Dorian Holding & Trading Corp. v. Brunswick Terminal & 

Railway Securities Co., 230 App. Div. 514, 245 N. Y. S. 

410, aff'd 256 N. Y. 674, 177 N. E. 188, has again renewed a discussion of the question in many legal periodicals. In this case, the plaintiff, who was the president of the defendant corporation, sued the defendant for breach of an alleged contract whereby the defendant agreed to sell to the plaintiff a certain number of shares of the defendant's capital stock. The question litigated was whether a letter written and signed by the plaintiff and addressed to the defendant constituted a memorandum sufficient to satisfy the Statute of Frauds. Nowhere in the letter, except in the address, did the defendant's name appear; and the defendant in no way adopted the address in the letter as its signature. The Court held that the letter lacked the signature of the defendant, the party sought to be charged, and, hence, that the letter was insufficient to meet the requirements of the Statute of Frauds.

For an exhaustive study of the sufficiency of the signature under the Statute of Frauds, see Editorial, New York 

Law Journal, April 24, 1930; also, article in 9 New York 

University Law Quarterly Review 87, reprinted, editorial page, New York Law Journal, October 24, 1931. 

§ 281. Illustration of Brevity of Form. 

The following instrument was held to be sufficient to satisfy the Statute of Frauds: "Setauket, N. Y., Feb. 14, 1901. "Received from Thomas N. Bayles two hundred and twenty-five dollars for locust at Oakwood. 

Selah B. Strong." 

Bayles v. Strong, 104 App. Div. 153, 93 N. Y. S. 346, aff'd 

185 N. Y. 582, 78 N. E. 1099.

§ 282. When Agreement May Be Signed. 

The agreement may be signed at any time after the contract is concluded, provided that it is signed before an action to enforce it is brought. Mentz v. Newwitter, 122 

N. Y. 491, 25 N. E. 1044, 11 L. R. A. 97; Bird v. Munroe, 66 Me. 337, 22 Am. Rep. 571. 

§ 283. Effect of Non-Compliance with Statute. 

A failure to comply with the statute does not render the contract void, but merely unenforceable. The statute enacts a rule of evidence and requires that the contract, if it is within the statute, be proved by a writing. Witnesses cannot take the place of the note or memorandum. Warth v. Kastriner, 120 App. Div. 480, 104 N. Y. S. 1056; Bird v. Munroe, 66 Me. 337, 22 Am. Rep. 571.

As a rule, where a statute expressly declares a contract to be "void," it is void for all intents and purposes. But since our statutes requiring certain contracts to be reduced to writing were taken from the English Statute of Frauds which provides that certain oral contracts are merely unenforceable, our courts now hold that notwithstanding the express declarations of our statutes rendering certain oral contracts void they are merely unenforceable. 

Whether an oral agreement which is within the Statute of Frauds is merely "unenforceable" or "void" was a question of much controversy in the earlier cases in New York. 

In Dung v. Parker, 52 N. Y. 494, the Court said: "A contract void by the statute is void for all purposes. It confers no right and creates no obligation as between the parties to it; and no claim can be founded upon it as against third persons." But in Crane v. Powell, 139 N. Y. 379, 34 N. E. 

911, the Court held that verbal contracts within the Statute of Frauds may be enforced if the defendant does not specifically plead the statute. The Court said: "The Statute of Frauds does not prohibit the making of any agreement in any way that the parties may see fit nor render them illegal or immoral if not made in some particular way. It sim-

ply requires that certain agreements must be proved by a writing. It introduced a new rule of evidence in certain cases without condemning as illegal any contract that was legal before." See, also, Bean v. Flint, 204 N. Y. 153, 162, 97 N. E. 490. Likewise, in Banta v. Banta, 84 App. 

Div. 138, 82 N. Y. S. 113, it was held to be error, where the

Statute of Frauds was not pleaded as a defense, to dismiss the complaint on the ground that the contract was unenforceable by reason of the statute. Therefore, it may be said that, notwithstanding the use of the term "void" in the statute, an oral contract within the statute is not void but is merely unenforceable at the election of the party against whom the plaintiff seeks to enforce it. Sanger v. 

French, 157 N. Y. 213, 51 N. E. 979. 

§ 284. Pleading Statute as Defense. 

Where one seeks to enforce a contract which is within the Statute of Frauds, he need not allege in his complaint a compliance with the statute, but if the defendant desires to avail himself of the benefit of the statute, he must plead it as a defense. Where the complaint sets out a contract void under the Statute of Frauds, the defendant may either demur or answer. If he answers, expressly pleading the statute, he may move for judgment on the pleadings at the opening of the trial. Seamans v. Barensteen, 180 N. Y. 

333, 73 N. E. 42. But where the complaint does not disclose whether the contract is oral or written, the defendant must plead the statute in order to avail himself of the objection. If the objection is not taken by demurrer or answer, the defendant will be deemed to have waived it. 

Crane v. Powell, 139 N. Y. 379, 34 N. E. 911; Matthews v. Matthews, 154 N. Y. 288, 48 N. E. 531. Under the Civil 

Practice Act, section 277, the demurrer is abolished. Where the invalidity or illegality of a contract sued upon appears upon the face of the complaint, a defense of the Statute of Frauds may now be raised by motion under the Civil Practice Act, section 476, for judgment on the pleadings. Har-

mon v. Peats Co., 216 App. Div. 368, 214 N. Y. S. 353.

Subject to this limitation, the law as stated in this paragraph remains unchanged. 

§ 285. Part Performance of Contracts Relating to Land. 

A part performance of an oral contract for the conveyance of land, void by the Statute of Frauds, may be sufficient to enable the party who has so performed to obtain relief in a court of equity by a decree of specific performance. But payment of the purchase money is not such performance as will enable the buyer to obtain specific performance in equity, since a recovery of the money so paid in an action at law will fully indemnify the party by whom the purchase price was paid. Where, however, the purchaser, relying upon the oral contract, takes possession and makes improvements of a permanent and substantial nature, any relief obtainable in a court of law is deemed inadequate, and he is entitled to enforce the contract in equity. McKinley v. Hessen, 202 N. Y. 24, 95 N. E. 32; 

Milholland v. Payne, 169 App. Div. 712, 155 N. Y. S. 773; aff'd 218 N. Y. 675, 113 N. E. 1061; Burns v. McCormick, 233 N. Y. 230, 135 N. E. 273.

CHAPTER XIV  § 286. 

Limits of Contractual Relation. 

The limits of the contractual relation embrace three points for consideration: 

1. The power to impose liabilities or to confer rights upon persons not parties to the contract; 

2. The assignment of rights and liabilities arising under a contract; 

3. The operation and effect of joint and several contractual obligations.

IMPOSING LIABILITIES UPON THIRD PERSONS § 287. General Rule. 

As one of the essential elements of a contract is consent, it necessarily follows that contractual obligations cannot be imposed upon a person without his consent. For example, A cannot make B his debtor, by paying B's debts without his consent. Nor will the delivery of goods or the rendering of services by a third party impose upon the party benefited thereby an obligation to pay for them, where there was no intention to enter into legal relations with the party furnishing them. Boston Ice Co. v. Potter, 123 Mass. 

28, 25 Am. Rep. 9; Noble v. Williams, 150 Ky. 439, 150 

S. W. 507, 42 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1177; Bartholomew v. Jackson, 20 Johns. (N.Y.) 28, 11 Am. Dec. 237. § 288.

Malicious Interference with Contractual Obligation. 

While a contract cannot impose a contractual obligation upon third persons to perform the contract, yet it does impose a duty upon third persons not to interfere with its performance. This duty is imposed upon all the world. 

Therefore, as a rule, where one maliciously interferes with 247

a contract between two parties and induces one of them to break his contract to the injury of the other, an actionable wrong is committed. This doctrine is supported by the English authorities and by the weight of American authority. Lumley v. Gye, 2 El. & Bl. 216, 118 Eng. Rep. 

749; Wheeler-Stenzel Co. v. American Window Glass Co., 202 Mass. 471, 89 N. E. 28, L. R. A. 1915F 1076 and note; 

Westinghouse Electric & Manufacturing Co. v. Diamond 

State Fibre Co., 268 Fed. 121. 

§ 289. Malicious Interference with Contract-New York 

Rule.

The New York rule formerly was that an action would not lie against one who caused a breach of contract, unless he acted fraudulently or was guilty of misrepresentations, and these false representations induced the party in default to act against his will or contrary to his purpose by coercion or deception. Ashley v. Dixon, 48 N. Y. 430, 8 Am. 

Rep. 559; Rice v. Manley, 66 N. Y. 82, 23 Am. Rep. 30.

However, the New York rule was brought in line with the great weight of authority by the Court of Appeals in Lamb v. S. Cheney & Son, 227 N. Y. 418, 125 N. E. 817, wherein the Court approved the rule that if one maliciously interferes with a contract between two parties and induces one of them to break that contract to the injury of the other, the party so injured can maintain an action against the wrongdoer. This doctrine is not limited to contracts relating to services but is a general rule applicable to all contracts except contracts to marry. Campbell v. Gates, 236 N. Y. 457, 141 N. E. 914. The word "malicious" as used in this connection does not necessarily mean actual malice or ill will. A person is guilty of maliciously inducing a breach of contract where, having knowledge of an existing contract between others, he intentionally, knowingly, and without justification or excuse induces one of the parties to the contract to breach it to the damage of the

other. Lamb v. S. Cheney & Son, supra; Hornstein v. Podwitz, 254 N. Y. 443, 173 N. E. 674. 

§ 290. Inducing Breach of Marriage Contract. 

There is no liability on the part of a third person for inducing the breach of a marriage contract, even though the act was done maliciously and wrongfully and accomplished by means of threats and false representations. For example, a man has no right of action against a third person for maliciously debauching and seducing his fiancee, alienating her affections, and interfering with the contract to marry. The modern doctrine of malicious interference with contractual relations has not been extended to this class of cases. Davis v. Condit, 124 Minn. 365, 144 N. W. 

1089, 50 L. R. A. (N.S.) 142; Homan v. Hall, 102 Neb. 

70, 165 N. W. 881, L. R. A. 1918C 1195; Ryther v. Lefferts, 232 App. Div. 552, 250 N. Y. S. 699. § 291.

Inducing Breach of Employment Contracts. 

By the weight of authority, an action will lie against one who maliciously causes a breach of an employment contract. 

Hitchman Coal & Coke Co. v. Mitchell, 245 U. S. 229, 62 

Law. Ed. 260, 38 Sup. Ct. Rep. 65; Old Dominion S. S. Co. v. McKenna, 30 Fed. 48, 18 Abb. N. C. 262. Contra, Bourlier v. Macauley, 91 Ky. 135, 15 S. W. 60, 11 L. R. A. 550.

Formerly in New York, to constitute a cause of action in this class of cases, it was requisite that the defendant act not only maliciously but also that he use fraud or duress in bringing about the breach of contract. National Protective Assn. v. Cumming, 170 N. Y. 315, 63 N. E. 369; 

Park & Sons Co. v. National Druggists' Assn., 175 N. Y. 1, 67 N. E. 136, 62 L. R. A. 632, 644; De Jong v. B. G. Behrman Co., 148 App. Div. 37, 131 N. Y. S. 1083. But the rule in New York is now in harmony with the weight of authority. Posner Co. v. Jackson, 223 N. Y. 325, 119 N. E. 573.

CONFERRING RIGHTS ON THIRD PERSONS 

§ 292. 

General Rule.

As a general rule a person cannot acquire rights under a contract to which he is not a party. This rule is strictly followed in England. Dunlop Pneumatic Tyre Co., Ltd. v. 

Selfridge & Co., Ltd., App. Cas. (Eng.) 847 (1915). § 293. 

American Doctrine.

In most states, a third person may, under certain circumstances, acquire rights under a contract to which he is not a party. Seaver v. Ransom, 224 N. Y. 233, 120 

N. E. 639, 2 A. L. R. 1187; Hamilton v. Wheeling Public 

Service Co., 88 W. Va. 573, 107 S. E. 401, 21 A. L. R. 433. § 294. 

Massachusetts Rule. 

Massachusetts follows the English rule. In Exchange 

Bank v. Rice, 107 Mass. 37, 9 Am. Rep. 1, the Court said: "The general rule of law is, that a person who is not a party to a simple contract, and from whom no consideration moves, cannot sue on the contract, and consequently that a promise made by one person to another, for the benefit of a third person, who is a stranger to the consideration, will not support an action by the latter." Borden v. Boardman, 157 Mass. 410, 32 N. E. 469; Codman v. Deland, 231 

Mass. 344, 121 N. E. 14.

The same doctrine, with some difference as to the exceptions, prevails in Connecticut, Georgia, Michigan, New 

Hampshire, North Carolina, and Pennsylvania. Second 

National Bank v. Missouri Grand Lodge, Free & Accepted 

Masons, 98 U. S. 123, 25 Law. Ed. 75; Adams v. Kuehn, 119 Pa. St. 76, 13 Atl. 184. In some states, the matter is controlled by statute. American Soda Fountain Co. v. 

Hogue, 17 N. D. 375, 116 N. W. 339, 17 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1113.

§ 295. 

New York Rule.

The New York rule, which is followed by most of the states, is that a third person not a party to a contract made by others for his benefit may enforce the contract subject to the following limitations: a. The contracting parties must have intended that the third person should receive the benefit of the contract. b. The promisee, at the time the contract was made, must have been under an obligation to the person for whose benefit the promise was made.

The leading case on the right of a third person to sue on a contract made for his benefit is Lawrence v. Fox, 20 

N. Y. 268. In this case, one Holly lent Fox $300, stating at the time that he (Holly) owed Lawrence that same amount for money borrowed of him (Lawrence), and had agreed to pay it to him (Lawrence) the next day. Fox accordingly promised Holly to pay his (Holly's) debt to Lawrence at that time. Lawrence, who did not know of the agreement when made, upon learning the facts, sued Fox for the money, the original debtor, Holly, having failed. 

The defendant, Fox, moved for a non-suit on the ground, among others, that Lawrence was not a party to the contract; that his only obligation was to Holly; and that there was no privity between the plaintiff and the defendant. 

The Court ruled that Lawrence could maintain the action and held, subject to the two limitations above named, that neither privity of contract between the promisor and the party claiming the benefit nor consideration moving from the third person is essential to support an action. But a mere stranger to a contract between others, in which one of the parties promises to do something for the benefit of such stranger, there being no consideration moving from the stranger and no duty or obligation owing to him on the part of the promisee, cannot recover upon it. Thus, a mere direction by A to B to deliver to C money or property belonging to A in B's possession gives no right of action to C. Lawrence v. Fox, supra.

Discussing the limitations upon the doctrine of Lawrence v. Fox, supra, the Court in Vrooman v. Turner, 69 

N. Y. 280, 25 Am.
