Rep. 195, said: "To give a third party who may derive a benefit from the performance of the promise, an action, there must be, first, an intent by the promissee to secure some benefit to the third party, and second, some privity between the two, the promissee and the party to be benefited, and some obligation or duty owing from the former to the latter which would give him a legal or equitable claim to the benefit of the promise, or an equivalent from him personally. "It is true there need be no privity between the promissor and the party claiming the benefit of the undertaking, neither is it necessary that the latter should be privy to the consideration of the promise, but it does not follow that a mere volunteer can avail himself of it. A legal obligation or duty of the promissee to him, will so connect him with the transaction as to be a substitute for any privity with the promissor, or the consideration of the promise, the obligation of the promissee furnishing an evidence of the intent of the latter to benefit him, and creating a privity by substitution with the promissor. A mere stranger cannot intervene, and claim by action the benefit of a contract between other parties. There must be either a new consideration or some prior right or claim against one of the contracting parties, by which he has a legal interest in the performance of the agreement." § 296.

Necessity of Intent To Benefit Third Party. 

To give a third party a right of action, it must appear that the contract was made for his benefit. "It is not sufficient that the performance of the promise may benefit the third person. It must have been entered into for his benefit, or at least such benefit must be the direct result of performance, and so within the contemplation of the parties." Durnherr v. Rau, 135 N. Y. 219, 32 N. E. 49.

The right of a property owner to maintain an action

against a water company for failure to supply sufficient water for fire purposes, as required by its contract with the municipality, is denied by the great weight of authority. 

The interest of the property owner is regarded as indirect and his benefit as only incidental and this, too, notwithstanding the fact that the compensation for the water supply is furnished through special property taxes, by the individual property owners. German Alliance Insurance

Co. v. Home Water Supply Co., 226 U. S. 220, 57 Law. Ed. 

129, 33 Sup. Ct. Rep. 32; Moch Co. v. Rensselaer Water Co., 247 N. Y. 160, 159 N. E. 896, 62 A. L. R. 1199.

Although a contract between a municipal corporation and a water company for an adequate water supply for the extinguishment of fires is not for the direct benefit of the property owner, yet a contract between such parties, fixing the rate at which consumers shall be supplied, is for the benefit of the consumer, and he may sue to enjoin the collection of a higher rate than that stipulated in the contract between the company and the city. Farnsworth v. 

Boro Oil & Gas Co., 216 N. Y. 40, 109 N. E. 860; Matter of International R. R. Co. v. Rann, 224 N. Y. 83, 120 N. E. 

153; Smyth v. City of New York, 203 N. Y. 106, 96 N. E. 

409. Moreover, he may compel the water company to furnish the water at the contract rate by means of mandamus proceedings. Baker v. New York Interurban Water Co., 113 Misc. 459, 184 N. Y. S. 833. § 297. Necessity of Duty Owing by Promisee to Third Party. 

The second limitation placed on the rule giving a third party a right to enforce a contract made for his benefit is that the relation of.debtor and creditor must exist between the promisee and the third party, or there must be some other duty owing by the promisee to such third party. 

Durnherr v. Rau, 135 N. Y. 219, 32 N. E. 49; Wait v. Wilson, 86 App. Div. 485, 83 N. Y. S. 834.

The difficulty in applying this limitation arises where

there is apparently no legal obligation but only a duty owing by the promisee to the beneficiary. What must be the nature of this duty? Is a moral duty sufficient to satisfy the requirements of the New York rule? Undoubtedly, certain family relationships may be relied upon as furnishing the duty or obligation which must exist between the promisee and the beneficiary. Todd v. Weber, 95 N. Y. 181, 47 Am. Rep. 20. In Buchanan v. Tilden, 158 

N. Y. 109, 52 N. E. 724, 44 L. R. A. 170, the Court held that the moral duty resting on a husband to make provision for the future support of his wife was sufficient to give the wife a right of action on a contract made between her husband and a third person, whereby the third person promised to pay her a certain sum in the event of his success in contesting a will. De Cicco v. Schweizer, 221 N. Y. 

431, 117 N. E. 807, L. R. A. 1918E 1004. 

§ 298. Recapitulation-Right of Beneficiary To Sue. 

The right of a beneficiary to sue on contracts made for his benefit is confined, according to the case of Seaver v. 

Ransom, 224 N. Y. 233, 120 N. E. 639, 2 A. L. R. 1187: a. "To cases where there is a pecuniary obligation running from the promisee to the beneficiary; b. "To cases where the contract is made for the benefit of the wife, affianced wife, or child of a party to the contract;

C. "To public contract cases where the municipality seeks to protect its inhabitants by covenants for their benefit; d. "To cases where, at the request of a party to the contract, the promise runs directly to the beneficiary although he does not furnish the consideration."

This doctrine is illustrative of the progress of the ideals of equity in modifying the rigid rules of the common law as promulgated in the earlier cases. But that the question is still a matter of considerable uncertainty is evident from

the fact that the Court, in Seaver v. Ransom, supra, after apparently confining the doctrine in question to the above four classes of cases, quoted without disapproval the following declaration of the Court in Wright v. Glen Telephone Co., 48 Misc. 192, 95 N. Y. S. 101, aff'd 112 App. 

Div. 745, 99 N. Y. S. 85: "The right of a third person to recover upon a contract made by other parties for his benefit must rest upon the peculiar circumstances of each case rather than upon the law of some other case." 

§ 299. Applications of the Rule. 

Mortgages. The rule finds frequent application in cases where a landowner, who is liable to a mortgagee of the land for the mortgage debt, sells the land, and the grantee assumes and agrees to pay the mortgage. It is held that the mortgagee may sue the new owner on the promise made for his benefit. Burr v. Beers, 24 N. Y. 178; Wager v. 

Link, 134 N. Y. 122, 31 N. E. 213. But if the landowner is not personally liable to the mortgagee, the grantee is not liable on his promise, because the grantor, in such a case, does not owe the mortgagee any duty or obligation. 

Vrooman v. Turner, 69 N. Y. 280, 25 Am. Rep. 195. 

Partnership. Where an incoming partner promises an outgoing partner, whose interest he has purchased, to assume the existing partnership debts, the creditors of the old firm may sue on the promise. Claflin v. Ostrom, 54 

N. Y. 581; Hannigan v. Allen, 127 N. Y. 639, 27 N. E. 402.

But a promise to pay one-half, or other fractional part, of the debts cannot be enforced, because no single creditor can show that it was made for his benefit. Wheat v. Rice, 97 N. Y. 296; Serviss v. McDonnell, 107 N. Y. 260, 14 

N. E. 314.

Surety bond. In a number of cases, laborers or materialmen have been permitted to bring suit to enforce surety bonds given to the employer or owner, on the theory that such laborer or materialman is a third party beneficiary entitled to sue. Forburger Stone Co. v. Lion Bonding &

Surety Co., 103 Neb. 202, 170 N. W. 897; Knight & Jillson 

Co. v. Castle, 172 Ind. 97, 87 N. E. 976, 27 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

573 and note. And, in Strong v. American Fence Construction Co., 245 N. Y. 48, 156 N. E. 92, where a contractor failed to fulfill his promise to give a bond required by a federal statute, it was held that the laborer or materialman could maintain an action to recover damages for the breach, in an amount not exceeding the pro rata share that would have been his had the bond been given. 

However, in Fosmire v. National Surety Co., 229 N. Y. 

44, 127 N. E. 472, where a surety company furnished for a highway contractor a bond to the people of the state guaranteeing the payment of the wages of the laborers employed by the contractor, it was held that such a laborer could not sue the surety on such undertaking. In this case, the plaintiff was not the sole beneficiary and the primary purpose of the bond was the protection of the state, not the plaintiff. The Court observed that the conclusion might have been different had the state or municipality been plaintiff. "What the defendant's liability would be if the action were prosecuted by the people, we need not now determine." Fosmire v. National Surety Co., supra. The question expressly reserved by the Court was answered in Johnson Service Co. v. E. H. Monin, Inc., 253 

N. Y. 417, 171 N. E. 692, 77 A. L. R. 214, where it was held that a municipality, the promisee, may sue in equity to enforce a surety bond, although the proceeds of the judgment will be held in trust for the benefit of the materialman. The promisee, however, cannot be compelled to maintain a suit for the benefit of the sub-contractor or material- William S. Van Clief & Sons, Inc. v. City of New 

York, et al., 141 Misc. 216, 252 N. Y. S. 402.

Agent of undisclosed principal. Contracts made by an agent for an undisclosed principal are to be distinguished from contracts made for the benefit of a third person. An agent is the representative of the principal, and the promise of the other party, in reality, is made to him. Carter v.

Southern R. R. Co., 111 Ga. 38, 36 S. E. 308, 50 L. R. A. 354.

Assignments. The rule that one who is not a party to a contract may maintain a suit thereon only when it is made for his benefit does not apply to prevent suit by the assignee of an assignable contract. Merritt v. Booklover's 

Library, 89 App. Div. 454, 85 N. Y. S. 797. In such cases, the assignee asserts that the right to sue which resided in the assignor has been transferred to him. A third party beneficiary asserts a right in his own favor arising at the inception of the contract. See Chapter XV, infra. § 300. 

Sealed Instruments.

In many jurisdictions, a third party beneficiary can sue on a simple contract but not on a contract under seal. See cases collected in 25 L. R. A. 273. However, in New York and in many other states, the right of a third party to sue on a contract made for his benefit applies to promises under seal, as well as to simple contracts. Henricus v. 

Englert, 137 N. Y. 488, 33 N. E. 550; Jefferson v. Asch, 53 Minn. 446, 55 N. W. 604, 25 L. R. A. 257 and note. 

§ 301. Rescission by Parties to Contract. 

After the third party beneficiary has adopted or has acted in some manner upon the contract made for his benefit, the promisee and promisor cannot rescind the contract to the prejudice of the rights of the third party beneficiary. Gifford v. Corrigan, 117 N. Y. 257, 22 N. E. 756, 6 L. R. A. 610. But it may be avoided at any time before the third party elects to look exclusively to the promisor. 

Wood v. Moriarity, 15 R. I. 518, 9 Atl. 427. 

§ 302. Right of Promisee against Promisor. 

In mortgage cases, the promisee has been denied the right to sue on the promise made to him for the benefit of another. Thus, where the mortgagor sold real property and the grantee assumed and agreed to pay the mortgage thereon, it was held that the promisee could not sue on

the promise made to him for the benefit of the mortgagee. 

Williston on Contracts, Vol. I, sec. 390; Ayers v. Dixon, 78

N. Y. 318. However, in comparatively late cases not involving mortgages, the Court has upheld the right of the promisee to sue for specific performance of a third party beneficiary contract. Croker v. New York Trust Co., 245 N. Y. 

17, 156 N. E. 81; Johnson Service Co. v. E. H. Monin, Inc., 253 N. Y. 417, 171 N. E. 692, 77 A. L. R. 214; William

S. Van Clief & Sons, Inc. v. City of New York, et al., 141 

Misc. 216, 252 N. Y. S. 402. § 303.

Right of Beneficiary against Promisor. 

Where the beneficiary possesses the right to sue on a contract made for his benefit, he is not restricted to an action at law. He may, in a proper case, sue in equity for specific performance of the contract. Weinberger v. Van 

Hessen, 260 N. Y. 294, 183 N. E. 429.

CHAPTER XV ASSIGNMENT ASSIGNMENT BY ACT OF THE PARTIES § 304. 

Assignment of Choses in Action. 

It was an established rule of the common law that choses in action could not be assigned, except in the case of those governed by the law merchant. Their assignment was prohibited because it was a rule of the early common law that a man could not assign that which he did not have in his possession, and, further, because it was believed that to allow a man to make over to a stranger his right of action would enable the rich and more powerful to work oppression upon the lower classes, and thus violate the rules of champerty and maintenance. Thallhimer v. 

Brinckerhoff, 3 Cowen (N.Y.) 623, 15 Am. Dec. 308. But the early limitation of the common law against the assignability of choses in action was soon modified by the common law courts themselves and still more extensively by the equity courts, until the principle became established that practically all rights arising ex contractu, including rights of action for breaches of contract and contracts to pay money, were assignable unless there was something in the terms or nature of the contract itself which manifested a contrary intention. The change is due in part to statutes which may be divided into two classes: those statutes which expressly provide that choses in action may be transferred; and those statutes which provide that the action to enforce an obligation must be brought in the name of the real party in interest. The American Bonding & Trust Co. v. Baltimore & Ohio Southwestern Ry. Co., 124 Fed. 866; Atlantic & North Carolina R. R. Co. v. Atlantic & North Carolina 

Co., 147 N. C. 368, 61 S. E. 185, 23 L. R. A. (N.S.) 223.

Where the parties to the contract expressly stipulate that the contract shall not be assigned, such stipulation will 259

always be recognized and enforced by the courts, and a statute which, in general terms, makes contracts or other demands assignable does not have the effect of nullifying stipulations which the parties themselves may make on the subject. Lockerby v. Amon, 64 Wash. 24, 116 Pac. 463, 35 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1064 and note; LaRue v. Groezinger, 84 Cal. 281, 24 Pac. 42. § 305. When Assignee May Sue. name.

At common law, an assignee could not sue in his own He was compelled to sue, if at all, in the name of his assignor to his own use. Jessel v. Williamsburgh Insurance Co., 3 Hill (N.Y.) 88. But now, by statute in New

York and in most states, an assignee may sue in his own name. The Personal Property Law, section 41, provides: 

"Where a claim or demand can be transferred, the transfer thereof passes an interest, which the transferee may enforce by an action or special proceeding, or interpose as a defense or counter-claim, in his own name, as the transferrer might have done; subject to any defense or counterclaim, existing against the transferrer, before notice of the transfer, or against the transferee. But this section does not apply, where the rights or liabilities of a party to a claim or demand, which is transferred, are regulated by special provision of law; nor does it vary the rights or liabilities of a party to a negotiable instrument, which is transferred." Siebert v. Dunn, 216 N. Y. 237, 110 N. E. 447. . 

§ 306. What Claims or Demands May Be Assigned by 

Statute.

The Personal Property Law, section 41, provides that any claim or demand may be transferred, except in the following cases: "1. Where it is to recover damages for a personal injury, or for a breach of promise to marry. "2.

Where it is founded upon a grant, which is made

ASSIGNMENT 

261 void by a statute of the state; or upon a claim to or interest in real property, a grant of which, by the transferrer, would be void by such a statute. "3. 

Where a transfer thereof is expressly forbidden by a statute of the state, or of the United States, or would contravene public policy."

It provides, also, that "a judgment for a sum of money, or directing the payment of a sum of money, recovered upon any cause of action, may be transferred; but if it is vacated or reversed, the transfer thereof does not transfer the cause of action unless the latter was transferable before the judgment was recovered." The statutory provisions contemplate the assignment of contracts for the payment of money and not contracts for the performance of some personal act or individual duty. 

§ 307. Assignment of Choses in Action for Tort. 

Mere personal torts which die with the person and do not survive to his personal representatives are not capable of passing by assignment. The term "personal injury" includes libel, slander, criminal conversation, seduction, malicious prosecution, assault, battery, false imprisonment, or other actionable injury to the person either of the plaintiff, or of another. General Construction Law, sec. 37-a; 

Hunt v. Conrad, 47 Minn. 557, 50 N. W. 614, 14 L. R. A. 

512 and note; North Chicago Street Ry. Co. v. Ackley, 171 

Ill. 100, 49 N. E. 222, 44 L. R. A. 177 and note; Welch v. 

Verduin, 121 Misc. 545, 201 N. Y. S. 324. Claims arising from injuries strictly personal, whether arising from tort or from contract, are not assignable, but a right of action for injuries affecting the estate, rather than the person, is assignable. McKee v. Judd, 12 N. Y. 622, 64 Am. Dec. 515. 

Examples of claims which may be assigned are those which are for services rendered; for prize money; for bounty; for money lost at play or betting; for open or running accounts; for injuries affecting property rather than

persons; for property destroyed by a railroad company; for conversion and other claims against common carriers. 

Wolff v. Rausch, 22 Misc. 108, 48 N. Y. S. 716. An action for fraud is not for a personal injury and, therefore, is assignable. Keeler v. Dunham, 114 App. Div. 94, 99 

N. Y. S. 669. § 308.

Assignment of Salaries. 

It is generally held that the salary or fees of public officers cannot be assigned before they are earned. The assignment of such funds before they are due is forbidden by public policy because of the danger of impairing the efficiency of public service. This limitation upon the assignment of salaries applies to all public officers. Bowery National Bank v. Wilson, 122 N. Y. 478, 25 N. E. 855, 9 

L. R. A. 706; Walker v. City of New York, 72 Misc. 97, 129 N. Y. S. 1059. The prohibition against the assignment of salaries to be earned in the future has been held not to apply to private employments. Field v. City of New 

York, 6 N. Y. 179, 57 Am. Dec. 435. This rule, however, has been modified by the enactment in 1934 of section 46 of the Personal Property Law, which provides that the assignment of future salaries, securing or relating to any indebtedness of less than one thousand dollars, shall be void unless: (a) the assignment is in a separate written instrument which identifies specifically the transaction to which it relates; (b) no other assignment exists in connection with the same transaction; (c) the salary of the employee is at least twelve dollars a week. Only ten per centum of the assignor's salary may be collected under such assignment, and this only if prescribed statutory requirements are met. For a consideration of stipulations in employment contracts against the assignment of salary, see Editorial, New York Law Journal, September 26, 1932. 

§ 309. Equities and Defenses between Original Parties. 

The assignee of a chose in action takes it subject to all

ASSIGNMENT 

263 defenses against it which the debtor might have interposed against the assignor not only at the time of assignment but also up to the time notice thereof is given to the debtor. 

But he does not take the assignment subject to equities in favor of the debtor arising subsequent to such notice. Civil 

Practice Act, sec. 267. The assignee acquires the same but no greater rights than his assignor had. Personal Property Law, sec. 41; French v. Stevenson, 57 Hun 585, 10 

N. Y. S. 386; Merchants' Bank v. Weill, 163 N. Y. 486, 57

N. E. 749. An unmatured debt existing at the time of the assignment cannot be set off as a counterclaim. Roberts v. 

Carter, 38 N. Y. 107.

The general question of counterclaims is regulated by the Civil Practice Act, section 266, et seq. 

§ 310. Notice of Assignment. 

The assignment is complete as between the assignor and the assignee without notice to the debtor or person liable, but it will not operate as against the debtor until he has received notice of it. If, for example, a mortgage is assigned by the mortgagee, and the mortgagor, subsequent to the assignment and without notice, settles a part of or the whole debt by making payment to the mortgagee, the payment is good as against a subsequent claim by the assignee. The recording of an assignment of a mortgage is not in itself a notice of such assignment to a mortgagor, his heirs, or his personal representatives, or to a grantee of the mortgagor where the grantee's deed is recorded prior to the recording of the assignment, so as to invalidate a payment made by one of them to the mortgagee. Real 

Property Law, sec. 324; Muir v. Schenck, 3 Hill (N.Y.) 

228, 38 Am. Dec. 633; Chittick v. Thompson Hill Development Corp., 230 App. Div. 410, 245 N. Y. S. 71, aff'd 259 

N. Y. 223, 181 N. E. 458.

No special form of notice of an assignment is required. 

A demand by the assignee for payment is a sufficient notice, and a delay in making such a demand is immaterial if no

equity arose between the debtor and the assignor before notice was given. But in order to protect his rights under an assignment, the assignee should give notice to the debtor as soon as the assignment is made. Danvers v. Lugar, 30 

Misc. 98, 61 N. Y. S. 778; Crosby v. Kropf, 33 App. Div. 

446, 54 N. Y. S. 76.

The consent of the debtor is not essential to the creation of an obligation on his part to the assignee if the claim is, in its nature, assignable. Hinkle Iron Co. v. Kohn, 229 

N. Y. 179, 128 N. E. 113. If the rule were otherwise it would be a practical denial of the assignability of choses in action, because the right to assign would depend entirely upon the will of the debtor. § 311. Notice of Assignment of Salary. 

The Personal Property Law, section 42, provides that any person who shall make to an employee a loan on account of future wages on an assignment or note covering such a loan shall not collect or attach the same while it is in the possession or control of the employer, unless within three days after "the execution of such assignment or notes and the making of such loan or loans" a copy of "such agreement or assignment or notes under which the claim is made" is filed with the employer. Thompson v. Gimbel 

Brothers, 207 N. Y. 659, 100 N. E. 794; Vilbig v. Kyle &

Purdy, 182 N. Y. S. 905. In this connection, see section 

308, supra, and the Personal Property Law, section 46. § 312. Notice of Assignment of Non-Negotiable and 

Negotiable Paper.

The requirements as to notice to the debtor by the assignee apply to the assignment of non-negotiable paper. A failure to give notice is at the peril of the assignee either by reason of a new defense which may arise between the assignor and the debtor (maker) or by a payment by the debtor to the assignor. Moreover, if a negotiable instrument is assigned instead of negotiated, there arises the

ASSIGNMENT 

265 same necessity of giving notice as in the case of nonnegotiable instruments. The transfer of a negotiable note drawn to the order of the payee without the payee's indorsement constitutes a mere assignment thereof and not a negotiation. Hence, in such cases, the assignee must notify the debtor of the assignment in order to protect himself as against the assignor. Shaw v. Railroad Co., 101 

U. S. 557, 25 Law. Ed. 892; Goshen National Bank v. 

Bingham, 118 N. Y. 349, 23 N. E. 180, 7 L. R. A. 595. 

§ 313. Necessity of Consideration. 

No consideration is necessary to support an assignment as against the debtor. Therefore, in an action brought by the assignee against the debtor, the defendant cannot show that the assignment was made without consideration, since an assignment may be made as a gratuity. Richardson v. 

Mead, 27 Barb. 178; Guy v. Craighead, 6 App. Div. 463, 39 N. Y. S. 688; In re Farley's Estate, 91 Misc. 185, 155 

N. Y. S. 63. § 314. Liability of Assignor to Assignee. 

The assignor makes no implied warranty that the debt will be paid and incurs no greater liability than the seller of chattels generally. Giffert v. West, 33 Wis. 617. In some states, however, the assignor is deemed to be virtually a guarantor of collection, and, therefore, impliedly warrants that the debt will be paid at maturity by the debtor. Welsh v. Ebersole, 75 Va. 651. § 315.

Priority of Title as between Assignees. 

As between successive assignees, the rule in New York and a number of other states is that the one which is prior in time will be protected. "As between different assignees of a chose in, action by express assignment from the same person, the one prior in point of time will be protected, although he has given no notice of such assignment to either the subsequent assignee or the debtor." Fortunato

v. Patten, 147 N. Y. 277, 41 N. E. 572; Salem Trust Co. v. 

Manufacturers' Finance Co., 264 U. S. 182, 68 Law. Ed. 

628, 44 Sup. Ct. Rep. 266; Superior Brassiere Co. v. Zimetbaum, 214 App. Div. 525, 212 N. Y. S. 473. But the rule is otherwise in many states. See note in 31 A. L. R. 876. 

§ 316. Assignment of Whole Fund. 

The common law rule that the creditor shall not be permitted to split his cause of action into many actions without the assent of his debtor does not find favor in New York. In this State the debt or obligation may be divided and assigned to various persons. The objection formerly urged to the splitting of a cause of action; namely, that it might subject the debtor to several actions to enforce a single obligation, has little force under our system of practice, which requires all parties in interest to be joined as parties to the action. Civil Practice Act, sec. 211, et seq.; 

Risley v. Phenix Bank of New York, 83 N. Y. 318, aff'd 

111U. S. 125, 28 Law. Ed. 374, 4 Sup. Ct. Rep. 322; Whittemore v. Judd Linseed & Sperm Oil Co., 124 N. Y. 565, 27 

N. E. 244; Porter v. Lane, 212 App. Div. 528, 209 N. Y. S. 

54, aff'd 244 N. Y. 523, 155 N. E. 881.

A check drawn in the ordinary form for the payment of money does not constitute a common law assignment, in whole or in part, of the fund against which it is drawn, but is governed by the law merchant. Glennan v. Rochester 

Trust & Safe Deposit Co., 209 N. Y. 12, 102 N. E. 537, 52 

L. R. A. (N.S.) 302. 

§ 317. Form of Assignment. 

In New York, no formality is required to perfect the assignment. It may rest in parol, or it may be in writing. 

Risley v. Phenix Bank of New York, 83 N., Y. 318, aff'd 

111 U. S. 125, 28 Law. Ed. 374, 4 Sup. Ct. Rep. 322; Riker v. Curtis, 17 Misc. 134, 39 N. Y. S. 340. Where there is no written assignment, it is merely a question of fact

ASSIGNMENT 

267 whether or not there was a transfer, and this may be established by oral evidence. Riker v. Curtis, supra. § 318. 

Recording-Filing Assignments.

In the absence of a specific statutory requirement, an assignment of a chose in action need not be recorded in order to be valid. Bates v. Salt Spring National Bank, 157 

N. Y. 322, 51 N. E. 1033. But under the requirements of the Lien Law of New York, section 15, an assignment of a contract for labor and materials for the improvement of real property must be filed in the office of the county clerk within ten days after the assignment. If not so filed, the assignment is void as against subsequent assignees in good faith and for a valuable consideration. Edison Electric Illuminating Co. v. Frick Co., 221 N. Y. 1, 116 N. E. 369, L. R. A. 1917F 1123; Williams Engineering & Contracting 

Co. v. City of New York, 222 N. Y. 1, 117 N. E. 944. 

§ 319. Assignment of Liabilities. 

A person cannot assign his liabilities under a contract. A debtor cannot drop out and substitute another so as to escape his personal liability to his own creditor. Thus, it was held that a contract for the sale and delivery of lead ore by a mining company to persons conducting a smelter, the ore to be assayed after delivery and the price to be ascertained and subsequently paid according to the result of the assay, was not assignable by the vendees to a smelting company, since the mining company could not be compelled to accept the liability of a smelting company as a substitute for the liability of those with whom it had contracted. Arkansas Valley Smelting Co. v. Belden Mining Co., 127 U. S. 379, 32 Law. Ed. 246, 8 Sup. Ct. Rep. 

1308. But by novation, a party may absolve himself from liability. This, in reality, is a rescission of the old contract by agreement and a substitution of a new one in which the same acts are to be performed by different parties with

the approval and consent of the creditor. Heaton v. Angier, 7 N. H. 397, 28 Am. Dec. 353. 

§ 320. Assignability of Executory Contracts. 

The general rule as to the assignability of executory contracts, as stated by the New York Court of Appeals, is that "an executory contract not necessarily personal in its character, which can, consistent with the rights and interests of the adverse party, be sufficiently executed by the assignee, is assignable in the absence of agreement in the contract." Quinn v. Whitney, 204 N. Y. 363, 97 N. E. 724; 

Paige v. Faure, 229 N. Y. 114, 127 N. E. 898. In Rosenthal 

Paper Co. v. National Folding Box & Paper Co., 226 N. Y. 

313, 123 N. E. 766, the Court said: "The general rule now prevailing (as the successor of the archaic view that a contract created strictly personal obligations between the parties and non-assignability was a logical attribute), that any property right, not necessarily personal, is assignable, is overcome only by agreement of the contracting parties or a principle of law or public policy." If a contract, however, involves a personal liability-a relation of personal confidence or calls for the personal skill or experience of one of the parties, it is not assignable. The real question to be considered, therefore, is whether a given contract comes within the principles thus stated. This is not always an easy matter to determine. Cases within the rule that contracts involving personal confidence cannot be assigned are New York Bank Note Co. v. Hamilton Bank 

Note Engraving & Printing Co., 180 N. Y. 280, 73 N. E. 

48, and Arkansas Valley Smelting Co. v. Belden Mining 

Co., 127 U. S. 379, 32 Law. Ed. 246, 8 Sup. Ct. Rep. 1308. 

§ 321. Assignment of Contracts for Sale of Goods. 

Contracts to sell and deliver goods for a fixed price, payable in cash on delivery, in which the owner would receive the price at the time of parting with his property, are generally assignable. In such cases, the rights of the seller

ASSIGNMENT 

269 could not be affected by the question whether or not the price was paid by the person with whom he originally contracted or by his assignee. So, too, a seller may assign his right to receive payment for the goods sold and delivered. 

In New York, even in certain cases wherein goods were sold on credit, it has been held that the purchaser may assign his right of purchase under the contract. The purchaser's right to assign was recognized because of his continued contractual liability to the seller after the assignment, and hence the seller's rights were held to be in no way jeopardized by the assignment. Rochester Lantern Co. v. 

Stiles & Parker Press Co., 135 N. Y. 209, 31 N. E. 1018.

In Liberty Wall Paper Co. v. Stoner Wall Paper Co., 59 

App. Div. 353, 69 N. Y. S. 355, aff'd 170 N. Y. 582, 63 

N. E. 1119, the contract provided for the sale of paper hangings on thirty days' credit. The Court, holding the contract to be assignable by the purchaser, said: "The contract is not a personal one in the sense that Stoner [the purchaser] was bound to perform in person. Stoner had a right to assign the contract, or in case of his death, his executors or administrators would have succeeded to his rights and liabilities under the contract. The obligations of Stoner under the contract could have been discharged by any one. If the assignment was made without the consent of the plaintiff, the obligations of the contract would still have rested upon Stoner, and resort could have been had to him for the fulfillment of the contract if the same had not been carried out and discharged by his assignee." 

The seller, however, could not in all such cases assign a contract and thereby impose upon the buyer the duty to accept paper hangings manufactured by one other than the seller. In such cases the contract would, quite commonly, be classified as a personal contract. Where the seller is to manufacture the goods, there is usually the personal or human element involved, and this personal equation may be of the utmost importance to the parties; but the personal element is wholly lacking on the part of the buyer where

his only duty is to make payment for the goods purchased. 

In Schlessinger v. Forest Products Co., 78 N. J. L. 637, 76 Atl. 1024, 30 L. R. A. (N.S.) 347, the Court said: "The injustice of permitting an assignment of a contract for personal services, for the painting of a picture, for a partnership, is obvious. A contract for the sale of goods to be manufactured stands on similar grounds where the vendee relies upon the skill and experience of the manufacturer, as well as upon the implied warranty of quality. 

No man who has employed a tailor to make a suit of clothes ought to be compelled to accept a suit made by the tailor's assignee." 

§ 322. Contracts Not Involving Personal Confidence. 

Executory contracts to perform a particular task and not involving the element of personal confidence, such as a contract to dig a trench, or to make an excavation for a cellar, or to cut cordwood, are assignable. Thus, in Janvey v. 

Loketz, 122 App. Div. 411, 106 N. Y. S. 690, it was held that a contract for painting, decorating, whitewashing, and wall papering involved no personal confidential relation, and no exceptional personal skill or knowledge, and was, therefore, assignable. The same is true in the case of a contract for street cleaning. Devlin v. New York, 63 

N. Y. 8, 50 How. Pr. 1. 

§ 323. Assignment of Contracts in Restraint of Trade. 

There is no rule of public policy which makes a contract in restraint of trade non-assignable. A contract whereby a vendor agrees not to engage in a certain business within designated territory for a certain period of time is assignable by the vendee and when the business is sold by the vendee, the purchaser may enforce the obligation against the original vendor. Francisco v. Smith, 143 N. Y. 488, 38 N. E. 980.

ASSIGNMENT 

271 

§ 324. 

Assignment of Building Contracts. 

While there are authorities which support the view that building contracts are assignable, the weight of authority is against the right of the contractor to assign. This class of contracts would seem to involve the element of personal confidence. The knowledge, experience, or pecuniary ability of the contractor is usually the inducement to, and the essence of, the contract. Johnson v. Vickers, 139 Wis. 145, 120 N. W. 837, 21 L. R. A. (N.S.) 359 and note. But it is generally understood that the contractor cannot do all the work in person. Hence, in the absence of a stipulation to the contrary, the contractor may perform the contract through workmen over whom he places a superintendent, and need not himself perform any of the actual labor. 

Council v. Teal, 122 Ga. 61, 49 S. E. 806. § 325.

Agreement Not To Assign. 

The parties may, in all cases, prohibit the assignment of a contract by express agreement and this, too, irrespective of the nature of the contract. In Devlin v. New York, 63 

N. Y. 8, 50 How. Pr. 1, the Court said: "Parties, may, in terms, prohibit the assignment of any contract and declare that neither personal representatives nor assignees shall succeed to any rights in virtue of it, or be bound by its obligations." A right which is contracted away is no longer a right to be asserted by the contractor. In other words, the party who has agreed that the contract shall be non-assignable cannot object because he is held to his bargain. Barringer v. Bes Line Construction Co., 23 Okla. 131, 99 Pac. 

775, 21 L. R. A. (N.S.) 597. 

§ 326. Recapitulation of Non-Assignable Contracts. 

Generally speaking, all contracts are assignable, except those within the three following classes: (a) personal contracts; (b) contracts containing a provision against assignment; (c) contracts forbidden by statute to be assigned.

ASSIGNMENT BY OPERATION OF LAW § 327. In General. 

Rules of law may operate to transfer rights and liabilities arising under a contract from one person to another. Common law rules operate to transfer these rights and liabilities in the following cases: 

1. Upon conveyance of real estate; 

2. Upon marriage; 

3. Upon death. § 328. Assignment of Obligations upon Transfer of Interests in Land-Covenants Affecting Leasehold Interests. 

All covenants which touch or concern land which has been conveyed from one person to another are said to run with the land. They are binding upon the assignee of the covenantor and may be enforced by the assignee of the covenantee, even though not expressly made to run to "his assigns." Such covenants relate to the enjoyment, or title, or possession of the land, and so may be said to be made for the land, or to its advantage. The law, therefore, requires that there shall exist between the contracting parties and between those as to whom the question arises a "privity of estate," a "mutual or successive relationship to the same rights of property." Belonging to this class are covenants to pay rent; to repair; not to carry on a particular trade on the premises; to renew; to pay taxes; to surrender at the end of the term with improvements; of title (until breach); and of quiet enjoyment. Stewart v. Long Island R. R. Co., 102 N. Y. 601, 8 N. E. 200. The mere intention of the parties to the covenant that it shall run with the land, as shown by the use of the word "assigns," or otherwise, will not cause it to do so if it does not, in its nature, touch or concern the land. Sebald v. Mulholland, 155 N. Y. 455, 50 N. E. 260; Craword v. Krollpfeiffer, 195 N. Y. 185, 88 N. E. 29. Even though a covenant touch or concern the land, if it concerns likewise a thing which is not in existence

ASSIGNMENT 

273 at the time of the covenantor's demise, but which is to be built or created thereafter, the covenant will not bind the assignees unless they are expressly mentioned. Spencer's 

Case, 5 Coke 16, 77 Eng. Rep. 72; Tallman v. Coffin, 4 N. Y. 

134; Coffin v. Tallman, 8 N. Y. 465.

All covenants that do not touch or concern the land are said to be personal or collateral and do not pass to the assignee. Among this class are covenants to pay a certain sum to a stranger; that the lessor will not build or keep any house for the sale of intoxicating liquor within a certain distance of the premises; and that the lessor will not maintain a competing business within a certain distance of the premises. Dolph v. White, 12 N. Y. 296; Merly 

Realty Corp. v. Wallack, 134 Misc. 96, 234 N. Y. S. 491. $329. Covenants Affecting Freehold Estates. 

In deeds of conveyance of real property, the covenants which touch or concern the land involved; i.e., relate to its enjoyment, or title, or possession, are ordinarily entered into by parties in privity with each other, since one succeeds to the interests of the other; and these covenants, when not broken at the time of the transfer, uniformly run with the land and are binding upon and inure to the benefit of the assignees. Belonging to this class are covenants against nuisances; against encumbrances; for further assurance; for quiet enjoyment; and of warranty. Geiszler v. DeGraaf, 166 N. Y. 339, 59 N. E. 993; Olmstead v. Rawson, 188 N. Y. 517, 81 N. E. 456. Covenants of seisin and of the right to convey are broken, if at all, at the time when the deed is delivered to the grantee. They become choses in action as soon as broken; therefore, they do not run with the land, and hence, do not pass to subsequent grantees. Mygatt v. Coe, 124 N. Y. 212, 26 N. E. 611, 11 

L. R. A. 646; Hilliker v. Rueger, 228 N. Y. 11, 126 N. E. 266.

§ 330. Assignment of Contractual Obligations upon Marriage.

At common law, a wife's rights and liabilities were assigned by marriage to the husband. See Robins v. Mc- 

Clure, 100 N. Y. 328, 3 N. E. 663. But by statute, in New

York and many other states, no assignment is thus effected by the marriage. No rights to the wife's property are acquired by marriage, and the husband becomes liable for his wife's debts incurred before marriage only when he acquires property of his wife by antenuptial contract, and then he is liable for her debts contracted before marriage only to the extent of the property thus acquired. Domestic 

Relations Law, secs. 50 and 54. 

§ 331. Assignment of Contractual Obligations by Death. 

Death passes to the executor or administrator of the deceased all rights of action affecting the personal estate of the deceased as well as all liabilities chargeable against it. 

This does not include: 

1. Contracts depending upon the personal skill or services of the deceased; 

2. Contracts, the breach of which involves a purely personal loss.

Covenants which are attached to leasehold estates pass, as to benefit and liability, with the personalty to the personal representatives; covenants affecting freehold estates, however, such as covenants of title in a conveyance of freehold property, pass to the devisee of the realty or to the heir (designated as distributee since September 1, 1930).

Where one contracts to build upon his own land a house for another and dies before the building is completed, his executor is bound to perform the contract. If the land descends to the heirs, the covenants still remain in force. 

Chamberlain v. Dunlop, 126 N. Y. 45, 26 N. E. 966.

A contract of guaranty survives the death of the guarantor. Kernochan v. Murray, 111 N. Y. 306, 18 N. E. 868,

ASSIGNMENT 

275 

2 L. R. A. 183; Assets Realization Co. v. Roth, 226 N. Y. 

370, 123 N. E. 743. Contracts for personal service expire with the death of either party to the contract. Thus, an apprenticeship contract is terminated by the death of the master, and no claim to the services of the apprentice survives to the executor. Lacy v. Getman, 119 N. Y. 109, 23 

N. E. 452, 6 L. R. A. 728. In Sargent v. McLeod, 209 N. Y. 

360, 103 N. E. 164, 52 L. R. A. (N.S.) 380, the Court said: "Contracts for personal services requiring skill, which can only be performed by the person named, are held not of absolute obligation, under all circumstances, but subject to the implied condition that the person designated shall be able to perform. If a lawyer dies before he has prosecuted to a judgment or settlement a litigation which he has undertaken to conduct for a certain compensation, his contract is at an end, * * * and the compensation agreed upon cannot be recovered by his representative under the agreement because he did not, and his representatives cannot, carry out his agreement. The extent of the recovery permitted his representative is the full reasonable value of the services rendered under the contract, not exceeding, however, the sum or the rate fixed by it." Likewise, where the loss resulting from the breach of a contract is personal, no right of action survives or is conferred upon the personal representatives. Thus, in a case where the executor brought an action for breach of promise to marry his testatrix, the promise having been broken during her life, the executor was non-suited because the breach of a contract to marry results in a personal injury and not in an injury to property. Wade v. Kalbfleisch, 58 N. Y. 282, 17 Am. Rep. 250; note in 34 A. L. R. 1363.

CHAPTER XVI  

§ 332. Classification.

With relation to the number of persons involved, contracts may be divided into those wherein the persons involved are: 

1. One promisor and one promisee; 

2. Two or more promisors and one promisee; 

3. One promisor and two or more promisees; or 

4. Two or more promisors and two or more promisees. 

§ 333. One Promisor and One Promisee. 

Contracts having but one promisor and one promisee need no special consideration, for this is the normal case and presents no difficulties in this connection. CO-OBLIGORS $ 334. Two or More Independent Promisors. 

If a promise in a contract is made by two or more several promisors, the promise is treated as if the document contained the separate promise of each promisor. For example, such expressions as "we promise each to pay," or "we promise to pay the amount set opposite our respective names" as in contracts of subscription, where the promisors are severally liable-allow the promisee to sue each merely for his portion of the indebtedness. Tibbits v. 

Percy, 24 Barb. 39; Cornish & Co. v. West, 82 Minn. 107, 84 N. W. 750. $335. 

Joint Promisors.

Where a contractual obligation is incurred by two or more persons, it is said to be a joint contract. Rosenzweig v. McCaffrey, 28 Misc. 485, 59 N. Y. S. 863. 276

It is a general presumption of law that when two or more persons undertake an obligation they undertake it jointly, unless words indicating a contrary intention are used. The fact that the interests of the obligors in a contract are diverse does not prevent the obligation from being joint. 

United States Printing & Lithograph Co. v. Powers, 233 

N. Y. 143, 135 N. E. 225.

At common law, the liability of co-partners in respect to partnership contracts was joint; while in equity it was several. It is now provided by statute that the partners are liable jointly for all debts and obligations of the partnership, exclusive of torts and breaches of trust for which they are jointly and severally liable. Any partner may, however, enter into a separate obligation to perform a partnership contract. Partnership Law, sec. 26.

Both at common law and under the statute, joint promisors cannot be sued separately over the objection of one of them. All must be joined as defendants. 

By statute in New York, it is now provided that in an action against two or more defendants jointly indebted on a contract, the plaintiff may proceed to final judgment without having served all of the defendants, and such judgment may be taken against all of the defendants. Civil 

Practice Act, sec 1197. Such judgment is conclusive against such defendants as were served personally or who entered their appearance in the action, but, against the others, is evidence only of the extent of the plaintiff's de mand. Civil Practice Act, sec. 1198. An execution upon such judgment may be levied only upon the sole property of the defendants served personally, and upon the joint property of all the defendants. Civil Practice Act, sec. 

1199. The sole property of the joint debtors who were not served in such action may be charged in a subsequent action against them. Civil Practice Act, secs. 1185 and 1186.

Similar statutes have been enacted in other states.

§ 336. 

Joint Promisors Survivorship.

At common law, upon the death of one joint promisor, the entire liability devolved upon the survivors, and no action at law could be maintained against the estate of the deceased promisor. In equity, unless he was a guarantor or surety without benefit or advantage, his estate could be charged upon proof that the survivors were insolvent and unable to pay. But the estate of the last deceased promisor remained liable. Davis v. Van Buren, 72 N. Y. 587; Richardson v. Draper, 87 N. Y. 337; Gere v. Clark, 6 Hill (N.Y.) 350; see note, 67 A. L. R. 609, 614.

In New York, the rule has been modified by statute. The estate of a joint promisor is not now discharged by his death. His personal representatives may be substituted in his place, but only when the surviving debtors are insolvent. Civil Practice Act, sec. 85; Potts v. Dounce, 173 

N. Y. 335, 66 N. E. 4. However, under the Debtor and

Creditor Law, section 236, which did not specifically repeal the Civil Practice Act, section 85, the estate of a joint obligor upon his death becomes bound jointly and severally with the surviving obligors. It seems, therefore, that it is no longer necessary to allege the insolvency of the surviving joint obligor in order to proceed directly against the estate of the deceased joint obligor. Sisto v. Bambara, 228 App. 

Div. 456, 240 N. Y. S. 121. § 337.

Joint Promisors-Release. 

At common law, a voluntary release of one joint promisor is a release of all. The reason is that the release is conclusive that the releasor has been fully satisfied, and after satisfaction, although the release absolved only one, no foundation remains for an action against the coobligor. Hillas v. Fuller, 143 N. Y. S. 15. But where a release of one of two joint debtors contains an express provision or reservation that it shall not affect or impair the claim of the creditor against the other debtor, the latter is not discharged. A covenant not to sue does not operate as

a release, and a release containing such a reservation is construed to be merely a covenant not to sue and not a technical release. Hale v. Spaulding, 145 Mass. 482, 14 N. E. 

534; Whittemore v. Judd Linseed & Sperm Oil Co., 124 

N. Y. 565, 27 N. E. 244. The common law rule requiring a release to be under seal no longer prevails, except where the liability is evidenced by a specialty. Post v. Thomas, 180 App. Div. 627, 168 N. Y. S. 226. 

The Debtor and Creditor Law, sections 231 to 240, expressly provides that a joint debtor may make a separate composition with his creditor. If the creditor in his release or discharge expressly reserves his rights against the other co-obligors, the latter are discharged only to the extent of the consideration received by the obligee for such release. 

Where the obligee in releasing one of the joint obligors fails to reserve his rights against the remaining joint obligors, the latter are discharged only to the extent provided by the Debtor and Creditor Law, section 235, which reads as follows: "(a) If an obligee releasing or discharging an obligor without express reservation of rights against a co-obligor, then knows or has reason to know that the obligor released or discharged did not pay so much of the claim as he was bound by his contract or relation with that co-obligor to pay, the obligee's claim against that co-obligor shall be satisfied to the amount which the obligee knew or had reason to know that the released or discharged obligor was bound to such co-obligor to pay. "(b) If an obligee so releasing or discharging an obligor has not then such knowledge or reason to know, the obligee's claim against the co-obligor shall be satisfied to the extent of the lesser of two amounts, namely (1) the amount of the fractional share of the obligor released or discharged, or (2) the amount that such obligor was bound by his contract or relation with the co-obligor to pay." 

A partner, who is jointly liable for contract obligations and jointly and severally liable for torts and breaches of

trust, cannot make a separate composition with a firm creditor until the partnership has been dissolved. Partnership Law, sec. 26. Therefore, a release of one of the partners before dissolution will discharge all the partners, even though the release contains a reservation by the creditor of his rights against the co-partners. Debtor and Creditor 

Law, sec. 237. After dissolution, the release of one partner will operate to discharge his co-partners as though they were merely co-obligors. 

§ 338. Joint Promisors-Payment. 

Payment by one joint debtor of a debt owing jointly by two or more persons extinguishes the debt as to all the debtors. Storz v. Boyce, 34 Misc. 279, 69 N. Y. S. 612, note 

68 L. R. A. 514. But merely taking the note of one joint debtor does not operate to release co-joint debtors unless there is an agreement to that effect. The reason for this is that a note is presumed to be conditional and not absolute payment. So, too, a check is presumptively regarded as only a conditional payment. Bates v. Rosekrans, 37 

N. Y. 409; Leschen & Sons Rope Co. v. Mayflower Gold Mining & Reduction Co., 173 Fed. 855, 35 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1 and note, 30, 61; Watson v. First State Bank of Dallas, 237 

S. W. (Tex. Com. App.) 1106. 

§ 339. Joint Tort-Feasors-Release. 

Although a release under seal of one of several joint tort-feasors, prior to obtaining a judgment against them, operates as a release of all, if such release contains a reservation of rights against the others, it will be construed to be simply a covenant not to sue the one nominally released, and the liability of the others will not be extinguished thereby. Gilbert v. Finch, 173 N. Y. 455, 66 N. E. 

133, 61 L. R. A. 807; Walsh v. New York Central & Hudson 

River R. R. Co., 204 N. Y. 58, 97 N. E. 408, 37 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1137; Berry v. Pullman Co., 249 Fed. 816, L. R. A. 

1918F 358 and note, 363. For a collation of the New York

cases, see German-American Coffee Co. v. O'Neil, 102 Misc. 

165, 169 N. Y. S. 421. The rule is the same under the 

Debtor and Creditor Law, section 234.

The Debtor and Creditor Law, section 235, does not apply unless there is a duty between and among the joint tortfeasors to contribute to the joint debt. Therefore, a release of one of the joint tort-feasors, executed prior to obtaining a judgment against them, will discharge them all if the release contains no reservation of rights against the others. 

Gavin v. Malherbe, 240 App. Div. 779, 266 N. Y. S. 897, aff'd 264 N. Y. 3, 191 N. E. 486, memo.

Under the Civil Practice Act, section 211-a, contribution may be enforced between and among those joint tort-feasors whom the plaintiff has voluntarily joined as co-defendants, but only after a joint judgment has been rendered against them. Fox v. Western New York Motor Lines, Inc., 257 

N. Y. 305, 178 N. E. 289. When the obligation to contribute has thus arisen, the Debtor and Creditor Law, section 235, applies, and the release of one joint tort-feasor without reservation of rights against the others should release the others only to the extent as provided by that section. See section 337, supra. 

§ 340. 

Joint Promisors-Contribution. 

Where one joint promisor pays more than his pro rata share, he is entitled to contribution from his co-promisors unless he has in some manner precluded himself from such right. Morgan v. Smith, 70 N. Y. 537; Jeffries v. Ferguson, 87 Mo. 244. However, in United States Printing & Lithograph Co. v. Powers, 233 N. Y. 143, 157, 135 N. E. 225, the Court of Appeals said: "It is the rule that a plaintiff who enters judgment against one of several joint obligors causes a merger of his entire claim against all of the obligors in the judgment which he has thus entered and thenceforth cannot prosecute those who have been omitted from the judgment, and they would not be liable for contribution." This rule has been modified by the Debtor and

Creditor Law, section 232, which provides that the entry of judgment against one or more joint obligors shall not discharge a co-obligor who was not a party to the proceeding in which the judgment was entered. Where all are joined as defendants and some have not been served, the judgment should be entered against all the joint obligors as provided by the Civil Practice Act, section 1197. If judgment is entered only against those who have been served, the others are discharged not only from the debt but also from their obligation to contribute to the joint coobligor who paid the debt. United States Printing & Lithograph Co. v. Powers, supra. 

§ 341. Joint and Several Promisors. 

As to promisors, a promise may be both joint and several, in which case all the promisors may be joined in one action, or they may be sued separately, at the election of the promisee. Schilling v. Black, 49 Kan. 552, 31 Pac. 143; 

Cummings v. People, 50 Ill. 132. But, in the absence of a statutory provision to the contrary, all must be sued jointly, or each severally. An action cannot be maintained against an intermediate number. Cummings v. People, supra; Bangor Bank v. Treat, 6 Me. 207, 19 Am. Dec. 210. 

In New York, the Civil Practice Act, provides: "All persons may be joined as defendants against whom the right to any relief is alleged to exist, whether jointly, severally or in the alternative; and judgment may be given against such one or more of the defendants as may be found liable, according to their respective liabilities." Civil 

Practice Act, sec. 211. "Two or more persons severally liable upon the same written instrument, including the parties to a bill of exchange or a promissory note, whether the action is brought upon the instrument, or by a party thereto to recover against other parties liable over to him, may all or any of them be included as defendants in the same action at the option of the plaintiff. ** * *

"This section does not affect a defense or other objection of a defendant, growing out of the failure to join in the action two or more persons jointly liable; and, as regards the other parties to the action, persons jointly liable are regarded as one party for every purpose contemplated by this section." Civil Practice Act, sec. 216.

By statute in New York, it is now provided that in an action against two or more defendants severally liable, the plaintiff may proceed to final judgment against those who have been served as if they were the only defendants. Upon the entry of the judgment, the action is severed and may be continued against the remaining defendants. Civil Practice Act, sec. 475. Compare this procedure with that required where the action is against joint debtors. Section 

335, supra. 

§ 342. Joint and Several Promisors-Survivorship. 

The doctrine of survivorship does not apply to several contracts, and, therefore, has no application to joint and several obligations. Erie County v. Baltz, 125 App. Div. 

144, 109 N. Y. S. 304. 

§ 343. 

Joint and Several Promisors-Release. 

At common law, a release of one of a number of joint and several promisors operates to release all. But a covenant not to sue one will not release the others. Whittemore v. 

Judd Linseed & Sperm Oil Co., 124 N. Y. 565, 27 N. E. 244; 

Morgan v. Smith, 70 N. Y. 537.

The language of the Debtor and Creditor Law, sections 

232, 233, and 234 (sections 337 and 340, supra), specifically includes several and joint and several promisors as well as joint obligors. Although the Debtor and Creditor Law, section 235 (section 337, supra), does not enumerate the three classes of obligors; viz., joint, several, and joint and several, it undoubtedly applies to all three. Thus, the effect of the release of one of a number of joint and several obligors is the same as though they were joint obligors.

CO-OBLIGEES § 344. 

Joint Promisees.

Where the obligation of a contract runs to two or more joint promisees, all must unite in bringing an action for its enforcement. Ehle v. Purdy, 6 Wend. (N.Y.) 629.

But if the consent of any one who should be joined as a plaintiff cannot be obtained, he may be made a defendant, the reason therefor being stated in the complaint. Civil 

Practice Act, sec. 194. 

§ 345. Joint Promisees-Survivorship. 

If one of several joint promisees dies, the right of action devolves on the survivors. Birdsall v. Delaware & Hudson 

Co., 216 Fed. 717. The personal representative of the deceased promisee need not be joined as party plaintiff. Upon the death of the last survivor, the right of action devolves on his representative. Murray v. Mumford, 6 Cowen (N.Y.) 441; Supreme Lodge v. Portingall, 167 Ill. 291, 47 

N. E. 203. 

§ 346. Joint Promisees-Release. 

A release by one joint promisee is, in the absence of fraud, a bar to an action by the others, and payment of the debt to one discharges the debt. An accord and satisfaction of the debt made with one or more joint promisees is a discharge of the obligation to all promisees. Osborn v. Martha's Vineyard R. R. Co., 140 Mass. 549, 5 N. E. 486; 

People v. Keyser, 28 N. Y. 226, 228, 84 Am. Dec. 338. § 347. Joint or Several Promisees.

The promisees must be either joint or several; they cannot be both joint and several. Willoughby v. Willoughby, 5 N. H. 244. 

Thus, in Eveleth v. Sawyer, 96 Me. 227, 52 Atl. 639, the 

Court said: "One and the same contract cannot be so

framed as to give the promisees the right to sue upon it both jointly and separately. They must be entitled under it either jointly only or separately only, and must sue accordingly." However, it has been held that under the Negotiable Instruments Law, a negotiable instrument may be made payable to the order of A or B and may be indorsed either by A or B so as to make the indorsee a holder in due course. Voris v. Schoonover, 91 Kan. 530, 138 Pac. 607, 50 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1097 and note. But see Passut v. Heubner, 81 Misc. 249, 142 N. Y. S. 546. 

§ 348. Several Contracts. 

Where two or more parties bind themselves severally to another, in respect to the same matter or debt, their liability is separate and distinct, and they cannot be joined as party defendants, in the absence of some statutory provision to the contrary. So, if one party severally binds himself to each of several persons, their right to enforce the promise is several, and each must sue separately. Emmeluth v. Home Benefit Assn., 122 N. Y. 130, 25 N. E. 234, 9 L. R. A. 704; Yates v. Foot, 12 Johns. (N.Y.) 1. However, the Civil Practice Act makes the following provisions: "All persons may be joined in one action as plaintiffs, in whom any right to relief in respect of or arising out of the same transaction or series of transactions is alleged to exist whether jointly, severally or in the alternative, where if such persons brought separate actions any common question of law or fact would arise." Civil Practice Act, sec. 209. "All persons may be joined as defendants against whom the right to any relief is alleged to exist, whether jointly, severally or in the alternative; and judgment may be given against such one or more of the defendants as may be found to be liable, according to their respective liabilities." 

Civil Practice Act, sec. 211.

CHAPTER XVII  § 349. 

Purpose of Construction. 

The primary purpose of construction in the law of contracts is to ascertain the intention of the parties and to give effect to that intention if it can be done consistently with legal principles. "The ascertainment of the substantial intent of the parties is the fundamental rule in the construction of all agreements." People v. Walsh, 211 

N. Y. 90, 105 N. E. 136. This intention in express contracts is embodied, in the first instance, in the words which the parties have used and is to be deduced therefrom. This rule applies to oral contracts as well as to contracts in writing. It must not be supposed, however, that the courts will attempt to ascertain the actual mental processes of the parties to a particular contract. The law presumes that the parties understood the import of their contract and that it expresses their intention. In the absence of ambiguity and uncertainty, the parties will not be permitted to prove that they intended something different from that which is expressed in the agreement. Dwight v. Germania 

Life Insurance Co., 103 N. Y. 341, 8 N. E. 654. The purpose of construing the contract, therefore, is to ascertain the intention of the parties as disclosed by the contract itself, and not the intention of the parties independent of the contract. Where, by their words, the parties have left no reasonable ground for doubt, there is no defensible excuse for construction. Gans v. Aetna Life Insurance Co., 214 N. Y. 326, 108 N. E. 443. A court cannot, by implication, add to or change a contract which is clear and complete in itself. South Utah Mines & Smelters v. Utah 

Leasing Co., 260 Fed. 149. 

In Lynch v. Houston, 138 Mo. App. 167, 119 S. W. 994, the Court held that: "Aside from some technical rules 66 286

adopted for the general good, there is, ordinarily, no hard and fast rule to govern the interpretation of an intention. 

In conversation it is shown by a look, a gesture, an emphasis, or tone of voice. In writing, mere word, phrase, or sentence will put a meaning on a contract which may control implications of law, and which will give it a direction and application contrary to what it otherwise would have received." 

§ 350. Interpretation and Construction Defined. 

Interpretation consists in determining the meaning of the words as used by the parties; while construction embraces the thought or intention of the parties as gathered from, not only the words employed, but the whole transaction, including subjects that lie beyond the direct expressions of the contract. Construction includes interpretation. Therefore, a contract must be interpreted before it can be construed. However, the rules of interpretation and construction are necessarily more or less blended, since in determining the meaning of a word, it is sometimes necessary to appeal to the context, just as in determining the meaning of the context, the court finds it necessary to place itself in the position of the author when he used the particular words. Hull Coal & Coke Co. v. Empire Coal & 

Coke Co., 113 Fed. 256; Ullman v. Chicago & Northwestern 

Ry. Co., 112 Wis. 150, 88 N. W. 41, 56 L. R. A. 246. In 

Bloomer v. Todd, 3 Wash. T. 599, 19 Pac. 135, 1 L. R. A. 111, the Court said: "Interpretation differs from construction in this: that it is used for the purpose of ascertaining the true sense of any form of words, while construction involves the drawing of conclusions regarding subjects that are not always included in the direct expression."

Does a clause in a lease which provides that the lessee "is given first privilege to buy said property" give to the lessee an absolute option to purchase the property or merely the right to buy prior to any one else, if the owner sells? 

R. I. Realty Co. v. Terrell, 254 N. Y. 121, 172 N. E. 262.

§ 351. Meaning of Words. 

Words are to be construed in their grammatical and ordinary sense, unless such construction would lead to some manifest absurdity or to some inconsistency or would defeat the purpose of the agreement. While the courts cannot substitute a contract different from the one actually made, yet they will not allow the purpose of a contract to be defeated where, by the application of certain legal principles of construction, they can ascertain the intention of the parties as expressed in the contract. Manson v. Curtis, 223 N. Y. 313, 119 N. E. 559. "Unless, therefore, the terms of a written instrument have generally, in respect to the subject matter, as, by the known usage of trade or the like, acquired a peculiar sense, distinct from the popular sense of the same words, or unless the context points out that, in the particular instance, and in order to effectuate the immediate intention of the parties, they should be understood in a peculiar sense, they are to be understood in their plain, ordinary and popular sense." Pohalski v. Mutual Life Insurance Co., 45 How. 

Pr. 504, aff'd 56 N. Y. 640. If the word in question has two well-recognized meanings, one inclusive and the other exclusive, the meaning intended must be determined by the context. Chicago Great Western R. R. Co. v. Kansas City, North Western R. R. Co., 75 Kan. 167, 88 Pac. 1085. 

§ 352. Preliminary Negotiations. 

While the parol evidence rule excludes all preliminary and concurrent negotiations as a part of the contract, yet these may be resorted to for the purpose of explaining certain terms of a contract where such terms are susceptible of various interpretations. United States v. Bethlehem 

Steel Co., 205 U. S. 105, 51 Law. Ed. 731, 27 Sup. Ct. Rep. 

450. For instance, where a reaper was sold by a written contract, the buyer, who alleged a breach of warranty, was permitted to testify what the plaintiff said before the sale was consummated, for the purpose of explaining the mean-

ing of the word "team" as used in the contract. Ganson v. Madigan, 15 Wis. 144, 82 Am. Dec. 659. So, too, where goods were sold under a contract to pay "ruling market rates," and there were two rates, one for goods bought of importers, and another for goods bought of jobbers, evidence of the conversation of the parties and the surrounding circumstances is admissible for the purpose of showing which rate was intended by the parties. Manchester 

Paper Co. v. Moore, 104 N. Y. 680, 10 N. E. 861. But such oral statements or declarations are not admissible for the purpose of showing an intention not therein expressed. Tuttle v. Burgett's Administrator, 53 Ohio St. 

498, 42 N. E. 427, 30 L. R. A. 214. 

§ 353. Surrounding Circumstances. 

When the language used in a contract is susceptible of more than one interpretation, the court will look not only to the language of the contract and the subject matter but also to the surrounding circumstances and the object to be accomplished. It is a cardinal rule of construction that, in order to arrive at the intention of the parties, the contract itself must be read in the light of the circumstances under which it was executed; and parol evidence of extrinsic circumstances is admissible not for the purpose of varying the terms of the contract but to show the sense and understanding in which the language was used. Fleischman v. Furgueson, 223 N. Y. 235, 119 N. E. 400; Smith v. 

Webb, 176 Ala. 596, 58 So. 913, 40 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1191. "Contracts are not to be interpreted by giving a strict and rigid meaning to general words or expressions without regard to the surrounding circumstances or the apparent purpose which the parties sought to accomplish." Robertson v. Ongley Electric Co., 146 N. Y. 20, 40 N. E. 390. Thus, where a marine insurance policy provided that the risk was "to be suspended while the vessel is at Baker's Island loading," the question arose as to whether the parties intended this clause to mean while the vessel was at Baker's Island

for the purpose of loading, or while it was at the island actually loading. The Court ruled that evidence was admissible to show the dangerous condition of the water surrounding the island and that it was during the entire time that the vessel was in these dangerous waters for the purpose of loading that the parties intended the risk to be suspended and not merely while loading actually was going on. Reed v. Merchants' Mutual Insurance Co., 95 U. S. 

23, 24 Law. Ed. 348. 

§ 354. Meaning of Words Controlled by Usage. 

Usages, such as those of a trade, are admissible as evidence to show the special meaning of words. Thus, parol evidence is admissible to show the meaning of "on approval" in the diamond trade; "order" in a contract of agency for the sale of books; "for the season" in a contract of employment; "mill culls" and "shipping culls" in a contract for the sale thereof; "factor" in a contract for the disposition of goods. Smith v. Clews, 114 N. Y. 190, 21 

N. E. 160, 4 L. R. A. 392; Newhall v. Appleton, 114 N. Y. 

140, 21 N. E. 105, 3 L. R. A. 859; Shoyer v. Edmund 

Wright-Ginsberg Co., 240 N. Y. 223, 148 N. E. 328. In like manner, it is competent to prove the meaning of the terms in contracts by telegraph when a cipher code is used. 

Thus, the meaning of "Buy three May" may be explained to mean 3,000 bushels of May wheat. Without such evidence, a contract in cipher could have no meaning. Carland v. Western Union Telegraph Co., 118 Mich. 369, 76 

N. W. 762, 43 L. R. A. 280; Weld v. Postal Telegraph Co., 199 N. Y. 88, 92 N. E. 415.

In arriving at the "net profits" of a business, the customary procedure in any given business must be followed in arriving at the amount of such profits. Bolles v. Scheer, 225 

N. Y. 118, 121 N. E. 771. Likewise, in a contract for the delivery of paper of a certain quality, evidence is admissible to show that there was a trade usage as to variation and thickness of the paper. Oswego Falls Pulp & Paper Co. v.

71 et

Stecher Lithograph Co., 215 N. Y. 98, 109 N. E. 92, L. R. A. 

1916B 1257. The Personal Property Law, section 125, sub division 4, expressly provides that, in reference to delivery of the proper quantity of goods, the statutory provisions contained in such section "are subject to usage of trade, * * * between the parties." Mutual Chemical Co. v. 

Marden, Orth & Hastings Co., Inc., 235 N. Y. 145, 139 N. E. 221. § 355.

Parol Evidence Rule. 

The parol evidence rule excludes evidence of all previous and contemporaneous negotiations which are offered for the purpose of adding to or varying the terms of a written contract which appears to be complete. But this rule does not operate to exclude parol evidence when such evidence is offered for the purpose of showing a non-contractual relationship because the contract has had no legal inception. 

For instance, parol evidence is admissible to show (a) want of consideration; (b) fraud; (c) want of consent, by reason of mistake as to the nature of the contract, the person with whom the contract was made, or the subject matter of the contract; (d) illegality; (e) material alteration; or (f) to show that the enforcement of the contract was dependent upon a condition precedent, which condition has not been fulfilled. Richardson on Evidence, 4th Ed., sec. 426; 

Grannis v. Stevens, 216 N. Y. 583, 111 N. E. 263; Thomas v. 

Scutt, 127 N. Y. 133, 27 N. E. 961; Reynolds v. Robinson, 110 N. Y. 654, 16 N. E. 127. § 356.

Custom and Usage as Part of Contract. 

With certain well-defined exceptions which are not within the scope of this work, the parol evidence rule excludes proof of prior and contemporaneous negotiations as affecting a written contract. St. Regis Paper Co. v. Hubbs & 

Hastings Paper Co., 235 N. Y. 30, 138 N. E. 495. However, parol evidence of a general custom and usage is admissible. 

This is based upon the theory that such custom is a part of

the law of the land, and the law is a part of the contract, as much so as if incorporated therein. Therefore, the existing 

I statutes and the settled common law principles in force at the time and place where the contract is made become a part of the contract and must be read into it. Walls v. 

Bailey, 49 N. Y. 464, 10 Am. Rep. 407; Pennell v. Delta 

Transportation Co., 94 Mich. 247, 53 N. W. 1049; People v. Metropolitan Surety Co., 211 N. Y. 107, 105 N. E. 99;

Armour Packing Co. v. United States, 209 U. S. 56, 52 Law. 

Ed. 681, 28 Sup. Ct. Rep. 428. 

§ 357. Distinction between General and Local Custom. 

Where the contract involves a local usage or the usage of a particular trade or profession, the defendant may show that he had no knowledge of the existence of such usage, and thus defeat the presumption that he contracted with reference to it. The authorities on this question are collated and discussed in Walls v. Bailey, 49 N. Y. 464, 10 Am. Rep. 

407; Miller v. Fischer, 142 App. Div. 172, 126 N. Y. S. 996. 

§ 358. Requisites of Custom and Usage. 

The common law itself is based upon customs established by long usage. A universal custom becomes a part of the common law. But to establish and give validity to a custom as part of a contract, it must be: a. 

General and uniform; b. Not inconsistent with the terms of the contract; C.

Not opposed to any statute or principle of the common law; d. Reasonable. 

Wadley v. Davis, 63 Barb. 500; Hatch v. Luckman, 64 

Misc. 508, 118 N. Y. S. 689, aff'd 155 App. Div. 765, 140 

N. Y. S. 1123.

To illustrate: an alleged custom of stenographers to charge twenty cents per folio for copies of their minutes is not established by evidence that "the custom is to pay from

fifteen to twenty cents a folio," since such evidence negatives the requirement of uniformity. Cavanagh v. O'Neill, 20 Misc. 233, 45 N. Y. S. 789. Also, where a contract provides for the delivery of 8,000 barrels of cement, each barrel to weigh 396 pounds gross, evidence of a usage to deliver less than this for a barrel is inadmissible, because it is inconsistent with the terms of the contract. Richard v. Hoebler, 36 App. Div. 94, 55 N. Y. S. 583. Likewise, the custom of sawmill owners to keep the culls, clippings, and slabs of their customers' logs which they saw into lumber is unreasonable and will not be sanctioned by the courts, although this custom has been uniformly acquiesced in from time immemorial. Wadley v. Davis, supra. 

§ 359. Matter in Letterheads and Billheads. 

The printed matter in letterheads and billheads, or on the margin of paper on which a contract is written, is not a part of the contract unless referred to in the agreement. 

Sturtevant Co. v. Fireproof Film Co., 216 N. Y. 199, 110 

N. E. 440, L. R. A. 1916D 1069. See section 15, supra. 

§ 360. Written and Printed Matter. 

Where part of a contract is written and part is printed, the words in writing will control the construction of the contract. The reason for this rule is that the written words are the immediate language and terms selected by the parties themselves for the expression of their meaning, while the printed form is intended for general use without reference to particular objects and aims. Hill v. Miller, 76 N. Y. 

32; Heyn v. New York Life Insurance Co., 192 N. Y. 1, 84 N. E. 725. § 361. Grammar.

It is a legal maxim that bad grammar does not vitiate a contract. Therefore, grammatical inaccuracies are immaterial, provided the intention of the parties is clearly indicated by the contract. Castelli v. Burns, 156 App. Div. 200,

140 N. Y. S. 1057. To give effect to the intention of the parties, the word "and" may be read "or." Jackson v. 

Topping, 1 Wend. (N.Y.) 388, 19 Am. Dec. 515. The converse is equally true. The word "or" may be read "and." 

Bettman v. Harness, 42 W. Va. 433, 26 S. E. 271, 36 L. R. A. 

566. In Potthoff v. Safety Armorite Conduit Co., 143 App. 

Div. 161, 127 N. Y. S. 994, the Court said: "To carry out the intention of a contract, words may be transposed, rejected, or supplied, if necessary, to make its meaning more clear." $ 362. Punctuation.

The want of proper punctuation will not vitiate a contract. It may be considered when the meaning is doubtful, but it cannot control if the meaning otherwise plainly appears. Kinkele v. Wilson, 151 N. Y. 269, 45 N. E. 869; 

Holmes v. Phoenix Insurance Co., 98 Fed. 240, 47 L. R. A. 308.

In the case of In re Brooklyn Elevated R. R. Co., 125 

N. Y. 434, 26 N. E. 474, the Court transposed a comma in construing a statute, but declared that "in so doing we violate no canon of construction as we think the legislative sense requires it, and such liberty with punctuation is frequently taken by the courts." § 363.

Construction To Uphold Contract. 

Where a contract is open to two constructions, one legal and the other illegal, the courts will adopt that construction which will impute to the parties a legal intention.
