Powers v. Clarke, 127 N. Y. 417, 28 N. E. 402. "The written undertakings of parties are always to be construed so as to be upheld and enforced, instead of rendering them nugatory, if it can be done without violence to the terms employed." Auburn City Bank v. Leonard, 40 Barb. 119. 

§ 364. Construction When Party Uses Ambiguous Words. 

Ambiguous words will be construed against the party

using them. Liverpool, London & Globe Insurance Co. v. 

Kearney, 180 U. S. 132, 45 Law. Ed. 460, 21 Sup. Ct. Rep. 

326. "The reason of the rule that the language of an instrument is to be construed against the person who proposes it rather than against the person who is invited to accept it, is that men are supposed to take care of themselves, and that he who chooses the words by which a right is given ought to be held to the strict interpretation of them, rather than he who only accepts them." Gillet v. Bank of 

America, 160 N. Y. 549, 55 N. E. 292. 

§ 365. In General-Time of Performance. 

The time within which a contract is to be performed may or may not be of the essence of the contract. Where the parties have expressly stipulated that time is to be regarded as of the essence of the contract, there is no room for doubt. 

The question becomes important when there is a failure to perform the contract within the agreed time. 

Where time is of the essence of the contract, the failure by one party to perform within the prescribed time discharges the contractual obligation of the other party. But where time is neither expressly nor by implication of the essence of the contract, the failure or inability of one party to perform within the time designated gives the other no right to rescind or abandon the contract but only the right to recover damages in consequence of the delay. As to whether the date set for the closing of the title in the sale of real property is of the essence of the contract, see Ballen v. 

Potter, 251 N. Y. 224, 167 N. E. 424. It is a well-settled rule that the breach of an independent covenant which does not go to the whole consideration of the contract, but is subordinate and incidental to its main purpose, does not entitle the injured party to rescind the contract. The remedy of the injured party in such a case is merely an action for damages sustained by delay in performance. A few hours' delay in the performance of a contract may be most vital; while on the other hand it may be of little or no conse-

quence. For instance, a delay of two hours in making a delivery of programs for an entertainment might defeat the very purpose for which the programs were printed; while a few hours' delay in the delivery of other printed matter would result in no loss. Mercantile National Bank v. Heinze, 75 Misc. 551, 135 N. Y. S. 962; Taylor v. Goelet, 208 N. Y. 253, 101 N. E. 867. A frequently cited English case on this point is Bettini v. Gye, 1 Q. B. Div. 183 (1876).

Bettini entered into a contract with Gye, the director of Italian opera in London, for his services as a singer in operas. He agreed to be in London for rehearsals at least six days before the commencement of his engagement, but arrived only two days before his engagement commenced. 

Gye thereupon repudiated the contract. The Court held, in interpreting the contract, that the provision as to rehearsals was not vital to the contract, and, therefore, was not a condition, but merely a collateral undertaking, and its breach did not operate as a discharge of the contract but gave to Gye merely a cause of action for compensation in damages. 

Holt v. United Security Life Insurance & Trust Co., 76 

N. J. L. 585, 72 Atl. 301, 21 L. R. A. (N.S.) 691 and note. § 366.

Performance When Time Not Fixed by Contract. 

An implication of law is as much a part of a written contract as if embodied in the contract itself. Therefore, a contract, silent as to the time of performance, and hence, by implication of law to be performed within a reasonable time, cannot be varied by oral testimony showing that a definite time of performance was agreed upon by the parties. Richardson on Evidence, 4th Ed., sec. 423; Stange v. Wilson, 17 Mich. 342; Morowsky v. Rohrig, 4 Misc. 167, 23 N. Y. S. 880. § 367. 

Employment Contracts at Will.

Where an employment contract does not specify the time or duration of the contract of hiring but provides for weekly, monthly, or yearly payments, the New York rule

is that the employment is at will and that the employer has the right to discharge, and the employee the right to leave, at any time. For instance, a contract to pay one $2,000 a year for services, or at a salary of $2,000 a year, is not a contract for a year, but a contract to pay at the rate of $2,000 a year for services actually rendered. Watson v. Gugino, 204 N. Y. 535, 98 N. E. 18, 39 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

1090; Cuppy v. Stollwerck Bros., 216 N. Y. 591, 111 N. E. 249. § 368.

Time Fixed by Extrinsic Act. 

The time of performance may be fixed with reference to the doing of some specified act. But the act is not necessarily a condition precedent to the time of performance. 

It has been held, as a matter of construction, in certain cases, that it merely fixes the time of performance, which is construed to be within a reasonable time. This principle has been applied to a promise to pay "on the sale" of certain property, or "as soon as the crops can be sold," or "as soon as convenient." Payment in such cases does not depend upon the happening of the contingency but is made absolute after the lapse of a reasonable time. Simon v. 

Etgen, 213 N. Y. 589, 107 N. E. 1066. The weight of authority, however, supports the rule that, where a promise is made by a debtor to pay "as soon as he can," "when able," or "when in his power," the promise is conditional and does not become absolute merely upon the lapse of a reasonable time, and it is incumbent upon the creditor to show that the condition has been fulfilled; in other words, that the debtor is now able to pay. Tebo v. Robinson, 100 

N. Y. 27, 2 N. E. 383; Benton v. Benton, 78 Kan. 366, 97 

Pac. 378, 27 L. R. A. (N.S.) 300 and note. § 369. 

Waiver of Time of Performance.

The parties may waive the right to insist upon performance within the time fixed by the contract, and when so waived, the time fixed is no longer an essential element of

the contract. Performance is then required only within a reasonable time. But time may be again made the essence by a reasonable notice demanding performance and stating that the contract will be rescinded if the notice is not complied with. Taylor v. Goelet, 208 N. Y. 253, 101 N. E. 867. 

§ 370. Effect of Notice When No Time Fixed for Per formance.

If the time of performance is left indefinite in the contract, neither party can thereafter rescind the contract on account of delay in performance without notice to the other party requiring performance within a reasonable time specified in the notice and stating that the contract will be abrogated in case of non-compliance with such notice. Taylor v. Goelet, 208 N. Y. 253, 101 N. E. 867. 

§ 371. When Time Is Not of Essence of Contract. 

In contracts for the construction of buildings and railroads, and other contracts involving work and labor, time is not generally of the essence of the contract. This is so, even though the contract fixes the time of performance. 

In such cases, the aggrieved party will not be justified in repudiating the entire contract because of delay in performance. His sole remedy is an action for damages for the delay. Thurston v. Arnold, 43 Iowa 43; Beck & Pauli 

Lithographing Co. v. Colorado Milling & Elevator Co., 52 

Fed. 700; Brady v. Oliver, 125 Tenn. 595, 147 S. W. 1135, 41 L. R. A. (N.S.) 60 and note. However, the duty of the landowner to make payments agreed upon as the work progresses generally goes to the essence of the contract. 

The contractor can hardly be expected to finance the operation to completion without receiving the stipulated payments as the work progresses. "In such cases a substantial compliance as to advance payments is a condition precedent to the contractor's obligation to proceed." Guerini Stone 

Co. v. P. J. Carlin Construction Co., 248 U. S. 334, 63 Law. 

Ed. 275, 39 Sup. Ct. Rep. 102.

§ 372. Time as Essence of Contract in Equity. 

In equity, as a general rule, time is not of the essence of the contract, but it may be made so by express stipulation or by circumstances indicating that the parties require prompt performance at the time stipulated. At law, as a rule, the promisee acquires no interest in the property under an executory contract until he performs or tenders performance; while in equity he acquires an interest in the property when the contract of sale is made. Hence, in the latter, there is less reason for insisting on punctual performance. Phillips & Colby Construction Co. v. Seymour, 91 U. S. 646, 23 Law. Ed. 341; King v. Connors, 222 Mass. 

261, 110 N. E. 289.

DAMAGES 

§ 373. 

Liquidated Damages.

When the parties have ascertained and assessed the damages and have embodied the provisions concerning them in the contract itself, the damages are said to be liquidated. "The meaning of the words 'liquidated damages' is of wellunderstood and recognized significance. These words are used in reference to the breach of a contract or the nonperformance of a duty as expressing a fixed sum which is agreed upon between the parties as the ascertained damage which the one is to receive and the other to pay because of the default." United States Fidelity & Guaranty Co. v. 

United States, 151 Fed. 534, aff'd 209 U. S. 306, 52 Law. 

Ed. 804, 28 Sup. Ct. Rep. 537. 

§ 374. Unliquidated Damages. 

If the contract is silent as to the damages that shall be assessed in case of its breach, the damages are said to be unliquidated, because the right to ascertain them is not fixed by the parties to the contract but is left to be determined by the court and jury. "A demand is not liquidated even if it appears that something is due, unless it appears

how much is due." Nassoiy v. Tomlinson, 148 N. Y. 326, 42 N. E. 715. 

§ 375. Penalty Distinguished from Liquidated Damages. 

The enforcement of a provision in a contract that the party in default shall pay or forfeit to the other party a certain sum of money depends upon whether the sum named is a penalty or liquidated damages. If it is in the nature of a penalty, the courts will not enforce it. The party aggrieved will be entitled to actual damages only. 

But, if the stipulated amount to be paid or forfeited is liquidated damages, it may be recovered. The authorities are in hopeless confusion on the question as to whether a stipulated amount shall be treated as a penalty or as liquidated damages. They furnish "conspicuous examples of judicial efforts to make for parties wiser and more prudent contracts than they had made for themselves." Kemp v. 

Knickerbocker Ice Co., 69 N. Y. 45; Mosler Safe Co. v. 

Maiden Lane Safe Deposit Co., 199 N. Y. 479, 93 N. E. 81, 37 L. R. A. (N.S.) 363. 

In Lenco, Inc. v. Hirschfeld, 247 N. Y. 44, 159 N. E. 718, the Court held that security deposited by a tenant for the faithful performance of his lease, which contained a clause providing for the forfeiture of the security as liquidated damages for the breach of any covenant, irrespective of any loss that the landlord might sustain, was a penalty and unenforceable notwithstanding it was denominated by the parties as liquidated damages. 

§ 376. Rules To Determine Nature of Provision. 

The following rules are resorted to by the courts to assist them in determining whether the sum named is a penalty or liquidated damages: 

1. It is wholly immaterial whether the sum fixed upon is stated in the contract to be a "penalty" or "liquidated damages." If it be designated "liquidated damages" and it is, in fact, a penalty, it will be so regarded notwithstand-

1 ing its designation. On the other hand, if the parties designate a sum a "penalty," it may be treated as liquidated damages where there is a manifest difficulty in ascertaining the actual damage sustained. Pastor v. Solomon, 26 

Misc. 125, 55 N. Y. S. 956. If the rule were otherwise, the prohibition against a penalty could be readily avoided. 

Ayers v. Houston, 193 App. Div. 145, 183 N. Y. S. 808. 

2. The courts will consider in each case: a. The subject matter; b. The nature of the agreement; and C.

The intention of the parties. 

The intention of the parties is to be gathered from the language used in making the contract, read in the light of circumstances at the time, and if the measure of damages, as agreed upon, is not so excessive as to shock the moral sense of man, the courts will hold the parties to their agreement. Curtis v. Van Berg, 161 N. Y. 47, 55 N. E. 398; 

Ward v. Hudson River Building Co., 125 N. Y. 230, 26 

N. E. 256.

The intention of the parties is the most important criterion. The court is not to be wiser than the parties. As a rule, therefore, when the parties say that a sum is to be payable as liquidated damages, they will be deemed to mean what they say. Guerin v. Stacy, 175 Mass. 595, 56 N. E. 

892. And a disproportion between the sum fixed and the loss is not enough to induce the court to ignore the agreement by which the parties fixed their own estimate of damages. Sun Printing & Publishing Assn. v. Moore, 183 

U. S. 642, 46 Law. Ed. 366, 22 Sup. Ct. Rep. 240; Fleisher v. Friob, 97 Misc. 343, 161 N. Y. S. 940, aff'd 177 App. Div. 

921, 164 N. Y. S. 1092. 

3. If the matter of the contract is of certain value, a sum greatly in excess of that value is likely to be held to be a penalty; if the matter is of uncertain value, the courts are inclined to treat the sum fixed as liquidated damages. "Where an amount, stipulated as liquidated damages, would

302

be grossly in excess of the actual damages, they [the courts] have leaned to hold it a penalty. Where the actual damages were uncertain and difficult of ascertainment, they have leaned to hold the stipulated amount to have been intended as liquidated damages." Kemp v. Knickerbocker Ice Co., 69 N. Y. 45; Chicago, Burlington & Quincy R. R. Co. v.

Dockery, 195 Fed. 221. In accordance with this principle, where a greater sum of money is to be paid out upon the failure to pay a lesser sum with interest, it is construed to be a penalty. Legal interest on the money is all that the law allows the plaintiff to recover as damages. London v. 

Taxing District, 104 U. S. 771, 26 Law. Ed. 923; Niver v. 

Rossman, 18 Barb. 50. 

4. If some of the terms of a contract are of certain value and some are not, and a gross sum is to be paid in the event of a breach of any one of them, it will be regarded as a penalty. Niver v. Rossman, supra; Chicago, Burlington & Quincy R. R. Co. v. Dockery, supra; Seidlitz v. Auerbach, 230 N. Y. 167, 129 N. E. 461. 

5. When it is provided that a sum deposited in part performance of a contract is to be forfeited upon failure of the other party to complete it, if the sum is excessive, it is a penalty. Whether it be a penalty or liquidated damages is governed by the same general rules that control in other cases. Chaude v. Shepard, 122 N. Y. 397, 25 N. E. 358.

However, in the case of executory contracts for the sale of real or personal property, the buyer cannot recover a partial payment which he has made if the seller is not in default and the buyer fails to complete his payments. Chaude v. Shepard, supra; Beveridge v. West Side Construction 

Co., 130 App. Div. 139, 114 N. Y. S. 521. 

6. A stipulation for the payment of a sum of money for the breach of a contract in restraint of trade is generally held to be liquidated damages, because the damage in such a case is wholly uncertain and incapable of being determined except by conjecture. Tode v. Gross, 127 N. Y. 480, 28 N. E. 469, 13 L. R. A. 652. "A more suitable case, for

the liquidation of the damages by the parties themselves, can scarcely be imagined." Nobles v. Bates, 7 Cowen (N.Y.) 307; 24 L. R. A. (N.S.) 930, note. 

7. The damages for non-acceptance or refusal to deliver merchandise or personal property are, ordinarily, the difference between the contract price and the market price at the time and place of delivery. Therefore, if the parties agree that the buyer shall pay, as liquidated damages, a sum greatly in excess of such an amount, it will be regarded as a penalty. Tufts v. Bennett, 163 Mass. 398, 40 N. E. 172; 

Bridgford v. Crocker, 60 N. Y. 627; 42 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

673, note. § 377.

Effect of Determination. 

Where the sum agreed upon for the breach of a contract is therein termed liquidated damages, and is such in reality, the plaintiff is limited to the stipulated amount, even though his actual loss is much greater. The stipulation is mutually obligatory. Winch v. Mutual Benefit Ice Co., 86 N. Y. 

618. On the other hand, if the sum stipulated is held to be a penalty, it is binding on neither party. In such cases, the plaintiff may recover more than the sum stipulated. "Parties are not released from performing their agreement by inserting a penalty for non-performance." Noyes v. Phillips, 60 N. Y. 408. 

§ 378. Damages Personal Property Law. 

The measure of damages in an action involving a breach of contract to sell personal property is now regulated by statute. The Personal Property Law, section 144, provides: "1. Where, under a contract to sell or a sale, the property in the goods has passed to the buyer, and the buyer wrongfully neglects or refuses to pay for the goods according to the terms of the contract or the sale, the seller may maintain an action against him for the price of the goods. "2. Where, under a contract to sell or a sale, the price is

payable on a day certain, irrespective of delivery or of transfer of title, and the buyer wrongfully neglects or refuses to pay such price, the seller may maintain an action for the price, although the property in the goods has not passed, and the goods have not been appropriated to the contract. But it shall be a defense to such an action that the seller at any time before judgment in such action has manifested an inability to perform the contract or the sale on his part or intention not to perform it. "3. Although the property in the goods has not passed, if they cannot readily be resold for a reasonable price, and if the provisions of subdivision four of section one hundred and forty-five [infra] are not applicable, the seller may offer to deliver the goods to the buyer, and if the buyer refuses to receive them, may notify the buyer that the goods are thereafter held by the seller as bailee for the buyer. Thereafter the seller may treat the goods as the buyer's and may maintain an action for the price."

The Personal Property Law, section 145, provides: 

"1. Where the buyer wrongfully neglects or refuses to accept and pay for the goods, the seller may maintain an action against him for damages for non-acceptance. The measure of damages is the estimated loss directly and naturally resulting, in the ordinary course of events, from the buyer's breach of contract. "3. Where there is an available market for the goods in question, the measure of damages is, in the absence of special circumstances, showing proximate damage of a greater amount, the difference between the contract price and the market or current price at the time or times when the goods ought to have been accepted, or if no time was fixed for acceptance, then at the time of the refusal to accept. "4. If, while labor or expense of material amount are necessary on the part of the seller to enable him to fulfill his obligations under the contract to sell or the sale, the buyer repudiates the contract or the sale, or notifies the

? seller to proceed no further therewith, the buyer shall be liable to the seller for no greater damages than the seller would have suffered if he did nothing toward carrying out the contract or the sale after receiving notice of the buyer's repudiation or countermand. The profit the seller would have made if the contract or the sale had been fully performed shall be considered in estimating such damages." 

The Personal Property Law, section 148, provides: 

"1. Where the property in the goods has not passed to the buyer, and the seller wrongfully neglects or refuses to deliver the goods, the buyer may maintain an action against the seller for damages for nondelivery. "2. The measure of damages is the loss directly and naturally resulting in the ordinary course of events from the seller's breach of contract. "3. Where there is an available market for the goods in question, the measure of damages, in the absence of special circumstances showing proximate damages of a greater amount, is the difference between the contract price and the market or current price of the goods at the time or times when they ought to have been delivered, or, if no time was fixed, then at the time of the refusal to deliver." § 379.

Damages-Real Property. 

There is a conflict of authority in this country as to the measure of damages in an action by a prospective buyer for the breach of a contract for the sale of real estate. The 

United States Supreme Court allows the same measure of damages in such an action as in actions involving the sale of personal property. Hopkins v. Lee, 6 Wheat. (U.S.) 

109, 5 Law. Ed. 218.

However, in New York, the rule is that if the seller has acted in good faith and is unable to give to the buyer a good title to the land, owing to a defect in the title thereto, the buyer cannot recover substantial damages. Pumpelly v. Phelps, 40 N. Y. 59, 100 Am. Dec. 463; Margraf v. Muir, 57 N. Y. 155.

In case of breach by the buyer, where the seller is able and willing to give to the buyer a good title to the real estate, the measure of damages is the same as in actions involving personal property; namely, the difference between the contract price and the value of the land. Stevenson v. Maxwell, 2 N. Y. 408. The seller cannot recover the full purchase price. Bensinger v. Erhardt, 74 App. 

Div. 169, 77 N. Y. S. 577.

CHAPTER XVIII DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY OPERATION OF LAW § 380. 

Modes of Discharge.

The term "discharge of contract" relates to the modes by which the contractual tie may be severed and the parties wholly freed from their obligations. The term also includes the method of discharging a right of action resulting from a breach of a contract. The various modes of discharging a contract may be classified as follows: 

1. By operation of law; 

2. By agreement; 

3. By performance; or 

4. By breach. § 381.

By Operation of Law. 

The term "discharge by operation of law" includes the following methods of discharge: 

1. By impossibility of performance; 

2. By merger; 

3. By alteration of instrument; or 

4. By bankruptcy. 

§ 382. By Impossibility of Performance. 

When a person seeks to avoid a contract on the ground that its performance is impossible, it becomes necessary to distinguish between an impossibility of performance existing at the time the contract was made and an impossibility arising subsequent to the making thereof. 

Impossibility of performance existing at the time of making the contract may be divided into: 

1. Objective impossibility: a. Physical; b. Legal. 

2. Subjective impossibility. 307

§ 383. Existing Impossibility-Objective-Physical. 

Where the performance of a promise is impossible because of facts existing at the time the promise was made and of which neither party had knowledge, performance is excused. Thus, a contract for the sale of an article which, unknown to either party, has ceased to exist creates no duty of performance. A contract made under such circumstances is, of course, also void on the ground of mistake preventing consent. Uniform Sales Act, sec. 7; Personal

Property Law, sec. 88; Duncan v. New York Mutual Insurance Co., 138 N. Y. 88, 33 N. E. 730, 20 L. R. A. 386.

Where the defendant agreed to mine, within a certain tract of land, not less than 1,500 tons of ore annually and to pay therefor thirty five cents per ton, and it was later found that there was not so much ore in the land, the Court held that, since the parties were mutually mistaken as to the quantity of ore in the land, the contractual obligation ceased because of an impossibility of performance that existed at the time the contract was made. Muhlenberg v. 

Henning, 116 Pa. St. 138, 9 Atl. 144. 

Similarly, in Lord Clifford v. Watts, 5 C. P. (Eng.) 577 (1870), where the defendant, a lessee, covenanted to dig from the land in question not less than 1,000 tons of potter's clay annually, it was held that the fact that there was not so much as 1,000 tons of clay in the land was a good defense to an action for breach of the covenant. Likewise, where the performance of a contract for the excavation of a canal was rendered impossible by the condition of the subsoil, the contractors were excused from performance. 

Kinzer Construction Co. v. State, 204 N. Y. 381, 97 N. E. 

871; Virginia Iron Coal & Coke Co. v. Graham, 124 Va. 

692, 98 S. E. 659. See, also, Clarksville Land Co. v. Harriman, 68 N. H. 374, 44 Atl. 527. 

§ 384. Existing Impossibility-Objective-Legal. 

A promise to do something which the law forbids cannot be enforced. However, it is well settled that an unmar-

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY OPERATION OF LAW 309 

1 3 ried woman may recover damages from a married man for breach of a contract to marry, provided she had no knowledge of the promisor's existing marriage at the time of the alleged engagement. One theory of liability in such a case is that the defendant impliedly warrants that he has the legal capacity to make the contract in question. Waddell v. 

Wallace, 32 Okla. 140, 121 Pac. 245. The position has also been taken that the basis of liability in such cases is the fraud practiced on the plaintiff, giving rise to liability in tort. Cammerer v. Muller, 60 Hun 578, 14 N. Y. S. 511, aff'd 133 N. Y. 623, 30 N. E. 1147. 

§ 385. Subjective Impossibility. 

A subjective impossibility is one that is wholly due to the inability of the individual promisor. Performance is impossible for him, but not for others. It neither prevents the formation of a contract nor excuses performance thereunder. An application of this rule arises where a debtor promises to pay a sum of money which he neither has nor can procure. Money exists everywhere, and it is merely the present situation of the debtor that prevents him from procuring it. His individual inability is no excuse for nonpayment. Similarly, that the lessee has met with financial reverses is no excuse for not paying rent. This must be deemed to result from his own improvidence. Taylor v. 

Syme, 162 N. Y. 513, 57 N. E. 83. 

§ 386. Subsequent Impossibility-Common Law Rule. 

Impossibility of performance which arises subsequent to the formation of a contract does not, as a rule, excuse its performance. "When a party by his own contract creates a duty or charge upon himself, he is bound to a possible performance of it, because he promised it, and did not shield himself by proper conditions or qualifications." 

Cameron-Hawn Realty Co. v. City of Albany, 207 N. Y. 377, 101 N. E. 162, 49 L. R. A. (N.S.) 922; Strochak v. Glass

Paper Making Supplies Co., 239 App. Div. 312, 267 N. Y. S. 282.

It is necessary to distinguish between cases in which a duty is imposed by law and those in which a duty is imposed by contract. An act of God will excuse non-performance of a duty imposed by law, but not one imposed by contract; for where a party, by his own contract, creates a duty or charge upon himself, he is bound to make it good, notwithstanding any accident or delay by an act of God, as provision for such contingency might have been embodied in the contract. Anderson v. May, 50 Minn. 280, 52 N. W. 530, 17 L. R. A. 555; Tompkins v. Dudley, 25 

N. Y. 272, 82 Am. Dec. 349; Runyan v. Culver, 168 Ky. 

45, 181 S. W. 640, L. R. A. 1916F 3 and note; Mardment v. 

Charles A. Kraise Milling Co., 225 App. Div. 492, 233 

N. Y. S. 621.

The following may serve as illustrations of this doctrine: 

A person who agreed to ship three hundred barrels of apples at a certain time was not excused from a performance of his contract because the harbor froze and thus prevented the sailing of the ship. Kribbs v. Jones, 44 Md. 396.

Performance of a contract to build a house for another within a certain time was not excused by the fact that there was a latent defect in the soil, in consequence of which the walls sank and cracked and had to be taken down and rebuilt on artificial foundations. Dermott v. Jones, 2 Wall. (U.S.) 1, 17 Law. Ed. 762.

Where one agreed to build a schoolhouse by a certain time, he was held not to be relieved from liability on the contract because of the fact that, when nearly completed, the building was destroyed by lightning. School District v. 

Dauchy, 25 Conn. 530, 68 Am. Dec. 371.

Failure to perform a contract to dig a well was held not to be excused by accidental injury to the tools of the contractor. Janes v. Scott, 59 Pa. St. 178, 98 Am. Dec. 328.

An agreement to carry goods from B to X within twenty-

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY OPERATION OF LAW 311 

six days was not discharged by the fact that the canal upon which they were to be transported was made impassable by a freshet. Harmony v. Bingham, 12 N. Y. 99, 62 Am. 

Dec. 142. 

§ 387. Subsequent Impossibility-Implied Conditions or 

Exceptions.

The modern decisions reveal a tendency to extend the "exceptions"-not as "exceptions" but as implied conditions of the contract-to the rule that a subsequent impossibility will not discharge a contract. In New York, the courts invariably refer to the "exceptions" as implied conditions. 

Thus, where performance of a contract is rendered legally impossible by a change in domestic law, the promisor is excused from his duty of performance on the ground that there was an implied condition in the contract that such change in the law should discharge the parties from further liability. Dexter v. Norton, 47 N. Y. 62; Cameron-Hawn 

Realty Co. v. City of Albany, 207 N. Y. 377, 101 N. E. 162, 49 L. R. A. (N.S.) 922.

An implied condition is a legal fiction based upon purely equitable principles; it does not rest upon the actual intent of the parties. The danger inherent in too great a relaxation of the harsh common law rule was noted by the Court in Retail Merchants' Business Expansion Co. v. Randall, 103 Vt. 268, 153 Atl. 357, wherein it was said: "While it is undoubtedly true that the tendency of the law is toward the enlargement of the defense of impossibility, such process should be regarded with great caution, since there is danger that courts, in their desire to relieve parties in hard cases, may go too far. The province of courts is to construe and enforce contracts, not to make or modify them. 

Nor will it be assumed that the people of the present generation are less competent to make their own contracts than were the people of former generations."

§ 388. 

Classification of Implied Conditions. 

Implied conditions may be classified as follows: 

1. Continued existence of the law governing the contract and in force at the time the contract was made: a. Of our own state or country; or b. Of a foreign country. 

2. Continued existence of the specific thing or condition in reference to which the contract is made. 

3. Continued health or life on the part of one who has contracted to render some personal service. 

In a well-considered opinion the New York Court of Claims in Kinzer Construction Co. v. State, Claim No. 9749, 125 N. Y. S. 54, aff'd 204 N. Y. 381, 97 N. E. 871, makes a fourth classification as follows: "Where conditions essential to performance do not exist." The facts in this case show that the impossibility of performance existed at the time of making the contract, and the case should have been determined upon the principle of law governing such cases and not on the ground of an exception to the subsequent impossibility rule. 

§ 389. Continued Existence of the Laws of Our Own State or Country. 

One of the implied conditions in a contract is that the promisor shall not be compelled to perform if performance is subsequently prohibited by law or governmental authority. Therefore, a legal impossibility, arising from a change of the law of our own state or country or from the exercise of governmental authority wholly terminates the contract. American Mercantile Exchange v. Blunt, 102 Me. 

128, 66 Atl. 212, 10 L. R. A. (N.S.) 414 and note; Panto v. 

Kentucky Distilleries & Warehouse Co., 215 App. Div. 511, 214 N. Y. S. 19; People v. Globe Mutual Life Insurance Co., 

91 N. Y. 174; 6 L. R. A. (N.S.) 809, note. For instance, a contract between a school teacher and the school directors is discharged by an order of the board of health directing

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY OPERATION OF LAW 313 

the closing of the school. Likewise, a city ordinance prohibiting the erection of wooden buildings will discharge the promisor from his obligations to erect such buildings. 

School District No. 16 v. Howard, 5 Neb. 340, 98 N. W. 666; 

Bown Bros. v. Merchants' Bank of Rochester, 243 N. Y. 

366, 153 N. E. 493.

The effect of a prohibitory law on a lease for a saloon is subject to controversy. In Heart v. East Tennessee 

Brewing Co., 121 Tenn. 69, 113 S. W. 364, 19 L. R. A. (N.S.) 964 and note, the Court held that the enactment during the term of the lease of a law forbidding the sale of intoxicating liquors terminated the lease. The weight of authority is apparently to the contrary. Goodrum Tobacco

Co. v. Potts-Thompson Liquor Co., 133 Ga. 776, 66 S. E. 

1081, 26 L. R. A. (N.S.) 498; L. R. A. 1916F 66, note.

Much depends on the wording of the lease. If the lease allows the premises to be used for saloon purposes only, there is reason for holding that the lease is terminated with the enactment of prohibitory laws. The Stratford, Inc. v. 

Seattle Brewing & Malting Co., 94 Wash. 125, 162 Pac. 

31, L. R. A. 1917C 931 and note; Palombi v. Volpe, 249 

N. Y. 194, 163 N. E. 607. § 390. 

Performance Affected by Imprisonment.

Where the contractual obligation is one which in its nature continues, notwithstanding death or illness, the imprisonment of one of the parties is no excuse for nonperformance. But where the contract is for personal service, the imprisonment of one of the parties has been held to discharge the contractual obligation. But the better rule, supported by reason and the weight of authority, is that the imprisonment of one guilty of an offense will not excuse performance of such a contract. His imprisonment effects an abandonment or repudiation of the contract on his part. Jinnings v. Amend, 101 Kan. 130, 165 Pac. 845, L. R. A. 1917F 626 and note. However, as a matter of construction, an employee is not guilty of quitting his em-

ployment "voluntarily" where his absence from work is due to his arrest and imprisonment. Kowalski v. McAdoo, 93 N. J. L. 340, 107 Atl. 477, aff'd 96 N. J. L. 293, 114 Atl. 927. 

§ 391. Continued Existence of the Laws of a Foreign State or Country.

When performance of a contract becomes impossible because of a change in the law of another state or country, the seemingly harsh rule has been laid down that the promisor is not excused on that account. Furness v. Rederiaktiegolabet Banco, 2 K. B. Div. (Eng.) 873 (1917). Thus, in 

Tweedie Trading Co. v. James P. McDonald Co., 114 Fed. 

985, the libelant entered into a contract in the United States to make four trips with its steamship from Barbadoes to Colon to transport laborers; but after two trips had been made a regulation of the colonial government of Barbadoes was promulgated forbidding the future embarkation of laborers. By reason of this, the defendant was unable to furnish further transportation, and it was held that such embargo did not constitute a defense to an action to recover the hire of the ship for the remaining voyages at the contract price. So, too, impossibility of performance due to a foreign war does not excuse non-performance of a contract. 

Richards & Co. v. Wreschner, 174 App. Div. 484, 156 

N. Y. S. 1054. 

§ 392. Continued Existence of Specific Thing or Con dition.

Where, by the terms of the contract, the continued existence of a specific thing or condition is essential to the performance of a contract, the destruction or non-existence of the thing or condition, without fault of either party, operates as a discharge of the contractual obligation. This rule of law is based upon the theory that the parties impliedly agreed that in case the specific thing should perish, they would be excused from the performance of their con-

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY OPERATION OF LAW 315 

1 tract. Runyan v. Culver, 168 Ky. 45, 181 S. W. 640, L. R. 

A. 1916F 3 and note; Matousek v. Galligan, 104 Neb. 731, 178 N. W. 510, 12 A. L. R. 1270 and note.

A leading case on this subject is one in which the defendant had agreed to let the plaintiff have the use of a music hall for the purpose of giving concerts upon certain days. Before performance of the contract the hall was destroyed by fire. It was held that the defendant was not liable for breach of contract. Taylor v. Coldwell, 3 Best & Smith 826, 122 Eng. Rep. 309. So, too, where the contract is for the sale of specific goods, which, without the seller's fault, perish before the day of delivery, the seller is excused from his obligation to deliver, and the buyer from his obligation to pay. Uniform Sales Act, sec. 7; Personal 

Property Law, sec. 88; Dexter v. Norton, 47 N. Y. 62; 

Stewart v. Stone, 127 N. Y. 500, 28 N. E. 595, 14 L. R. A. 215.

The following are illustrations of the application of the same principle:

A breach of a contract to supply a hotel with water from a certain spring is excused by the drying up of the spring. 

Ward v. Vance, 93 Pa. St. 499.

A contract to sell two hundred tons of potatoes to be grown on the promisor's land, and not two hundred tons of potatoes generally, becomes unenforceable if the crop fails by reason of a blight. In this case, the Court said: "There was an implied term in the contract that each party should be free if the crop perished." Howell v. Coupland, 9 Q. B. Div. (Eng.) 462 (1774).

Performance of a contract for the sale and delivery of wood to be cut from trees on a particular tract of land is excused when, without fault of the vendor, the trees are destroyed by fire. International Paper Co. v. Rockefeller, 161 App. Div. 180, 146 N. Y. S. 371.

A contract for a term of years to conduct a particular line of business in a building which was occupied by a de-

partment store was held to be terminated by the destruction of the building. The promisee, therefore, had no right to an allotment of space in the new building to which the department store was moved. Martin Emerich Outfitting 

Co. v. Siegel, Cooper & Co., 237 Ill. 610, 86 N. E. 1104, 20

L. R. A. (N.S.) 1114. 

Where a contract is entered into on the assumption by both parties that a particular event or state of things will occur, the non-occurrence of the event or state of things, without the fault of either party, discharges the contractual liability. This proposition of law finds application in contracts for renting window or other space from which to view a procession, race, or contest which is abandoned by reason of circumstances over which the parties have no control. Alfred Marks Realty Co. v. Hotel Hermitage Co., 170 App. Div. 484, 156 N. Y. S. 179; Chandler v. Webster, 1 K. B. Div. (Eng.) 493 (1904). 

If a party agrees to deliver a certain number of bales of cotton to another, and the parties contemplate that the cotton shall be that raised on a certain farm, the farmer may excuse himself from liability if a drought occurs and no cotton is produced in consequence thereof. But whether or not the contract involves the production on a particular tract of land is often a close question of construction. Davis & Co. v. Bishop, 139 Ark. 273, 213 S. W. 744. If an agreement for the sale of milk is not made with reference to the milk produced on a certain farm, it is no defense for the seller to show that his cows were quarantined and killed. 

Whitman v. Anglum, 92 Conn. 392, 103 Atl. 114.

One court has gone so far as to hold that "a thing is impossible in legal contemplation when it is not practicable; and a thing is impracticable when it can only be done at an excessive and unreasonable cost." Mineral Park 

Land Co. v. Howard, 172 Cal. 259, 156 Pac. 458, L. R. A. 1916F 1.

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY OPERATION OF LAW 

317 I 1 § 393.

Covenant To Repair Building or Chattel. 

One who contracts to erect a building upon the land of another assumes the risk of the accidental destruction of the building before its completion, in the absence of a provision in the contract providing for such a contingency. Moreover, the accidental destruction of the building does not discharge the contractor's liability, for even an act of God will not excuse the performance of a contractual obligation. 

Tompkins v. Dudley, 25 N. Y. 272, 82 Am. Dec. 349; Butterfield v. Byron, 153 Mass. 517, 12 L. R. A. 571. However, where one agrees to make repairs or do any other work on a building already constructed and owned by another, and performance of the contract becomes impossible because of the destruction of the building without fault on his part, a conflict of authority exists as to whether or not he may recover in quasi-contract for that part of the work which was completed. The general rule in this country is that the contractor may recover because the owner is under an implied obligation to keep the building in readiness to receive the labor and material. Carroll v. Bowersock, 100 Kan. 

270, 164 Pac. 143, L. R. A. 1917D 1006 and note; Angus v. 

Scully, 176 Mass. 357, 57 N. E. 674. See, also, Editorial, New York Law Journal, November 18, 1931. However, in

England and a number of states, a recovery has been denied in such cases. Appleby v. Meyers, 2 C. P. (Eng.) 651 (1867); Krause v. Crothersville, 162 Ind. 278, 70 N. E. 264, 65 L. R. A. 111; Taulbee v. McCarthy, 144 Ky. 199, 137 

S. W. 1045, 36 L. R. A. (N.S.) 43. In New York, recovery has been allowed on the contract for work completed, and in quasi-contract for the work partly completed. Whelan v. Ansonia Clock Co., 97 N. Y. 293. 

§ 394. Continued Health or Life of Party. 

When the performance of a contract depends upon the continued health or life of a particular person, his death or such illness as makes performance by him impossible effects a discharge of his contractual liability. Thus, a contract

which has for its object the rendering of personal service is discharged by the death or incapacitating illness of the promisor. In contracts for personal service, there is an implied condition that, should one of the parties become disabled through sickness or death, the obligation to perform is extinguished. This rule is applied to skilled, as well as to unskilled, labor. Spalding v. Rossa, 71 N. Y. 

40, 27 Am. Rep. 7; Lacy v. Getman, 119 N. Y. 109, 23 

N. E. 452, 6 L. R. A. 728; Mendenhall v. Davis, 52 Wash. 

169, 100 Pac. 336, 21 L. R. A. (N.S.) 914 and note; L. R. A. 

1916F 80, note.

An interesting case involving this principle is Brearton v. De Witt, 252 N. Y. 495, 170 N. E. 119, where the plaintiff agreed to isolate herself and subject herself to certain experimental treatments by defendant's testator, a physician, and the testator promised to pay the plaintiff $1,000 monthly during her life. In an action on the contract against the testator's estate, the Court of Appeals, reversing the Appellate Division, held that the performance of the promise to make monthly payments did not depend upon the continued existence of the testator and, hence, that the contract survived his death. $395. Illness of Employee-Right of Employer To

Terminate Contract. 

When the employee's illness is of more than a temporary nature, the employer may rescind the contract; if he does not do so, but takes the employee back, it has been held that he can make no deduction from the wages of the employee for the lost time. Rubin v. International Film Co., 122 Misc. 413, 204 N. Y. S. 81. 

In Gaynor v. Jonas, 104 App. Div. 35, 93 N. Y. S. 287, it was held that illness, to justify the dismissal of an employee, must be more than a mere temporary malady; it must be a serious sickness, lasting or likely to last so long as to interfere substantially with the interests of the employer. It was specifically decided that, under a contract

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY OPERATION OF LAW 319 

period for three months, illness of the employee for a day and a half did not entitle the employer to discharge him and that to do so rendered the master liable to an action for breach of the contract. The rule is that an employer is not bound to wait an unreasonable time for the recovery of a servant incapacitated by illness, before terminating the contract. What is an unreasonable time is generally a question of fact. The authorities are collated in Rubin v. 

International Film Co., supra. § 396. 

Dissolution of Corporation. ues- Where a corporation was a party to a contract, the tion has arisen as to whether the dissolution of the corporation is to be treated in the same way as the death of a natural person. The rule that where the performance of a contract depends upon the continued existence of a person the death of the person terminates the obligation has been applied in the case of the involuntary dissolution of a corporation. Lorillard v. Clyde, 142 N. Y. 456, 37 N. E. 489, 24 L. R. A. 113. However, the tendency of modern authority is to hold the corporation for future obligations, notwithstanding its dissolution, and to give the other party a right to damages for the failure of the corporation to perform its part of the contract. In re Mullings Clothing 

Co., 238 Fed. 58, L. R. A. 1918A 539; Huber v. Martin, 127 Wis. 412, 105 N. W. 1031, 3 L. R. A. (N.S.) 653. § 397. 

By Merger.

The term "merger" refers to the absorption of a thing of lesser importance by a greater, whereby the lesser ceases to exist, but the greater is not increased. Thus, where an obligation arising under a contract is reduced to judgment, or a simple or oral contract is put in the form of a contract under seal or a written contract, respectively, the prior obligation is merged in the new one. For example, a simple contract for the sale of real property is merged in a subsequent deed executed and delivered in full performance

of the contract, and such deed supersedes the contract. 

Schoonmaker v. Hoyt, 148 N. Y. 425, 42 N. E. 1059. Likewise, a written contract merges all prior preliminary negotiations. Societa Italiana Di Beneficenza v. Sulzer, 138 

N. Y. 468, 34 N. E. 193. 

§ 398. By Alteration. 

An instrument is altered when its meaning or language is changed, either in a material or immaterial particular. 

A material alteration changes the legal effect of the instrument; an immaterial one does not. The term alteration without any qualifications generally means, however, a material alteration. Bull Remedy Co. v. Clark, 109 Minn. 396, 124 N. W. 20, 32 L. R. A. (N.S.) 519; Ryan v. First National Bank, 148 Ill. 349, 35 N. E. 1120.

The following are material alterations: a change in the parties to the instrument; the addition or mutilation of seal where the same is of some legal effect; a change in the date of an instrument; a change in the amount of money called for by the instrument, even though the amount be decreased; a change in the rate of interest, even though the amount be diminished; and a change in the place of performance. Farmers' Loan & Trust Co. v. Siefke, 144 

N. Y. 354, 39 N. E. 358; Wood v. Steele, 6 Wall. (U. S.) 80, 18 Law. Ed. 725. The following are immaterial alterations: inserting the rate of interest which the law allows; a change in the Christian name of the party; and an alteration in printed figures which does not affect the obligation. First National Bank of Port Huron v. Carson, 60 Mich. 432, 27 N. W. 589; Lombardo v. Lombardini, 57 

Wash. 352, 106 Pac. 907, 32 L. R. A. (N.S.) 515.

If a deed or contract in writing is materially altered by addition or erasure, it is discharged, provided the alteration is made: a. By a party to the contract or by a stranger with the party's consent. Alteration by a stranger without authority is a mere spoliation and does not discharge the obliga-

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY OPERATION OF LAW 

321 tion. Gleason v. Hamilton, 138 N. Y. 353, 34 N. E. 283; 

Martin v. Tradesman Insurance Co., 101 N. Y. 498, 5 N. E. 338. b. Intentionally. Alteration by mistake or accident has no effect. Van Brunt v. Eoff, 35 Barb. 501; 32 L. R. A. (N.S.) 517, note.

C. Without consent of the other party. Booth v. Powers, 56 N. Y. 22. 

§ 399. Effect of Material Alteration. 

A material alteration, innocently or fraudulently made, destroys the instrument. If fraudulently made, it discharges the original debt as well; if, however, the material alteration is innocently made, a recovery can be had on the original debt or obligation. 

Thus, where a note which is secured by a mortgage is fraudulently altered, the mortgage as well as the note is discharged. Walton Plow Co. v. Campbell, 35 Neb. 173, 52 N. W. 883, 16 L. R. A. 468. One reason for the rule is that the instrument as altered has lost its identity and is no longer the same. To allow one who has fraudulently altered a contract to recover upon it is said to be a departure from the just principle that no one shall be permitted to take the chance of committing a fraud, without incurring the risk of losing by the act when it is detected. Meyer v. Huneke, 55 N. Y. 412; Booth v. Powers, 56 N. Y. 22. But if the alteration is without fraudulent intent, an action will lie upon the original consideration. Edington v. McLeod, 87 

Kan. 426, 124 Pac. 163, 41 L. R. A. (N.S.) 230 and note.

A different rule applies in the case of a material alteration on a negotiable instrument where the instrument, as altered, has passed into the hands of a holder in due course. 

The Negotiable Instruments Law, section 205, provides that, notwithstanding the alteration, the holder in due course may recover according to the original tenor of the instrument.

$ 400. 

Lost Instrument.

The loss of an instrument affects the parties only in so far as it occasions difficulty of proof. In the case of negotiable instruments, before the owner of lost paper can recover, he may be required to give a bond of indemnity which will secure the maker against loss. Civil Practice 

Act, sec. 333; Church v. Stevens, 56 Misc. 572, 107 N. Y. S. 

310. However, there are instances wherein such provisions appear to be ineffective. Thus, where an agent is authorized to pay interest coupons only upon their surrender, it has been held to be a breach of agency to pay upon proof that the coupons were lost and upon the giving of an indemnity bond. Erb v. Banco di Napoli, 243 N. Y. 45, 152 N. E. 

460, 50 A. L. R. 1009. 

§ 401. By Bankruptcy. 

The National Bankruptcy Act, section 17, provides as follows: "A discharge in bankruptcy shall release a bankrupt from all of his provable debts, except such as (1) are due as a tax * * * ; (2) are liabilities for obtaining property by false pretenses or false representations, or for wilful and malicious injuries to the person or property of another, or for alimony due or to become due, or for maintenance or support of wife or child, or for seduction of an unmarried female, or for breach of promise of marriage accompanied by seduction, or for criminal conversation; (3) have not been duly scheduled in time for proof and allowance, with the name of the creditor if known to the bankrupt, unless such creditor, had notice or actual knowledge of the proceedings in bankruptcy; (4) were created by his fraud, embezzlement, misappropriation, or defalcation while acting as an officer or in any fiduciary capacity; (5) are for wages due to workmen, clerks, * * sales- men, or servants, which have been earned within three months before the date of commencement of the proceedings in bankruptcy; or (6) are due for moneys of an employee received or retained by his employer to secure the faithful performance *** of a contract of employment."

CHAPTER XIX DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY AGREEMENT § 402. Forms of Discharge by Agreement. 

The discharge of a contract by agreement will assume one of three forms: Rescission-abandonment; 1. 

2. Substituted contract-novation; or 

3. Discharge in accordance with a provision in the original contract. § 403. Rescission-Abandonment.

A bilateral contract may be discharged or rescinded by mutual consent so long as each party thereto remains subject to some duty to the other. The consideration to support the promise to abandon the contract is the mutual promise of each to waive or surrender his rights against the other. A contract of employment even though partially performed is discharged by implication by making a new contract with a modified salary, though all other conditions of the new contract are identical with the terms of the original contract. Schwartzreich v. Bauman-Basch, Inc., 231 N. Y. 196, 131 N. E. 887; Coletti v. Knox Hat Co., 252 

N. Y. 468, 169 N. E. 648. When a partially performed bilateral contract is rescinded, the question often arises as to the right of restitution or compensation for prior performance or for expenses. In New York, such claims are usually denied unless expressly or impliedly reserved in the agreement of rescission. In McCreery v. Day, 119 N. Y. 1, 23 N. E. 198, 6 L. R. A. 503, the Court held that the duty to reimburse the plaintiff for expenses was discharged by the rescission agreement. 

If the contract is fully executed by one party, the other cannot be released from his obligation without giving some 323

consideration to the one who has performed. An agreement for rescission must have all the elements necessary for the formation of a new contract. But in New York, a right or claim may be discharged or extinguished by a release under seal, since as to such matters the seal is conclusive of consideration. Therefore, a party may be discharged from his contract by a delivery to him of a sealed release by the creditor. Civil Practice Act, sec. 342; Stiebel v. Grosberg, 202 N. Y. 266, 95 N. E. 692, 36 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1147.

It should be noted also that the promisee may make a gift to the debtor or promisor of the outstanding obligation. A mere promise to give is executory and is without legal effect and should be distinguished from a gift which implies that the gift is executed or completed. The intention to make a gift must be clearly established. The surrender of a bill or a note or the delivery of a receipt is evidence of a gift. But a mere statement by a creditor to his debtor, such as "I am giving you this debt," "I release you," or "I discharge you," does not necessarily operate to discharge the debtor. Gray v. Barton, 55 N. Y. 68, 14 Am. Rep. 181; McKenzie v. Harrison, 120 N. Y. 260, 24 N. E. 458, 8 L. R. A. 257. § 404. Discharge of Negotiable Instrument by Renunciation. 

The Negotiable Instruments Law, section 203, provides: "The holder [of a negotiable instrument] may expressly renounce his rights against any party to the instrument, before, at or after its maturity. An absolute and unconditional renunciation of his rights against the principal debtor made at or after the maturity of the instrument, discharges the instrument. A renunciation must be in writing, unless the instrument is delivered up to the person primarily liable thereon." The renunciation is not required to be in writing if a consideration is given therefor.

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY AGREEMENT 

325 

Bank of United States v. Manheim, 264 N. Y. 45, 189 N. E. 776.

B made his note payable to C. After C's death, the note was found enclosed in an envelope together with a writing signed by C, the payee, addressed to his executor, as follows: "In the case of my death, B's note herewith enclosed is to become null and void." Is B liable to C's executor on the note? 

§ 405. Substituted Contract. 

Parties who make a contract may unmake it and substitute in whole or in part therefor another contract. Therefore, a contract may be abandoned by mutual agreement of the parties, and in its place the parties may substitute a new and different contract, or they may waive or modify their rights under it and ingraft new terms upon it. As is the case with all other contracts, however, in order to be valid, a new agreement by the parties to an older one, altering, canceling, supplementing, or supplanting their former contract, requires a new consideration to support it. The new obligations assumed in the substituted contract annulling the previous one between the parties are sufficient consideration to support the new agreement. McCreery v. 

Day, 119 N. Y. 1, 23 N. E. 198, 6 L. R. A. 503; Colvin v. 

Post Mortgage & Land Co., 225 N. Y. 510, 122 N. E. 454.

Again, it should be noted that a written agreement signed by both debtor and creditor extending the time of payment of any debt to a fixed or determinable future date is valid without consideration. Personal Property Law, sec. 33 (1934). Similarly, the postponement of the payment of a debt secured by real property is valid without consideration. Real Property Law, sec. 279 (1934).

At any time prior to the breach of an executory contract, the time fixed for the performance of the contract may be extended, or any other obligation in the contract may be modified, without any consideration other than the mutual consent of the contracting parties. Thus, it is competent

for the parties in an executory contract of sale and purchase of personal property to agree upon another purchase price, time of payment, or place of delivery than that fixed in the original contract. Green-Shrier Co. v. State Realty & Mortgage Co., 199 N. Y. 65, 92 N. E. 98; De Klyn v. 

Silver Lake Ice Co., 12 N. Y. S. 917, aff'd 128 N. Y. 582, 28 N. E. 250. § 406.

Substituted Contract-Novation. 

A contract may be modified by the substitution of a new party for one of the original parties. This can be done only by the consent of all. This amounts to a substitution of debtors, and is known as "novation." Thus, where A owed 

B a sum of money, and C owed A a like sum, it was agreed among all parties that C should give his note to B for the amount and be substituted in the place of A as B's debtor. 

C was insolvent at the time, but this was unknown. It was held that A's liability to B was extinguished by the novation. Cadens v. Teesdale, 53 Vt. 460, 38 Am. Rep. 697; 

Millerd v. Thorn, 56 N. Y. 402.

The term "novation" is derived from the civil law. While the earlier usage of the common law confines it to cases where there is a substitution of parties, the term is now quite commonly used in a more comprehensive sense, as including all forms of substituted agreements, whether there is a substitution of parties or of subject matter. Hence, in its broader sense, the term novation is properly applied to all cases wherein a new obligation is substituted for an old one. Griggs v. Day, 136 N. Y. 152, 32 N. E. 612, 18 

L. R. A. 120; Bandman v. Finn, 185 N. Y. 508, 78 N. E. 175, 12 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1134. 

§ 407. Substituted Contract-Account Stated. 

The doctrine of account stated had its origin in the transactions of merchants. Freeland v. Heran, Lenox & Co., 7 Cranch (U.S.) 147, 3 Law. Ed. 297. But it has since been extended to embrace transactions between other per-

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY AGREEMENT 

327 sons. 

Stenton v. Jerome, 54 N. Y. 480. Briefly defined, an account stated is a contract, express or implied, between the parties regarding the amount due on past transactions. 

Rodkinson v. Haecker, 248 N. Y. 480, 162 N. E. 493; Corr v. Hoffman, 256 N. Y. 254, 176 N. E. 382. The elements are a mutual examination of their claims and an implied or expressed agreement as to their allowance or disallowance and as to the balance due. Carlisle v. Norris, 144 App. 

Div. 690, 129 N. Y. S. 585. The promise must be founded on previous transactions of a monetary character creating the relation of debtor and creditor. There must be an antecedent enforceable debt. An unenforceable debt is an insufficient basis for an account stated. Tucker v. Columbian National Life Insurance Co., 232 Mass. 224, 122 N. E. 

285; Parsons v. Batchelor, 233 App. Div. 517, 253 N. Y. S. 728.

The mere rendering of a bill or statement of account does not constitute an account stated. If the debtor, however, upon receipt of the bill or statement of account, by silence or otherwise, admits its correctness, the bill or statement of account becomes conclusive as an account stated. Failure to object within a reasonable time shows assent. Schmid v. Du Val, 200 App. Div. 514, 193 N. Y. S. 278; Lockwood v. Thorne, 11 N. Y. 170, 62 Am. Dec. 81.

An account stated can be opened only by showing fraud, mistake, or manifest error. Murphy v. Ross, 118 N. Y. 

676, 23 N. E. 1146. But where a confidential relationship exists, the fiduciary must show that the account stated was fairly brought about. Rodkinson v. Haecker, supra. 

§ 408. Form of Substituted Contract. 

The form of a later agreement modifying an earlier one becomes important only where the original contract is (a) under seal, or (b) within the Statute of Frauds. A contract under seal cannot be modified by a parol unexecuted contract, but an executed parol modification of a sealed contract is valid, and evidence of the executed contract is ad-

missible to vary or modify the sealed instrument. McKenzie v. Harrison, 120 N. Y. 260, 24 N. E. 458, 8 L. R. A. 257; 

Enthoven v. Enthoven, 225 App. Div. 309, 232 N. Y. S. 

599. In Harris v. Shorall, 230 N. Y. 343, 130 N. E. 572, the ancient rule that a contract under seal cannot be modified by a parol unexecuted contract was considered and questioned, but not disapproved. The old rule was reaffirmed in Cammack v. J. B. Slattery & Bro., 241 N. Y. 39, 148 N. E. 781. But, still later, in National Cash Register 

Co. v. Remington Arms Co., 242 N. Y. 99, 151 N. E. 144, the Court held that where a subsequent parol contract is made in abrogation of a contract under seal and it is acted upon by both parties, the parol agreement will be enforced and the parties will be excused from a performance of the original contract. A written contract not under seal and not within the Statute of Frauds may be modified after execution, but before breach, by an oral agreement. Eagle 

Iron Works v. Farley, 83 App. Div. 82, 82 N. Y. S. 503, aff'd 178 N. Y. 595, 70 N. E. 1098. 

§ 409. Oral Modification of Contracts under the Statute of Frauds.

Where the law requires a contract to be in writing under the Statute of Frauds, any modification thereof must also be in writing. Imperator Realty Co. v. Tull, 228 N. Y. 447, 127 N. E. 263. However, a contract within the Statute of

Frauds may be modified by a subsequent oral agreement if the latter is not, itself, within the Statute of Frauds. For example, a written contract not to be performed within a year may be modified by a subsequent oral agreement for a shorter period, if such new contract is not, itself, required to be in writing. "The validity of the substituted contract must be determined, like that of any other, in the light of the situation existing at the hour of its making." Lieberman v. Templar Motor Co., 236 N. Y. 139, 140 N. E. 222, 29 A. L. R. 1089 and note. Nevertheless, the broad rule that a written contract within the Statute of Frauds can-

DISCHARGE OF CONTRACT BY AGREEMENT 

329 not be modified by an oral agreement still obtains in a number of jurisdictions. Kerzner v. Chanin, 98 N. J. L. 

38, 118 Atl. 693; 29 Columbia Law Review 497, article reprinted, editorial page, New York Law Journal, August 

10, 1929.

A written agreement may be modified by a subsequent oral agreement, even though the written agreement provides that it cannot be thus modified. For instance, the provisions of a contract that the contractor shall make no claim for extra work unless the same shall be done in pursuance of a written order, may be waived, and an oral order or agreement for extra work made subsequent thereto will be regarded as a waiver of the written stipulation forbidding a recovery for such extra work without a written agreement. Solomon v. Vallette, 152 N. Y. 147, 46 N. E. 

324; General Electric Co. v. National Construction Co., 178 

N. Y. 369, 70 N. E. 928; Headley v. Cavileer, 82 N. J. L. 

635, 82 Atl. 908, 48 L. R. A. (N.S.) 564 and note. "Whenever two men contract, no limitation self-imposed can destroy their power to contract again." Beatty v. Guggenheim Co., 225 N. Y. 380, 122 N. E. 378. 

§ 410. 

Provision for Discharge Contained in Contract. 

A contract may contain within itself stipulations providing for its discharge upon the occurrence of certain events, or at the option of one of the parties. These provisions are, in their nature, conditions subsequent. They may become operative upon : 

1. The non-fulfillment of some term of the contract; 

2. The occurrence of a particular event; or 

3. The exercise by one of the parties of an option to terminate the contract, the option being given: a. By express provisions in the contract; or b. By a custom or usage forming part of the contract. 

Moore v. Phoenix Insurance Co., 62 N. H. 240, 13 Am. 

St. Rep. 556; Gray v. Gardner, 17 Mass. 188.

Illustrations: The ordinary clause of defeasance in a

chattel mortgage discharges the contract and conveyance upon payment of the mortgage debt. The usual excepted risks in a charter-party also illustrate conditions subsequent. Likewise, where a servant is engaged to carry on his master's business "to the master's satisfaction," the master has the absolute right to discharge the servant whenever the master becomes dissatisfied, and he is the sole judge as to whether or not the servant is satisfactory. 

Where a contract of employment gives to either party a right to terminate it upon notice, the giving of the notice operates to discharge the contract. Graves v. Calvin S. 

Edwards, 50 Fed. 477; Ginsberg v. Friedman, 146 App. 

Div. 779, 131 N. Y. S. 517; Crawford v. Mail & Express 

Publishing Co., 163 N. Y. 404, 57 N. E. 616; Mackenzie v. 

Minis, 132 Ga. 323, 63 S. E. 900, 23 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1003 and note. 

§ 411. Sales on Approval-Provisions for Discharge. 

Where a person buys goods on approval or with the privilege of returning them if unsatisfactory, the Personal Property Law, section 100, subdivision 3, confers an option upon the buyer to either accept or reject the goods. If the goods are rejected and returned, the contract is discharged and not broken. If the goods, though unsatisfactory, are not returned within a reasonable time or within the time agreed upon, the purchaser will be obligated to pay the full contract price. Brown & Lowe Co. v. Potolski, 221 

App. Div. 299, 223 N. Y. S. 71; Kelley v. Thomas G. Plant 

Co., 274 Mass. 102, 174 N. E. 224.

Where goods are sold under a contract which contains a warranty and the goods do not comply with the warranty, the buyer is given a choice of remedies, one of which is to rescind the contract. It must be noted that while the buyer has, under such circumstances, an option to discharge the contract, yet the sale is not an optional sale such as has been discussed above. Personal Property Law, secs. 130 and 150.

CHAPTER XX  § 412. In General. 

The most natural mode of discharging a contract is by performance. What must be done in order to comply with the terms of the contract is in each case primarily a question of construction. Whether the promisee has received all that he has a right to demand often involves interesting and difficult questions. The rights of the parties must be determined by the contract construed in the light of surrounding circumstances. The functions of both the court and the jury may be required in answering the question: 

Has the promisee received all that he has a right to demand? For instance, where one has agreed to drill a well for another, when is the contract performed? Sweezy v. 

O'Rourke, 226 N. Y. 378, 123 N. E. 752. When are contracts for permanent employment or employment for life discharged by performance? Cox v. Baltimore & Ohio 

Southwestern R. R. Co., 180 Ind. 495, 103 N. E. 337, 50 

L. R. A. (N.S.) 453 and note. 

§ 413. Payment-Place. 

In the absence of an agreed place of payment, it is the duty of the debtor to seek his creditor and make payment. 

The debtor, however, need not go out of his own state to make payment on a contract which is made within his state. Where the creditor is a non-resident, he should provide for a place of payment within the state of his debtor. 

But if the contract was made out of the state, the debtor should make payment at the place where the contract was made. Weyand v. Park Terrace Co., 202 N. Y. 231, 95 

N. E. 723, 36 L. R. A. (N.S.) 308 and note. Where a lease for the payment of rent for the use of real property is silent as to the place of payment, the law fixes the place of 331

payment upon the leased premises. House v. Lewis, 108 

Neb. 257, 187 N. W. 784, 23 A. L. R. 877 and note. § 414. Payment-Money.

If the contract requires the payment of money, such payment extinguishes the debt and discharges the contract. 

Lancey v. Clark, 64 N. Y. 209, 21 Am. Rep. 604.

The transmission of money by mail by a debtor to his creditor is at the hazard of the debtor. If, however, a debtor sends the money as directed by his creditor, it is at the latter's risk, and the debt is discharged, even though the remittance is lost. Palmer v. Phoenix Mutual Life Insurance Co., 84 N. Y. 63; Masterson v. Union Bank & Trust 

Co., 86 Wash. 560, 150 Pac. 1126, L. R. A. 1918A 531 and note; Mitchell-Henry v. Norwich Union Life Insurance 

Society, 2 K. B. Div. (Eng.) 67 (1918), 2 A. L. R. 1644 and note. 

§ 415. Payment-Note of Debtor. 

Whether the payment of a debt by a note of the debtor acts as a discharge of the debt is a question of intention. 

If the note is accepted expressly as absolute payment, it will discharge the debt. If litigation is necessary, the payee must bring his action on the note. But in the absence of such an agreement, the note is, at most, a conditional, and not an absolute, payment. The law presumes that the note was not accepted as absolute payment. Hence, as a rule, if the note is not paid at maturity, an action may be maintained on the original consideration. This presumption prevails, whether the debt is a preexisting one or is concurrent with the giving of the note. Winsted Bank v. 

Webb, 39 N. Y. 325, 100 Am. Dec. 435; St. Albans Beef Co. v. Aldridge, 112 App. Div. 803, 99 N. Y. S. 398; Leschen & 

Sons Rope Co. v. Mayflower Gold Mining & Reduction Co., 173 Fed. 855, 35 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1 and note; State v. 

Royal Indemnity Co., 44 N. D. 550, 175 N. W. 625. In a minority of states, the rule is that the negotiable note of a

debtor given to his creditor is prima facie a satisfaction of a prior simple contract debt. See note in 35 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1. It should be noted that there is a third possibility in addition to the acceptance of the note as absolute or conditional payment; namely, that it may be accepted merely as collateral security, in which case the right of the creditor to sue on the original contract is not suspended and exists even prior to the maturity of the note. See 

Weymouth v. Sanborn, 43 N. H. 171, 80 Am. Dec. 144; 

Smith v. Ryan, 66 N. Y. 352, 23 Am. Rep. 60. § 416.

Payment-Check of Debtor. 

In the absence of an agreement to take the debtor's check in absolute payment or satisfaction of a debt, the law presumes that it is received as conditional payment only, the same as in the case of the receipt of the debtor's note. 

If the check is paid, the debt will be discharged; if the check is not paid, the debt will not be discharged. Delivery of a certified check though the certification by the bank was procured by the debtor (drawer) at the request of the creditor (payee) does not operate to extinguish the debt, unless the check is duly paid. Randolph National 

Bank v. Hornblower, 160 Mass. 401, 35 N. E. 850; Davenport v. Palmer, 152 App. Div. 761, 137 N. Y. S. 796, reversed on other grounds in 211 N. Y. 596, 105 N. E. 800.

Where, however, the creditor procures a certification of his debtor's check, it will constitute a payment to the amount of the check. St. Regis Paper Co. v. Tonawanda 

Board & Paper Co., 107 App. Div. 90, 94 N. Y. S. 946, aff'd 186 N. Y. 563, 79 N. E. 1115. A creditor's failure to use due diligence in presenting a check for payment operates as payment, but only to the extent that the debtor is damaged thereby. Carroll v. Sweet, 128 N. Y. 19, 27 N. E. 

763, 13 L. R. A. 43. § 417.

Payment-Note, Check, or Draft of Third Person. 

The acceptance of a note, check, or draft of a third per-

son by the creditor from his debtor does not operate as payment of an antecedent debt, unless the parties so agree. 

But the transfer of a note, check, or draft of a third person, upon a purchase of property or for an indebtedness contracted at the time of transfer, is presumably a payment pro tanto, and the burden of showing the contrary rests on the person receiving the paper. Noel v. Murray, 13 N. Y. 

167; Dille v. White, 132 Iowa 327, 109 N. W. 909, 10 

L. R. A. (N.S.) 510 and note; Grady v. Pink Hill Bank & 

Trust Co., 184 N. C. 158, 113 S. E. 667, 28 A. L. R. 660. 

In Hall v. Stevens, 116 N. Y. 201, 22 N. E. 374, 5 L. R. A. 

802, an action was brought for the purchase price of cattle sold by the plaintiff to the defendants. At the time of the sale, the plaintiff accepted from the defendants a draft for $1,311.25 drawn by the First National Bank of Buffalo upon the Fourth National Bank of New York. The Buffalo bank was insolvent, unknown to both plaintiff and defendants. The drawee refused to pay the draft. The 

Court said: "The plaintiff having proved the sale and delivery of the cattle and the acceptance by him of a draft growing out of the transaction itself, but neither signed nor indorsed by the defendants, who was required to show whether the draft was taken as payment or as security? 

The answer to this question depends upon whether the draft was taken for a present or a precedent debt. If it was for the former, the presumption is that it was agreed to be taken in payment, and the burden of proving the contrary rested upon the plaintiff; while if it was for the latter, the presumption is that it was not taken as payment, and the onus of establishing that it was so taken rested upon the defendants." For an exhaustive analysis of the authorities, see note to Leschen & Sons Rope Co. v. Mayflower Gold 

Mining & Reduction Co., 173 Fed. 855, in 35 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1.

It is generally conceded that there is no implied warranty that the drawer or maker is solvent by one selling, without indorsing, the negotiable instrument of another. Hecht v.

Batcheller, 147 Mass. 335, 17 N. E. 651, 9 Am. St. Rep. 

708; People's Bank v. Bogart, 81 N. Y. 101, 37 Am. Rep. 

481. But where a note, given in payment of an antecedent indebtedness, is worthless because of the insolvency of the maker, the debt is not discharged, and a recovery may be had on the original debt. Roberts v. Fisher, 43 N. Y. 159, 3 Am. Rep. 680. There is an obvious distinction between a sale of worthless paper in the open market and the payment of an antecedent debt with such paper. Hecht v. 

Batcheller, supra; note to Dille v. White, supra. 

§ 418. Application of Payment-By the Parties. 

Where a person owes several debts or items to another, he has a right to direct to which item any payment which he may make shall be applied. Goodman v. Snow, 81 Hun 

225, 30 N. Y. S. 672. The reason for the rule which confers upon the debtor the right primarily to direct the application of a payment voluntarily made by him is apparent. Until the money is actually paid over, it belongs to him, and he may do with it as he sees fit. If he makes a specific direction, the creditor must observe it or refuse to accept the payment. New York & Brooklyn Brewing Co. v. 

Angelo, 144 App. Div. 655, 129 N. Y. S. 713. In the absence of such direction, the creditor may apply the payment to whichever debt he pleases. For example, a creditor having secured and unsecured claims may apply an undirected payment on an unsecured claim. Bank of California v. 

Webb, 94 N. Y. 467; Wanamaker v. Powers, 102 App. Div. 

485, 93 N. Y. S. 19, aff'd 186 N. Y. 562, 79 N. E. 1118; 

Wilkes v. Kitchen, 187 Ky. 211, 218 S. W. 718. The creditor, however, cannot, without the debtor's consent, apply payments to an illegal claim, such as a debt for liquor sold in violation of law or a claim for usurious interest. Huffstater v. Hayes, 64 Barb. 573; Anderson v. Griffiths, 51 

Ore. 116, 93 Pac. 934. The payment must be applied to a claim on which the payor is liable. Sherman v. Harley, 178 Cal. 584, 174 Pac. 901, 7 A. L. R. 950. A conflict of

authority exists as to the right of the creditor to apportion the payment among several obligations. According to Sanborn v. Cole, 63 Vt. 590, 22 Atl. 716, 14 L. R. A. 208, he cannot. But Young v. Alford, 118 N. C. 215, 23 S. E. 973, holds that he may do so. 

§ 419. Application of Payment-By Law. 

When neither the debtor nor the creditor applies the payment to any particular debt or item, the law will make the application. In re Milligan's Estate, 112 App. Div. 373, 98 N. Y. S. 480.

The courts have met with many difficult questions in making the application. The common law rule requires the application to be made by law in the way most beneficial to the creditor. McCurdy v. Middleton, 82 Ala. 131, 2

So. 721. The civil law rule requires it to be applied in the way most beneficial to the debtor. McLaughlin v. 

Green, 48 Miss. 175. In New York, the courts have shown a preference for the civil law rule, and apply the payment in a way which would be most beneficial to the debtor. 

Thus, a payment will be applied to an interest-bearing debt, in preference to one that does not bear interest, because the former is more burdensome. So, also, a payment will be applied to a mortgage and judgment debt, in preference to an account, because the former would bear more heavily on the debtor. Pattison v. Hull, 9 Cowen (N.Y.) 747.

However, the rule has been recognized that the court will make the application, in each instance, in a manner to accomplish the ends of justice and in accordance with equitable principles. Armstrong v. McLean, 153 N. Y. 

490, 47 N. E. 912; Korbly v. Springfield Institution for 

Savings, 245 U. S. 330, 62 Law. Ed. 326, 38 Sup. Ct. Rep. 

88. The common law rule, that it is not inequitable to extinguish first those debts less burdensome and for which the security is more precarious, is illustrated by Field v. 

Holland, 6 Cranch (U.S.) 8, 3 Law. Ed. 136, wherein the

United States Supreme Court applied a payment to other

demands rather than to a judgment debt, on the ground that the former were not so well secured. To the same effect is Coons & Braine v. Tome, 9 Fed. 532. All courts agree that an equitable application should be made. But what is equitable? Is it more equitable to adopt the civil law rule and apply payments that would be most beneficial to the debtor, or to follow the common law rule which considers the creditor's interest? See In re Craig Lumber 

Co., 269 Fed. 755. 

In Camp v. Smith, 117 N. Y. 354, 22 N.
