E. 1044, where a creditor held both a secured and an unsecured claim against a debtor, the Court applied a payment by the debtor to the creditor's unsecured claim under the rule that a payment will be applied to the debt of which the security is most precarious; and in Stanwix v. Leonard, 125 App. Div. 299, 109

N. Y. S. 804, it was held that payments made to one holding several different obligations will be first applied to the discharge of the oldest obligations.

In a discussion of the rule that payments should be applied first to the obligations first contracted when neither party makes direction therefor, Chief Judge Cardozo in 

Carson v. Federal Reserve Bank, 254 N. Y. 218, 172 N. E. 

475, 70 A. L. R. 435, stated: "We have no thought to suggest that this or any other formula as to the application of payments to the items of an account is of such inflexible validity as to admit of no exceptions. Whatever rule is framed will be subordinated to the broader principle that an application, usually appropriate, may be varied by a court when variance is necessary to promote the ends of justice. None the less, the formula is expressive of a rule that must prevail in the absence of persuasive reasons for qualification or exception." TENDER 

§ 420. What Is Tender-Tender of Goods. 

A tender is an offer to do what a party has promised to do but is prevented from so doing by acts of the other

party. People ex rel. Haas v. New York Motor Boat Club, 

70 Misc. 603, 129 N. Y. S. 365. Where it is applied to a performance of a promise to do something other than the payment of money, its effect is to extinguish the other party's rights to demand performance. In employment contracts, it is not necessary for the employee, upon a repudiation of the contract by the employer, thereafter to tender his services or to keep himself in readiness to perform. The employee's readiness to perform at the time stipulated constitutes a tender of his services, and the employer's refusal to accept the tendered service operates to discharge the employee's contractual obligation. Howard v. Daly, 61 N. Y. 362, 19 Am. Rep. 285.

The buyer's refusal to accept goods when tendered by the seller discharges the seller's duty of making further efforts to deliver, since the tender and refusal discharges the contract of sale. Jardine Matheson & Co. v. Haghet Silk Co., 203 N. Y. 273, 96 N. E. 449. In this connection, the Sales

Act, which is merely declaratory of the common law, provides: "When the seller is ready and willing to deliver the goods, and requests the buyer to take delivery, and the buyer does not within a reasonable time after such request take delivery of the goods, he is liable to the seller for any loss occasioned by his neglect or refusal to take delivery, and also for a reasonable charge for the care and custody of the goods. If the neglect or refusal of the buyer to take delivery amounts to a repudiation, or breach of the entire contract, the seller shall have the rights against the goods and on the contract hereinafter provided in favor of the seller when the buyer is in default." Personal Property 

Law, Sales Act, sec. 132. A tender of delivery and refusal to accept is not the same for all purposes as an actual delivery. Thus, a tender of delivery and refusal to accept before title has passed would not authorize a recovery of the purchase price; while an actual delivery would give the seller this right. Where there is a refusal to accept delivery, the seller's remedy is an action for damages only.

Reichbart v. Smith-Eisemann Corp., 199 App. Div. 571, 191 N. Y. S. 803; Bready v. B. W. Wechsler Co., 200 App. 

Div. 78, 192 N. Y. S. 660, aff'd 235 N. Y. 539, 139 N. E. 726. 

§ 421. Tender of Money. 

Where the performance of the contract requires the payment of money, the tender must be for the full amount. 

The tender of any less sum is nugatory and ineffective as a tender. But the production of the money may be waived by the creditor. The tender must be absolute and unconditional; it must observe the terms of the contract as to time, place, and mode of payment; and the debtor must continue always ready and willing to pay the debt. Noyes v. 

Wyckoff, 114 N. Y. 204, 21 N. E. 158; Werner v. Tuch, 127 N. Y. 217, 27 N. E. 845; Graves v. Burch, 26 Wyo. 

192, 181 Pac. 354, 5 A. L. R. 1216 and note. A conflict of opinion exists as to whether or not a trifling variation from the exact amount due will render the tender ineffective. In Woodworth v. Morris, 56 Barb. 97, failure to include a single day's interest was held to render the alleged tender ineffective. The rule that the tender must be kept good is not adhered to in a court of equity, as in a court of law. In equity, the necessity for doing so depends on the circumstances in each case. Thompson v. 

Crains, 294 Ill. 270, 128 N. E. 508, 12 A. L. R. 931 and note; Zebley v. Farmers' Loan & Trust Co., 139 N. Y. 461, 34 N. E. 1067. 

§ 422. Effect of Tender. 

The only effect of tender, if it be sufficient, is to stop interest and throw the cost of suit on the other party. Nelson v. Loder, 132 N. Y. 288, 30 N. E. 369.

A tender at any time before a sale on foreclosure of a debt secured by a mortgage extinguishes the lien, and as it leaves the debt unaffected, it is not necessary to keep the tender good. The leading case on this question is Kortright v. Cady, 21 N. Y. 343, 78 Am. Dec. 145. In Cresco Realty

Co. v. Clark, 128 App. Div. 144, 112 N. Y. S. 550, it was held that a tender of interest prior to an action of foreclosure for its non-payment bars the action, and that such tender need not be kept good by payment into court to be available as a defense. Under the rule formerly prevailing in New York, a tender and payment into court admitted the debt or contract and transferred the title of the money to the creditor, even if it was not accepted. The money could not afterwards be withdrawn by the debtor, and this, too, even though the plaintiff was defeated in the action or recovered a verdict for less than the sum tendered. Mann v. Sprout, 185 N. Y. 109, 77 N. E. 1018, 5 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

561. However, the Civil Practice Act now provides: "Section 171. Tender after suit. Where the complaint demands judgment for a sum of money only, and the action is brought to recover a sum certain, or which may be reduced to certainty by calculation, or to recover damages for a casual or involuntary personal injury or a like injury to property, the defendant or his attorney, at any time before the trial, may tender to the plaintiff or his attorney such a sum of money as he conceives to be sufficient to make amends for the injury or to pay the plaintiff's demand, together with the costs of the action to that time. "Section 172. Amount to be paid into court. A tender made as prescribed in the last section does not avail the defendant, unless the money is accepted or is paid into court and notice thereof in writing served upon the plaintiff's attorney before the trial and within ten days after the tender. In making such a payment into court the defendant shall file therewith a memorandum specifying the portion thereof deposited on account of costs. If, before the trial and within ten days after such notice, the plaintiff takes out the amount paid in, he accepts the tender. "Section 173. Effect of tender. If the plaintiff accept a tender, it shall be deemed in satisfaction of the cause of action in respect of which the tender was made, and the defendant may move for a judgment dismissing the com-

plaint, without costs. Where money constituting a tender shall have been paid into court and not accepted, "1. If the defendant recover judgment, the money shall be paid to him; "2. If the plaintiff recover judgment, the money shall be applied upon his judgment, and the surplus, if any, paid to the defendant; "3. If the plaintiff's judgment exclusive of costs is not in excess of the tender exclusive of the portion thereof deposited on account of costs, the plaintiff cannot recover costs or interest from the time of the tender, but must pay the defendant's costs from that time; "4. The plaintiff's right to costs is determined by the excess of his judgment exclusive of costs over the amount of the tender exclusive of the amount thereof deposited on account of costs. "Section 174. Tender on counterclaim. A tender may be made by the plaintiff in respect of a counterclaim, in the same cases, in the same manner, and with the same effect as to the counterclaim, as in case of a tender by a defendant on account of a demand for payment in a complaint. "Section 175. Offer to liquidate damages conditionally.

In an action to recover damages for breach of a contract, the defendant's attorney may serve, with the answer upon the plaintiff's attorney, a written offer that if the defendant fails in his defense the damages may be assessed at a specified sum. If the plaintiff serves notice, with or before the notice of trial, that he accepts the offer, and damages are awarded to him on the trial, they must be assessed accordingly. "Section 176. Effect of refusal of offer. If the plaintiff does not accept the offer, he cannot prove it upon the trial. If the damages awarded to the plaintiff do not exceed the sum offered, the defendant is entitled to recover the expenses necessarily incurred by him in preparing for the trial of the question of damages. The expenses must be

ascertained and the amount thereof determined by the judge, or the referee, before whom the cause is tried." 

For the application of the Civil Practice Act, section 172, see Hirsch v. Spitzer, 208 App. Div. 711, 202 N. Y. S. 338. 

§ 423. Duty To Make Change. 

The sum tendered should be such as not to require the creditor to make change. However, a duty is imposed upon carriers of passengers to make change for a reasonable amount. The question of the reasonableness of the rules of these companies as to the amount, where the facts are undisputed, is one of law for the courts. A rule of a street railway company that not more than two dollars will be accepted and change given in payment of a five cent fare is reasonable. And where a passenger tenders a five dollar bill, it may be refused and the passenger may be ejected, since the amount tendered is unreasonable. Barker v. Central Park, North & East River R. R. Co., 151 N. Y. 237, 45 N. E. 550, 35 L. R. A. 489 and note. However, in Barrett v. Market Street Ry. Co., 81 Cal. 296, 22 Pac. 859, 6 L. R. A. 336, it was held that it was not unreasonable for a passenger to tender a five dollar bill to a street car conductor in payment of his fare. And in Jones v. Louisville & Nashville R. R., 109 Miss. 655, 68 So. 924, where a passenger tendered a railroad conductor a hundred dollar bill, with the request that the conductor return the necessary change only when they reached their destination in a large city, the size of the bill was held to be immaterial to the question of the reasonableness of the amount tendered.

The duty of a telegraph company to make change is in doubt. Apparently, the company may justly refuse to send a message unless the sender tenders the exact amount of the charges. Dale v. Western Union Telegraph Co., 181 

App. Div. 292, 168 N. Y. S. 783.

§ 424. Legal Tender-Lawful Money. 

Since June 5, 1933, all coins and currencies are legal tender for any amount. Formerly there were certain restrictions as to fractional silver coins, coppers, nickels, silver certificates, and bank notes. An Act of the 73d Congress, Extra Session, provides: "All coins and currencies of the United States (including Federal Reserve notes and circulating notes of Federal Reserve banks and national banking associations) heretofore or hereafter coined or issued, shall be legal tender for all debts, public and private, public charges, taxes, duties and dues." United States 

Code, Title 31, Chap. 9, sec. 462 (1933). 

§ 425. 

Waiver of Legal Tender.

While a legal tender can be made only in money which has been made legal tender by law, the objection to the medium in which the tender is made may be waived by the creditor. Thus, if a check is tendered by a debtor, and the creditor refuses to receive it on some ground other than that it is not legal tender, the tender is valid. Gaunt v. 

Alabama Bound Oil & Gas Co., 281 Fed. 653, 23 A. L. R. 

1279 and note; Mitchell v. Vermont Copper Mining Co., 67 N. Y. 280. So, too, where a debtor was in the act of drawing his check for the amount due, he was interrupted by the creditor with an inquiry as to the amount of the check, and a dispute arose as to the amount due. The creditor finally declared that he would not accept the amount offered. It was held that there was a sufficient tender. Link v. Mack, 25 Misc. 615, 56 N. Y. S. 115. Furthermore, it has been held that, in the absence of notice to the effect that property of large value must be paid for in cash, a buyer is entitled to time to secure the money from the bank on the following day if the seller refuses to accept the buyer's check after banking hours, at least, where, from previous dealings, the buyer had reason to believe that his check would be accepted. Farris v. Ferguson, 146 Tenn. 

498, 242 S. W. 873, 23 A. L. R. 624 and note.

SUBSTANTIAL PERFORMANCE § 426. Sufficiency of Performance. 

It is a general rule of law that a contract must be performed according to the terms of the agreement before a party can have any right of action thereon. A person cannot be compelled to accept one thing when he bargained for another. He has the right to have the thing contracted for in the form agreed upon. While these general principles were strictly enforced under the common law, yet the more modern decisions indicate a tendency to relax the rigors of the earlier rules. Harrild v. Spokane School District, 112 Wash. 266, 192 Pac. 1, 19 A. L. R. 811. The modern tendency is to look to the spirit of the contract and not to the letter of it, and to make the vital question, therefore, not whether a party has literally complied with the terms of the contract, but whether he has substantially done so. Although a plaintiff is not absolutely free from fault in every particular, the court will not always turn him away without any remedy if he has in good faith made substantial performance, but will accord to him certain rights on the one hand, and preserve the rights of the defendant on the other, by permitting a recoupment. "The courts never say that one who makes a contract fills the measure of his duty by less than full performance. 

They do say, however, that an omission, both trivial and innocent, will sometimes be atoned for by allowance of the resulting damage, and will not always be the breach of a condition to be followed by a forfeiture." Jacob & Youngs v. Kent, 230 N. Y. 239, 129 N. E. 889, 23 A. L. R. 1429 and note. For example, it has been held that a contract to place and keep up fourteen hundred signs on an elevated railway is substantially performed where all except twenty of the signs were kept up, and these twenty were to have been placed in galleries leading to a bridge but were placed in temporary galleries while the other galleries were being reconstructed. Desmond Dunne Co. v. Friedman- 

Doscher Co., 162 N. Y. 486, 56 N. E. 995. So, also,

where plumbing of the same quality but of a different brand from that called for in the specifications is placed in a building, there may be a recovery on the theory of substantial performance. Jacob & Youngs v. Kent, supra. But there can be no recovery under the theory of substantial performance unless the plaintiff acted in good faith. Gillespie Tool Co. v. Wilson, 123 Pa. St. 19, 16 Atl. 36. Thus, where A contracted to keep B's automobile in "good running condition," putting it in "fair condition" was not substantial performance. Kehoe v. Newman, 169 N. Y. S. 71.

A contract to build a monument is not substantially performed if the monument is of different dimensions from those specified in the contract. Cutler v. Dix, 67 Vt. 347, 31 Atl. 780; Blitz v. Toovey, 28 N. Y. St. Rep. 160, 9 N. Y. S. 

439. The doctrine of substantial performance has been recognized for many years in the courts of equity in actions for specific performance. A court of equity in such actions may give the plaintiff relief and compel a defendant to perform, although the plaintiff cannot tender all he contracted to deliver. Thus, a contract for the sale of a farm of two hundred acres may be enforced, even though the farm consists of but one hundred and ninety-five acres. The court in its discretion may grant the vendor specific performance against the vendee, with a deduction of a proportionate amount from the purchase price. But if the variation is great, equity will not compel the purchaser to perform. 

Wetmore v. Bruce, 118 N. Y. 319, 23 N. E. 303; King v. 

Bardeau, 6 Johns. Ch. (N.Y.) 38, 10 Am. Dec. 312. 

$ 427. Substantial Performance of Building Contracts. 

The doctrine of substantial performance finds frequent application in building contracts. Jacob & Youngs v. Kent, 230 N. Y. 239, 129 N. E. 889, 23 A. L. R. 1429 and note.

Substantial performance entitles the builder to recover under a complaint for performance, with allowances to the owner for unsubstantial omissions. Nolan v. Whitney, 88 

N. Y. 648, 24 Hun 530. This is now the accepted view in

England. Dakin & Co. v. Lee, 1 K. B. Div. (Eng.) 566 

(1916). The deviations in the completion of or failure to complete the building must not have been wilful or intentional. There must be an honest attempt to perform the contract. If the contract is performed in a careless and negligent manner and defects appear throughout the entire building, there is not a substantial performance. Carpenter 

Co. v. Ellsworth, 151 App. Div. 532, 136 N. Y. S. 108; Easthampton Lumber & Coal Co. v. Worthington, 186 N. Y. 

407, 79 N. E. 323.

The principal reason for allowing a recovery in case of substantial performance is that the building so constructed is an additional value to the land and it is not deemed fair to allow to the owner such increased value without requiring compensation therefor. Approximate justice is arrived at in such cases by allowing the contractor to recover the contract price less a deduction for the damages caused by the omission or defects. But if there be structural defects, or defects in material, or in specifications as to size of rooms, windows, doors, etc., there can be no recovery even on quantum meruit, and this, too, though the building as completed may be substantial, habitable, and of much value. 

Spence v. Ham, 163 N. Y. 220, 57 N. E. 412, 51 L. R. A. 238; 

Foeller v. Heintz, 137 Wis. 169, 118 N. W. 543, 24 L. R. A. 

(N.S.) 327 and note; Steel Storage & Elevator Construction 

Co. v. Stock, 225 N. Y. 173, 121 N. E. 786.

CONTRACTS CONDITIONED UPON PERSONAL SATISFACTION § 428. Performance to the Satisfaction of Adverse Party. 

A contract in which one party agrees to perform his part to the "satisfaction" of the adverse party presents important questions as to what constitutes satisfaction. Contracts involving this question may be classified as follows: 

1. Contracts involving personal taste; 

2. Contracts not involving personal taste; 

3. Contracts for the sale of goods.

§ 429. 

Contracts Involving Personal Taste. 

Where a contract stipulates that it is to be performed to the satisfaction of one of the parties and its performance involves personal taste, fancy, judgment, or individual preference, the party who is to be satisfied is the sole judge as to whether or not he has received all that he has a right to demand. There can be no recovery under such contracts unless the party who is to make compensation is satisfied. For example, where the publishers of a newspaper engaged the plaintiff to write for the paper for a certain period, provided his services were "satisfactory to the publishers," it was held that the publishers were the sole judges of the quality of the work done. The Court said: "An article printed in a paper or magazine may please one person and displease another. It may gratify the taste of one and become the subject of criticism and ridicule on the part of another. The plaintiff did not agree to satisfy a court or jury, but undertook to satisfy the publishers. It was their taste, their fancy, their interest and their judgment that was to be satisfied." Crawford v. Mail & Express Publishing Co., 163 N. Y. 404, 57 N. E. 616. 

An interesting case involving a contract for personal service in which the employee conceded his services were "special, unique, and extraordinary" is Applegate v. Macfadden Newspaper Publishing Corp., 214 App. Div. 221, 212 N. Y. S. 67. The Court held that when the employer becomes dissatisfied with the services of the employee he has a right to dismiss him and is not called upon to give reasons, subject to one limitation; i.e., that the employer's dissatisfaction must be real and genuine and not merely feigned or simulated. The employer may act unreasonably, but he must act in good faith. The question of performance to the satisfaction of a party is further illustrated in the following cases: Saxe v. Shubert Theatrical Co., 57 Misc. 

620, 108 N. Y. S. 683 (an actor); Adams Radiator & Boiler 

Works v. Schnader, 155 Pa. St. 394, 26 Atl. 745 (steam heating apparatus); Gibson v. Cranage, 39 Mich. 49, 33

Am. Rep. 351 (portrait); Zaleski v. Clark, 44 Conn. 218, 26 Am, Rep. 446 (bust); Brown v. Foster, 113 Mass. 136, 18 Am. Rep. 463 (suit of clothes); Haven v. Sol Smith 

Russell, 34 N. Y. S. 292 (manuscript for play); Gray v. 

Alabama National Bank, 38 N. Y. St. Rep. 169, 14 N. Y. S. 

155 (design for steel plate); Walker v. Edward Thompson 

Co., 37 App. Div. 536, 56 N. Y. S. 326 (literary work). 

§ 430. Contracts Not Involving Personal Taste. 

Where, under a contract not involving personal taste, one agrees to perform to the satisfaction of a party, and the work and labor would be wholly lost to the party who rendered the services unless he is paid therefor, the courts hold it would be manifestly inequitable to allow a repudiation of the contract on the ground of dissatisfaction, and rule that what "the law will say a contracting party ought in reason to be satisfied with, that the law will say he is satisfied with." Therefore, if he ought to be satisfied, the law says he is satisfied. In such cases he cannot, arbitrarily and unreasonably, deny satisfaction. This rule of law has been applied in a contract for the repair of a boiler, to be paid for only when the defendant was "satisfied" that it was, as changed, a "success." The contract does not involve a matter of personal taste. It is, at most, a promise to give reasonable satisfaction, subject to the judgment of judicial triers. Duplex Safety Boiler Co. v. Garden, 101 

N. Y. 387, 4 N. E. 749; Doll v. Noble, 116 N. Y. 230, 22

N. E. 406, 5 L. R. A. 554. So, also, an agreement of a life insurance company to reinstate a former policy holder on the production of satisfactory evidence of his insurability does not contemplate the exercise of the company's taste, fancy, or caprice. When it ought to be satisfied, it is re garded as satisfied. Thompson v. Postal Life Insurance 

Co., 226 N. Y. 363, 123 N. E. 750. 

A contract for a ventilating machine, guaranteed to operate to "the purchaser's satisfaction," involves no question of personal taste or individual preference. Hummel v.

Stern, 21 App. Div. 544, 48 N. Y. S. 528, aff'd 164 N. Y. 

603, 58 N. E. 1088.

In doubtful cases the courts are strongly inclined to construe contracts of this class as agreements to perform or to do the thing in such a way as ought reasonably to satisfy the defendant. Thus, where a contract to furnish a system of heating for a building specifies that the system shall prove satisfactory, the satisfactoriness is to be determined by the mind of a reasonable man and not by the private taste or liking of the owner of the building. Hawkins v. Graham, 149 Mass. 284, 21 N. E. 312. The view in the latter case is expressly approved in Erikson v. Ward, 266 Ill. 259, 107 N. E. 593. To the same effect is Parlin & Orendorff Co. v. Greenville, 127 Fed. 55. Where a builder agreed to pay a plastering contractor a bonus for cooperating in hastening the work to the builder's satisfaction, the builder to be the sole judge thereof, the Court held that the contractor was entitled to the bonus if he did the work in proper and workmanlike manner, though the builder was not satisfied with it. Duffy Bros. v. Bing & Bing, 217 

App. Div. 10, 215 N. Y. S. 755. § 431.

Contracts for the Sale of Goods. 

In the sale of goods under a contract that the goods may be returned if not satisfactory to the vendee, the rule of personal taste or individual preference quite generally applies. McClure Bros. v. Briggs, 58 Vt. 82, 2 Atl. 583 (organ); McCarren v. McNulty, 7 Gray (Mass.) 139 (bookcase). Even though personal taste is not involved, if the contract makes the buyer the sole judge, he may return the goods at least, where the seller can be substantially placed in status quo. Singerly v. Thayer, 108 Pa. St. 291, 2 Atl. 

230 (passenger elevator); Exhaust Ventilator Co. v. Chicago, Milwaukee & St. Paul R. R. Co., 66 Wis. 218, 28 N. W. 

343 (exhaust fans); Campbell Printing Press Co. v. Thorp, 36 Fed. 414, 1 L. R. A. 645 (printing press); Wood Machine 

Co. v. Smith, 50 Mich. 565, 15 N. W. 906 (harvesting ma-

chine); Aiken v. Hyde, 99 Mass. 183 (gas generator). See, also, Dvorak v. Prucha, 156 Ill. App. 514, wherein it is held that if a seller contracts to furnish an article satisfactory to the purchaser, with the right to return the same within a specified time if not satisfactory, the purchaser has an absolute right to return the same within such time. 

A case frequently cited by the courts is Sabarsky v. Drew, 176 

App. Div. 80, 162 N. Y. S. 505, aff'd 226 N. Y. 557, 123 

N. E. 887. 

§ 432. 

Approval by Third Person.

Where the articles sold or the work to be done or performed is to be subject to the approval of, or satisfactory to, some third person, the approval of such person becomes a condition precedent to a recovery of the price. In the absence of fraud or bad faith, his judgment or determination is final and conclusive. Where the parties stipulate that the legality or conditions of a contract shall be first approved by the attorney of one of the parties as to its validity, his approval becomes a condition precedent to the inception of the contract. Wilhelm v. Wood, 151 App. Div. 

42, 135 N. Y. S. 930; Atkins v. Trowbridge, 162 App. Div. 

629, 148 N. Y. S. 181; Church v. Shanklin, 95 Cal. 626, 30 Pac. 789, 17 L. R. A. 207 and note. Where in a cost. plus contract the contractor agrees to build houses for the owner, under the direction and to the satisfaction of the owner's superintendent, is the contractor entitled to compensation if the superintendent is honestly dissatisfied either with the work as completed or with the methods used by the contractor, provided he believes that such methods are extravagant and have led to an increase in the cost? 

Thompson-Starrett Co. v. La Belle Iron Works, 17 Fed. 

(2d) 536. 

§ 433. Building Contract-Architect's Certificate. 

Where a building or construction contract provides that the contractor shall not be entitled to any payment until

he has obtained a certificate from the architect or the engineer that the work has been done in strict accordance with the drawings and specifications and that the payment is properly due, the contractor cannot recover for work performed under the contract without obtaining such certificate, unless the provisions requiring it have been waived, or he is prevented from obtaining it by some wrongful act of the owner, or the fraudulent, collusive, or unreasonable refusal of the architect. Such is the rule in New York and in many other states. Sweet v. Morrison, 116 N. Y. 19, 22 

N. E. 276; Neidlinger v. Onward Construction Co., 107 

App. Div. 398, 95 N. Y. S. 1148, aff'd 188 N. Y. 572; 

Hamilton College v. Roberts, 223 N. Y. 56, 119 N. E. 97;

Hennebique Construction Co. v. Boston Cold Storage & 

Terminal Co., 230 Mass. 456, 119 N. E. 948, L. R. A. 1918F 

374 and note. However, there are authorities which hold that, unless the architect acts fraudulently, there can be no recovery by the builder, and that the fact that the architect acted unreasonably or unfairly does not dispense with the necessity of obtaining his certificate. See Second National Bank v. Pan-American Bridge Co., 183 Fed. 301; 

Tullis v. Jacson, 3 Ch. (Eng.) 441 (1892); Landstra v. 

Bunn, 81 N. J. L. 680, 80 Atl. 496.

As a matter of law, the failure of a contractor to secure the architect's or engineer's certificate required by the contract is not excused by full and complete performance of all of the other terms of the contract. Bush v. Jones, 144 

Fed. 942, 6 L. R. A. (N.S.) 774.

CHAPTER XXI  § 434. Definition of Breach. 

A breach of a contract consists of an unjustified nonperformance of a contractual duty by a party to the contract. It may take place through a repudiation of the contract or by conduct inconsistent with the contract. Howard v. Daly, 61 N. Y. 362, 19 Am. Rep. 285. § 435. 

Effect of Breach.

The effect of a breach of a contract is to create a new obligation; namely, the duty to compensate the party injured by the breach. The breach always gives to the injured party a right of action and frequently discharges him from further contractual obligations to the other party. 

It does not have this effect, however, where the breach relates merely to a subsidiary part of the contract. For instance, where there is substantial performance of a building contract, the failure of the contractor fully to perform does not discharge the owner from his obligation to make payment, although the owner may make proper deductions for damages. Nolan v. Whitney, 88 N. Y. 649; Roehm v. 

Horst, 178 U. S. 1, 44 Law. Ed. 953, 20 Sup. Ct. Rep. 780. § 436. 

Rights of Party Discharged.

Where a party to a contract is discharged by the default of the other party, he may : 1. 

Consider himself exonerated and resist an action brought against him for non-performance on his part, and sue on quantum meruit to recover the reasonable value of the part, if any, which he has performed. Kokomo Strawboard Co. v. Inman, 134 N. Y. 92, 31 N. E. 248; Henderson 

Tire & Rubber Co. v. Wilson & Son, 235 N. Y. 489, 499, 139 

N. E. 583; or 352

2. Sue at once for such damages as he has sustained, without showing full performance on his part. He need not wait until the time fixed for performance. Canda v. 

Wick, 100 N. Y. 127, 2 N. E. 381; Henderson Tire & Rubber Co. v. Wilson & Son, supra. 

§ 437. Modes of Discharge by Breach. 

A breach of contract may occur in one of three ways: 

Either party may renounce the contract: 1. a. 

Before performance is due, or b. In the course of performance. 

2. Either party may make it impossible for the other to perform: a. 

Before performance is due, or b. In the course of performance. 

3. Either party may, without justification or excuse, totally or partially fail to perform that which he promised. 

§ 438. Renunciation before Performance Is Due. 

An unqualified and positive refusal to perform a contract before performance is due constitutes a complete breach of the contract, often called "an anticipatory breach." The parties have a right to rely upon the contractual relationship being maintained from the date of the making of the contract until the performance is due, and renunciation before this time gives the injured party a right to an immediate action. A contract is a contract from the time it is made and not from the time it is to be performed. For instance, on February 1, 1920, A employs B for one year, the employment to begin September 1, 1920. On March 

1, 1920, A writes B that he will not require his services. B may bring an action instantly for damages for the breach of the contract. Roehm v. Horst, 178 U. S. 1, 44 Law. Ed. 953, 20 Sup. Ct. Rep. 780; Petschke v. Rohdiek, 173 N. Y. S. 380.

The rule that an immediate right of action arises upon renunciation applies to only three classes of contracts:

1. 

Contracts to marry; 2. 

Contracts for personal service; 

3. Contracts for the sale or manufacture of goods. 

Kelly v. Security Mutual Life Insurance Co., 186 N. Y. 16, 

78 N. E. 584.

The right to sue immediately upon a renunciation does not apply to contracts for the payment of money at a future time. In some jurisdictions the principle is disapproved unqualifiedly. Porter v. Supreme Council, 183 Mass. 326, 67

N. E. 238. An interesting and important case in this connection is Hall v. Nassau Consumers Ice Co., 260 N. Y. 417, 183 N. E. 903.

The party not in default is not compelled to act immediately upon the renunciation. He may treat the contract as still subsisting, and defer his action until the time for performance arrives. Ga Nun v. Palmer, 202 N. Y. 483, 96 

N. E. 99, 36 L. R. A. (N.S.) 922; De Forest Radio Telephone & Telegraph Co. v. Triangle Radio Supply Co., 243 

N. Y. 283, 153 N. E. 75. So, the seller, upon an anticipatory repudiation of the contract by the buyer, may elect to consider his contractual obligation at an end and sue at once as for an anticipatory breach of the contract. The seller may elect, however, to waive the anticipatory breach and to treat the contract as alive, in which case the contract is kept alive for the benefit of both parties, so that the buyer has as much right to a performance on the seller's part as the seller has a right to a performance on the buyer's part. 

Lace Selling Co. v. Shapiro, 249 N. Y. 68, 162 N. E. 586.

If, after an election to treat the contract as still subsisting, the seller fails to perform according to the terms of the contract, the buyer may avail himself of any legal advantage which he may have by reason of the seller's failure to perform. "The buyer may now insist that the seller's misconduct shall be cast in the balance with its own." Rubber Trading Co. v. Manhattan Rubber Manufacturing Co., 221 N. Y. 120, 126, 116 N. E. 789.

§ 439. 

Anticipatory Breach-Essential Elements. 

To give the injured party an immediate right of action, the renunciation must be: a. 

Unequivocal and absolute; b. Regarded as renunciation by the other party; and 

C. Must terminate the entire contract.

There is authority for the principle that under certain circumstances an anticipatory inability to perform may amount, in legal contemplation, to an anticipatory repudiation of the contract. Restatement, Contracts, sec. 318; Hall v. Nassau Consumers Ice Co., 260 N. Y. 417, 183 N. E. 903. § 440.

Renunciation in Course of Performance. 

A repudiation of a contract in the course of its performance brings the contract to an end. The other party is not at liberty to disregard the renunciation. He cannot complete the contract, if, by so doing, the damages are increased. However, he is fully protected. Two remedies are available to him. He may elect to treat the contract as rescinded and recover, on quantum meruit, the reasonable value of that part of the contract which he has performed, or he may sue for damages for breach of contract and recover what the labor and material furnished are actually worth plus the loss of profits measured by legal principles applicable for the assessment of damages in the particular case. For instance, in building and construction contracts the loss of profits would be the difference between the contract price and the cost of performance, assuming the cost to be less than the contract price. Clark v. Marsiglia, 1 

Denio (N.Y.) 317, 43 Am. Dec. 670; Wright v. Reusens, 133 N. Y. 298, 305, 31 N. E. 215; Valente v. Weinberg, 80 

Conn. 134, 67 Atl. 369, 13 L. R. A. (N.S.) 448 and note. So, also, where there is a contract for advertising and the defendant notifies the publisher to discontinue further insertions of the advertisements contracted for, the publisher is entitled to recover the profits he would have made if the notice had not been given and he had fully performed. J. K.

Rishel Furniture Co. v. Stuyvesant Co., 123 Misc. 208, 204 

N. Y. S. 659.

Where the action is based on quantum meruit, the contract price is important only in so far as it is evidence of the value of the labor and material furnished. The value may be, but is not necessarily, measured by the contract price. Connolly v. Sullivan, 173 Mass. 1, 53 N. E. 143.

The repudiation by a client of a contract with an attorney whereby the attorney was to serve for a contingent fee entitles the lawyer to recover only for the value of services rendered and not for the profits that would result from a successful prosecution of the litigation. This is true for the reason that the client may discontinue the suit at any time. 

Andrewes v. Haas, 214 N. Y. 255, 108 N. E. 423, 3 A. L. R. 458. 

§ 441. Impossibility Created by Either Party before Per formance Is Due.

Where the performance of a contract is made impossible by one of the parties, the effect is the same as repudiation. 

The party making the performance impossible does not discharge himself from liability. The injured party has an immediate right of action. Thus, A promises to sell and deliver certain goods to B at a certain time, but before the time arrives, he sells and delivers the goods to C. A promises to marry B, but before the time arrives, he marries C. In these cases, A disables himself from performing his contracts. B is discharged and may bring action at once. Where one thus disables himself from the performance of a contract, he waives performance by the other party. Woolner v. Hill, 93 N. Y. 576. 

§ 442. Impossibility Created by One Party in Course of 

Performance.

The rule applicable to cases where performance is made impossible before the time of performance is due is applied to cases of impossibility created by one party during the

course of performance. Where A agreed to furnish air propellers to remove the smoke from B's tempering room, B to furnish the power from a shaft in such room, and the machine to be subject to a thirty days' successful trial, it was held that the fact that the shaft broke and that B declined to furnish a new shaft, thus making impossible the stipulated test, rendered B liable to A for the contract price. 

Howard v. American Manufacturing Co., 162 N. Y. 347, 56

N. E. 986. Similarly, where a heating plant was sold under a contract by which the vendor was allowed a year in which to remedy any defects, the removal of the plant by the vendee before the expiration of the year made the vendee liable for the contract price. Lehmann v. Warren Webster & Co., 209 Ill. 264, 70 N. E. 600.

So, too, where the plaintiff sold to the defendant a monument upon which were to be inscribed four lines of verse to be furnished by the defendant, it was held that, if the defendant refused to furnish the lines of verse, the plaintiff could erect the monument without the verse and recover the contract price, less the cost of inscribing the verse. Eastern 

Granite Co. v. Heim, 89 Iowa 698, 57 N. W. 437.

FAILURE OF PERFORMANCE § 443. Partial or Total. 

Whether a partial or a total failure to perform a promise or covenant of a contract discharges the adverse party depends upon the nature and character of the respective promises. The question concretely stated is this: Where A and B make mutual promises, must a performance by A precede a performance by B; or may B be liable to A even though A has not performed; or must both A and B be ready to perform at the same time? This can be answered only by a determination of the relation of the promises. 

There are three possible relations: a. 

The promises, although mutual, may be independent of each other;

b. One promise may be dependent and the other independent;

C. The promises may be mutually dependent, one upon the other.

Where the promises are mutually independent of each other, either party to the contract may sue without showing performance. Where one promise is independent and the other dependent, the performance of the independent promise must precede the performance of the dependent promise. 

Where the promises are dependent or concurrent, the performance of both must be simultaneous-there must be a readiness or willingness to perform at the same time. 

Where the promises are thus concurrent, neither party can sue until he has put the other in default. Delaware Trust 

Co. v. Calm, 195 N. Y. 231, 88 N. E. 53.

In any particular case, whether the promises are dependent or independent, or dependent in part and independent in part is to be determined by the intention of the parties as ascertained from the contract. The general rule, however, is to consider all covenants dependent, in the absence of a contrary intention, for this is the way that most men make their bargains-neither party intends to perform unless the other at the same time performs on his part. The Personal Property Law, Sales Act, section 123, expressly provides that, unless otherwise agreed, delivery of the goods and payment of the price are concurrent conditions. A different construction would lead in many cases to the greatest injustice, since a purchaser might have payment enforced against him without procuring the property for which he paid. Delaware Trust Co. v. Calm, supra. 

But where the contract clearly indicates that one promise is to be performed prior to the other, a suit may be had on the prior promise without showing a readiness to perform the promise dependent thereupon. To illustrate: A land contract contained the following stringent provisions:

The seller covenanted that "after the making of the payments and the full performance of the covenants to be

made and performed by the purchaser," the vendor in consideration thereof, would convey by deed certain real property. The purchaser went into possession, made improvements, and paid all the instalments except the last, but tendered the last payment upon condition that the deed be delivered simultaneously. The vendor was not ready to tender a deed, but sued for the last payment without tender or offer to convey. The actual payment or tender of payment was held to be a condition precedent to the right to a conveyance. Eastern Oregon Land Co. v. Moody, 198 

Fed. 7.

Where a contract for the sale of land provides for partial or instalment payments of the purchase money prior to a delivery of the deed, the vendor may sue for each instalment when due without tendering a conveyance. But when the last instalment falls due, the payment of the unpaid purchase money and the conveyance or delivery of the deed become dependent upon acts concurrent in point of time. The vendor cannot maintain an action to recover the purchase price without proving a readiness or offer, before suit, to convey the land to the vendee upon receipt of the purchase price. Ewing v. Wightman, 167 N. Y. 107, 60 N. E. 322; 

Beecher v. Conradt, 13 N. Y. 108, 64 Am. Dec. 535; Walker v. Hewitt, 109 Ore. 366, 220 Pac. 147, 35 A. L. R. 100 and note.

Mutually independent promises give rise to mutual remedies in case of default by either promisor. For instance, where A gave B his non-negotiable note and B, in consideration thereof, agreed to have satisfied, as of record, certain claims held by X which were liens on A's property, no time of performance being specified, it was held that the promises were independent of each other and that A should not succeed in an action for a cancelation of the note by reason of B's failure to perform his promise. A's remedy is to sue for damages for the breach of B's promise. Tronson v. 

Colby University, 9 N. D. 559, 84 N. W. 474.

Independent promises are further illustrated in the case

of Waterhouse v. Kendall, 11 Cush. (Mass.) 128. In this case the action was upon a promissory note and the defendant pleaded the general issue, and relied upon a receipt for the note in which the plaintiff promised to procure and deliver up a certain execution issued in favor of a third party against the defendant, which execution, he attempted to show, had never been so delivered up. Upon appeal from a verdict in favor of the plaintiff, the Court, by Shaw, C.J., overruling the defendant's exceptions, said: "This is a note unlimited in its terms for $105.37, payable to the plaintiff, or the same thing. Can the defendant set up a receipt in defense? What is the undertaking? That Springer will procure and deliver up the execution. That is a good consideration for the note. Is it a condition precedent to a recovery upon it? We think not. Here was one undertaking to pay money, another to deliver up the execution. Neither was precedent to the other. The stipulation to procure and deliver up the execution on receiving the note is a mutual and independent stipulation, executory in its character, a failure to perform which gives a good ground of action against the party undertaking it. Suppose the defendant were sued on the judgment, and these facts could not be proved in defense. Then the other party would be responsible on the undertaking to procure and deliver up the execution, which is, in effect, a contract to indemnify. The contracts were to be fulfilled at different times, and were independent, though mutual."

Promises, one dependent on the other, in which the performance of one depends on the prior performance of the other are illustrated by Northrup v. Northrup, 6 Cowen (N.Y.) 296, wherein A promised to pay to one X certain rent due and in arrears on a certain farm, and to pay all that should become due on March 25, 1825. B promised that on A's so paying the rent, he would give up and discharge a certain bond and mortgage. In a suit by B for the rent, A pleaded that on March 25, B did not give up and discharge the bond and mortgage. It was held that A's

plea was bad, as the performance of his promise was a condition precedent to performance by B. § 444. 

Waiver of Condition Precedent. 

Performance of a condition precedent may be waived by the party for whose benefit it was introduced into the contract. Clark v. West, 193 N. Y. 349, 86 N. E. 1. "Although conditions precedent must be performed, and a partial performance is not sufficient, yet when a contract has been performed in a substantial part, and the other party has voluntarily received and accepted the benefit of the part performance, knowing that the contract was not being fully performed, the latter may be thereby precluded from relying upon the performance of the residue as a condition precedent to his liability to pay for what he has received, and may be compelled to rely upon his claim for damages in respect to the defective performance." Wiley v. Athol, 150 Mass. 426, 23 N. E. 311, 6 L. R. A. 342; Rosenthal Paper Co. v. National Folding Box & Paper Co., 226 

N. Y. 313, 123 N. E. 766.

ENTIRE AND DIVISIBLE CONTRACTS 

§ 445.

Entire and Divisible Contracts Defined. 

An entire contract is one where full and complete performance on one side constitutes a consideration for the performance on the other. It is one bargain or unit, and it matters not whether there are one or several things to be done. Until the performance is completed, there is no obligation to pay. Full performance becomes a condition precedent to payment. Ming v. Corbin, 142 N. Y. 334, 37 

N. E. 105; Gorse v. Lynch, 36 Misc. 150, 72 N. Y. S. 1054.

A divisible contract is one which in its nature and purposes is susceptible of division and apportionment. If the contract is divisible, a failure to perform one item does not entitle the other party to rescind the contract and to refuse to accept further performance, nor does it discharge him

from the obligation to pay for the part which has been performed. Tipton v. Feitner, 20 N. Y. 423.

Whether a contract is entire or divisible is often a question of intention and not infrequently one of fact. Portfolio v. Rubin, 233 N. Y. 439, 445, 135 N. E. 843; Stearns 

Salt & Lumber Co. v. Dennis Lumber Co., 188 Mich. 700, 154 N. W. 91, 2 A. L. R. 638 and note. 

§ 446. General Rules for Determining Entirety and Divisi bility of Contracts.

Parsons lays down the following rule for determining whether a contract is entire or divisible: "If the part to be performed by one party consists of several distinct and separate items, and the price to be paid by the other is apportioned to each item to be performed, or is left to be implied by law, such a contract will generally be held to be severable. * * * And if the consideration to be paid is single and entire, the contract must be held to be entire, although the subject of the contract may consist of several distinct and wholly independent items." Parsons on 

Contracts, 8th Ed., Vol. II, p. 634. This doctrine is narrower than the cases, as a whole, would seem to justify. 

The entirety of the contract does not necessarily depend on the nature of the items and the price apportioned to each or all thereof. If the seller was unwilling to sell one portion without selling the whole, and the buyer was unwilling to take a part unless he could have the whole, the contract is entire in its nature. Rugg v. Moore, 110 Pa. St. 236, 1 Atl. 

320. So, too, the contract is presumably entire where it is for the sale of a quantity of goods, although the price is based upon weight or measure, at a certain rate per unit; or where the taking of the whole is essential for the purposes of the buyer; or where the contract, by its terms, nature, and purpose shows that all of its parts should be common each to the others, and that all were necessary to accomplish the purpose for which the contract was made, and this, too, even though the consideration was appor-

tioned to the separate items. Hull v. Littauer, 8 App. Div. 

227, 40 N. Y. S. 338, aff'd 162 N. Y. 569, 57 N. E. 102; 

Seibert v. Dunn, 216 N. Y. 237, 110 N. E. 447; Manda v. 

Sullivan County Club, 16 Misc. 366, 38 N. Y. S. 55; Hoggson Bros. v. Spiekerman, 175 App. Div. 144, 161 N. Y. S. 

930. See, also, the authorities collected in 2 A. L. R. 643. 

Illustrative cases:

The plaintiff contracted to sell to the defendant his stock of goods and, also, two stores and lots, all at the same time and embraced in the same contract. The contract treated the goods and the real property as distinct subjects of sale, the price of each being definitely fixed. It was held to be a divisible contract, and the avoidance of the contract by the plaintiff as to the land did not avoid the contract as to the stock of goods. Wooten v. Walters, 110 N. C. 251, 14 

S. E. 734.

A contract, by which the plaintiff agreed to deliver oak logs for $1 per thousand, and other logs for 75 cents per thousand, from certain points on a stream to a certain boom, and to deliver cross-ties at 3 cents each to a certain place, was held to be severable, and to entitle the plaintiff to recover at the specified rates for all logs and ties delivered at the specified points. Gill v. Johnstown Lumber 

Co., 151 Pa. St. 534, 25 Atl. 120.

Where a landscape architect submitted an estimate for labor and materials in laying out grounds, specifying the different items of labor, trees, vines, grass seed, etc., giving the price and sum of each item, and also recommended three tons, at $38 per ton, of sheep manure for fertilizing the ground, and his estimate, including the manure, was accepted, it was held that the contract was entire and that the gardener could not sue separately for the manure without proof of the performance of the entire contract. Manda v. 

Sullivan County Club, supra.

A contract to cut, cure, and stack hay on a ranch, at so much per ton, which did not specify the number of tons,

or the number of acres, or the work to be done, or the gross amount to be paid, was held to be a divisible contract and recovery could be had for each ton stacked. Hindrey v. 

Williams, 9 Colo. 371, 12 Pac. 436.

A contract to build a brick house, brick stable, and other buildings, each at a specified sum, was held to be divisible and the contractor entitled to recover for building the stable, although he abandoned the contract as to the other buildings. White v. Livingston, 69 App. Div. 361, 75 

N. Y. S. 466, aff'd 174 N. Y. 538, 66 N. E. 1118.

A contract to paint a house, barn, and windmill for a lump sum has been held to be entire, and the workman cannot recover for part, or unsubstantial, performance. 

Pearse v. Divver, 23 Misc. 691, 52 N. Y. S. 87. § 447.

Rescission of Entire and Divisible Contract. 

Where the contract is entire, the rescission must be as to the whole contract. A party cannot disaffirm the contract in part and enforce it in part. He must also place the other party in status quo. If this cannot be done, the contract cannot be rescinded. Where the contract is divisible, a failure by one of the parties to perform one of the terms does not authorize the other party to rescind the whole contract and refuse performance of the other terms by the party in default in the first instance. Morgan v. Mc- 

Kee, 77 Pa. St. 228; Rubin v. Sturtevant, 80 Fed. 930.

A contract for the sale of two piles of lumber for a single consideration is entire, and the purchaser cannot accept part of the lumber and reject the remainder on the ground that the part rejected does not meet the requirements of the contract. Levy v. Dettra & Co., 91 Misc. 41, 154 

N. Y. S. 176.

Divisibility of the price does not of itself make what would otherwise be an entire contract divisible. But where in a contract for the sale of goods, the price is divisible, the parties may elect to treat the contract as divisible. This

they did in Portfolio v. Rubin, 233 N. Y. 439, 135 N. E. 

843, where the defendant agreed to purchase four pieces of goods to be made into clothes. Upon delivery he accepted two only and rejected the other two as not in accordance with the order. The price of the goods accepted and those rejected could be easily ascertained by computation. It was held that the defendant had the right to accept part and reject the portion which did not comply with the contract. 

§ 448. Instalment Delivery and Payments. 

Where there is a contract for the sale of goods to be delivered in instalments which are to be paid for separately, authorities differ as to whether the contract is entire or divisible. If the seller makes defective deliveries in respect to one or more instalments, or the buyer neglects or refuses to accept deliveries or to pay for one or more instalments, is there a breach of the whole contract? Does a breach in respect to one instalment give the injured party a right to repudiate the entire contract? 

§ 449. Federal and New York Common Law Rule. 

The federal and New York courts have repudiated the doctrine that a sale and delivery of goods in instalments, to be paid for as each instalment is delivered, creates a divisible contract. They have held that such contracts are entire, and that provisions for periodical deliveries and payments do not render the contract divisible. Norrington v. 

Wright, 115 U. S. 188, 29 Law. Ed. 366, 6 Sup. Ct. Rep. 12. 

In Wolfert v. Caledonia Springs Ice Co., 195 N. Y. 118, 88 N. E. 24, 21 L. R. A. (N.S.) 864, it was held, in a contract for the sale of goods to be delivered in instalments, that the failure of the seller to deliver or the buyer to accept one instalment constituted such a breach of the contract as to give to the party not in default the right to rescind the entire contract. But in this case there was a waiver of the right to insist on full performance. Seibert v. Dunn, 216

N. Y. 237, 110 N. E. 447. So, also, a contract for the sale of flour by the barrel, to be shipped in instalments, was held to be entire. Consumers Bread Co. v. Stafford County 

Flour Mills Co., 239 Fed. 693. Contra, Gerli v. Poidebard 

Silk Manufacturing Co., 57 N. J. L. 432, 31 Atl. 401, 30 

L. R. A. 61.

Default in an instalment payment also has been held to constitute a breach of the entire contract and to justify repudiation by the seller. Raabe v. Squier, 148 N. Y. 81, 42 

N. E. 516; Ferguson v. Chuck, 236 N. Y. 149, 140 N. E. 225. 

§ 450. Sales Act Affecting Instalment Sales. 

The common law rule that a default in an instalment delivery or payment operated as a breach of the entire contract has been modified by statute. The Personal Property 

Law, Sales Act, section 126, subdivision 2, provides: "Where there is a contract to sell goods to be delivered by stated installments, which are to be separately paid for, and the seller makes defective deliveries in respect of one or more installments, or the buyer neglects or refuses to take delivery of or pay for one or more installments, it depends in each case on the terms of the contract and the circumstances of the case whether the breach of contract is so material as to justify the injured party in refusing to proceed further and suing for damages for breach of the entire contract, or whether the breach is severable, giving rise to a claim for compensation, but not to a right to treat the whole contract as broken." The statute thus establishes a like test for vendor and vendee. A mere failure to make a proper delivery of an instalment, of itself, does not necessarily give to the injured party a right to repudiate the entire contract. Such failure, however, when coupled with evidence of an intention not to complete performance, may justify the injured party in refusing to proceed further. 

Bahnsen & Co. v. Gerst, 235 N. Y. 426, 139 N. E. 563. 

Deliveries which are defective in quantity or quality may give a right to rescind the contract as for a material breach.

Thus, where ice is to be delivered in instalments and the quality of some of the ice delivered is defective and the seller admits he cannot furnish better ice, the buyer has been allowed to rescind the contract. Interboro Brewing 

Co. v. Independent Consumer's Ice Co., 93 Misc. 24, 156 

N. Y. S. 410; Ellison, Son & Co. v. Flat Top Grocery Co., 69 W. Va. 380, 71 S. E. 391, 38 L. R. A. (N.S.) 539. 

Failure To Pay Instalment When Due. 

A failure to pay an instalment when due does not necessarily effect a breach of the entire contract. In Helgar 

Corp. v. Warner's Features, 222 N. Y. 449, 119 N. E. 113, the Court said: "The vendor who fails to receive payment of an installment the very day that it is due, may sue at once for the price. But it does not follow that he may be equally precipitate in his election to declare the contract at an end. That depends upon the question whether the default is so substantial and important as in truth and in fairness to defeat the essential purpose of the parties. Whatever the rule may once have been, this is the test that is now prescribed by statute. The failure to make punctual payment may be material or trivial according to the circumstances." See, however, Bahnsen & Co. v. Gerst, 235 N. Y. 426, 139 N. E. 563; Ferguson v. Chuck, 236 N. Y. 149, 140 N. E. 225. 

§ 452. One Action for Breach of Instalment Contract. 

Since there is but one breach where the default in an instalment delivery or an instalment payment operates to discharge the entire contract, only one cause of action may be maintained thereon, and the injured party cannot sue successively for a breach of each remaining instalment. 

Pakas v. Hollingshead, 184 N. Y. 211, 77 N. E. 40, 3 

L. R. A. (N.S.) 1042 and note. § 453. Waiver of Default of Instalment. 

The failure to perform any instalment may be waived,

and when waived the right to rescind is lost. However, where a purchaser of a quantity of gloves of different styles, at a certain price per dozen, claims that the gloves do not comply with the contract, he must reject them as a whole. 

If he accepts a part of the gloves, he becomes liable for the entire lot. Mason v. Smith, 130 N. Y. 474, 29 N. E. 

749. So, too, if the buyer with knowledge of the delivery of defective instalments, remains silent and permits the vendor to incur the expense of preparing and delivering further instalments, he will be estopped from claiming a release from his obligation to receive and pay for further deliveries on account of defects in the earlier deliveries. 

McDonald v. Kansas City Bolt & Nut Co., 149 Fed. 360, 8 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1110 and note; Wolfert v. Caledonia 

Springs Ice Co., 195 N. Y. 118, 88 N. E. 24, 21 L. R. A. (N.S.) 864. § 454.

No Date for Payment of Instalments. 

Where a contract is for the delivery of goods in instalments and is silent as to the time of payment, the contract is entire, and payment need not be made at the time of delivery, but only after delivery of all the instalments. But where the contract is divisible, payment is to be made for each instalment on its delivery. Baker v. Higgins, 21 N. Y. 

397; Tipton v. Feitner, 20 N. Y. 423. A contract for the sale of all the brick to be used in the construction of a building, which has no fixed time of payment, is entire; and a vendor would breach his entire contract by a refusal to deliver further instalments until he receives payment for those already delivered. Kelly Construction Co. v. 

Hackensack Brick Co., 91 N. J. L. 585, 103 Atl. 417, 2 

A. L. R. 685.

INCOMPLETE PERFORMANCE OF ENTIRE CONTRACT 

$455. 

General Rule as to Recovery. 

Where the contract is entire, the plaintiff cannot recover in an action for breach of contract unless he proves:

a.

A complete performance of the contract; b. That a performance of the contract was waived by the defendant;

C. That the defendant prevented a complete performance of the contract; or d. That complete performance of the contract was excused by operation of law. 

EXCEPTIONS TO GENERAL RULE § 456. Subsidiary Promises. 

There are cases where the incomplete performance of an entire contract affects only a subordinate part of the undertaking. Such subordinate parts are known as subsidiary promises, the breach of which will not operate to discharge the contract, because it does not defeat the object to be accomplished. Thus, a contract requires an opera singer, whose services are engaged for the season, to be on hand for rehearsal a few days before the season begins. His failure to do so will not justify his employer in rescinding the contract. Bettini v. Gye, 1 Q. B. Div. (Eng.) 183 (1876). § 457. Benefits Conferred.

Cases frequently occur where one party has not fully performed his contract, but, nevertheless, has conferred a benefit upon the other party, and although a complete performance is a condition precedent to payment, under certain conditions the plaintiff may have some redress without having fully performed his obligation. Kauffmann v. Raeder, 108 Fed. 171, 54 L. R. A. 247; In re Morgantown Tin Plate

Co., 184 Fed. 109. The question most frequently arises in: a. Contracts where the rule as to substantial performance may be invoked; b. Contracts to repair buildings or to render service on the property of another;

C. 

Contracts of employment; d. Contracts for the sale of goods. § 458.

Substantial Performance. 

For the doctrine of substantial performance, see section 

426, supra.

In building contracts the owner may waive his rights resulting from an unsubstantial performance by acquiescing in the deviations, but mere occupation or use of the building will not constitute a waiver. Notwithstanding the fact that the building as completed has greatly added to the value of the land, the builder cannot recover compensation for unsubstantial performance. Smith v. Brady, 17 N. Y. 173, 72 Am. Dec. 442; Spence v. Ham, 163 N. Y. 

220, 57 N. E. 412, 51 L. R. A. 238. 

§ 459. Contracts To Repair Buildings or Chattels. 

The question as to who must bear the loss caused by the destruction of a building or chattel undergoing repair is discussed in section 393, supra.

Where the contract is for the repair of a building which is destroyed before the work is completed, the owner is liable for the work performed, as he is under an obligation to keep the building in readiness to receive the work. 

The same principle is applied to chattels which are destroyed before the completion of the work. The workman is allowed to recover at the price stipulated in the contract, or on quantum meruit if the contract price cannot be made applicable. Whelan v. Ansonia Clock Co., 97 N. Y. 293.

A recovery may be had for the value of the work done under a contract to move a building for a specified price, if the performance of the contract becomes impossible by reason of the destruction of the building through no fault of the contractor. Angus v. Scully, 176 Mass. 357, 57 N. E. 

674, 49 L. R. A. 562; L. R. A. 1917D 1012, note. A contrary holding prevails in a minority of jurisdictions. Taul-

bee v. McCarthy, 144 Ky. 199, 137 S. W. 1045, 36 L. R. A. (N.S.) 43. 

§ 460. 

Contracts of Employment. 

Where a servant voluntarily abandons his contract of employment before the end of his term of service, there can be no recovery for what he has done. Where the contract is entire, complete performance by the servant is a condition precedent to payment. If A engages B to work for one year at a stipulated price, B must perform the entire year's work before he can collect anything from A. 

If A should engage B to work for a year, at a specified rate, payable monthly, B could sue and recover his salary at the end of each month. But if without just cause, B leaves A's employment before the end of the contract period, A may retain from B's wages such damages as were the result of the breach of the contract. The measure of damages is the difference between the contract price and the amount which the employer has to pay to procure the performance of the services by some other person. Triangle Waist Co. v. Todd, 223 N. Y. 27, 119 N. E. 85. See note to Timberlake v. Thayer, 71 Miss. 279, 14 So. 446, 24 L. R. A. 231. 

Where an artist, who had contracted with a theatrical producer to stage a ballet for a play to the satisfaction of the producer, deliberately and intentionally breached the contract and walked out during a rehearsal prior to the dress rehearsal and before completion of his contract, it was held that there could be no recovery by the artist on the theory of contract or of quantum meruit. It was further held that the doctrine of substantial performance does not apply and that, therefore, plaintiff's judgment must be reversed and his complaint dismissed. Fokine v. Shubert, 210 App. Div. 468, 206 N. Y. S. 311.

If the completion of the contract is prevented by the employee's death or physical disability, his contractual obligation is discharged. Lacy v. Getman, 119 N. Y. 109, 23 N. E. 452, 6 L. R. A. 728; note to Runyan v. Culver, 168

Ky. 45, 181 S. W. 640, in L. R. A. 1916F 3, at p. 79. A like effect results from the imprisonment of an employee. 

Hughes v. Wamsutta Mills, 11 Allen (Mass.) 201. Where the relation is not that of employer and employee, the imprisonment of one of the parties may operate as a breach and not as a discharge of the contract. Jinnings v. Amend, 101 Kan. 130, 165 Pac. 845, L. R. A. 1917F 626 and note. 

§ 461. Contracts of Employment-Recovery on Quantum 

Meruit-Statute of Frauds.

The right to recover on quantum meruit for services rendered under an oral contract which is within the Statute of Frauds is discussed in section 249, supra. § 462. Employee Rightfully Discharged.

If a servant is rightfully and lawfully discharged because of conduct which incapacitates or disqualifies him from fulfilling his contract, he forfeits his wages for the time he has served. The effect is the same as a voluntary abandonment of the contract without cause. But if the employment is for a year, and the contract provides for monthly payments during the year, the employee, although discharged for just cause, may recover any arrears of monthly salary, subject to recoupment for any damages suffered by the employer by reason of neglect, unskillfulness, or non-performance by the employee. If the servant is discharged during the month, and before the salary therefor becomes due and payable, no recovery can be had under the contract for the work done during that month, for the reason that the contract is an entirety as to each month and a recovery for any portion thereof can be had only in an action for damages for breach. Tipton v. Feitner, 20 N. Y. 423; Huntington v. Claffin, 38 N. Y. 182; 

Walsh v. New York & Kentucky Co., 88 App. Div. 477, 85 N. Y. S. 83.

§ 463. Employee Wrongfully Discharged. 

Where an employee is wrongfully discharged; that is, without just cause, he may choose one of three remedies: 

1. He may consider the contract rescinded and sue on quantum meruit for services rendered, less such sum as he has received. 

2. He may wait until the end of the term and then sue for the full amount, less such sum as the defendant has a right to recoup. 

3. He may bring action at once for a breach of the contract. Banta v. Banta, 84 App. Div. 138, 82 N. Y. S. 113; 

Howard v. Daly, 61 N. Y. 362, 19 Am. Rep. 285. $ 464. Measure of Damages-Wrongful Discharge.

Where an employee is wrongfully discharged, the measure of damages, in an action for damages for the breach of the contract, is the amount of his salary under the contract, less any wages he has received or might reasonably have earned in a similar employment since his discharge. 

The burden of showing that the employee earned or might have earned something in another employment is upon the employer. Howard v. Daly, 61 N. Y. 362, 19 Am. Rep. 285; 

Banta v. Banta, 84 App. Div. 138, 82 N. Y. S. 113; Wooldridge v. Shea, 186 App. Div. 705, 175 N. Y. S. 130. Damages for injury to plaintiff's good name or character or for injury resulting in decreased ability to obtain employment in the future are not recoverable in an action for wrongful discharge. Sinclair v. Positype Corp. of America, 237 App. 

Div. 525, 261 N. Y. S. 900.

The discharged employee is under no obligation to accept an employment of a nature different from that for which he contracted, or in a different locality, nor is he under obligation to accept employment from a person against whom there is reasonable ground for objection. Levin v. 

Standard Fashion Co., 16 Daly 404, 11 N. Y. S. 706. See the authorities collected in 28 A. L. R. 736.

The employee may recover damages for the full contract period, although the contract period may not have ended at the time of the trial. Denniston v. Finnegan, 174 

App. Div. 8, 160 N. Y. S. 5. Contra, Howay v. Going 

Northrup Co., 24 Wash. 88, 64 Pac. 135, 6 L. R. A. (N.S.) 

48. Although the damages succeeding the trial may be largely conjectural or uncertain, the jury may approximately estimate the loss by taking into consideration all the facts, probabilities, certainties, and uncertainties. 

Wakeman v. Wheeler & Wilson Manufacturing Co., 101 

N. Y. 205, 4 N. E. 264; Davis v. Dodge, 126 App. Div. 469, 110 N. Y. S. 787; 32 A. L. R. 236, note.

Where the damages, however, are purely conjectural, they will not be allowed. In one case, the plaintiff was employed "for the purposes of promoting the club, acquiring members therefor, and developing and managing the club, or for any one or more of said purposes in the discretion of the owner" for a stated period of time at a rate of compensation equal to a certain percentage of payments made by club members for stock and for initiation fees. In an action to recover damages for wrongful discharge, it was held that the plaintiff could not recover, in addition to the agreed percentage on the amount paid by members before his discharge, a percentage on a sum fixed by the jury as the amount he would have earned if he had not been discharged. The latter is too speculative to constitute legal damages. Willis v. S. M. H. Corp., 259 N. Y. 144, 181 

N. E. 79. § 465. 

Offer To Reinstate Discharged Employee.

Where an employee is wrongfully discharged and thereafter is offered reinstatement by his former employer, it is a debatable question in many cases whether he must accept the offer and waive damages accruing after the offer of reemployment. The answer to the question seems to depend upon the circumstances attending the discharge, and the reasons, if any, for not accepting reemployment. If the em-

ployee is discharged after being wrongfully accused by his employer of theft or dishonesty, or if the employee's refusal to resume work is caused by fear of assault, he is justified in refusing reemployment. Horn v. Luntz, 125 N. Y. S. 

786; Levin v. Standard Fashion Co., 16 Daly 404, 11

N. Y. S. 706. Even in cases where the offer to reinstate the employee may be shown in mitigation of damages, the burden is on the employer to show the good faith of the offer. 

Bigelow v. American Forcite Powder Manufacturing Co., 39 Hun 599; Gray v. Pacific Suction Cleaner Co., 171 Cal. 

234, 155 Pac. 469. 

§ 466. Implied Renewal of Contract. 

Parties dealing with each other after the expiration of the period specified by a contract for its operation are presumed to proceed upon the same terms as those specified in the contract; the original contract is renewed with all its terms and conditions. Huntington v. Claffin, 38 N. Y. 182; 

Douglas v. Merchants Insurance Co., 118 N. Y. 484, 23 N. E. 

806, 7 L. R. A. 822; Stewart Dry Goods Co. v. Hutchison, 177 Ky. 757, 198 S. W. 17, L. R. A. 1918C 704 and note.

The rule that the terms of the original contract control, where services are continued without any new agreement, is also applied to leases where the period of leasing is for one year. Adams v. City of Cohoes, 127 N. Y. 175, 28 

N. E. 25; Haynes v. Aldrich, 133 N. Y. 287, 31 N. E. 94, 28 Am. St. Rep. 636. But where the lease is for a longer period than a year, there seems to be some question as to whether a holding over renews the original lease for the full period of its duration or from year to year only. Suppose, for instance, that A leases to B a dwelling or other building for a term of two years. At the end of the second year, without any agreement or even a conference as to a renewal of the lease, B, with A's implied consent, continues in possession, fulfilling all the conditions of the lease, for another year. Six months before the expiration of the third year of tenancy, B gives A notice that he will vacate

the premises at the end of the year. A contends that a holding over after the two-year period was, by implication of law, a renewal of the lease for two more years and, hence, seeks to hold B liable for another year's rent. B's contention is that a holding over under a two-year lease constituted a tenancy from year to year. Who is right, A or B? Kennedy v. City of New York, 196 N. Y. 19, 89 N. E. 360, 25 L. R. A. (N.S.) 847 and note. 

§ 467. Sale of Goods-Defective Delivery New York 

Common Law Rule. 

When the seller delivers only a part of the goods contracted for, he cannot recover for the part delivered, where the buyer received such part without knowledge that the balance would not be delivered. Champlin v. Rowley, 18

Wend. (N.Y.) 187. In this case the contract called for the sale of 100 tons of hay, to be delivered between certain dates and at a certain price per ton, part to be paid for in advance and the residue when the whole should be delivered. 

The seller delivered only 52 tons, and after the expiration of the time fixed for the delivery of the whole, brought action to recover for the quantity delivered at the stipulated price. It was held that there could be no recovery, since the buyer had neither waived nor prevented a full performance. The ruling in this case has been followed in New 

York and in some other jurisdictions. Catlin v. Tobias, 26 

N. Y. 217, 84 Am. Dec. 183; Nightingale v. Eiseman, 121 

N. Y. 288, 24 N. E. 475. A limitation of the doctrine of

Champlin v. Rowley, supra, was introduced in Avery v. 

Willson, 81 N. Y. 341, 37 Am. Rep. 503. The contract involved in this case was for the delivery of 699 boxes of glass at one time, and the buyer accepted the delivery of a part, without knowledge that the rest was not to be delivered, but without any reservation. It was held that the seller could recover for the glass delivered. The case was distinguished from the earlier one on the ground that as the hay was capable of delivery in parcels, the buyer

could not reject a partial delivery, and, hence, there was no waiver of the condition that the whole must be delivered; but that in the case at bar, the delivery of the whole being required to be made at one time, the buyer could decline to receive a partial delivery, and, consequently, acceptance of a partial delivery operated as a waiver of the condition. 

Brady v. Cassidy, 145 N. Y. 171, 39 N. E. 814; 2 A. L. R. 

663, note. § 468.

Defective Delivery Statutory Rule. 

The common law rule that a seller of goods who has only partly performed cannot recover, even on quantum meruit, has been modified by statute. The Personal Property Law, Sales Act, section 125, provides that if 'the buyer has used or disposed of the goods delivered before he knows that the seller is not going to perform his contract in full, the buyer shall not be liable for more than the fair value to him of the goods so received." This, apparently, makes the buyer liable for the "fair value to him" of the goods used, although it is not affirmatively so stated. Kirshman v. Crawford-Plummer Co., 165 App. Div. 259, 150 N. Y. S. 886; Dancey-Davis

Press, Inc. v. American Fashion Co., 120 Misc. 157, 197 

N. Y. S. 844. Where the buyer waives full performance he is liable at the contract rate. Weinberg v. Gash, 94 Misc. 

303, 158 N. Y. S. 179. The failure of the seller to make prompt delivery under one contract is no excuse for the buyer's rejection of a tender of goods under another contract made at the same time. Doxey v. Coates, Bennett & 

Reidenbach, Inc., 181 App. Div. 207, 168 N. Y. S. 76. § 469.

Abandonment of Contract after Part Payment. 

A vendee who, after making a part payment, abandons or refuses to complete performance of a contract, cannot recover any part of the money; and this seems to be true, whether the abandonment be wilful or from necessity occasioned by poverty or illness. Lawrence v. Miller, 86 N. Y. 

131; Steinhardt v. Baker, 163 N. Y. 410, 57 N. E. 629; Hur-

ley v. Anicker, 51 Okla. 97, 151 Pac. 593, L. R. A. 1918B 

538 and note. 

§ 470. Right To Recover Payments Made on Oral Con tract within Statute of Frauds.

Where the vendor is unable or unwilling to perform a contract for the sale of land which does not satisfy the Statute of Frauds, the vendee may recover payments made upon the contract; but where the vendor is willing to perform, the vendee cannot recover the payments made. 

Graham v. Healy, 154 App. Div. 76, 138 N. Y. S. 611; Cook v. Griffith, 76 W. Va. 799, 86 S. E. 879, L. R. A. 1916D 466 and note.

SPECIFIC PERFORMANCE AND INJUNCTION § 471. Specific Performance of Contract. 

A court of equity may enforce a promise to do a thing by a decree of specific performance; and, by the issuance of an injunction, it may enforce a promise not to do a particular act. These remedies may be had only when the common law remedy of an action for damages for breach of contract would be inadequate to repair the loss sustained and where the contract is valid, fair and mutual, its enforcement equitable, and it is susceptible of present performance. The fact that an action at law could not be based upon the contract does not prevent a suit for specific performance. 

Winne v. Winne, 166 N. Y. 263, 59 N. E. 832. While a conmust be mutual in its remedy to warrant specific performance, such mutuality need not exist at the inception of the contract.
