The requirement of mutuality is regarded as satisfied if there is mutuality of remedy at the time the relief is sought. Epstein v. Gluckin, 233 N. Y. 490, 135 

N. E. 861. § 472.

Contracts Relating to Real Property. 

Contracts for the sale of real property may be enforced specifically, because the land may have been selected by con-

sideration of health, convenience, or neighborhood, and money damages cannot replace the same property. The remedy of specific performance is open to both vendor and vendee. Boehly v. Mansing, 52 Misc. 382, 102 N. Y. S. 171;

Reformed Protestant Dutch Church v. Madison Avenue 

Building Co., 214 N. Y. 268, 108 N. E. 444, L. R. A. 1915F 651. 

§ 473. 

Contracts Relating to Personal Property. 

If the contract is for the sale of goods and chattels which may be obtained readily from other dealers, the contract will not be enforced in equity, because the legal remedy for damages is adequate and enables the party recovering damages to buy the same goods or chattels from others with the money recovered; but, if the contract is for the sale of an article which no one but the vendor can supply, the vendee may obtain a decree for specific performance. "While it may be conceded that in general a court of equity will not take upon itself to make such decree where chattel property alone is concerned, its jurisdiction to do so is no longer to be doubted, and it is believed that no good reason exists against its exercise in any case where compensation in damages would not furnish a complete and satisfactory remedy." 

Johnson v. Brooks, 93 N. Y. 337; Menier v. Donald, 98 Misc. 

684, 165 N. Y. S. 50; Adams v. Messinger, 147 Mass. 185, 17 N. E. 491; Southern Iron & Equipment Co. v. Vaughan, 201 Ala. 356, 78 So. 212, L. R. A. 1918E 594 and note.

A contract for the purchase of stock freely sold in the market is not specifically enforceable, since there is an adequate legal remedy; but if the stock is unobtainable from any person other than the vendor, the contract may be specifically enforced. Waddle v. Cabana, 220 N. Y. 18, 114 

N. E. 1054; Rimes v. Rimes, 152 Ga. 721, 111 S. E. 34, 22 

A. L. R. 1030 and note.

On this question, the Personal Property Law, Sales Act, section 149, provides: "Where the seller has broken a contract to deliver specific or ascertained goods, a court having

the powers of a court of equity may, if it thinks fit, on the application of the buyer, by its judgment or decree direct that the contract shall be performed specifically, without giving the seller the option of retaining the goods on payment of damages. The judgment or decree may be unconditional, or upon such terms and conditions as to damages, payment of the price and otherwise, as to the court may seem just." This section is probably declaratory of the rules that already existed. Godley, Outline of Equity, 2d 

Ed., sec. 130. § 474. 

Contracts for Personal Services.

Contracts for personal services never can be specifically enforced, although where a contract stipulates for special, unique, or extraordinary personal services or acts, as, for example, by an eminent actor, singer, artist, or the like, a court of equity will grant an injunction prohibiting the rendition of services for others while under contract with the plaintiff. An injunction thus restraining the breach of a contract is characterized as a negative specific enforcement of the contract. If, however, the services are not special or unique, an injunction will not be granted. Hammerstein v. 

Mann, 206 App. Div. 514, 201 N. Y. S. 577; Shubert Theatrical Co. v. Gallagher, 200 App. Div. 596, 193 N. Y. 

401; Cort v. Lassard, 18 Ore. 221, 22 Pac. 1054, 6 L. R. A. 

653; Chambers v. Davis, 128 Miss. 613, 91 So. 346, 22 

A. L. R. 114 and note. 

Parties cannot contract that courts shall or shall not exercise their discretion against or in favor of either party. 

Dockstader v. Reed, 121 App. Div. 846, 106 N. Y. S. 795. 

Where there is no express negative covenant in a contract, there is some doubt as to whether the courts will imply one. 

Apparently they will in New York. Harry Rogers Theatrical Enterprises v. Comstock, 225 App. Div. 34, 232 

N. Y. S. 1.

An injunction will lie to restrain an employee whose position gives him possession of his employer's trade secrets

from violating his contract not to enter the service of a rival concern within the duration of the contract. McCall Co. v. 

Wright, 198 N. Y. 143, 91 N. E. 516, 31 L. R. A. (N.S.) 249 and note; Clark Paper & Manufacturing Co. v. Stenacher, 236 N. Y. 312, 140 N. E. 708, 29 A. L. R. 1325; Kaumagraph Co. v. Stampagraph Co., 235 N. Y. 1, 138 N. E. 485.

CHAPTER XXII DISCHARGE OF RIGHT OF ACTION 

§ 475. 

Three Modes of Discharge.

The right of action arising from a breach of contract may be discharged in any one of three ways: 

1. By consent of the parties; 2. 

By judgment of a court of competent jurisdiction; or 

3. By lapse of time. § 476. 

Discharge by Consent.

The discharge of a right of action by consent may be by: 

1. Release; or 2. 

Accord and satisfaction. § 477. Release.

A release is a written declaration discharging a right or cause of action. When comprehensive in its scope or effect, as it frequently is, it is familiarly known as a "general release."A release, like other instruments, must be supported by a consideration; but where the release is under seal, the consideration to support it cannot be questioned, since the seal raises a conclusive presumption that there was a sufficient consideration. The common law rule that a sealed instrument is conclusive of consideration is still applicable to releases. Civil Practice Act, sec. 342; Stiebel v. Grosberg, 202 N. Y. 266, 95 N. E. 692, 36 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1147; 

Orthey v. Bogan, 236 N. Y. 351, 140 N. E. 722. But it may be shown by parol: a.

That the consideration expressed in the release under seal is not the true consideration. However, since the seal on an executed instrument is conclusive of a consideration, parol evidence is not admissible for the purpose of estab- 382

DISCHARGE OF RIGHT OF ACTION 

383 lishing that the release under seal is without any consideration to sustain it. b. That the agreed consideration has not been paid. C.

That the release under seal was delivered under an agreement that it should not become operative until the happening of some condition precedent. Richardson on 

Evidence, 4th Ed., sec. 427; Stiebel v. Grosberg, supra. A release may be employed also to discharge a right of action arising out of a tort. Where there are several tortfeasors, the release of one will discharge all, unless the release expressly reserves the right to sue any person not named therein. Gilbert v. Finch, 173 N. Y. 455, 66 N. E. 

133, 61 L. R. A. 807; Berry v. Pullman Co., 249 Fed. 816, L. R. A. 1918F 358.

A right of action arising upon a negotiable instrument, however, may be discharged by a delivery of the instrument, or by a gratuitous renunciation in writing. Negotiable Instruments Law, sec. 203. § 478. 

Discharge By Accord and Satisfaction.

An accord and satisfaction results from the performance of an agreement between two persons whereby a disputed claim is to be settled by one party paying or giving something to the other party in full satisfaction of the claim. 

When the new agreement is executed, it is called "accord and satisfaction." The "accord" is the agreement, and its performance is the "satisfaction." The terms are copulative. Kromer v. Heim, 75 N. Y. 574, 31 Am. Rep. 491.

Perhaps there is no technical expression in the law of contracts that has given rise to so much controversy as that of "accord and satisfaction." Various expressions, such as "compromise and settlement," "novation," and "substituted agreement," have been intermingled with the doctrine of "accord and satisfaction," until even the courts do not seem clear as to the extent to which the doctrine applies. If the settlement of the disputed claim is construed to be a "com-

promise settlement," "novation," or "substituted agreement," the settlement of the disputed claim is scarcely open to further controversy, because the new agreement itself, and not its performance, is accepted as a complete adjustment and satisfaction of the disputed claim. Whether the new agreement is to displace the original contract is primarily a question of intention, usually determinable by the facts. If the conclusion be that the new agreement is to replace the original contract, the latter is discharged independently of whether the new agreement is carried out or not. Katz v. Bernstein, 236 App. Div. 456, 260 N. Y. S. 13.

The difficulty in reconciling the decisions lies in the application of the principles rather than in the principles themselves. Thus, in Moers v. Moers, 229 N. Y. 294, 128 N. E. 

202, 14 A. L. R. 225, the Appellate Division, reversing the trial court, held the agreement to be an "accord." The 

Court of Appeals unanimously called it a "substituted agreement" which probably means a "novation." It should be observed that if the new agreement is accepted in satisfaction, the original cause of action is merged and extinguished. For an interesting discussion of the question, read the opinion by Chief Judge Cardozo in Hudson v. 

Yonkers Fruit Co., 258 N. Y. 168, 179 N. E. 373, 80 A. L. R. 

1052; also note in Brooklyn Law Review, May, 1933, p. 301.

The federal courts manifest a disposition to use the terms "accord and satisfaction" and "compromise and settlement" interchangeably. Shubert v. Rosenberger, 204 Fed. 934, L. R. A. 1918F 358. In the instant case, the debtor not having performed the new "compromise and settlement" agreement, the creditor was permitted to maintain an action on the original contract. The doctrine of "accord and satisfaction" was thus applied to a "compromise and settlement" agreement. Referring to these terms, the Court stated: "The general rule is that to be a defense to the original claim an accord must be followed by satisfaction and a compromise by settlement."

An accord and satisfaction may result from the express

DISCHARGE OF RIGHT OF ACTION 

385 agreement of the parties, or it may be implied from their conduct. Thus, where there is a bona fide dispute as to the amount due a creditor by a debtor and the debtor remits his check to the creditor stating that it is in full for the amount of the creditor's claim, the retention and appropriation of the check is an accord and satisfaction and, therefore, extinguishes the creditor's claim. Schuttinger v. 

Woodruff, 259 N. Y. 212, 181 N. E. 361; Fuller v. Kemp, 138 N. Y. 231, 33 N. E. 1034, 20 L. R. A. 785 and note; 

Nassoiy v. Tomlinson, 148 N. Y. 326, 42 N. E. 715. However, in Gaston & Co. v. Storch, 253 N. Y. 68, 170 N. E. 

496, the Court of Appeals, reversing the Appellate Division, First Department, held that the acceptance and retention of a check marked in "full settlement" was not an accord and satisfaction where the debtor thereafter failed to perform a certain condition subsequent. § 479.

Discharge of Right of Action by Judgment. 

A judgment by a court of competent jurisdiction discharges all remedies growing out of the same cause of action. If the judgment is in favor of the plaintiff, the cause of action is merged in the judgment; if the judgment is against the plaintiff, he is precluded from further proceedings. The judgment is also conclusive even as to matters which might have been presented and litigated, but were not. Reich v. Cochran, 151 N. Y. 122, 45 N. E. 367, 37 

L. R. A. 805; Pakas v. Hollingshead, 184 N. Y. 211, 77 

N. E. 40, 3 L. R. A. (N.S.) 1042 and note. § 480. 

Discharge by Arbitration and Award.

If parties submit their controversy to a board of arbitrators, as they may, their award, when judgment has been properly entered thereon, effectually discharges all rights of action and is a bar to an action on the original claim. "It may be enforced as if it had been rendered in an action in the court in which it is entered." Civil Practice Act, sec. 19

1463; Arbitration Law, secs. 2 and 3; Young v. Crescent 

Development Co., 240 N. Y. 244, 148 N. E. 510. § 481. 

Discharge by Lapse of Time.

By virtue of the statute of limitations, a right of action is lost if a suit is not begun within a certain time after such right or cause of action arose. The statute of limitations is embraced in the Civil Practice Act, sections 10 to 61, inclusive, but particular reference is made to sections 47, 48, and 56, because such sections regulate the time within which actions based upon contractual rights must be commenced.
